\hline\cr M\Rightarrow M\end{array}\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}M\Rightarrow M^{\prime}\quad N\Rightarrow N^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr(\lambda x.M)N\Rightarrow[N^{\prime}/x]M^{\prime}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}M\Rightarrow M^{\prime}\quad N\Rightarrow N^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr MN\Rightarrow M^{\prime}N^{\prime}\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}M\Rightarrow M^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\lambda x.M\Rightarrow\lambda x.M^{\prime}\end{array}~.\par\end{array}

Exercise 7.4

Let
M\equiv(\lambda x.Ix)(II)
where

I\equiv\lambda z.z
.
What is the minimum number of parallel reductions
needed to reduce
M
to
I
?

First we notice the following structural and substitution properties
of parallel reduction.

Proposition 7.2

Parallel reduction enjoys
the following structural properties:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\lambda x.M\Rightarrow N\\
\hline\cr N\equiv\lambda x.M^{\prime},\qquad M\Rightarrow M^{\prime}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}MN\Rightarrow L\\
\hline\cr\begin{array}[]{c}(L\equiv M^{\prime}N^{\prime},\qquad M\Rightarrow M^{\prime},\qquad N\Rightarrow N^{\prime})\ \mbox{ or }\\
(M\equiv\lambda x.P,\quad P\Rightarrow P^{\prime},\quad N\Rightarrow N^{\prime},\quad L\equiv[N^{\prime}/x]P^{\prime})\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Proof
. By case analysis on the definition of parallel reduction.
{\Box}

Proposition 7.3

Parallel reduction enjoys the following
substitution property:

\begin{array}[]{c}M\Rightarrow M^{\prime}\qquad N\Rightarrow N^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr[N/x]M\Rightarrow[N^{\prime}/x]M^{\prime}\end{array}~.

Proof
. By induction
on the definition of
M\Rightarrow M^{\prime}
.
For the base case we also need an induction on the structure of
M
.
{\Box}

We are then ready to prove
strong confluence
of parallel reduction.

Proposition 7.4

Parallel reduction enjoys the following strong confluence property:

\begin{array}[]{c}\forall\,M,N_{1},N_{2}\;\>(N_{1}\Leftarrow M\Rightarrow N_{2})\\
\hline\cr\exists\,P\;\>(N_{1}\Rightarrow P\Leftarrow N_{2})\end{array}~.

Proof
. One can proceed by induction on
M\Rightarrow N_{1}
and case analysis
on
M\Rightarrow N_{2}
to close the diagram.
{\Box}

Corollary 7.1
(confluence,
\beta
)

\beta
-reduction is confluent.

Proof
. We have:

\rightarrow_{\beta}\ \subset\ \Rightarrow\ \subset\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}~.

If
M\rightarrow_{\beta}\cdots\rightarrow_{\beta}N_{i}
,
i=1,2
then

M\Rightarrow\cdots\Rightarrow N_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
Now apply strong confluence to close the diagram and build
P
such
that
N_{i}\Rightarrow\cdots\Rightarrow P
,
i=1,2
.
This implies
N_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}P
,
i=1,2

and, by transitivity of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}
, we conclude that

N_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}P
,
i=1,2
.
{\Box}

The
\beta
-rule is the basic rule of the
\lambda
-calculus.
The second most popular rule is the
\eta
-rule.

Definition 7.8
(
\eta
-reduction)

The
\eta
-rule is defined by:

\begin{array}[]{ll}(\eta)&C[\lambda x.Mx]\rightarrow C[M]\qquad\mbox{if }x\not\in{\sf fv}(M)~,\end{array}

for
C
context,
M

\lambda
-term, and
x
variable.

The
\eta
-rule is a kind of
extensionality rule
.
If we read it backwards, it asserts that ‘every
\lambda
-term is a
function’. This intuition can actually be made precise in
the model theory of
\lambda
-calculus.

Proposition 7.5
(confluence
\beta\eta
)

The following properties hold:

1.

\eta
reduction is strongly confluent in the following
sense:

\begin{array}[]{c}M\rightarrow_{\eta}N_{i}\quad i=1,2\quad N_{1}\not\equiv N_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,P\;\>(N_{i}\rightarrow_{\eta}P,\quad i=1,2)\end{array}~.

2.

The
\beta
and
\eta
reductions commute
in the following sense:

\begin{array}[]{c}M\rightarrow_{\beta}N_{1}\quad M\rightarrow_{\eta}N_{2}\quad N_{1}\not\equiv N_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,P\;\>(N_{1}(\rightarrow_{\eta})^{*}P,\quad N_{2}\rightarrow_{\beta}P)\end{array}~.

3.

The
(\rightarrow_{\beta})^{*}
and
(\rightarrow_{\eta})^{*}
reductions commute in the
following sense:

\begin{array}[]{c}M(\rightarrow_{\beta})^{*}N_{1}\quad M(\rightarrow_{\eta})^{*}N_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,P\;\>(N_{1}(\rightarrow_{\eta})^{*}P,\quad N_{2}(\rightarrow_{\beta})^{*}P)\end{array}~.

4.

\beta\eta
reduction is confluent.

Proof
.
(1)\;

Two redexes that superpose have the shape:
\lambda x.C[\lambda y.My]x
.
Analyze what can happen.

(2)\;
If the
\beta
redex contains the
\eta
redex we can have the following situations:

•

(\lambda x.Mx)N
: the reduced are identical.

•

(\lambda x.C[\lambda y.My])N
: close the diagram in one step.

•

(\lambda x.M)C[\lambda y.Ny]
: it may take
0
,
1
or more
\eta
steps to close the diagram.

On the other hand, if the
\eta
redex contains the
\beta
redex we can have:

•

\lambda x.(\lambda y.M)x
: the reduced are identical.

•

\lambda x.C[(\lambda y.M_{1})M_{2}]x
: close in one step.

(3)\;

First show commutation of
(\rightarrow_{\eta})^{*}
with respect to
\rightarrow_{\beta}\cup{\it Id}
. Then proceed by induction on the number of
\beta
reductions.

(4)\;

Consider the number of alternations of
(\rightarrow_{\beta})^{*}

and
(\rightarrow_{\eta})^{*}
.
{\Box}

Example 7.2

Here is an extension of the
\lambda
-calculus that
does not preserve confluence (we refer to
[
Bar84
]

for a proof).
We add to the language a constant
D
and the rule:

Dxx\rightarrow x~.

This rule may seem artificial, but it is actually
a simplification of a natural rule called
surjective paring (an extensionality rule for pairs)
which also leads to a non-confluent system:

D(Fx)(Sx)\rightarrow x~.

Here
D
,
F
,
S
are constants where intuitively
D

is the pairing while
F
and
S
are the first and second projection.
We stress that here the property that fails is just confluence (not local confluence). Indeed a surjective pairing rule is introduced in terminating
typed
\lambda
-calculi. By proposition
2.8
,
surjective pairing in these calculi is confluent.

7.3
Programming

All
partial recursive functions
can be represented in the (type free)

\lambda
-calculus. Thus the
\lambda
-calculus,
regarded as a computational model, is
Turing equivalent
.
Proving this result is a matter of programming in the
\lambda
-calculus.
The proof we outline below relies on the following

definition
of the partial recursive functions.

Definition 7.9
(minimalisation)

Given a total function
f:\mathbf{N}^{k+1}\rightarrow\mathbf{N}
a
partial function
\mu(f):\mathbf{N}^{k}\rightharpoonup\mathbf{N}
is defined
by minimization as follows:

\mu(f)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{0}&\mbox{if }x_{0}={\it min}\{x\in\mathbf{N}\mid f(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=0\}\\
\uparrow&\mbox{if }\forall\,x\;\>f(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})>0\end{array}\right.

where
\uparrow
means that the function is undefined.

Definition 7.10
(partial recursive functions)

The set of partial recursive functions is the smallest set of functions on
(vectors of) natural numbers which contains the basic functions (zero, successor, projections) and
is closed under function composition, primitive recursion
(see chapter
6.2
), and minimization.

We discuss next the representation of partial recursive functions
in the
\lambda
-calculus.

Definition 7.11
(Church numerals)

A natural number
n
is represented by the following
\lambda
-term
\underline{n}

known as Church numeral:

\underline{n}\equiv\lambda f.\lambda x.(f\cdots(fx)\cdots)\qquad\mbox{(Church numerals)}

(7.1)

where
f
is applied
n
times.

In a sense this is similar to the
tally natural numbers

considered in chapter
6.2
.
We shall see in chapter
13
that the inductive definition
of natural numbers actually
suggests
their
representation in the
\lambda
-calculus as Church numerals.

We also have to fix a class of
\lambda
-terms that represent a
diverging computation. A natural choice is to consider the

\lambda
-terms that do
not
have a
head normal form
.

Definition 7.12
(head normal form)

A
\lambda
-term is (has)
a head normal if it has the shape (it reduces to a
\lambda
-term
of the shape):

\lambda x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}.xM_{1}\cdots M_{m}\qquad n,m\geq 0.

Definition 7.13
(function representation)

A
\lambda
-term
F
represents
a partial function
f:\mathbf{N}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbf{N}
if
for all
n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbf{N}
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}f(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})=m&\mbox{ iff }&F\underline{n_{1}}\cdots\underline{n_{k}}=_{\beta}\underline{m}\\
f(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})\uparrow&\mbox{ iff }&F\underline{n_{1}}\cdots\underline{n_{k}}\mbox{ has no head normal form}.\end{array}

We can represent the arithmetic functions
addition, successor,
and multiplication
with the following
\lambda
-terms:

\begin{array}[]{lll}A&\equiv\lambda n.\lambda m.\lambda f.\lambda x.(n\ f)(m\ f\ x)&\mbox{(addition)}\\
S&\equiv\lambda n.A\ n\ \underline{1}&\mbox{(successor)}\\
M&\equiv\lambda n.\lambda m.\lambda f.n(m\ f)&\mbox{(multiplication).}\end{array}

To represent
boolean values
we introduce the following

\lambda
-terms:

\begin{array}[]{llll}T\equiv\lambda x.\lambda y.x&\mbox{(true)}~,\qquad F\equiv\lambda x.\lambda y.y&\mbox{(false).}\end{array}

Then an
if-then-else

\lambda
-term can be defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\it C}&\equiv\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda z.x\ y\ z&\mbox{(if-then-else).}\end{array}

The reader may check that:

CTxy\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}x
and
CFxy\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}y
.
A
test-for-zero

\lambda
-term on Church numerals can
be defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\it Z}&\equiv\lambda n.n(\lambda x.F)T&\mbox{(test-for-zero).}\end{array}

We can also introduce
\lambda
-terms to
build pairs
and
to
project pairs
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}P&\equiv\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda z.z\ x\ y&\mbox{(pairing)}\\
P_{1}&\equiv\lambda p.p(\lambda x,y.x)&\mbox{(first projection)}\\
P_{2}&\equiv\lambda p.p(\lambda x,y.y)&\mbox{(second projection).}\end{array}

Again, the reader may check that
P_{i}(PM_{1}M_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}M_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

Exercise 7.5

Check that the
\lambda
-term:

{\it Pd}\ \equiv\ \lambda n,f,x.n(\lambda g,h.h(gf))(\lambda y.x)(\lambda z.z)

represents the predecessor function where it is assumed that the predecessor
of
0
is
0
(chapter
13
provides
a rational reconstruction of this complicated
\lambda
-term).
Define
\lambda
-terms to represent the subtraction function,
where
m-n=0
if
n>m
, and the exponential function
n^{m}
.

Let us now consider the
3

composition mechanisms
, namely:
function composition, primitive recursion, and minimization.
It should be clear that
function composition
can be directly represented
in the
\lambda
-calculus. Primitive recursion can be regarded as a particular
case of recursive function definition.
In turn, a
recursive function definition
such as:

{\sf letrec}\ g(x)=M\ {\sf in}\ N~,

where
g
may appear in
M
and
N
is coded in the
\lambda
-calculus as:

(\lambda g.N)(Y(\lambda g.\lambda x.M))~,

where
Y
is the fixed point combinator of exercise
7.2
or
7.3
.
Moreover, recursive definitions provide a direct
mechanism to mimick definitions by minimization.
Given a function
f
, consider the following recursive
definition of the function
g
:

g(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})={\sf if}\ (f(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=0)\ {\sf then}\ x_{0}\ {\sf else}\ g(x_{0}+1,x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})~.

Then
\mu(f)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=g(0,x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
.
Putting all together, we have the following result.

Proposition 7.6

For all partial recursive functions
f:\mathbf{N}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbf{N}
there
is a closed
\lambda
-term
F
which represents
f
in the
sense of definition
7.13
.

7.4
Combinatory logic (*)

Combinatory logic is a relative of the
\lambda
-calculus
which can be presented as a term rewriting system.

Definition 7.14
(combinatory logic)

We consider a binary application operation
@
and
two constants
K
and
S
.
As in the
\lambda
-calculus, we write
MN
for
@(M,N)

and let application associate to the left.
The system comes with two term rewriting rules:

\begin{array}[]{cc}K\ x\ y\rightarrow x~,&S\ x\ y\ z\rightarrow x\ z(y\ z)~.\end{array}

It turns out that in combinatory logic there
is a way to
simulate

\lambda
-abstraction.

Definition 7.15

We define a function
\lambda
that takes a variable and a
term of combinatory logic and produces a term of combinatory
logic. We abbreviate
SKK
as
I
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\lambda(x,x)&=I\\
\lambda(x,M)&=KM&\mbox{if }x\notin{\sf var}(M)\\
\lambda(x,MN)&=S(\lambda(x,M))(\lambda(x,N))~.\end{array}

The fact that we called the function above
`\lambda^{\prime}
is justified
by the following proposition.

Proposition 7.7

If
M,N
are terms of combinatory logic and
x
is a variable
then:

\lambda(x,M)N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}[N/x]M~.

Proof
. By induction on
M
following the definition of the translation.
{\Box}

Exercise 7.6

Using the fact that combinatory logic (CL) is a TRS prove
local confluence of CL.
Then adapt the
method of parallel reduction presented in section
7.2

to prove the confluence of CL.

Combinatory logic seems mathematically simpler than the
\lambda
-calculus.
Why is it not used? One reason is that terms written in combinatory logic
tend to be unreadable. Another deeper reason is that the notion
of conversion induced by the rules
S
and
K
is weaker than the one induced
by the
\beta
rule. For instance, the reader may check that
the translations in CL of the
\lambda
-terms

\lambda z.(\lambda x.x)z
and
\lambda z.z

do not have a common reduct.
An alternative approach goes through the notion of
closure

(see following chapter
8
). This is quite
appropriate for discussing implementation techniques,
but as in combinatory logic, the notation tends to become less
manageable.

7.5
Summary and references

The
\lambda
-calculus is a minimal notation to represent
higher-order functions. The
\lambda
-terms are transformed
according to one basic rewriting rule: the
\beta
-rule.
The
\lambda
-calculus with the
\beta
-rule
is a confluent rewriting system and it is sufficiently
expressive to represent all partial recursive functions.
A second rule, the
\eta
-rule, can be added to the system while
preserving confluence.
The
\lambda
-calculus is
not
a term rewriting system but
there are term rewriting systems such as
combinatory logic

which can mimick to some extent the behavior of
\lambda
-terms.

The
\lambda
-calculus was introduced by Church as
part of an investigation in the formal foundations of
mathematics and logic
[
Chu40
]
.
At the time, the
\lambda
-calculus provided one of
the concurrent formalizations
of
partial recursive functions
,
i.e.
, computable
functions, along with,
e.g.
, Turing machines.
The foundational character of the language is even stronger
when it is enriched with
types
. We shall start addressing this point
in chapter
10
.
The related system of combinatory logic is based on work
by Schönfinkel and Curry.
The book
[
Bar84
]
is the basic reference for the
type-free
\lambda
-calculus. It is enough to skim the first
introductory chapters to have an idea of the great variety
of results connected to the formalism.

Chapter 8
Weak reduction strategies, closures, and abstract machines

Full
\beta(\eta)
-reduction is the basis for the symbolic manipulation
of
\lambda
-terms,
e.g.
, in proof assistants, in program
transformations, and in higher-order unification and pattern-matching.
However, when the
\lambda
-calculus is regarded as the core
of a programming language it is sensible to consider
weaker
reduction
strategies. This chapter focuses on these weaker reduction strategies and their
implementation.

8.1
Weak reduction strategies

A
weak
reduction strategy is a strategy to reduce
\lambda
-terms
that does
not
reduce under functional abstractions. Thus in
a weak reduction strategy all
\lambda
-terms of the form
\lambda x.M

are normal forms.

Definition 8.1
(weak reduction)

We define the weak
\beta
-reduction relation
\rightarrow_{w}
as
the least binary relation on
\lambda
-terms such that:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr(\lambda x.M)N\rightarrow_{w}[N/x]M\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}M\rightarrow_{w}M^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr MN\rightarrow_{w}M^{\prime}N\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}N\rightarrow_{w}N^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr MN\rightarrow_{w}MN^{\prime}\end{array}~.\end{array}

As such weak reduction is
not
confluent. For instance,
we have:

K(II)\rightarrow_{w}KI~,\qquad K(II)\rightarrow_{w}\lambda x.II~,

and
KI
and
\lambda x.II
have no common reduct. The problem here is
that the redex
II
is under a
\lambda
and cannot be reduced.
When considering the
\lambda
-calculus as the core
of a programming language, the usual approach is to fix
a particular

deterministic
weak reduction strategy.
Two popular ones we discuss next are known as

call-by-name
and
call-by-value
.
The definition of these strategies relies on a notion of
value
.

Definition 8.2
(value)

A value
V
is a closed
\lambda
-term of the shape

\lambda x.M
(a
\lambda
-abstraction).

In the following, the call-by-name and call-by-value reduction
strategies are defined on
closed

\lambda
-terms. We
actually define the reduction strategies in
3
different ways
which turn out to be equivalent.

Definition 8.3
(call-by-name)

We define the call-by-name reduction relation
\rightarrow_{n}
as
the least binary relation on closed
\lambda
-terms such that:

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr(\lambda x.M)N\rightarrow_{n}[N/x]M\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}M\rightarrow_{n}M^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr MN\rightarrow_{n}M^{\prime}N\end{array}~.\par\end{array}

Definition 8.4
(call-by-value)

We define the call-by-value reduction relation
\rightarrow_{v}
as
the least binary relation on closed
\lambda
-terms such that:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr(\lambda x.M)V\rightarrow_{v}[V/x]M\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}M\rightarrow_{v}M^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr MN\rightarrow_{v}M^{\prime}N\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}N\rightarrow_{v}N^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr VN\rightarrow_{v}VN^{\prime}\end{array}~.\end{array}

Remark 8.1

The basic difference between call-by-name and call-by-value is that
in the latter we insist that the term passed to the function is a
value.
Also notice that in the definitions above, we have taken the convention that
the function is reduced before the argument. Of course, an alternative
definition where the argument is reduced before the function
is possible. This choice only matters if the language has
side-effects (cf. chapter
17
).

The definitions
8.3
and
8.4
give a strategy
to look for a subterm which is a redex of the right shape.
The one-hole context which sourrounds the redex is called

evaluation context
.

Definition 8.5
(evaluation contexts)

Call-by-name and call-by-value evaluation contexts are denoted with
E,E^{\prime},\ldots
and
are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}E::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid EM&\mbox{(call-by-name evaluation context)}\\
E::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid EM\mid\!\!\mid VE&\mbox{(call-by-value evaluation context).}\end{array}

Proposition 8.1
(decomposition)

Let
M
be a closed
\lambda
-term. Then either
M
is a value or there
is a unique call-by-name (call-by-value) evaluation context
E

such that:

M\equiv E[(\lambda x.M_{1})M_{2}]\qquad(M\equiv E[(\lambda x.M_{1})V])~.

Proof
. By induction on the structure of
M
.

M
cannot be a variable because it is closed.
If
M
is a
\lambda
-abstraction then it is a value.
Suppose,
M\equiv M^{\prime}M^{\prime\prime}
.
We consider the case for
call-by-name
.
If
M^{\prime}
is a value then
it must be a
\lambda
-abstraction and
E\equiv[~]
.
Otherwise, by inductive hypothesis
M^{\prime}\equiv E^{\prime}[\Delta]
where

\Delta
is a
\beta
-redex and we take
E\equiv E^{\prime}M^{\prime\prime}
.
{\Box}

We can rely on evaluation contexts to provide alternative and
equivalent definitions of call-by-name and call-by-value.

Definition 8.6

Let
\rightarrow_{en}
be the least binary reduction relation on closed

\lambda
-terms such
that:

M\rightarrow_{en}N\mbox{if }M\equiv E[(\lambda x.M_{1})M_{2}]\mbox{ and }N\equiv E[[M_{2}/x]M_{1}],E\mbox{ call-by-name evaluation context.}

Let
\rightarrow_{ev}
be the least binary reduction relation on closed

\lambda
-terms such that:

M\rightarrow_{ev}N\mbox{if }M\equiv E[(\lambda x.M_{1})V]\mbox{ and }N\equiv E[[V/x]M_{1}],E\mbox{ call-by-value evaluation context.}

Proposition 8.2

The call-by-name reduction relation
\rightarrow_{n}
coincides with
the relation
\rightarrow_{en}
and the call-by-value reduction relation

\rightarrow_{v}
coincides with the relation
\rightarrow_{ev}
.

Proof
. We consider the proof for call-by-name.
To show that
\rightarrow_{n}\subseteq\rightarrow_{en}
,
we proceed by induction of the
proof height of
M\rightarrow_{n}N
.
For the base case take
E=[~]
.
For the inductive case, suppose
MN\rightarrow_{n}M^{\prime}N
because

M\rightarrow_{n}M^{\prime}
. Then by inductive hypothesis, there are

E^{\prime}
and
\Delta\equiv(\lambda x.M_{1})M_{2}
such that

M\equiv E^{\prime}[\Delta]
and
M^{\prime}\equiv E^{\prime}[[M_{2}/x]M_{1}]
.
Then take
E=E^{\prime}N
,
MN\equiv E[\Delta]
,
M^{\prime}N\equiv E[\Delta^{\prime}]
,
and
\Delta^{\prime}\equiv[M_{2}/x]M_{1}
.

In the other direction, suppose
E[\Delta]\rightarrow_{{\it en}}E[\Delta^{\prime}]

where
\Delta\equiv(\lambda x.M_{1})M_{2}
and
\Delta^{\prime}\equiv[M_{2}/x]M_{1}
.
We proceed by induction on the
structure of the evaluation context
E
.
If
E=[~]
then
\Delta\rightarrow_{n}\Delta^{\prime}
.
If
E=E^{\prime}N
then by induction hypothesis,
E^{\prime}[\Delta]\rightarrow_{n}E^{\prime}[\Delta^{\prime}]

and therefore
E[\Delta]\rightarrow_{n}E[\Delta^{\prime}]
.
{\Box}

Yet another presentation of call-by-name and call-by-value
consists in defining a big-step (cf. section
1.1
)
evaluation relation
\Downarrow
.

Definition 8.7
(call-by-name evaluation)

The call-by-name evaluation relation
\Downarrow_{n}
is the least binary relation
on closed
\lambda
-terms such that:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr V\Downarrow_{n}V\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}M\Downarrow_{n}\lambda x.M^{\prime}\qquad[N/x]M\Downarrow_{n}V\\
\hline\cr MN\Downarrow_{n}V\end{array}~.\end{array}

Definition 8.8
(call-by-value evaluation)

The call-by-value evaluation relation
\Downarrow_{v}
is the least binary relation
on closed
\lambda
-terms such that:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr V\Downarrow_{v}V\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}M\Downarrow_{v}\lambda x.M^{\prime}\qquad N\Downarrow_{v}V^{\prime}\qquad[V^{\prime}/x]M\Downarrow V\\
\hline\cr MN\Downarrow_{v}V\end{array}~.\end{array}

Proposition 8.3

Let
M
be a closed
\lambda
-term. Then:

1.

If
M\Downarrow_{n}V
then
M(\rightarrow_{n})^{*}V
.

2.

If
M\rightarrow_{n}M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\Downarrow_{n}V
then
M\Downarrow_{n}V
.

3.

If
M(\rightarrow_{n})^{*}M^{\prime}\not\rightarrow_{n}
then
M\Downarrow_{n}M^{\prime}
.

The same properties hold if we replace
\Downarrow_{n}
with
\Downarrow_{v}

and
\rightarrow_{n}
with
\rightarrow_{v}
, respectively.

Proof
.
(1)\;
By induction on the proof height of the judgment

M\Downarrow_{n}V
. The base case follows by reflexivity of
(\rightarrow_{n})^{*}
.
For the inductive step, suppose
MN\Downarrow_{n}V
because

M\Downarrow_{n}\lambda x.M_{1}
and
[N/x]M_{1}\Downarrow_{n}V
. By inductive hypothesis,

M(\rightarrow_{n})^{*}\lambda x.M_{1}
. Then:

MN(\rightarrow_{n})^{*}(\lambda x.M_{1})N\rightarrow_{n}[N/x]M_{1}~,

and by inductive hypothesis
[N/x]M_{1}(\rightarrow_{n})^{*}V
.

(2)\;
If
M\rightarrow_{n}M^{\prime}
then

M\equiv(\lambda x.M_{1})M_{2}N_{1}\cdots N_{k}
and

M^{\prime}\equiv[M_{2}/x]M_{1}N_{1}\cdots N_{k}
.
If
M^{\prime}\Downarrow_{n}V
we must have:

[M_{2}/x]M_{1}\Downarrow_{n}\lambda x_{1}.P_{1},\quad[N_{1}/x_{1}]P_{1}\Downarrow_{n}\lambda x_{2}.P_{2},\quad\cdots\quad[N_{k}/x_{k}]P_{k}\Downarrow_{n}V~.

Then to prove
M\Downarrow V
it suffices to extend
the proof for
M^{\prime}
with an additional step

\lambda x.M_{1}\Downarrow_{n}\lambda x.M_{1}
.

(3)\;

By proposition
8.1
, if
M^{\prime}
does not reduce then it is
a value and we have
M^{\prime}\Downarrow_{n}M^{\prime}
. If
M
reduces to
M^{\prime}
in
k
steps
then we apply property (2)
k
times starting from
M^{\prime}
.
{\Box}

8.2
Static vs. dynamic binding

The implementation of the reduction of a
\beta
-redex such as

(\lambda x.M)N
is usually decomposed in two steps.

•

The formal parameter
x
is bound to the
argument

N
.
The collection of bindings is called an
environment
.

•

When the formal parameter
x
is
used
in the body of the function

M
, the argument bound to it is retrieved from the environment.

This high-level description leaves many design choices unspecified.
One basic issue is what exactly constitutes
an ‘argument’. Indeed, in the programming languages jargon,
one speaks of
static
vs.
dynamic
binding.
This issue already arises in a very simple language
of expressions whose syntax is as follows, where as usual

{\it id}::=x\mid\!\!\mid y\mid\!\!\mid\cdots
:

e::=\bot\mid\!\!\mid n\mid\!\!\mid{\it id}\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}~{\it id}=e~{\sf in}~e\mid\!\!\mid{\sf quote}(e)\mid\!\!\mid{\sf unquote}(e)~\qquad\mbox{(expressions).}

Here
\bot
represents a computation that
diverges
,

n
is an
integer
,
quote
allows to
freeze
the
evaluation of an expression and
unquote
to
unfreeze
it.
We denote with
{\it Exp}
the set of expressions in this language.
The collection of
values
is defined by:

v::=n\mid\!\!\mid{\sf quote}(e)~\qquad\mbox{(values).}

It is possible to encode this simple language in the
\lambda
-calculus
and reproduce the same phenomena we describe next.

Definition 8.9
(dynamic environment)

A dynamic environment is a
partial function
\eta:{\it Id}\rightharpoonup{\it Exp}

with finite domain mapping
identifiers to expressions.

Table
8.1
introduces two evaluation relations
for this language of expressions with dynamic binding following
either a
by-name
or a
by-value
strategy.
The basic assertion
e[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]
states that an expression

e
in an environment
\eta
evaluates to a value
v
in an environment

\eta^{\prime}
.
The first
3
rules defining the assertion
are shared while two distinct rules, one for by-name and
the other for by-value, cover expressions of the shape

{\sf let}~x=e^{\prime}~{\sf in}~e
.

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr v[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta]\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\eta(x)[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\\
\hline\cr x[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}e[\eta]\Downarrow{\sf quote}(e^{\prime})[\eta^{\prime}]\hskip 9.24994pte^{\prime}[\eta^{\prime}]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime\prime}]\\
\hline\cr{\sf unquote}(e)[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime\prime}]\end{array}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{By-name:}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}e[\eta[e^{\prime}/x]]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}],\\
\hline\cr({\sf let}~x=e^{\prime}~{\sf in}~e)[\eta])\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\end{array}\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{By-value:}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}e^{\prime}[\eta]\Downarrow v^{\prime}[\eta^{\prime}]\hskip 9.24994pte[\eta[v^{\prime}/x]]\Downarrow v[\eta]\\
\hline\cr({\sf let}~x=e^{\prime}~{\sf in}~e)[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta]\end{array}\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 8.1:
Dynamic binding with by-name and by-value evaluation

In
dynamic binding
, an environment binds an expression with an identifier.
However, in turn the expression may contain identifiers and their
binding with other expressions may be lost.
In
static binding
, we introduce a more complex object
which is called a
closure
. This is an expression along with
an
environment
that associates identifiers with
closures
.
This looks like a circular definition of closure and environment
but things can be well-defined in an inductive style as follows.

Definition 8.10
(static environment)

The set of environments
{\it Env}
is the smallest set of
partial functions on Id such that
if

e_{i}\in{\it Exp}
,
\eta_{i}\in{\it Env}
and
{\sf fv}(e_{i})\subseteq{\it dom}(\eta_{i})

for
i=1,\ldots,n
(
n\geq 0
) then

[e_{1}[\eta_{1}]/x_{1},\ldots,e_{n}[\eta_{n}]/x_{n}]\in{\it Env}~.

We denote with
\emptyset
the empty environment.

Definition 8.11
(closure)

A closure is a pair
e[\eta]
composed of an expression
e\in{\it Exp}
and an
environment
\eta\in{\it Env}
such that
{\sf fv}(e)\subseteq{\it dom}(\eta)
.

Table
8.2
defines an evaluation relation whose
basic assertion is
e[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta]
. The first
3
rules are
formally identical to those for dynamic binding but recall that
now
\eta
is a
static
environment.

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr v[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta]\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\eta(x)\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\\
\hline\cr x[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}e[\eta]\Downarrow{\sf quote}(e^{\prime})[\eta^{\prime}]\hskip 18.49988pte^{\prime}[\eta^{\prime}]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime\prime}]\end{array}\\
\hline\cr{\sf unquote}(e)[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime\prime}]\end{array}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{By-name:}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}e[\eta[e^{\prime}[\eta]/x]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\\
\hline\cr({\sf let}~x=e^{\prime}~{\sf in}~e)[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\end{array}\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{By-value:}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}e^{\prime}[\eta]\Downarrow u[\eta^{\prime\prime}]\hskip 18.49988pte[\eta[u[\eta^{\prime\prime}]/x])\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\end{array}\\
\hline\cr({\sf let}~x=e^{\prime}~{\sf in}~e)[\eta]\Downarrow v[\eta^{\prime}]\end{array}\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 8.2:
Static binding with by-name and by-value evaluation

We have presented four possible semantics of our language of expressions:
dynamic by-name, dynamic by-value, static by-name, and static by-value.
We can deem that two of them are different if we can a find
a closed expression where one produces a value and the other another
value or no value at all.

Proposition 8.4

The four presented semantics are different.

Proof
. The expression
e\equiv{\sf let}\ x=\bot\ {\sf in}\ 3

distinguishes evaluation
by-name
and
by-value
in
both static and dynamic binding.
Indeed, the evaluation
by-name
returns a value and
the one
by-value
does not.
Next consider the following expressions and evaluations:

\begin{array}[]{lll}e_{1}\equiv{\sf let}\ x=3\ {\sf in}\ e_{2},&e_{2}\equiv{\sf let}\ y=x\ {\sf in}\ e_{3},&e_{3}\equiv{\sf let}\ x=5\ {\sf in}\ y~.\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{llll}e_{1}[\emptyset]\Downarrow 5[\emptyset]&\mbox{(dynamic, by-name)}&e_{1}[\emptyset]\Downarrow 3[\emptyset]&\mbox{(dynamic, by-value)}\\
e_{1}[\emptyset]\Downarrow 3[\emptyset]&\mbox{(static, by-name)}&e_{1}[\emptyset]\Downarrow 3[\emptyset]&\mbox{(static, by-value).}\end{array}

Thus it remains to distinguish dynamic and static binding with
a by-value evaluation. To do this, we rely on the

quote
,
unquote
operations and
modify the expressions above as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}e_{1}\equiv{\sf let}\ x=3\ {\sf in}\ e_{2},\par&e_{2}\equiv{\sf let}\ y={\sf quote}(x)\ {\sf in}\ e_{3},\par&e_{3}\equiv{\sf let}\ x=5\ {\sf in}\ {\sf unquote}(y)~.\end{array}

Now we have:

e_{1}[\emptyset]\Downarrow 5[\emptyset]
with dynamic binding, by-value and

e_{1}[\emptyset]\Downarrow 3[\emptyset]
with static binding, by-value.
{\Box}

The examples in the previous proof show that the correct
implementation of
\lambda
-calculus relies on static
binding; henceforth dynamic binding will be ignored.

8.3
Environments and closures

We adapt to the call-by-name and call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus the notions of environment and closure
we have discussed in the previous section
8.2
.
To this end, we reuse the notion of environment
modulo the replacement of the expressions
(denoted
e
) by the
\lambda
-terms (denoted
M
).
A
closure
, denoted with
c,c^{\prime},\ldots
,
is now a pair composed of a
\lambda
-term and an environment that
we shall write as
M[\eta]
.
A (closure)
value
, denoted with
v,v^{\prime},\ldots
,
is a closure whose term is a
\lambda
-abstraction.
Table
8.3
describes the evaluation rules for
closures according to a call-by-name and a call-by-value strategy.
The first two rules are shared by both strategies.
Notice that the
\beta
-rule is now decomposed in a rule where the
argument is bound as a closure to the formal parameter in the environment
and a rule where the closure associated with the formal parameter is retrieved
from the environment.

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr v\Downarrow v\end{array}\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\eta(x)\Downarrow v\\
\hline\cr x[\eta]\Downarrow v\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{By-name:}\\
M[\eta]\Downarrow_{n}\lambda x.M_{1}[\eta^{\prime}]\\
M_{1}[\eta^{\prime}[M^{\prime}[\eta]/x]]\Downarrow_{n}v\end{array}\\
\hline\cr(MM^{\prime})[\eta]\Downarrow_{n}v\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{By-value:}\\
M[\eta]\Downarrow_{v}\lambda x.M_{1}[\eta^{\prime}]\quad M^{\prime}[\eta]\Downarrow_{v}v^{\prime}\\
M_{1}[\eta^{\prime}[v^{\prime}/x]]\Downarrow_{v}v\end{array}\\
\hline\cr(MM^{\prime})[\eta]\Downarrow_{v}v\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 8.3:
Evaluation of closures: call-by-name and call-by-value

In the presentation of the evaluation relations, at each reduction step, we have
to traverse the evaluation context in order to reach the redex
to be reduced. A more efficient approach consists in
storing the traversed evaluation context in a
stack
and then
to push and pop elements on the stack as needed (cf. small-step reduction rules
for
{\sf Imp}
in chapter
1
).
The form of the stack depends on the reduction strategy.
In call-by-name, the evaluation context is the composition
of
elementary contexts
of the shape
[~]N
, where
N
is
a
\lambda
-term.
Then a stack representation of the evaluation context is
just a list of closures (arguments with their environment):

s=c_{1}:\ldots:c_{n}\qquad\mbox{(stack for call-by-name).}

The reduction relation presented in table
8.4

now operates on pairs

(M[\eta],s)
composed of a closure and a stack.
Initially
\lambda
-terms are supposed closed and
the stack is supposed empty.

\begin{array}[]{lll}(x[\eta],s)&\rightarrow&(\eta(x),s)\\
((MM^{\prime})[\eta],s)&\rightarrow&(M[\eta],M^{\prime}[\eta]:s)\\
((\lambda x.M)[\eta],c:s)&\rightarrow&(M[\eta[c/x]],s)\par\end{array}

Table 8.4:
Abstract machine for call-by-name

A similar approach works for call-by-value.
This time an evaluation context can be regarded
as the composition of
elementary contexts
of the
shape:
[~]N
or
V[~]
. We code these elementary contexts
as a list as follows:

\begin{array}[]{cc}[~]c\ \equiv\ r:c\quad\mbox{($r$ for right),}&v[~]\ \equiv\ l:v\quad\mbox{($l$ for left).}\end{array}

Then the stack
s
has the shape:

s=m_{1}:c_{1}:\ldots m_{n}:c_{n}\qquad\mbox{where }m\in\{l,r\}\qquad\mbox{(stack for call-by-value).}

The reduction rules are described in table
8.5
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}(x[\eta],s)&\rightarrow&(\eta(x),s)\\
((MM^{\prime})[\eta],s)&\rightarrow&(M[\eta],r:M^{\prime}[\eta]:s)\\
(v,r:c:s)&\rightarrow&(c,l:v:s)\\
(v,l:(\lambda x.M)[\eta]:s)&\rightarrow&(M[\eta[v/x]],s)\par\end{array}

Table 8.5:
Abstract machine for call-by-value

Exercise 8.1

Suppose we add to the
\lambda
-calculus with call-by-value
a certain number of operators

{\it op}_{1},\ldots,{\it op}_{m}
with
arity
n_{1},\ldots,n_{m}
,
n_{j}\geq 0
.
(1) What are the new evaluation contexts?
(2) How is the abstract machine to be modified?

The rules in the abstract machines described in tables
8.4

and
8.5
form the basis for an implementation.
As usual in the implementation of term rewriting rules, one
can avoid the costly duplication of terms by using pointers.
Specifically, in the rule for application one just needs to duplicate
the pointer to the environment rather than the whole environment.
In a machine implementation, variables can be replaced by
de Brujin indexes
which express the number of
\lambda
’s that
one has to traverse in the syntax tree to go from the variable
to the binder. For instance, the
\lambda
-term
\lambda x.x(\lambda y.xy)

is represented by
\lambda.0(\lambda.10)
. We can rely on this notation
for closures too. In this case, we regard the environment as a list and
let a de Brujin index express the number of
\lambda
’s and
elements in the environment that one has to traverse to go from
the variable to the term bound to the variable. For instance, in

(\lambda.20)[c;c^{\prime}]
the variable
2
refers to the closure
c^{\prime}
while
the variable
0
refers to the
\lambda
.
Using this notation,
e.g.
,
the last rule of Table
8.4
can be written as:

((\lambda.M)[\eta],c:s)\ \rightarrow\ (M[c:\eta],s)~.

It is interesting to notice that during the (abstract) machine computation
(de Brujin) indexes are
never
modified. This simple remark
makes manifest that the number of closures
in an environment is bounded by the largest index (plus
1
)
of the initial
\lambda
-term to be reduced. In practice, the
inputs of a functional program have indexes of bounded size and
therefore, in this case, the selection of an element in an environment
can be done in constant time. More generally, assuming that lists have
bounded length and that duplicated environments are shared each
computation step described by the rules in Tables
8.4
and

8.5
can be implemented in costant time, thus justfying
the ‘abstract machine’ terminology. Notice however, that this analysis
ignores the hidden cost of garbage collection.

Exercise 8.2

Implement the abstract machines in Tables

8.4
and
8.5

using De Brujin notation.

Exercise 8.3

Suppose we add to the call-by-name
\lambda
-calculus two
monadic operators:
{\cal C}
for control and
{\cal A}
for abort.
If
M
is a term then
{\cal C}M
and
{\cal A}M
are
\lambda
-terms.
An evaluation context
E
is always defined as:

E::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid EM
,
and the reduction of the control and abort operators is
governed by the following rules:

\begin{array}[]{llllll}E[{\cal C}M]&\rightarrow&M(\lambda x.{\cal A}E[x])~,\qquad E[{\cal A}M]&\rightarrow&M~.\end{array}

Adapting the rules in Table
8.4
, design an abstract machine
to execute the terms in this extended language (a similar exercise can
be carried on for call-by-value).
Hint: assume an operator ret which takes a whole stack and
retracts it into a closure; then, e.g., the rule for the control
operator can be
formulated as:

\ (({\cal C}M)[\eta],s)\ \rightarrow\ (M[\eta],{\it ret}(s))
.

8.4
Summary and references

We have focused on two popular weak reduction strategies:
call-by-name and call-by-value and considered alternative and
equivalent presentations via
evaluation contexts
and
via (big-step)
evaluation relations
.
We have highlighted the distinction between static and dynamic
binding and shown that the implementation of the former relies
on the notions of
closure
and
environment
.
Static binding leads to a correct implementation of the
call-by-name and call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus.
Further we have shown that
evaluation contexts
can be implemented
as stacks and that this leads to abstract machines for call-by-name
and call-by-value. The notion of
call-by-name and call-by-value evaluation strategy in the

\lambda
-calculus is studied in
[
Plo75
]

and an early notion of abstract machine is presented in
[
Lan64
]
.
de Brujin notation for
\lambda
-terms is introduced in
[
dB72
]
.
The implementation techniques studied for weak
reduction strategies can be extended to the (non-weak)

\beta
-reduction presented in chapter
7
(see,
e.g.
,

[
CHL96
]
).

Chapter 9
Contextual equivalence and simulation

We look for a notion of
pre-order
(and a derived
equivalence
)
among program expressions (not necessarily full programs).
It should be
natural
and
usable
.
To this end, we introduce first a notion of
contextual pre-order
which is

natural
and then we show that it can be characterized as a
certain
simulation
which is easier to reason about.
The notion of simulation is an example of
co-inductively defined
relation
. We take the opportunity to put on solid grounds
some basic notions on fixed points of monotonic functions and (co-)inductive
definitions.

9.1
Observation pre-order and equivalence

We focus on a (deterministic)
call-by-name
\lambda
-calculus

as presented in chapter
8
. However, the approach applies
to programming languages in general (including non-deterministic ones).
We work with the evaluation relation for call-by-name
in definition
8.7
and simply write
\Downarrow

rather than
\Downarrow_{n}
since no confusion with call-by-value can arise.
Also, in this chapter, all terms are
\lambda
-terms.

We write
M\Downarrow
and say that
M
converges if
\exists\,V\;\>M\Downarrow V
.
Note that for every closed term
M
either there is a unique value

V
to which
M
evaluates or the evaluation diverges (the derivation
tree is infinite). The situation were the evaluation is stuck
cannot arise.

In order to define a pre-order (or an equivalence) among two terms
we have to
decide
(cf. chapter
1
)
in which
contexts
the terms can be placed and
which
observations
can be performed on the terms
once they are placed in the contexts.
Our
hypotheses
are as follows.

•

All contexts
C
such that
C[M]
and
C[N]
are
closed terms
.
We insist on closing contexts because reduction is defined on closed terms.

•

We observe the
termination
of the term placed in a closing context.
Observing natural numbers or booleans would not change the
state of affairs.

Definition 9.1
(contextual pre-order and equivalence)

We define the contextual pre-order on terms as:

M\leq_{C}N\mbox{ if for all closing }C\ (C[M]\Downarrow\mbox{ implies }C[N]\Downarrow\ )~.

Contextual equivalence is derived by defining:

M\approx_{C}N\mbox{ if }M\leq_{C}N\mbox{ and }N\leq_{C}M~.

Thus two terms are deemed ‘equivalent’ if
from the point of view of the admitted observation they are
indistinguishable in any closing context.

Exercise 9.1

Prove the following properties:

1.

\leq_{C}
is a pre-order (reflexive and transitive).

2.

If
M\leq_{C}N
then for all contexts
C
(not necessarily closing)

C[M]\leq_{C}C[N]
.

3.

\lambda x.\lambda y.x\not\leq_{C}\lambda x.\lambda y.y
.

4.

If
\underline{n},\underline{m}
are Church numerals (cf. definition
7.11
) with
n\neq m
then
\underline{n}\not\leq_{C}\underline{m}
.

5.

Find a pair of terms
M,N
such that

M\not=_{\beta}N
and you expect
M\approx_{C}N
.

To prove that
M\not\leq_{C}N
it suffices to find a context
such that
C[M]\Downarrow
and
C[N]\not\Downarrow
. On the other hand,
to prove that
M\leq_{C}N
we have to consider
all closing contexts
.
For instance, proving
(\lambda x.M)N\leq_{C}[N/x]M
is not so easy!
This motivates the quest for a more practical proof method
based on the notion of simulation which will be discussed in section

9.4
.

Exercise 9.2

Let
\leq_{{\it IO}}
be a relation on closed terms defined by:

M\leq_{{\it IO}}N\mbox{ if }\forall\,P\mbox{ closed}\;\>MP\Downarrow\mbox{ implies }NP\Downarrow~

Show that
\leq_{{\it IO}}
is a pre-order and that it is not preserved by contexts.

9.2
Fixed points

In this and the following section,
we make a pause to state and prove some
general facts

on partial orders, monotonic/continuous functions,
fixed points, and (co-)inductive definitions.
These facts are
used all the time
when manipulating
programming languages, formal languages, logics,
\ldots

The reader would be well-advised to become
acquainted
with these concepts.
We start by recalling some standard definitions on partial orders
(notice that, unlike in chapter
2
,
partial orders are supposed to be reflexive).

Definition 9.2
(partial order)

A partial order
(L,\leq)
is a set
L
equipped with
a binary relation
\leq
which is
reflexive, anti-symmetric, and transitive.

Definition 9.3
(upper/lower bounds)

Suppose
(L,\leq)
is a partial order and
let
X\subseteq L
be a subset (possibly empty).
An element
y\in L
is an upper bound for
X

if
\forall\,x\in X\;\>x\leq y
.
An element
y\in L
is the supremum (sup) of
X
if it is the
least upper bound.
The notions of lower bound and infimum (inf)
are defined in a dual way.

Definition 9.4
(lattice)

A lattice is a partial order
(L,\leq)
such that
every pair of elements of
L
has a sup and an inf.
A complete lattice is a partial order
(L,\leq)
such that
every subset of
L
has a sup (the existence of the inf follows).

Exercise 9.3

Show that:
(1) The subsets of a set with the inclusion relation
as partial order form a complete lattice.
(2) Every subset of a complete lattice has an inf.
(3) Every finite lattice is complete.

Next we introduce the notion of monotonic,
i.e.
, order-preserving
function and consider the structure of its fixed points in a complete lattice.

Definition 9.5
(monotonic function)

A monotonic function
f:L\rightarrow L
on a partial order
L
is a
function respecting the order:

\forall\,x,y\;\>(\ x\leq y\mbox{ implies }f(x)\leq f(y)\ )~.

We say that
x
is a fixed point of
f
if
f(x)=x
.

Proposition 9.1
(Tarski)

Let
f:L\rightarrow L
be a monotonic function on a complete lattice.
Then
f
has a greatest and a least fixed point expressed by:

{\it sup}\{x\mid x\leq f(x)\}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad{\it inf}\{x\mid f(x)\leq x\}~.

Proof
. Set
z={\it sup}\{x\mid x\leq f(x)\}
. If
f(y)=y
then
y\leq z
.
Hence it remains to show that
z
is a fixed point.
First, we show:

z\leq{\it sup}\{f(x)\mid x\leq f(x)\}\leq f(z)~.

Then by monotonicity:
f(z)\leq f(f(z))
.
And by definition of
z
, we derive
f(z)\leq z
.
{\Box}

Exercise 9.4

Let
(\mathbf{N}\cup\{\infty\},\leq)
be the set of natural numbers
with an added maximum element
\infty
,
0<1<2<\ldots<\infty
.
Show that every monotonic function
f
on this order has
a fixed point.

The following exercises consider two situations which often arise in practice.

Exercise 9.5
(fixed points on finite lattices)

Let
(L,\leq)
be a finite lattice and
f:L\rightarrow L
be a
monotonic function. Let
\bot
(
\top
) be
the least (greatest) element of
L
.
If
x\in L
then let
f^{n}(x)
be the
n
-time iteration of
f
on
x
,
where
f^{0}(x)=x
.

1.

Show that there is an
n\geq 0
such that the
least fixed point of
f
equals
f^{n}(\bot)
.

2.

State and prove a dual property for the greatest fixed point.

3.

Show that these properties fail to hold if one removes the hypothesis
that the lattice is finite.

Exercise 9.6
(fixed points of continuous functions)

A subset
X
of a partial order is directed if

\forall\,x,y\in X\;\>\exists\,z\in\;\>(x\leq z)\mbox{ and }(y\leq z)~.

A function on a complete lattice is continuous if it preserves
the sup of directed sets:

f({\it sup}(X))={\it sup}(f(X))\qquad\mbox{(if }X\mbox{ directed).}

1.

Show that a continuous function is monotonic.

2.

Give an example of a function on a complete
lattice which is continuous but does not
preserve the sup of a (non-directed) set.

3.

Show that the least fixed point of a continuous function
f

is expressed by:

{\it sup}\{f^{n}(\bot)\mid n\geq 0\}~.

We can summarize the exercises

9.5
and
9.6
as follows.
If the the lattice is finite, to compute the least
(greatest) fixed point it suffices to iterate
a finite number of times the monotonic function starting from the
least (greatest) element.
Otherwise, if the function is continuous (preserves directed sets),
then the
least fixed point
is the
sup

of the (countable) iteration of the function starting from the least
element.
Similar remarks apply to the greatest fixed point modulo suitable
definitions of the notions of co-directed set and co-continuous function.

In general, it is possible to build the least or greatest fixed point
of a monotonic function on a complete lattice as an
iterative

process provided one accepts a
transfinite number of iterations
.
To do this, we can rely on the notion of
ordinal
in set theory. Intuitively, ordinals are obtained by iterating the operations of
successor and supremum; the former are
called
successor ordinals

and the latter
limit ordinals
:

0,1,2,\ldots,\omega,\omega+1,\omega+2,\ldots,\omega+\omega,\ldots\qquad\mbox{(first few ordinals).}

Formally, in
set theory
, the set
X
is an ordinal if:

•

Z\in Y\in X
implies
Z\in X
.

•

All sequences such that
X_{0}\ni X_{1}\ni X_{2}\ni\cdots
are finite.

Notice that if we read
X\in Y
as
X<Y
the first property corresponds
to
transitivity
and the second to
well-foundation
.
So an ordinal is a set which is transitive and well-founded with respect to
the
\in
-relation.
In set theory, the role of
0
is played by the empty set, the successor of an ordinal set

\kappa
is the set
\{\kappa\}\cup\kappa
, and the limit of a sequence
of ordinals is their union.
Now given a complete lattice
L
and a monotonic function
f:L\rightarrow L
,
we can define the (transfinite) sequence:

\begin{array}[]{llllll}f_{0}&=\bot~,&f_{\kappa+1}&=f(f_{\kappa})~,&f_{\kappa}&=\bigvee_{\kappa^{\prime}<\kappa}f_{\kappa^{\prime}}\qquad\kappa\mbox{ limit ordinal.}\end{array}

This defines an increasing sequence which must reach the least fixed point
when the cardinality of the ordinal
\kappa
is greater than the
cardinality of the complete lattice
L
; for otherwise, we would have
a subset of
L
whose cardinality is greater than
L
.
A dual argument shows that we can approximate the greatest fixed
point starting from the top element of the lattice.

9.3
(Co-)Inductive definitions

We discuss examples of inductive and co-inductive set definitions.
Behind these definitions there is a complete lattice and
a monotonic function, and the (co-)inductive set
which is defined is nothing but the least (the greatest) fixed point
of the monotonic function.

Example 9.1
(an inductive definition)

Let
{\bf Z}
be the set of integer numbers and
{\it suc}
and
+
the
standard successor and addition operations, respectively.
We could define:

The least subset of
{\bf Z}
which contains
\{0,2\}

and is closed under the addition operation.

It is not so obvious that we are indeed defining a set.
One has to make sure that the ‘least set’
does exist.
To do this, we explicit a function
f:2^{{\bf Z}}\rightarrow 2^{{\bf Z}}
,

f(X)=\{0,2\}\cup\{x+y\mid x,y\in X\}~,

such that “
X
contains the set
\{0,2\}
and
X
is closed under
the addition operation” iff
f(X)\subseteq X
.
Then one remarks that
2^{{\bf Z}}
is a complete lattice
and
f
is monotonic (indeed continuous, see exercise
9.6
).
Hence the least fixed point exists and is expressed by:

\bigcap\{X\mid f(X)\subseteq X\}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}f^{n}(\emptyset)~.

Example 9.2
(another inductive definition)

Let
R
be a binary relation on a set
D
.
The reflexive and transitive closure
R^{*}
is the
least relation that contains the identity relation, the relation

R
and such that if
(x,y),(y,z)\in R^{*}
then
(x,z)\in R^{*}
.
Let us show that we can regard
R^{*}
as a least fixed point.
As complete lattice, we take the binary relations on the set

D
ordered by inclusion. As monotonic function
f
, we define:

f(S)={\it Id}_{D}\cup R\cup S\circ S

where
{\it Id}_{D}
is the identity relation on
D
and
S\circ S
is
the (relational) composition of
S
with itself.

Example 9.3
(buggy inductive definition)

Monotonicity is a key property.
As usual, let us write
(x\equiv y)\ {\it mod}\ 2
if the integers

x,y
have the property that
(x-y)
is a multiple of
2
.
Suppose we ‘define’
X
as the least set of
integers such that:
(1)
0\in X
and
(2) if
x\in X
then
\forall\,y\in X\;\>(\ (x\equiv(y+1))\ {\it mod}\ 2\ )
.
Unfortunately such a set does not exist. We should have
a set of integers
X
such that
0\in X
and
(0\equiv 1)\ {\it mod}\ 2
.

The notion of
co-inductive definition
is obtained by
dualization
.
Rather than looking for the
least
set such that
\ldots
, we now look
for the
greatest
set such that
\ldots

Example 9.4
(co-inductive definition)

A typical example of co-inductive definition
arises in the theory of finite automata.
Let
M=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},F,\delta)
be a
finite deterministic automaton with

Q
set of states,
\Sigma
input alphabet,
q_{0}
initial state,

F
set of accepting states, and

\delta:\Sigma\times Q\rightarrow Q
transition function.
Consider the function
f:2^{(Q\times Q)}\rightarrow 2^{(Q\times Q)}
defined by

(q,q^{\prime})\in f(R)
if

1.

q\in F
iff
q^{\prime}\in F
.

2.

\forall\,a\in\Sigma\;\>(\delta(a,q),\delta(a,q^{\prime}))\in R
.

It is easy to check that the function
f
is monotonic on the
set of binary relations on
Q
ordered by inclusion.
The least
R
such that
R=f(R)
is simply the empty relation
which is not very interesting. However, the greatest
R
such
that
f(R)=R
is the relation that corresponds to
state equivalence in finite automata. Indeed, since
2^{Q\times Q}
is
a finite lattice, the definition gives a way to compute the equivalence
on states (see exercise
9.5
).
Start with the full relation
Q\times Q
and iterate the function
f

till you reach a fixed point.

Co-inductive definitions are quite useful in defining various
notions of diverging computation. We illustrate this point
in the following example.

Example 9.5
(another co-inductive definition)

Let
(S,\rightarrow)
be a set of states and

\rightarrow\subseteq S\times S
be a transition relation.
Define
D
as the greatest subset of
S

such that if
s\in D
then:

\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\mbox{ and }s^{\prime}\in D
.
We take as complete lattice the parts of
S

ordered by inclusion. The monotonic function
f
associated with
the definition is for
X\subseteq S
:

f(X)=\{s\mid\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>(s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\mbox{ and }s^{\prime}\in X)\}~.

To see the definition at work, suppose
S=\{1,2,3,4\}
with transitions:

1\rightarrow 2,3,4
,
3\rightarrow 1
,
4\rightarrow 4
.
The greatest fixed point of
f
is
\{1,3,4\}
on the other hand
the least fixed point is just the empty set.
Intuitively, the greatest fixed point of
f
is the collection of elements
starting from which there is an infinite reduction sequence.

To summarize a (co-)inductive definition is well-defined
if the associated function is
monotonic
. In this case,
the defined set corresponds to a least
(greatest)
fixed point
of the associated function.

Exercise 9.7

Modify example
9.5
so as to define the collection of elements
which are not normalizing, i.e., there is no
reduction sequence leading to an element in normal form
(cf. definition
2.3
).

9.4
Simulation

Simulation is a standard example of co-inductive definition
of a binary relation which can be used to compare programs’ behaviors.

Definition 9.6
(simulation)

We say that a binary relation on closed terms

S
is a simulation
if whenever
(M,N)\in S
we have: (1) if
M\Downarrow
then
N\Downarrow
and
(2) for all
P
closed
(MP,NP)\in S
.
We shall also use the infix notation
M\ S\ N
for
(M,N)\in S
.
We define
\leq_{S}
as the largest simulation.

Exercise 9.8

Show that
\leq_{S}
is the largest fixed point of the following function
on binary relations:

\begin{array}[]{lll }f(S)&=\{(M,N)\mid&M\Downarrow\mbox{ implies }N\Downarrow~,\quad\forall\,P\mbox{ closed}\;\>(MP,NP)\in S\}~.\end{array}

Definition 9.7

We extend
\leq_{S}
to open terms by defining:

M\leq_{S}N\mbox{ if for all closing substitutions }\sigma\ (\sigma M\leq_{S}\sigma N)~.

We also write
M=_{S}N
if
M\leq_{S}N
and
N\leq_{S}M
.

To prove that
M\leq_{S}N
(
M,N
closed)
it suffices to find a relation
S
which is a
simulation and such that
M\ S\ N
.
The following proof contains several examples of this technique.

Proposition 9.2

The following properties of simulation hold:

1.

\leq_{S}
is a pre-order (on open terms).

2.

If
M\leq_{S}N
then for any substitution
\sigma
(not necessarily closed)

\sigma M\leq_{S}\sigma N
.

3.

If
M\Downarrow V
and
N\Downarrow V
,
M,N
closed, then
M=_{S}N
.

4.

(\lambda x.M)N=_{S}[N/x]M
(
M,N
can be open).

5.

If
M\leq_{C}N
then
M\leq_{S}N
(
M,N
can be open).

Proof
.
(1)\;

On closed terms
\leq_{S}
is reflexive and transitive.
If
M
is an open term then
M\leq_{S}M
because
for every closing substitutions
\sigma M\leq_{S}\sigma M
.
Suppose
M\leq_{S}N
and
N\leq_{S}P
. Given a closing substitution

\sigma
for
M,P
we can always extend it to a closing substitution

\sigma^{\prime}
for
N
. Then we have:

\sigma M\equiv\sigma^{\prime}M\leq_{S}\sigma^{\prime}N\leq_{S}\sigma^{\prime}P\equiv\sigma P~.

(2)\;
Suppose
M\leq_{S}N
and
\sigma
is a substitution.
To prove
\sigma M\leq_{S}\sigma N
, we have to check that for all
closing substitution
\sigma^{\prime}
,
\sigma^{\prime}(\sigma M)\leq_{S}\sigma^{\prime}(\sigma N)
.
And this holds because
(\sigma^{\prime}\circ\sigma)
is a closing substitution
for
M,N
.

(3)\;
We check that the following relation on closed terms
is a simulation:

S=\{(MP_{1}\cdots P_{n},NP_{1}\cdots P_{n})\mid M\Downarrow V,N\Downarrow V,n\geq 0\}~.

If
MP_{1}\cdots P_{n}\Downarrow
then we must have
VP_{1}\cdots P_{n}\Downarrow
and
therefore
NP_{1}\cdots P_{n}\Downarrow
.

Also if
(MP_{1}\cdots P_{n},NP_{1}\cdots P_{n})\in S
then for all
P
closed,

(MP_{1}\cdots P_{n}P,NP_{1}\cdots P_{n}P)\in S
.

(4)\;
Both the following relation and its inverse are simulations:

S=\{((\lambda x.M)NP_{1}\cdots P_{n},[N/x]MP_{1}\cdots P_{n})\mid n\geq 0\}~.

Thus
(\lambda x.M)N=_{S}[N/x]M
holds if
(\lambda x.M)N
is closed.
If
(\lambda x.M)N
is open and
\sigma
is a closing substitution then
we observe:

\sigma(\lambda x.M)N\equiv(\lambda x.\sigma M)\sigma N=_{S}[\sigma N/x]\sigma M\equiv\sigma([N/x]M)~.

(5)\;
First check that
\leq_{C}
on closed terms is
a simulation. Thus
\leq_{C}\subseteq\leq_{S}
on closed terms.
For open terms, suppose
M\leq_{C}N
,
let
x^{*}
be the list of variables free in
M,N
, and
let
\sigma
be any closing substitution.
Then take the closed terms:

M^{\prime}\equiv(\lambda x^{*}.M)\sigma(x^{*})

and
N^{\prime}\equiv(\lambda x^{*}.N)\sigma(x^{*})
.
We have
M^{\prime}\leq_{C}N^{\prime}
and therefore
M^{\prime}\leq_{S}N^{\prime}
.
Moreover
\sigma M=_{S}M^{\prime}
and
\sigma N=_{S}N^{\prime}
.
Therefore:
\sigma M\leq_{S}\sigma N
.
{\Box}

Exercise 9.9

Prove that:

1.

If
M\not\Downarrow
and
N\not\Downarrow
then
M=_{S}N
.

2.

Let
\Omega_{n}\equiv\lambda x_{1}.\ldots.\lambda x_{n}.\Omega
.
Then
\Omega_{n}<_{S}\Omega_{n+1}
(strictly) and, for all
M
,

\Omega_{0}\leq_{S}M
.

3.

Let
K^{\infty}\equiv YK
. Then for all
M
,
M\leq_{S}K^{\infty}
.

4.

\lambda x,y.xy\not\leq_{S}\lambda x.x
(thus
\eta
-conversion is
unsound).

So it seems
easier
proving
M\leq_{S}N
than proving
M\leq_{C}N
.
However, we still need to check that
\leq_{S}
is
preserved
by contexts
. If this property holds, then it is easy to
conclude that the largest simulation coincides with the contextual pre-order.

Proposition 9.3

Let
x
be a variable and
M,N,P
be terms.
If
M\leq_{S}N
then: (1)
MP\leq_{S}NP
and
(2)
\lambda x.M\leq_{S}\lambda x.N
.

(1)\;
Because
\leq_{S}
is a simulation.

(2)\;
Suppose
M\leq_{S}N
and let
\sigma
be a closing substitution
for
\lambda x.M,\lambda x.N
. As usual, suppose
\sigma
commutes
with
\lambda
up to renaming. Now for all closed
P
, define

\sigma^{\prime}
as the substitution that extends
\sigma
so that

\sigma^{\prime}(x)=P
. Then, by hypothesis:

[P/x]\sigma M=\sigma^{\prime}M\leq_{S}\sigma^{\prime}N=[P/x]\sigma N~.

Then we have:

(\lambda x.\sigma M)P=_{S}[P/x]\sigma M\leq_{S}[P/x]\sigma N=(\lambda x.\sigma N)P~.

Clearly
(\lambda x.\sigma M)\Downarrow
implies
(\lambda x.\sigma N)\Downarrow
.
With reference to the function
f
defined in exercise
9.8
,
we have shown
(\lambda x.\sigma M)\ f(\leq_{S})\ (\lambda x.\sigma N)
,
and we know
f(\leq_{S})=\leq_{S}
.
{\Box}

Exercise 9.10

Let us revise the pre-order considered in exercise
9.2
by
defining a relation
\leq_{{\it IO}^{*}}
on closed terms as:

M\leq_{{\it IO}^{*}}N\mbox{ if for all $n\geq 0$, $P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}$ closed,
$MP_{1}\cdots P_{n}\Downarrow$ \mbox{ implies }$NP_{1}\cdots P_{n}\Downarrow$.}

Prove that
\leq_{{\it IO}^{*}}
coincides with
\leq_{S}
.

9.5
Simulation is a congruence (*)

Unfortunately, it is
not
so easy to prove
that
M\leq_{S}N
implies
PM\leq_{S}PN
.
The proof plan is to introduce an
auxiliary relation

\leq_{A}

on
open
terms, which includes
\leq_{S}
, is preserved by contexts,
and, with some work, turns out to coincide with
\leq_{S}
.

Definition 9.8
(auxiliary simulation relation)

The auxiliary relation
M\leq_{A}N
is defined inductively on
M

by the following rules:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{c}x\leq_{S}N\\
\hline\cr x\leq_{A}N\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}M\leq_{A}M^{\prime}\quad\lambda x.M^{\prime}\leq_{S}N\\
\hline\cr\lambda x.M\leq_{A}N\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}M_{1}\leq_{A}M^{\prime}_{1}\quad M_{2}\leq_{A}M^{\prime}_{2}\quad M^{\prime}_{1}M^{\prime}_{2}\leq_{S}N\\
\hline\cr M_{1}M_{2}\leq_{A}N\end{array}~.\par\end{array}

The definition of the auxiliary relation seems rather
mysterious
.
To have a clue, let us look at its
properties
.

Proposition 9.4

The auxiliary relation
\leq_{A}
enjoys the following properties:

1.

\leq_{A}
is reflexive.

2.

\leq_{A}\circ\leq_{S}\ \subseteq\ \leq_{A}
.

3.

\leq_{S}\ \subseteq\ \leq_{A}
.

Proof
.
(1)\;
By induction on the structure of the term.

(2)\;

Suppose
M\leq_{A}N\leq_{S}P
and proceed by induction
on the proof of
M\leq_{A}N
.

(3)\;
By the previous property (2),
using the fact that
\leq_{A}
is reflexive.
{\Box}

The next proposition introduces the key properties of
the auxiliary relation.

Proposition 9.5
(key properties)

Let
M,M^{\prime},N,N^{\prime}
be terms. Then:

1.

If
M\leq_{A}M^{\prime}
and
N\leq_{A}N^{\prime}
then
[N/x]M\leq_{A}[N^{\prime}/x]M^{\prime}
.

2.

If
M\leq_{A}M^{\prime}
and
N\leq_{A}N^{\prime}
then
MN\leq_{A}M^{\prime}N^{\prime}
.

3.

If
M\Downarrow V
and
M\leq_{A}N
then
V\leq_{A}N
.

Proof
.
(1)\;
By induction on the proof of
M\leq_{A}M^{\prime}
. For instance,
suppose:

\begin{array}[]{c}M\leq_{A}M^{\prime\prime}\quad\lambda y.M^{\prime\prime}\leq_{S}M^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\lambda y.M\leq_{A}M^{\prime}\end{array}~.

We have to prove:

\lambda y.[N/x]M\leq_{A}[N^{\prime}/x]M^{\prime}
.
By inductive hypothesis, we know:

[N/x]M\leq_{A}[N^{\prime}/x]M^{\prime\prime}
.
Also, by substitutivity of
\leq_{S}
we have:

[N^{\prime}/x](\lambda y.M^{\prime\prime})\equiv\lambda y.[N^{\prime}/x]M^{\prime\prime}\leq_{S}[N^{\prime}/x]M^{\prime}~.

Hence, by definition of
\leq_{A}
, we conclude.

(2)\;
Consider the terms
Mx
and
M^{\prime}x
, with
x
fresh.
From
M\leq_{A}M^{\prime}
we can derive
Mx\leq_{A}M^{\prime}x
.
Then by property (1) above, we know that:

N\leq_{A}N^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }[N/x](Mx)\equiv MN\leq_{A}[N^{\prime}/x](M^{\prime}x)\equiv M^{\prime}N^{\prime}~.

(3)\;
We proceed by induction on
M\Downarrow V
. We detail the main case. Suppose:

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}M_{1}\Downarrow\lambda x.M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\quad[M_{2}/x]M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\Downarrow V\\
\hline\cr M_{1}M_{2}\Downarrow V\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}M_{1}\leq_{A}M^{\prime}_{1}\quad M_{2}\leq_{A}M^{\prime}_{2}\quad M^{\prime}_{1}M^{\prime}_{2}\leq_{S}N\\
\hline\cr M_{1}M_{2}\leq_{A}N\end{array}~.\end{array}

By induction hypothesis on
M_{1}\Downarrow\lambda x.M^{\prime\prime}_{1}
we derive:

\lambda x.M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\leq_{A}M^{\prime}_{1}~.

(9.1)

The proof of the property (
9.1
) above must have the following shape:

\begin{array}[]{c}M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\leq_{A}M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\quad\lambda x.M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\leq_{S}M^{\prime}_{1}\\
\hline\cr\lambda x.M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\leq_{A}M^{\prime}_{1}\end{array}~.

(9.2)

By the substitutivity property (proposition
9.5
.1),
we derive:

[M_{2}/x]M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\leq_{A}[M^{\prime}_{2}/x]M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}~.

(9.3)

Also by proposition
9.3
, we know that:

[M^{\prime}_{2}/x]M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}=_{S}(\lambda x.M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1})M^{\prime}_{2}\leq_{S}M^{\prime}_{1}M^{\prime}_{2}(\leq_{S}N)~.

(9.4)

So we have:

[M_{2}/x]M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\Downarrow V
,
[M^{\prime}_{2}/x]M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\leq_{A}N
,
and by inductive hypothesis, we conclude:
V\leq_{A}N
.
{\Box}

We can now prove the announced result: the largest simulation
coincides with the contextual pre-order.

Proposition 9.6

Let
M,N
be terms. Then:

1.

\leq_{A}
is a simulation (and therefore
\leq_{A}\subseteq\leq_{S}
).

2.

M\leq_{A}N
implies
M\leq_{C}N
.

Proof
.
(1)\;
If
M\leq_{A}N
and
M\Downarrow V
then
V\leq_{A}N
by
proposition
9.5
.3. Since
V
has the shape

\lambda x.M^{\prime}
we must have:

\begin{array}[]{c}M^{\prime}\leq_{A}M^{\prime\prime}\quad\lambda x.M^{\prime\prime}\leq_{S}N\\
\hline\cr\lambda x.M^{\prime}\leq_{A}N\end{array}~,

and, by definition of simulation,
N\Downarrow
.
Also by proposition
9.5
.2, we know that
M\leq_{A}N
implies

MP\leq_{A}NP
.

(2)\;
Suppose
M\leq_{A}N
and
C
one hole, closing context. Then

C[M]\leq_{A}C[N]
, and this implies
C[M]\leq_{S}C[N]
. So if

C[M]\Downarrow
then
C[N]\Downarrow
too.
{\Box}

Exercise 9.11

We define a notion of contextual pre-order
\leq_{C}
for the call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus simply by taking definition
9.1
and
considering that the predicate
\Downarrow
corresponds to call-by-value evaluation.
We also say that a (call-by-value) simulation is a binary relation

S
on closed
\lambda
terms such that whenever
(M,N)\in S
we have:
(1) if
M\Downarrow
then
N\Downarrow
and (2) for all closed values
V
,
(MV,NV)\in S
.
Denote with
\leq_{S}
the largest simulation. If
M,N
are terms
(possibly open) say that
M\leq_{S}N
if for all closing substitutions
\sigma

mapping variables to values
\sigma M\leq_{S}\sigma N
.
Adapt the theory developed in this chapter to prove that
the pre-orders
\leq_{C}
and
\leq_{S}
coincide.

9.6
Summary and references

The contextual pre-order is a natural compositional way to
compare terms and the simulation pre-order is an effective
method to reason on this relation.
Simulation is a typical example of co-inductive definition
and corresponds to the greatest fixed point of a monotonic
function. Dually, inductive definitions correspond to the
least fixed point of a monotonic function. Such fixed points
are guaranteed to exist for monotonic functions over
complete lattices and in many practical situations they
can be effectively computed or at least approximated.
The notion of simulation (and bisimulation, see chapter
23
)
was introduced in
[
Par81
]
in the context of the
semantics of concurrent processes where it is extensively
used. The proof that simulation is preserved
by contexts is based on
[
How96
]

and it can be extended to a number of other calculi,
including,
e.g.
, the call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus
(exercise
9.11
and
[
Pit11
]
).

Chapter 10
Propositional types

The reader is supposed to be familiar with the usage
of types in programming languages.
Then, if we regard the
\lambda
-calculus as the kernel
of a programming language, it is natural to wonder what
kind of types could be associated with
\lambda
-terms.

10.1
Simple types

For the time being, we shall focus on a collection of

propositional
types which include basic types such as
integers, booleans,
\ldots
and functional, product, and sum types.
We may also refer to these types as
simple
types as opposed
to more complex types including quantifications we shall discuss
in chapters
12
and
13
.

Definition 10.1
(types)

We define the collection of (functional) propositional types as follows:

A::=b\mid\!\!\mid{\it tid}\mid\!\!\mid(A\rightarrow A)~,

where
b
is a basic type (there can be more) and

{\it tid}::=t\mid\!\!\mid s\mid\!\!\mid\ldots
are type variables.

By default, we assume
\rightarrow
associates to the right; so

A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C
stands for
A\rightarrow(B\rightarrow C)
.

Definition 10.2
(contexts)

A type context
\Gamma
is a set of pairs

\{x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\}
where
all variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are distinct.

We use
\Gamma,x:A
as an

abbreviation
for
\Gamma\cup\{x:A\}

where
x
does not occur in
\Gamma
. Also we abbreviate type context
to context whenever no confusion may arise with
term
contexts.
Table
10.1
presents a first system to assign
types to
\lambda
-terms. In this formulation, the variable
of a
\lambda
-abstraction is decorated with a type as in

\lambda x:A.M
. As in the usual programming practice,
the type
A
specifies the type of the parameter of the function.
The presented system is composed of
a rule (
asmp
) to discharge an
assumption
from the context,
a rule
(\rightarrow_{I})
which
introduces
a functional type,
and a rule
(\rightarrow_{E})
which
eliminates
a functional type.
This presentation style where the rules associated with the type
operators are split into introduction and elimination rules
comes from logic where it is called
natural deduction
. The following exercises are a first illustration of the
connection between type systems and logic.

\begin{array}[]{c}({\it asmp})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}x:A\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash x:A\end{array}\\
\\
(\rightarrow_{I})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\lambda x:A.M:A\rightarrow B\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt(\rightarrow_{E})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A\rightarrow B\hskip 17.00024pt\Gamma\vdash N:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash MN:B\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 10.1:
Assignment of propositional types to
\lambda
-terms

Exercise 10.1

Show that if
x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\vdash M:B

is derivable then
(A_{1}\rightarrow\cdots(A_{n}\rightarrow B)\cdots)
is
a tautology of propositional logic where we interpret
\rightarrow
as
implication and atomic types as propositional variables.
Conclude that there are types
A
which are not inhabited,
i.e., there is no (closed)
\lambda
-term
M
such that
\emptyset\vdash M:A
.

Exercise 10.2

Show that there is no
\lambda
-term
M
such that:

\emptyset\vdash M:(b\rightarrow b)\rightarrow b
.
Write
A\rightarrow b
as
\neg A
.
Show that there are
\lambda
-terms
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
such that:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\emptyset\vdash N_{1}:A\rightarrow(\neg\neg A)~,&\emptyset\vdash N_{2}:(\neg\neg\neg A)\rightarrow(\neg A)~.\par\end{array}

On the other hand, there are tautologies which are not inhabited!
For instance, consider:

A\equiv((t\rightarrow s)\rightarrow t)\rightarrow t
.
Show that there is no
\lambda
-term
M
in normal form such that
\emptyset\vdash M:A
is derivable. This is enough because later we shall show that all
typable
\lambda
-terms normalize to a
\lambda
-term of the same type.
For another example, show that there is no
\lambda
-term
M
in normal
form such that
\emptyset\vdash M:\neg\neg t\rightarrow t
is
derivable (the intuitionistic/constructive negation is not
involutive!).

Next we review a few alternative presentations of the type system.
In Table
10.1
,
\lambda
-abstractions are decorated with
types. However, we can also consider a presentation where types
are assigned to
pure
,
i.e.
, type-less,
\lambda
-terms.
Then one speaks of a presentation in
Curry-style
, as opposed
to the previous one which is in
Church-style
.
In our case, the only difference between the two is that
the rule
(\rightarrow_{I})
in Curry-style becomes:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:A\rightarrow B\end{array}~.

An important consequence of this change is that now
a
\lambda
-term may have more than one type.
This makes the
type inference problem
(see chapters
11
and
12
) more interesting and so this problem
is often studied for systems in Curry-style.

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\it id}&::=x\mid\!\!\mid y\mid\!\!\mid\ldots\\
M&::={\it id}^{A}\mid\!\!\mid\lambda{\it id}^{A}.M\mid\!\!\mid MM\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr x^{A}:A\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{array}[]{c}M:A\rightarrow B\hskip 8.50012ptN:A\\
\hline\cr MN:B\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{array}[]{c}M:B\\
\hline\cr\lambda x^{A}.M:A\rightarrow B\end{array}

Table 10.2:
System with type labelled variables

Yet another presentation of the type system is possible by

labeling every variable
with its type and by
dropping the context
.
This gives the system presented in Table
10.2
.
Finally, we may decide to
label every
\lambda
-term

(not just the variables)
with its type and in this case we can
drop the type
since the type
of a
\lambda
-term is just the outermost label. The resulting system
is given in Table
10.3
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\it id}&::=x\mid\!\!\mid y\mid\!\!\mid\ldots\\
M&::={\it id}^{A}\mid\!\!\mid(\lambda{\it id}^{A}.M)^{A}\mid\!\!\mid(MM)^{A}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr x^{A}\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{array}[]{c}M^{A\rightarrow B}\hskip 8.50012ptN^{A}\\
\hline\cr(M^{A\rightarrow B}\ N^{A})^{B}\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{array}[]{c}M^{B}\\
\hline\cr(\lambda x^{A}.M^{B})^{(A\rightarrow B)}\end{array}

Table 10.3:
System with type-labelled
\lambda
-terms

The previous exercises
10.1
and
10.2

suggest that the functional type constructor can be regarded as a logical
implication. It turns out that one may push this connection further
by regarding the product (sum) type constructor as a logical
conjunction (disjunction). The resulting type system in natural deduction
style is presented in Table
10.4
. Later in chapters

12
and
13
, we shall see that this connection
can be extended even further to types with universal and existential
quantifications.

\begin{array}[]{c}(\times_{I})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M_{1}:A_{1}\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash M_{2}:A_{2}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle:A_{1}\times A_{2}\end{array}\\
\\
(\times_{E,1})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{1}\times A_{2}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\pi_{1}(M):A_{1}\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt(\times_{E,2})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{1}\times A_{2}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\pi_{2}(M):A_{2}\end{array}\\
\\
\\
(+_{I,1})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{1}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf in_{1}}^{A_{1}+A_{2}}(M):A_{1}+A_{2}\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt(+_{I,2})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{2}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf in_{2}}^{A_{1}+A_{2}}(M):A_{1}+A_{2}\end{array}\\
\\
(+_{E})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:(A_{1}+A_{2})\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash N_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B\hskip 8.50012pti=1,2\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf case}(M,N_{1},N_{2}):B\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 10.4:
Typing rules for product and sum

Not all term constructors found in a programming language have
a logical interpretation. For instance, Table
10.5

introduces typing rules for a constant zero
Z
, a successor
function
S
, and a fixed point combinator
Y
.
While the fixed point combinator allows to define general
recursive functions (see chapter
7
) its
logical interpretation is problematic.
Indeed, with the rule
({\sf Y})
, every type
A
is
inhabited

by the closed
\lambda
-term
Y(\lambda x:A.x)
. Thus the typing rule for
Y

is definitely incompatible with logic as it leads to inconsistency!

\begin{array}[]{ccc}({\sf Z})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf Z}:{\it nat}\end{array}\par&({\sf S})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:{\it nat}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf S}M:{\it nat}\end{array}\par&({\sf Y})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:(A\rightarrow A)\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf Y}M:A\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 10.5:
Non-logical extension of the type system

10.2
Subject reduction

If a
\lambda
-term is
well-typed
, then by inspection of the
rules we see,
e.g.
, that the
\lambda
-term
cannot contain the application of a natural number to a function.
However, to get
static guarantees
we
must make sure that
typing is invariant under reduction
,

i.e.
, if a
\lambda
-term is well-typed
and we reduce it then we still get a well-typed
\lambda
-term.
To establish this property we note the following
property.

Proposition 10.1
(substitution)

If
\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B
and

\Gamma\vdash N:A
then

\Gamma\vdash[N/x]M:B
.

Proof
. By induction on the
height of the proof
of
\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B
.
For instance, suppose the
root of the proof
has the shape:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A,y:B^{\prime}\vdash M:B^{\prime\prime}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma,x:A\vdash\lambda y.M:(B^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime\prime})\end{array}~,

with
x\neq y
.
Then by inductive hypothesis,

\Gamma,y:B^{\prime}\vdash[N/x]M:B^{\prime\prime}
and conclude
by
(\rightarrow_{I})
.
{\Box}

We can now state the invariance of typing under reduction as follows.
Historically, this property is called
subject reduction
.

Proposition 10.2
(subject reduction)

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A
and
M\rightarrow_{\beta}N
then

\Gamma\vdash N:A
.

Proof
. Recall, that
M\rightarrow_{\beta}N
means:

M\equiv C[(\lambda x.M_{1})M_{2}]\quad N\equiv C[[M_{2}/x]M_{1}]~.

To prove subject reduction we proceed by induction on the structure of
C
.
The
basic case
follows directly from the substitution lemma.
For the
inductive case
consider in turn the cases where:
(1)
C=\lambda y.C^{\prime}
, (2)
C=C^{\prime}P
, and (3)
C=PC^{\prime}
.
{\Box}

In the ‘pure’
\lambda
-calculus, we identify the normal
forms with the results of the computation.
In applications, however, one can distinguish two kinds
of normal/irreducible forms: those that correspond to
a
value
and those that correspond to an
erroneous
configuration
like dividing by zero, or applying an integer
to another integer.
Thus a program,
i.e.
, a closed
\lambda
-term, has
three possible outcomes
:
(1) it
returns a value
, (2) it reaches an
erroneous configuration
,
and (3) it
diverges
(cf. chapter
1
).
Besides being invariant by reduction, a desirable
property for a type system is that:

well-typed programs cannot go wrong
,
or at least that they go wrong in some expected way
(
e.g.
, division by zero).
This property is often called
progress
, because in its simple form
it requires that if a program is not a value then it can reduce (progress).
The following exercise elaborates on this point.

Exercise 10.3
(on progress)

Suppose we reconsider the non-logical extension
of the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus
with a basic type
{\it nat}
, constants

{\sf Z}
,
{\sf S}
,
{\sf Y}
, and with the
following fixed-point rule:

C[{\sf Y}M]\rightarrow C[M({\sf Y}M)]~.

Let a program be a closed typable
\lambda
-term of type
{\it nat}

and let a value be a
\lambda
-term
of the shape
({\sf S}\cdots({\sf S}{\sf Z})\cdots)
.
Show that if
P
is a program in normal form (cannot reduce)
then
P
is a value.

10.3
A normalizing strategy for the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus

We describe a
normalizing
strategy for the
simply typed
\lambda
-calculus. To this end
we introduce some measures of the complexity of a type and a
\lambda
-term.

Definition 10.3
(type degree)

The degree of a type is defined as follows:

\delta(t)=1~,\qquad\delta(A\rightarrow B)=1+{\it max}(\delta(A),\delta(B))~.

Definition 10.4
(redex degree)

Let
R\equiv(\lambda x:A.M)N
be a redex.
The degree of the redex, written
\delta_{r}(R)

is the degree of the type associated with the
\lambda
-term
(\lambda x:A.M)
.

Definition 10.5
(term degree)

The degree of a
\lambda
-term, written
\delta_{t}(M)
,
is
0
if
M
is in normal form
and the maximum of the degrees of the redexes
contained in
M
otherwise.

Remark 10.1

A redex
R
is also a
\lambda
-term and we have
\delta_{r}(R)\leq\delta_{t}(R)
.

Proposition 10.3
(degree and substitution)

If
x
is of type
A
then

\delta_{t}([N/x]M)\leq{\it max}(\delta(A),\delta_{t}(M),\delta_{t}(N))~.

Proof
. The redexes in
[N/x]M
fall in the following categories.
(1) The redexes already in
M
.
(2) The redexes already in
N
.
(3) New redexes arising by the substitution if
N\equiv\lambda y.N^{\prime}
and

M=C[xM^{\prime}]
. These redexes have degree
\delta(A)
.
{\Box}

Proposition 10.4
(degrees and reduction)

If
M\rightarrow N
then
\delta_{t}(N)\leq\delta_{t}(M)
.

Proof
. We apply the previous analysis.
{\Box}

We now define a reduction strategy that reduces first
an innermost redex of maximal degree.

Definition 10.6
(innermost maximal degree strategy)

Let
M
be a
\lambda
-term which is not in normal form.
The innermost maximal degree strategy selects a redex

R
of maximal degree (
\delta_{r}(R)=\delta_{t}(M)
) and such
that all redexes contained in
R
have lower degree.

Proposition 10.5

The innermost maximal degree strategy is normalizing.

Proof
