. Notice that by reducing an innermost maximal degree redex
we guarantee that the reduced
\lambda
-term contains

strictly less redexes of maximal degree
.
Then we prove normalization by taking as
measure
:

\mu(M)=(n,m)~,

with the
lexicographic order
(from left to right),
where
n=\delta_{t}(M)
and
m
is the number of redexes of maximal degree.
If
m=0
then
M
is in normal form.
If
m=1
then the reduced term has lower degree (first component decreases).
If
m>1
then the first component does not increase (proposition
10.4
) and the second decreases.
{\Box}

10.4
Termination of the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus (*)

A
\lambda
-term
M
is called
strongly normalizable

if all
\beta
-reductions starting from
M
terminate
(thus strong-normalization is just a synonymous for termination!).

Definition 10.7

Let
{\it SN}
be the set of strongly normalizable
\lambda
-terms.

This set plays a role similar to the set
{\it WF}
for RPO termination
(section
4.3
).
The notion of size of a
\lambda
-term follows definition
7.1
.

Definition 10.8
(maximal length)

If
M\in{\it SN}
then the maximal length of
a derivation starting from
M
is called
the reduction depth of
M
, and is denoted

0pt{M}
.

Remark 10.2

The maximal length is well-defined because the reduction tree of
a
\lambda
-term is finitely branching (cf. proposition
2.6
).

In order to show that all simply typed
\lambda
-terms are

\rightarrow_{\beta}
-strongly normalizable,
the
key idea
is to interpret types as subsets
of the set
{\it SN}
of strongly normalizing
\lambda
-terms.

Definition 10.9
(type interpretation)

The interpretation of a propositional type
A

is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}[\![b]\!]=[\![t]\!]&=&{\it SN}\\
[\![A\rightarrow B]\!]&=&\{M\mid\forall\,N\in[\![A]\!]\;\>(MN\in[\![B]\!])\}~.\par\end{array}

Proposition 10.6

For any type
A
, the following properties hold:

1.

[\![A]\!]\subseteq{\it SN}
.

2.

If
N_{i}\in{\it SN}
for
i=1,\ldots,k

then
xN_{1}\cdots N_{k}\in[\![A]\!]
.

3.

If
[N/x]MM_{1}\cdots M_{k}\in[\![A]\!]
and
N\in{\it SN}
then

(\lambda x.M)NM_{1}\cdots M_{k}\in[\![A]\!]
.

Proof
. By induction on
A
.

Atomic types.

(1) By definition.
(2) The reductions of
xN_{1}\ldots N_{k}
are just an interleaving of the
reductions of
N_{1},\ldots N_{k}
.
(3) We have:

{\it depth}((\lambda x.M)NM_{1}\ldots M_{k})\leq{\it depth}(N)+{\it depth}([N/x]MM_{1}\ldots M_{k})+1~.

\mbox{Functional types $A\rightarrow B$}.\;

Suppose
M\in[\![A\rightarrow B]\!]
.

(1)\;
By inductive hypothesis,
x\in[\![A]\!]
.
Hence
Mx\in[\![B]\!]\subseteq{\it SN}
, by inductive hypothesis. This entails
M\in{\it SN}
.

(2)\;
Take
M=xN_{1}\ldots N_{k}
with
N_{i}\in{\it SN}
. Take
N_{k+1}\in[\![A]\!]\subseteq{\it SN}
.
By inductive hypothesis,
xN_{1}\ldots N_{k}N_{k+1}\in[\![B]\!]
.

(3)\;

If
[N/x]MN_{1}\ldots N_{k}\in[\![A\rightarrow B]\!]
then by the interpretation of
the functional types we have:

\forall\,N_{k+1}\in[\![A]\!]\;\>\ [N/x]MN_{1}\ldots N_{k}N_{k+1}\in[\![B]\!]~.

Then by inductive hypothesis on
B
:

\forall\,N_{k+1}\in[\![A]\!]\;\>\ (\lambda x.M)NN_{1}\ldots N_{k}N_{k+1}\in[\![B]\!]~,

which is equivalent to
(\lambda x.M)NN_{1}\ldots N_{k}\in[\![A\rightarrow B]\!]
.

{\Box}

Remark 10.3

These interpretations of types are called reducibility candidates.
These are sets of of strongly normalizable
\lambda
-terms (property 1)
which contain at least the variables (and more) (property 2), and
are closed under head expansions (property 3).

We can now state the soundness of the interpretation.

Proposition 10.7
(soundness)

If
x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:A_{k}\vdash M:B
(in Curry-style) and

N_{i}\in[\![A_{i}]\!]
for
i=1,\ldots,k
then

[N_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,N_{k}/x_{k}]M\in[\![B]\!]
.

Proof
. By
induction on the typing proof
.

({\it asmp})\;
Immediate by
definition
.

(\rightarrow_{E})\;
By the
interpretation
of
\rightarrow
.

(\rightarrow_{I})\;
Here is what goes on in a simplified case.
By inductive hypothesis on
x:A\vdash M:B
we have:

\forall\,N\in[\![A]\!]\;\>[N/x]M\in[\![B]\!].

Then, by the
closure under head expansions

of the interpretations we derive:

\forall\,N\in[\![A]\!]\;\>(\lambda x.M)N\in[\![B]\!]~,

which is equivalent to
(\lambda x.M)\in[\![A\rightarrow B]\!]
.

{\Box}

The strong normalization property follows as a simple corollary.

Corollary 10.1
(strong normalization)

If a
\lambda
-term is typable then it is strongly normalizing.

Proof
. Suppose
x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:A_{k}\vdash M:B
.
We know
x_{i}\in[\![A_{i}]\!]
.
By proposition
10.7
(soundness),

M\in[\![B]\!]
and we know
[\![B]\!]\subseteq{\it SN}
.
{\Box}

A rational reconstruction of the proof could go as follows.
(1) We decide to interpret types as sets of strongly normalizing

\lambda
-terms and show that
\vdash M:A
implies
M\in[\![A]\!]
.
(2) Then the definition
10.9
of the type interpretation
is natural and the properties 1 and 2 of proposition
10.6

amount to check that indeed a type interpretation is composed of
strongly normalizing
\lambda
-terms and it is not empty.
(3) Finally, the need for property 3 of proposition
10.6

(closure under head expansion)
appears in the proof of proposition
10.7

(soundness, case
(\rightarrow_{I})
).

Exercise 10.4
(recursive types)

Assume a recursively defined type
t
satisfying the
equation
t=t\rightarrow b
and suppose
we add a rule for typing up to type equality:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A\quad A=B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash M:B\end{array}~.

Show that in this case the following
\lambda
-term (Curry’s fixed point combinator) is typable (e.g., in Curry-style):

Y\equiv\lambda f.(\lambda x.f(xx))(\lambda x.f(xx))~.

Are the
\lambda
-terms typable in this system terminating?

10.5
Summary and references

A minimal property required for a type system is that
it is invariant under reduction.
Sometimes, it is possible to connect type systems
to logic. This is the so called Curry-Howard correspondence
which goes as follows:

\lambda
-calculus

proof system

type

proposition

\lambda
-term

proof

reduction

proof normalization

In a natural deduction presentation, an opportunity for a
proof normalization arises when the introduction of
an operator is followed by an elimination.
For instance,
\lambda
-abstraction is followed by an application,
a pairing is followed by a projection, and an injection is followed
by a case selection.
The book
[
GLT89
]
is a good introduction to the connections between
proof theory and type theory including alternative presentations
of the logical systems.

Chapter 11
Type inference for propositional types

Given a (pure)
\lambda
-term
M
and a context
\Gamma
, the

type inference problem
is the
problem of checking whether there is a type
A
such that

\Gamma\vdash M:A
.
Given a (pure)
\lambda
-term
M
, a
variant of the problem

is to look for a type
A

and
a context
\Gamma
such
that
\Gamma\vdash M:A
.
Connected to the type inference problem is the problem of actually
producing an
informative output
. Typically, if a
\lambda
-term
M
is
typable, we are interested in a
synthetic representation of its types
,
and if it is not, we look for an
informative error message
.

11.1
Reduction of type-inference to unification

We present a polynomial time reduction of the
type inference problem

for the propositional type system in Curry style
(chapter
10
) to
the
syntactic unification problem

(chapter
3
). The existence of a
most general unifier

for the unification problem leads to
the existence of a
most general type
for the type inference problem.

Definition 11.1

A goal is a finite set
G
of triples
(\Gamma,M,A)

where
\Gamma
is a context,
M
a
\lambda
-term, and

A
a propositional type.

We assume that all bound variables in
M
are
distinct
and
different from the free ones,
that all free variables occur in the context
\Gamma
, and
that for every variable
x
we have a type variable
t_{x}
.
We define a
reduction relation
on pairs
(G,E)
.
Assuming
G=\{g\}\cup G^{\prime}
and

g\equiv(\Gamma,M,A)\notin G^{\prime}
, all the rules produce
a pair
(G^{\prime}\cup G_{g},E\cup E_{g})
where

G_{g}
and
E_{g}
are defined in Table
11.1
.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|l|}\hline\cr g&G_{g}&E_{g}\\
\hline\cr(\Gamma,x,A)&\emptyset&\{t_{x}=A\}\\
(\Gamma,M_{1}M_{2},A)&\{(\Gamma,M_{1},t_{1}\rightarrow A),(\Gamma,M_{2},t_{1})\}&\emptyset\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012pt(t_{1}\mbox{ fresh})\\
(\Gamma,\lambda x.M_{1},A)&\{(\Gamma,x:t_{x},M_{1},t)\}&\{A=t_{x}\rightarrow t\}\hskip 17.00024pt(t\mbox{ fresh})\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 11.1:
Reduction of type inference to unification

Proposition 11.1

The reduction specified in Table
11.1

terminates.

Proof
. It is enough to notice that every reduction step replaces
a triple
(\Gamma,M,A)
by a finite number of triples

(\Gamma^{\prime},M^{\prime},A^{\prime})
where
M^{\prime}
is structurally smaller
than
M
.
{\Box}

We introduce some notation.
In the following, we consider substitutions
S
that act
on the first-order terms built over the signature

\Sigma=\{b^{0},\rightarrow^{2}\}
.
We define:

\begin{array}[]{ll}S\models E&\mbox{if }S\mbox{ unifies }E~,\\
S\models(\Gamma,M,A)&\mbox{if }S\Gamma\vdash M:S(A)\mbox{ is derivable,}\\
S\models G&\mbox{if }\forall\,g\in G\;\>S\models g~,\\
S\models(G,E)&\mbox{if }S\models G\mbox{ and }S\models E~.\par\end{array}

Given a
\lambda
-term
M_{0}
with free variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, we set
the initial pair to
(G_{0},\emptyset)
, with
G_{0}=\{(\Gamma_{0},M_{0},t_{0})\}
,

t_{0}
fresh, and
\Gamma_{0}=x_{1}:t_{x_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}:t_{x_{n}}
. Next, we state
the main properties of the reduction.

Proposition 11.2

If
(G_{0},\emptyset)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(G,E)
then:

1.

If
S\models(G,E)
then
S\Gamma_{0}\vdash M_{0}:St_{0}
.

2.

If
\Gamma\vdash M_{0}:A
then

\exists{S}{(S\models(G,E),S\Gamma_{0}\subseteq\Gamma,\mbox{ and }A=St_{0})}
.

Proof
. For both properties we proceed by induction on the length of the reduction.

(1)\;
For instance, suppose (1) true for:

(G\cup\{(\Gamma,MN,A)\},E)
.
The rule for application produces the pair
(G^{\prime},E)

with
G^{\prime}=G\cup\{(\Gamma,M,t_{1}\rightarrow A),(\Gamma,N,t_{1})\}
.
Suppose
S\models(G^{\prime},E)
. This means

S\models(G,E)
,
S\Gamma\vdash M:S(t_{1}\rightarrow A)
, and
S\Gamma\vdash N:St_{1}
.
By
(\rightarrow_{E})
, we conclude
S\Gamma\vdash MN:SA
. Thus
S\models(G\cup\{(\Gamma,MN,A)\},E)
, and by
hypothesis
S\Gamma_{0}\vdash M_{0}:St_{0}
.

(2)\;
For instance, suppose:

\Gamma\vdash M_{0}:A
,

S\models(G\cup\{(\Gamma^{\prime},\lambda x.M,A^{\prime})\},E)
,

S\Gamma_{0}\subseteq\Gamma
, and

A=St_{0}
.
This implies:
S\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\lambda x.M:S(A^{\prime})
,
which entails:

S\Gamma^{\prime},x:A_{1}\vdash M:A_{2}
,

SA^{\prime}=A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
, for some
A_{1},A_{2}
.
Suppose we reduce to the pair:

(G\cup\{(\Gamma^{\prime},x:t_{x},M,t)\},E\cup\{A^{\prime}=t_{x}\rightarrow t\})~.

Then take
S^{\prime}=S[A_{1}/t_{x},A_{2}/t]
.
{\Box}

Remark 11.1

Property (1) entails the soundness of the method.
Indeed, suppose from the initial goal we derive a set of equations
E

and a substitution
S
such that
S\models E
(a unifier).
Then we derive a correct typing
S\Gamma_{0}\vdash M_{0}:St_{0}
.
On the other hand, property (2) entails the completeness of the method.
Suppose
\Gamma\vdash M_{0}:A
is a valid typing.
Then we can reduce
(\Gamma_{0},M_{0},t_{0})
to

(\emptyset,E)
and find a unifier
S
for
E
such that

S\Gamma_{0}
is contained in
\Gamma
and
St_{0}=A
.
In particular, if we take the most general unifier
S
of
E
and
we apply it to
t_{0}
we obtain the most general type:
every other type is an instance of
St_{0}
.

Example 11.1

The most general type of the
\lambda
-term

\lambda f.\lambda x.f(f(x))

is

(t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow(t\rightarrow t)
.
Note that strictly speaking the most general type is not unique.
For instance,

(s\rightarrow s)\rightarrow(s\rightarrow s)

is also a most general type of the
\lambda
-term considered.

Remark 11.2
(graphical presentation)

It is possible to give an equivalent ‘graphical’ presentation
of the unification method.
(1)
Rename bound variables so that they are all distinct and different from
the free ones.
(2)
Draw the tree associated with the
\lambda
-term.
(3) Associate a distinct type variable with every internal node of the tree.
(4) Associate a type variable
t_{x}
with a leaf node corresponding to the
variable
x
.
(5) For every abstraction node
(\lambda x.M^{t^{\prime}})^{t}
generate the
equation
t=t_{x}\rightarrow t^{\prime}
.
(6) For every application node
(M^{t^{\prime}}N^{t^{\prime\prime}})^{t}
generate the
equation
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow t
.

Exercise 11.1

Compute, if they exist, the most general types of the following
\lambda
-terms:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda z.xz(yz),&\lambda x.\lambda y.x(yx),&\lambda k.(k(\lambda x.\lambda h.hx))~.\end{array}

11.2
Reduction of unification to type inference (*)

We discuss a method to reduce any unification problem
to a type-inference problem. We also show that the principal types are
exactly the types inhabited by a closed
\lambda
-term.
We suppose as usual that
K\equiv\lambda x.\lambda y.x
.

Proposition 11.3

The principal type of the (closed)
\lambda
-term
{\it E}
below is

t\rightarrow t\rightarrow s\rightarrow s
.

{\it E}\equiv\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda w.Kw(\lambda f.\lambda p.p(fx)(fy))~.

Proof
. The fact that
f
is applied to both
x
and
y
forces the
equality of the types of
x
and
y
.
On the other hand, since the principal type of

K
is
t\rightarrow s\rightarrow t
, the type of
w
must be equal to the
type of the result.
{\Box}

Exercise 11.2

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be closed
\lambda
-terms with principal types

A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, respectively.
Prove that the principal type of the
\lambda
-term

\lambda f.{\it E}(fM_{1})M_{2}
is
(A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2})\rightarrow s\rightarrow s
.

Exercise 11.3

It is easy to find a closed
\lambda
-term which has type

t\rightarrow t\rightarrow t
. What about finding a closed

\lambda
-term whose principal type is
t\rightarrow t\rightarrow t
?
Corollary
11.2
gives a general method; look for
a more direct argument in the special case under consideration.

Proposition 11.4

For every type
A
with (type) variables contained in

\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\}
there is a closed
\lambda
-term

M_{A}
whose principal type is:

t_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow t_{n}\rightarrow A\rightarrow s\rightarrow s
,
where
s\notin\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\}
.

Proof
. By induction on the structure of
A
.
If
A=t_{i}
we take:

\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\lambda y.{\it E}x_{i}y:t_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow t_{n}\rightarrow t_{i}\rightarrow s\rightarrow s~.

If
A=A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
, by inductive hypothesis we have:

M_{A_{i}}:t_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow t_{n}\rightarrow A_{i}\rightarrow s\rightarrow s\quad i=1,2~.

We define:

M_{A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}}\equiv\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\lambda y.\lambda z.KzP~.

This
\lambda
-term has the expected type provided
we can force in the
\lambda
-term
P

the type of
y
to be
(A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2})
.
We observe that if we write:

Q_{i}\equiv M_{A_{i}}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y_{i}~.

we force the type of
y_{i}
to be
A_{i}
.
Then we can define
P
as follows:

P\equiv\lambda y_{1},y_{2}.\lambda p.pQ_{1}Q_{2}({\it E}(yy_{1})y_{2})~,

and as required the type of
y
is
A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
.
{\Box}

Exercise 11.4

(1) Apply the method to the types
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
and
(t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2})

relatively to the set of (type) variables
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}
.
(2) Write a program that builds the equivalent of the
\lambda
-term

M_{A}
in a language of the
{\it ML}
-family and uses the type-inference
system to compute its principal type.

Proposition 11.5

Given two types
A
and
B
there is a
\lambda
-term
U_{A,B}

which is typable if and only if
A
and
B

are unifiable.

Proof
. Let
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
be the type variables in
A
or
B
.
Applying proposition
11.4
, we derive the following terms and
principal types:

\begin{array}[]{ll}M_{A}:t_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow t_{n}\rightarrow A\rightarrow s\rightarrow s~,&M_{B}:t_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow t_{n}\rightarrow B\rightarrow s\rightarrow s~.\end{array}

Then we build:

\begin{array}[]{lll}U_{A,B}\equiv\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\lambda y_{1}.\lambda y_{2}.\lambda p.pP_{A}P_{B}({\it E}y_{1}y_{2}),&P_{A}\equiv M_{A}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y_{1},&P_{B}\equiv M_{B}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y_{2}~.\end{array}

{\Box}

Exercise 11.5

Apply proposition
11.5

if: (i)
A=t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}\rightarrow t_{2}
and
B=(t_{2}\rightarrow t_{2})\rightarrow t_{3}
and
(ii)
A=t_{1}
and
B=t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}
.

Corollary 11.1

Every unification problem can be reduced to a type-inference problem.

Proof
. We know from exercise
3.5

that every unification problem reduces to a unification problem
composed of one equation
with terms built over a signature with exactly one binary symbol.
We take ‘
\rightarrow
’ as binary symbol and using the proposition

11.5
above we build two types
A

and
B
which are unifiable iff the
\lambda
-term
U_{A,B}
is typable.
{\Box}

Proposition 11.6

For every type
A
, there is a
\lambda
-term

F_{(A\rightarrow A)}
whose principal type is
(A\rightarrow A)
.

Proof
. Let
A
be a type whose type variables are contained in

\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\}
.
Let
M_{A}
be a
\lambda
-term with principal type:

t_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow t_{n}\rightarrow A\rightarrow s\rightarrow s

(proposition
11.4
).
Then build the
\lambda
-term:

F_{A\rightarrow A}\equiv\lambda y.Ky(\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.(M_{A}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y))~.

This
\lambda
-term has principal type
(A\rightarrow A)
.
{\Box}

Corollary 11.2

Let
M
be a closed
\lambda
-term with type
A

(not necessarily its principal type).
Then one can build a closed
\lambda
-term
N
whose principal type
is
A
. Thus the inhabited types are exactly the principal types.

Proof
. By proposition
11.6
,
the principal type of
F_{A\rightarrow A}
is
(A\rightarrow A)
.
Then the
\lambda
-term
F_{A\rightarrow A}M
has principal type
A
.
{\Box}

11.3
Summary and references

A
type inference problem
can be (efficiently)
reduced to a
syntactic unification problem
.
Then the existence of a
most general unifier

is reflected back in the existence of a
most general type
.
We have also shown that every unification
problem reduces to a type inference problem
and that for every inhabited type
A
it is possible to build
a
\lambda
-term whose principal type is
A
.
Notice however that knowing if a type is inhabited is a
Pspace
-complete
problem
[
Sta79
]
.
The connection between type inference and unification
was already pointed out in
[
Hin69
]
.
By now, the reduction of a program analysis problem
to the solution of a set of constraints has become
a standard technique. For instance,
the data flow analyses performed by
optimizing compilers are reduced to
systems of monotonic boolean equations.

Chapter 12
Predicative polymorphic types and type inference

Consider any standard sorting algorithm
sort
on lists.
Most likely, the sorting algorithm just depends on a boolean
predicate on the elements of the list while the type of the elements
of the list does not really matter.
One says that the sorting algorithm is
polymorphic
in that
it can be applied to data of different, but related, shape.
We could type
sort
as follows:

{\sf sort}:\forall\,t\;\>{\sf list}(t)\rightarrow(t\rightarrow t\rightarrow{\sf bool})\rightarrow{\sf list}(t)~,

with the following intuitive meaning:
for any type
t
, given a list of elements of type
t
, and
a binary predicate on
t
, the function
sort
returns a list of elements
of type
t
.
Notice that we are assigning to
sort
a type which is not quite
simple
,

i.e.
, propositional,
as it contains a
universal quantification
over types.
In this chapter, we introduce a particular class of universally
quantified types which can be used to type
polymorphic functions. We then study the type inference problem for
the type system extended with such types.

12.1
Predicative universal types and polymorphism

The reader is supposed to be familiar with propositional
and first-order logic. In the standard interpretation of propositional logic
predicates are boolean values while in first-order logic
they are regarded as relations over some universe.
In second order logic, we can quantify over predicates.
For instance, here are some formulae in propositional, first-order, and
second-order logic:

\begin{array}[]{ll}(P\supset P)\supset(P\supset P)&\mbox{(Propositional formula)}\\
\forall\,x\;\>(P(x)\supset P({\sf s}(x)))\supset(P({\sf z})\supset\forall\,x\;\>P(x))&\mbox{(First-order formula)}\\
\forall\,P\;\>(P\supset P)\supset(P\supset P)&\mbox{(Second-order formula).}\par\end{array}

Following the types-as-formulae correspondence outlined in chapter

10
,
we may consider
a type system where quantification over type variables is allowed.
One fundamental question is whether a type with quantified
types should be regarded as an ordinary type, or if it should
be lifted to a superior status. In this chapter, we take
the second option. In the logical jargon, this
corresponds to a
predicative
approach to
second-order quantification. We shall not dwell into foundational issues
and just assume a distinction between types without quantification and
types with quantification which will be called henceforth
type
schema
. Thus:

\begin{array}[]{lll}A&\equiv(t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow(t\rightarrow t)&\mbox{is a {type},}\\
\sigma&\equiv\forall\,t\;\>(t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow(t\rightarrow t)&\mbox{is a {type schema}.}\end{array}

The advantage of this approach is that we stay close to propositional types
and that in this way we can generalize the type inference techniques
presented in chapter
11
. The inconvenience is that we do
not have the full power of second-order quantification. This power
will be explored in chapter
13
.
The syntax of types, type schemas, and type contexts
is specified as follows.

\begin{array}[]{lll}A::=b\mid\!\!\mid{\it tid}\mid\!\!\mid(A\rightarrow A)&\mbox{(types)}\\
\sigma::=A\mid\!\!\mid\forall{\it tid}.\sigma&\mbox{(type schemas)}\\
\Gamma::={\it id}:\sigma,\ldots,{\it id}:\sigma&\mbox{(type contexts).}\end{array}

We stress that
\forall t.(t\rightarrow t)
is
not
a type and

\forall t.t\rightarrow\forall t.t
is
not
a type schema.
Table
12.1
presents an extended type system with type schemas.
Notice that types schemas can occur in type contexts and that
in the rules
(\rightarrow_{I})
and
(\rightarrow_{E})
,
we handle
types
(not type schemas)

({\it asmp})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}x:\sigma\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash x:\sigma\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{cc}(\rightarrow_{I})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:A\rightarrow B\end{array}\par&(\rightarrow_{E})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A\rightarrow B\hskip 17.00024pt\Gamma\vdash N:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash MN:B\end{array}\par\\
\\
(\forall_{I})\par\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\hskip 8.50012ptt\notin{\sf ftv}(\Gamma)\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash M:\forall t.\sigma\end{array}\par\par&(\forall_{E})\par\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:\forall t.\sigma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash M:[A/t]\sigma\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 12.1:
Predicative type system (Curry-style)

Next, we explore the connection between

universal (predicative) types
and

polymorphism
.
Sometimes, the same code/function can be applied to different
data-types. For instance, the functional that iterates twice a function

D\equiv\lambda f.\lambda x.f(fx)
,
will work equally well on a function over
booleans
or over

integers
.
In the context of
propositional types
,
we have already seen that we can automatically infer for
D

the
most general type
:

D:(t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow(t\rightarrow t)~.

The reader may be under the impression that this type is good enough to
represent the fact that
D
will work on any argument of type

(A\rightarrow A)
. Almost but
not quite
\ldots

Suppose:

\begin{array}[]{llll}F_{1}:&({\sf bool}\rightarrow{\sf bool})~,\qquad F_{2}:&({\sf int}\rightarrow{\sf int})~,\end{array}

and consider the
\lambda
-term
P\equiv{\sf let}\ f=D\ {\sf in}\ \langle fF_{1},fF_{2}\rangle
,
where as usual

{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N\equiv(\lambda x.N)M

and
\langle M,N\rangle\equiv\lambda z.zMN
.
The reader may check that the
\lambda
-term
P
has no propositional type and that
the example can be rephrased in the pure
\lambda
-calculus without
appealing to the basic types
{\sf bool}
and
{\sf int}
.
A possible
way out
is to consider that
D
has a
type schema
:

\sigma\equiv\forall t.(t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow(t\rightarrow t)~,

and then to
specialize
it just before it is applied to
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
.
This is
almost
what we can do with the predicative
type system in Table
12.1
.
The
problem
that remains is that we cannot really type the

\lambda
-term

\ (\lambda f.\langle fF_{1},fF_{2}\rangle)D\

as expected because
\sigma\rightarrow\cdots
is
not
even a type schema
according to our definitions.
One could allow
more complex types
\ldots
, but there is
a more
conservative solution
which consists in taking
the
let
-definition as a
primitive
and
giving the following typing rule for it:

\begin{array}[]{ll}({\sf let})\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:\sigma\vdash N:A\quad\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N:A\end{array}~.\par\end{array}

This is a
first formalization
(others will follow) of a type system
which captures the polymorphism available in the
{\it ML}
family of
programming languages.

Example 12.1

Consider
M\equiv\lambda y.{\sf let}\ x=\lambda z.z\ {\sf in}\ y(xx)
which is
not typable in the propositional type system but has type

A\equiv((t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow t^{\prime})\rightarrow t^{\prime}

in the
{\it ML}
type system.
The main difference is that we assign to the variable
x
the type schema

\sigma\equiv\forall s.(s\rightarrow s)
.
Then taking
B\equiv(t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow t^{\prime}
we can derive:

\begin{array}[]{c}y:B,z:s\vdash z:s\\
\hline\cr y:B\vdash\lambda z.z:(s\rightarrow s)\quad s\notin{\sf ftv}(B)\\
\hline\cr y:B\vdash\lambda z.z:\sigma\end{array}

On the other hand, one can derive:

y:B,x:\sigma\vdash(xx):(t\rightarrow t)
.

The rules
(\forall_{I})
and
(\forall_{E})
in Table
12.1
are
not syntax-directed
. When do we apply them?
One possibility is to
ask the programmer
to specify when
this must be done.
This requires an
enriched syntax
for
\lambda
-terms which
includes: (i) the possibility to abstract a
\lambda
-term
M
with respect to a
type variable
t
, a
type abstraction

\lambda t.M
, and (ii)
the possibility to apply a
\lambda
-term
M
to a type
A
,
a
type application

MA
.
The resulting system in
Church-style
is composed of the
rules
({\it asmp})
,
(\rightarrow_{I})
,
(\rightarrow_{E})
, and
({\sf let})

we have already presented modulo the fact that:
(1) the
\lambda
-abstraction
is decorated with a type (cf. Table
10.1
),
and (2) the rules in Table
12.2
replace
the homonymous rules in Table
12.1
.

\begin{array}[]{cc}(\forall_{I})\par\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\hskip 8.50012ptt\notin{\sf ftv}(\Gamma)\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\lambda t.M:\forall t.\sigma\end{array}\par\par&(\forall_{E})\par\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:\forall t.\sigma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash MA:[A/t]\sigma\end{array}\par\par\end{array}

Table 12.2:
Rules
(\forall_{I})
and
(\forall_{E})
in Church-style

Due to the simplicity of the
{\it ML}
system, it is
actually
possible to foresee
the points where the rules

(\forall_{I})
and
(\forall_{E})
need to be applied.
This leads to a
Curry-style

and
syntax directed
type system: the shape of the
\lambda
-term
determines the rule to apply.

Definition 12.1
(generalisation)

Given a pair composed of a context
\Gamma
and a type
A
,
its generalization
G(\Gamma,A)
is defined as
the type schema that results by quantifying the type variables which occur
in
A
but do not occur free in
\Gamma
.

Example 12.2

If
\Gamma=x:\forall t.(s\rightarrow t)
and

A=s\rightarrow(t\rightarrow r)
then
G(\Gamma,A)=\forall t.\forall r.A
.

The idea to define the syntax-directed type system presented in Table

12.3

is to
generalize as much as possible
let-variables and then

instantiate once
the type schema in the context.
We shall use the entailment symbol
\vdash^{{\it syn}}
when referring
to judgments in this system.
Unlike in the type system in Table
12.1
, the syntax-directed
type system in Table
12.3
can only assign
types

to
\lambda
-terms (
not
type schema).

\begin{array}[]{lclc}({\it asmp})&\begin{array}[]{c}x:\forall t^{*}.A\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it syn}}x:[B^{*}/t^{*}]A\end{array}\par\par&({\sf let})&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:G(\Gamma,B)\vdash^{{\it syn}}N:A\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash^{{\it syn}}M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it syn}}{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N:A\end{array}\\
\\
(\rightarrow_{I})&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{{\it syn}}M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it syn}}\lambda x.M:A\rightarrow B\end{array}\par\par&(\rightarrow_{E})&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash^{{\it syn}}M:A\rightarrow B\hskip 17.00024pt\Gamma\vdash^{{\it syn}}N:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it syn}}MN:B\end{array}\par\par\end{array}

Table 12.3:
Predicative type system (Curry-style, syntax directed)

Exercise 12.1
(running example, continued)

Consider again the
\lambda
-term:

M\equiv\lambda y.{\sf let}\ x=\lambda z.z\ {\sf in}\ y(xx)~,

and check that we can derive:

\emptyset\vdash^{{\it syn}}M:((t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow t^{\prime})\rightarrow t^{\prime}
.

12.2
A type inference algorithm

Building on the syntax directed presentation of the type system,
we describe next a type inference algorithm.
In this section, we rely on the following
notation
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}M,N&\mbox{type free $\lambda$-terms with {\sf let}-definitions,}\\
\Gamma&\mbox{type context with {propositional} types,}\\
\Theta&\mbox{partial function from identifiers to pairs $(\Gamma,A)$.}\end{array}

The (partial) function

{\it PT}(M,\Theta)

tries to infer a principal typing judgment
\Gamma\vdash M:A
for
M
.
The search is
driven by

M
while
\Theta

is a device that implicitly keeps track of the
type schema assigned to let-bound variables.
Because of this device, the algorithm just manipulates
simple, propositional types.

We assume: (i) all bound variables are renamed so as to be distinct
and different from the free variables, and (ii) in all subterms

{\sf let}\ x=N\ {\sf in}\ M
we have
x\in{\sf fv}(M)
.
Given two typing judgments
J_{i}\equiv\Gamma_{i}\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}
,
i=1,2
, we
denote by
{\it UnifyApl}(J_{1},J_{2})
a triple
(S,t,J^{\prime}_{2})
obtained as follows:

1.

obtain
J^{\prime}_{2}\equiv\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}\vdash M_{2}:A^{\prime}_{2}
by
renaming
the type variables of
J_{2}
so
that they are
disjoint
from those in
J_{1}
,

2.

select a
fresh
type variable
t
,

3.

build the
system of equations
:

E=\{A_{1}=A^{\prime}_{2}\rightarrow t\}\cup\{A=A^{\prime}\mid x:A\in\Gamma_{1},x:A^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}\},

4.

compute (if it exists) a
most general unifier

S
of
E
.

With this notation, the type inference algorithm is presented in
Table
12.4
.

\begin{array}[]{|l|}\hline\cr{\it PT}(M,\Theta)={\sf case}\ M\\
\hline\cr\\
\begin{array}[]{lll}x:&{\sf case}\ \Theta(x)\\
&(\Gamma,A)&:\Gamma\vdash x:A\\
&\_&:x:t_{x}\vdash x:t_{x}\end{array}\\
\\
\hline\cr\\
\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda x.M:&{\sf let}\ (\Gamma\vdash M:A)={\it PT}(M,\Theta)\ {\sf in}\\
&\begin{array}[]{ll}{\sf case}&x:A^{\prime}\in\Gamma\\
{\sf true}&:\Gamma\backslash(x:A^{\prime})\vdash\lambda x.M:A^{\prime}\rightarrow A\\
\_&:\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:t\rightarrow A,t\mbox{ fresh}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\\
\hline\cr\\
\begin{array}[]{ll}M_{1}M_{2}:&{\sf let}\ J_{i}\equiv(\Gamma_{i}\vdash M_{i}:A_{i})={\it PT}(M_{i},\Theta)\ i=1,2\ {\sf in}\\
&{\sf let}\ (S,t,\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}\vdash M_{2}:A^{\prime}_{2})={\it UnifyApl}(J_{1},J_{2})\ {\sf in}\\
&S(\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}\vdash M_{1}M_{2}:t)\end{array}\\
\\
\hline\cr\\
\begin{array}[]{ll}{\sf let}\ x=M_{1}\ {\sf in}\ M_{2}:\\
&{\sf let}\ (\Gamma_{1}\vdash M_{1}:A_{1})={\it PT}(M_{1},\Theta)\ {\sf in}\\
&{\sf let}\ \Theta^{\prime}=\Theta[(\Gamma_{1},A_{1})/x]\ {\sf in}\\
&{\sf let}\ (\Gamma_{2}\vdash M_{2}:A_{2})={\it PT}(M_{2},\Theta^{\prime})\ {\sf in}\\
&\Gamma_{2}\vdash{\sf let}\ x=M_{1}\ {\sf in}\ M_{2}:A_{2}\end{array}\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 12.4:
Type-inference algorithm

Exercise 12.2
(running example, continued)

Consider again the
\lambda
-term:

M\equiv\lambda y.{\sf let}\ x=\lambda z.z\ {\sf in}\ y(xx)

and check that

{\it PT}(M,\emptyset)=\emptyset\vdash M:((t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow t^{\prime})\rightarrow t^{\prime}
.

12.3
Reduction of stratified polymorphic typing to propositional typing (*)

We may consider a type system where to type
{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N
we actually
type
M
and
[N/x]M
, where by typing we mean
propositional typing
.
This way of proceeding is
not particularly efficient

because the
let
-expansion might take exponential time
(see following exercise
12.4
).
However, the interesting point is that the
\lambda
-terms
typable in this way are
exactly those typable in the original
{\it ML}
system. Therefore we have
the following
intuitive characterization
:

{\it ML}
typing = Propositional typing + let-expansion.

The type inference algorithm presented in Table
12.4

is a way to
keep
implicit the let-expansion

(but type renaming still forces a type expansion as we shall see shortly!).
Table
12.5
describes a type system based on let-expansion.
We use the entailment symbol
\vdash^{{\it let}}
to distinguish this system from the
previous ones. In the
({\sf let})
rule,
we just check that the substituted term
M
is typable with some type
B
;
this check is necessary if
x\notin{\sf fv}(M)
.

\begin{array}[]{lclc}({\it asmp})&\begin{array}[]{c}x:A\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it let}}x:A\end{array}\par\par\par&({\sf let})&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash^{{\it let}}[M/x]N:A\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash^{{\it let}}M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it let}}{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N:A\end{array}\par\\
\\
(\rightarrow_{I})&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{{\it let}}M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it let}}\lambda x.M:A\rightarrow B\end{array}\par&(\rightarrow_{E})&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash^{{\it let}}M:A\rightarrow B\hskip 17.00024pt\Gamma\vdash^{{\it let}}N:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it let}}MN:B\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 12.5:
Propositional typing with let-expansion

Exercise 12.3
(running example)

Consider again the
\lambda
-term:

M\equiv\lambda y.{\sf let}\ x=\lambda z.z\ {\sf in}\ y(xx)~,

and check that we can derive:

\emptyset\vdash^{{\it let}}M:((t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow t^{\prime})\rightarrow t^{\prime}
.

Exercise 12.4
(let-expansion)

Consider a
\lambda
-calculus extended
with
let
definitions of the shape
{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N
.
Let
C
denote a context with a hole (cf. definition
7.3
) and
define the reduction relation
\rightarrow_{{\sf let}}
as follows:

\rightarrow_{{\sf let}}=\{(\ C[{\sf let}\ x=N\ {\sf in}\ M]\ ,\ C[[N/x]M]\ )\mid C\mbox{ context},M,N\mbox{ $\lambda$-terms },x\mbox{ variable}\}~.

We extend the definition of size of a
\lambda
-term
|M|
(cf. definition
7.1
) with:

|{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N|=1+|M|+|N|
.
We also define the depth
d(M)
of a
\lambda
-term as follows:

\begin{array}[]{llll}d(x)=1,&d(MN)={\it max}(d(M),d(N)),\\
d(\lambda x.M)=d(M),&d({\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N)=d(M)+d(N)~.\end{array}

1.

Show that there is a strategy to reduce a
\lambda
-term
M
to a normal form
N
such that:

|N|\leq|M|^{d(M)}~.

2.

Show that the reduction relation
\rightarrow_{{\sf let}}
is locally confluent.

How hard is it to
decide
if a
\lambda
-term
is typable in the
{\it ML}
system?
Well, in
theory
it is hard but in
practice
it is easy!
The characterization via propositional typing with let expansion shows
that the problem can be solved in
exponential time
:
(1)
let
-expand the
\lambda
-term (exponential penalty),
(2) reduce the propositional type-inference problem to a unification problem
(efficient), and
(3) solve the unification problem (efficient).

In fact one can show that
any decision problem
that runs in exponential time can be coded as an
{\it ML}
type inference problem.
Hence any algorithm (including the symbolic one) that solves the
problem will run in
at least exponential time
.
The good news are that the complexity is exponential in the

let-depth
(example next) of the
\lambda
-term and
that deeply nested chains of let-definitions do not seem
to appear in practice.

Example 12.3

Here is a way to blow up
{\it ML}
type inference.

\begin{array}[]{lll}P&\equiv\lambda x,y,z.zxy&:t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}\rightarrow(t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}\rightarrow t_{3})\rightarrow t_{3}\\
M_{1}&\equiv\lambda y.Pyy&:t_{1}\rightarrow(t_{1}\rightarrow t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2})\rightarrow t_{2}\\
M_{2}&\equiv\lambda y.M_{1}(M_{1}y)&:t_{1}\rightarrow(((t_{1}\rightarrow t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2})\rightarrow t_{2})\rightarrow\\
&&((t_{1}\rightarrow t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2})\rightarrow t_{2})\rightarrow t_{3})\rightarrow t_{3}\\
M_{3}&\equiv\lambda y.M_{2}(M_{2}y)&:\cdots\end{array}

The number of distinct type variables and the
size of the principal type (roughly) doubles at each step so that
inferring the principal type of
M_{6}
is already problematic.

12.4
Summary and references

Universally quantified
types are the types of
polymorphic

\lambda
-terms. In particular we have considered a
predicative/stratified

form of universal quantification (as used in
{\it ML}
).
It turns out that the type inference techniques developed in chapter

11
can be
extended
to predicative polymorphism. The
complexity of type inference is then
exponential
in the number
of nested let-definitions. Still the approach works well because these
complex definitions do not seem to arise in practice.
The design of a polymorphic type system for the
{\it ML}
language is
due to
[
Mil78
,
LM82
]
. The complexity
of the type inference problem is characterized in

[
KTU90
,
Mai90
]
. The book
[
Mit96
]

contains a detailed analysis of the type inference algorithm
described in Table
12.4
.

Chapter 13
Impredicative polymorphic types

In chapter
12
, we have introduced
universally quantified types and observed that these types
can be regarded as the types of polymorphic functions.
In that context, a universally quantified type lives in a higher
universe of
type schemas
. In this chapter, we consider
an alternative approach where a universally quantified type
is still an ordinary type. Then one speaks of
impredicative

types as opposed to the
predicative
types introduced
in chapter
12
.
In order to formalize impredicative types we introduce an
extension of the propositional type system presented in chapter
10

known as
system F
.

A strong point of system F is its
expressive power. In particular, we show that the
addition of impredicative universal quantification suffices to
represent product, sum, and existential types.
The reader is supposed to be familiar with the usage of product and sum
types in programming. As for existential types, we shall see that they
arise naturally when hiding the representation details of a data type.

We also provide an encoding of inductively defined data structures such as
natural numbers, lists, and trees, and of the iterative functions
definable on them (iterative functions are related to the
primitive recursive functions introduced in chapter

6
).

While being quite expressive, system F can still be regarded
as a logical system. In particular,
\lambda
-terms typable
in system F are strongly normalizing. This is a difficult
result that relies on a generalization of the reducibility
candidates technique introduced in chapter
10
.

13.1
System F

System F is a
logical system
obtained from
the propositional intuitionistic system (propositional types as
far as we are concerned) by introducing second order
quantification. At the type level, we can quantify
over type variables:

A\equiv\forall\,t\;\>(t\rightarrow t)~.

At the term level, we can
abstract with respect to a type and apply a
\lambda
-term to a type.
For instance, we can define a ‘polymorphic’ identity

{\it pid}\equiv\lambda t.\lambda x:t.x
with the type
A
above.
By applying
{\it pid}
to the basic type
{\it nat}
,
we obtain an identity
{\it pid}\ {\it nat}
of type

{\it nat}\rightarrow{\it nat}
.
However, we may also apply
{\it pid}
to the type
A
itself to
obtain an identity
{\it pid}\ A
of type
(A\rightarrow A)
.
In System F, the type quantification in the type
A
quantifies on

all types
including
A
itself.
One says that the type system is
impredicative
, as opposed
to the
predicative/stratified

system we have considered in chapter
12
.

Table
13.1
defines the syntax of types and
\lambda
-terms
where we denote with
{\sf ftv}(\Gamma)
the collection of type variables that
occur free in types occurring in the (type) context
\Gamma
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\it tid}&::=t\mid\!\!\mid s\mid\!\!\mid\ldots&\mbox{(type variables)}\\
A&::={\it tid}\mid\!\!\mid A\rightarrow A\mid\!\!\mid\forall{\it tid}.A&\mbox{(types)}\\
{\it id}&::=x\mid\!\!\mid y\mid\!\!\mid\ldots&\mbox{(variables)}\\
M&::={\it id}\mid\!\!\mid\lambda{\it id}:A.M\mid\!\!\mid MM\mid\!\!\mid\lambda{\it tid}.M\mid\!\!\mid MA&\mbox{($\lambda$-terms)}\\
\Gamma&::={\it id}:A,\ldots,{\it id}:A&\mbox{(contexts)}\par\end{array}

Table 13.1:
Syntax of system F: types and
\lambda
-terms (Church style)

Table
13.2
introduces the typing rules in Church-style
and the reduction rules of system F.
The novelties with respect to the system for propositional types
(Table
10.1
)
are represented by the typing rules
(\forall_{I})

and
(\forall_{E})
and the
(\beta_{t})
-rule for reducing
the application of a type abstraction to a type.
We stress that in this chapter the
(\beta)
and
(\beta_{t})
rules,
as well as the following

(\eta)
and
(\eta_{t})
rules, can be applied in any context.

Typing rules

\begin{array}[]{c}{\it(asmp)}\par\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}x:A\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash x:A\end{array}\\
\\
(\rightarrow_{I})\par\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\lambda x:A.M:A\rightarrow B\end{array}\par\hskip 17.00024pt(\rightarrow_{E})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A\rightarrow B~~\Gamma\vdash N:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash MN:B\end{array}\\
\\
(\forall_{I})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A~~t\notin{\sf ftv}(\Gamma)\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\lambda t.M:\forall t.A\end{array}\par\hskip 17.00024pt(\forall_{E})\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:\forall t.A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash MB:[B/t]A\end{array}\par\end{array}

Reduction rules (in any context)

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\lambda x:A.M)N&\rightarrow[N/x]M&(\beta)\\
(\lambda t.M)A&\rightarrow[A/t]M&(\beta_{t})\end{array}

Table 13.2:
Typing (Church-style) and reduction rules in system F

Exercise 13.1

Show that without the side condition ‘
t\notin{\sf ftv}(\Gamma)
’ in rule

(\forall_{I})
, one can build a closed
\lambda
-term of type

A
, for any type
A
.
In other terms, without the side condition
the system is logically inconsistent!

As usual, we can add
extensional
rules.
The
(\eta)
and
(\eta_{t})
reduction rules
(applicable in any context) are the following:

\begin{array}[]{llll}(\lambda x:A.Mx)&\rightarrow M&\mbox{if }x\notin{\sf fv}(M)&(\eta)\\
(\lambda t.Mt)&\rightarrow M&\mbox{if }t\notin{\sf ftv}(M)&(\eta_{t})~.\end{array}

We leave it to the reader the check that in system F
with the
\beta
and
\beta_{t}
-rules (and possibly with
the
\eta
and
\eta_{t}
rules): (1)
typing is preserved by reduction, and
(2) reduction is locally confluent.
In section
13.3
, we shall prove that
typable
\lambda
-terms are
strongly normalizable
;
thus confluence will follow from local confluence.

As a first example of the expressivity of second order
quantification, we consider the representation of
product, sum, and existential types in system F.
The typing rules and the reduction rules are introduced
in Table
13.3
.
The reader should be familiar with the rules
for product and sum which have already been introduced
in Table
10.4
.
On the other hand, the rules for existential
types are new and deserve some comments.
A
\lambda
-term of existential type
\exists t.A
is (up to conversion)
a pair composed of a type
B
and a
\lambda
-term of
type
[B/t]A
. Existential types can
be used to hide the details of the implementation of
a data type and as such they can be regarded as ‘abstract data types’.
For instance, suppose we want to represent sets of numbers
with operations to create the empty set, test membership, insert a
number in the set, and remove a number from the set.
Assuming,
{\bf 1}
is the unit type,

\mathbf{N}
is the type for natural numbers, and
\mathbf{B}
the type
for booleans, we could specify the signature of a
set
data type as:

A\equiv\exists t.((1\rightarrow t)\times(\mathbf{N}\rightarrow t\rightarrow\mathbf{B})\times(\mathbf{N}\rightarrow t\rightarrow t)\times(\mathbf{N}\rightarrow t\rightarrow t))~.

(13.1)

We could then produce a concrete implementation of the data type
by instantiating the type
t
, say, with the type of the
lists of natural numbers along with the implementations of the
operations mentioned above.
We stress that the type
A
above
just describes the
signature
of a
set
data type
but
not
its expected
behavior
.
For instance, there is no guarantee that inserting a number
in a set and then removing it produces a set which equals the
original one.

Typing rules

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}\hskip 8.50012pti=1,2\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle:A_{1}\times A_{2}\end{array}\par&\left.\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{1}\times A_{2}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\pi_{1}(M):A_{1}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{1}\times A_{2}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\pi_{2}(M):A_{2}\end{array}\end{array}\right.\\
\\
\left.\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{1}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf in_{1}^{A_{1}+A_{2}}}(M):A_{1}+A_{2}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{2}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf in_{2}^{A_{1}+A_{2}}}(M):A_{1}+A_{2}\end{array}\end{array}\right.\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A_{1}+A_{2}\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash N_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow C\hskip 8.50012pti=1,2\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf case}(M,N_{1},N_{2}):C\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:[B/t]A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf pack}^{\exists t.A}(B,M):\exists t.A\end{array}\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:\exists t.A\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash N:\forall t.(A\rightarrow C)\hskip 8.50012ptt\notin{\sf ftv}(C)\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf unpack}(M,N):C\end{array}\par\end{array}

Reduction rules (in any context)

\begin{array}[]{llll}\pi_{i}\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle&\rightarrow&M_{i}&i=1,2\\
{\sf case}({\sf in_{i}^{A+B}}M)N_{1}N_{2}&\rightarrow&N_{i}M&i=1,2\\
{\sf unpack}({\sf pack}^{\exists t.A}(B,M),N)&\rightarrow&NBM\par\end{array}

Table 13.3:
Product, sum, and existential types

Type encoding

\begin{array}[]{ll}\underline{t}&=t\\
\underline{A\rightarrow B}&=\underline{A}\rightarrow\underline{B}\\
\underline{\forall t.A}&=\forall t.\underline{A}\\
\underline{A_{1}\times A_{2}}&=\forall s.(\underline{A_{1}}\rightarrow\underline{A_{2}}\rightarrow s)\rightarrow s\\
\underline{A_{1}+A_{2}}&=\forall s.(\underline{A_{1}}\rightarrow s)\rightarrow(\underline{A_{2}}\rightarrow s)\rightarrow s\\
\underline{\exists t.A}&=\forall s.(\forall t.(\underline{A}\rightarrow s))\rightarrow s\par\end{array}

Term encoding

\begin{array}[]{lll}\langle,\rangle&=\lambda x_{1}:\underline{A_{1}},x_{2}:\underline{A_{2}}.\lambda s.\lambda p:\underline{A_{1}}\rightarrow(\underline{A_{2}}\rightarrow s).px_{1}x_{2}\\
\pi_{i}&=\lambda p:\underline{A_{1}\times A_{2}}.p\underline{A_{i}}(\lambda x_{1}:\underline{A_{1}},x_{2}:\underline{A_{2}}.x_{i})&(i=1,2)\\
{\sf in_{i}^{A_{1}+A_{2}}}&=\lambda x:\underline{A_{i}}.\lambda s.\lambda y_{1}:\underline{A_{1}}\rightarrow s.\lambda y_{2}:\underline{A_{2}}\rightarrow s.y_{i}x&(i=1,2)\\
{\sf case}&=\lambda x:\underline{A_{1}+A_{2}}.\lambda s.\lambda y_{1}:\underline{A_{1}}\rightarrow s,y_{2}:\underline{A_{2}}\rightarrow s.xsy_{1}y_{2}\\
{\sf pack}^{\exists t.A}&=\lambda t.\lambda x:\underline{A}.\lambda s.\lambda y:\forall t.\underline{A}\rightarrow s.y\ t\ x\\
{\sf unpack}&=\lambda x:\underline{\exists t.A}.\lambda y:\forall t.\underline{A}\rightarrow C.xCy\par\end{array}

Table 13.4:
Representation of product, sum, and existential types in system F

Table
13.4
describes an encoding of product,
sum, and existential types and
\lambda
-terms in system F. This encoding
is quite good, as shown by the following proposition, and it can be used,

e.g.
, to reduce the strong normalization of the extended system
to the strong normalization of system F.

Proposition 13.1

Suppose
\Gamma\vdash M:A
in the system F extended with product,
sum, and existential types (table
13.3
).
Then, the encoding described in Table
13.4

preserves typing and reduction. Namely,
(1)
\underline{\Gamma}\vdash\underline{M}:\underline{A}
and (2)
if
M\rightarrow N
then
\underline{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}\underline{N}
.

Proof
.
(1)\;
First check that the type encoding commutes
with substitution. Then proceed by induction on the proof of
\Gamma\vdash M:A
.

(2)\;
First check that the term encoding commutes with substitution.
Then proceed by case analysis on the redex.
{\Box}

Exercise 13.2

Show that for every type context
\Gamma
and
\lambda
-term
M
there is
at most one type
A
such that
\Gamma\vdash M:A
is derivable
according to the rules in Tables
13.2
and

13.3
, and that in this case the derivation is unique.
What happens if we remove the type labels attached to the operators

{\sf in_{1}}
,
{\sf in_{2}}
, and
{\sf pack}
?

13.2
Inductive types and iterative functions (*)

Iterative functions
are defined on the ground (with no variables),
first-order terms over a signature
\Sigma
.
The basic idea is to define a function

by induction on the structure of a ground term
,
hence we have as many cases as function symbols in
the signature
\Sigma
.
Let us consider the signature of

tally natural numbers

\Sigma=\{{\sf s}^{1},{\sf z}^{0}\}

and let
T=T_{\Sigma}(\emptyset)
be the set of ground terms.
Given
g:T^{n}\rightarrow T
and
h:T^{n+1}\rightarrow T
the function

f:T^{n+1}\rightarrow T
is defined by
iteration
by the following
term rewriting rules:

\begin{array}[]{cc}f({\sf z},y^{*})\rightarrow g(y^{*})~,&f({\sf s}(x),y^{*})\rightarrow h(f(x,y^{*}),y^{*})~.\end{array}

At first sight this is less powerful than primitive recursive
definitions because the function
h

does not
depend directly on
x
.

However, one can first define
pairing
and
projections
and then
show that a function
f
defined by
primitive recursion
such as:

\begin{array}[]{cc}f({\sf z},y^{*})\rightarrow g(y^{*})~,&f({\sf s}(x),y^{*})\rightarrow h(f(x,y^{*}),x,y^{*})~,\end{array}

can also be defined by
iteration
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}f^{\prime}({\sf z},y^{*})\rightarrow\langle g(y^{*}),{\sf Z}\rangle~,&f^{\prime}({\sf s}(x),y^{*})\rightarrow h^{\prime}(f^{\prime}(x,y^{*}),y^{*})~,\end{array}

where

h^{\prime}(x,y^{*})\rightarrow\langle h(\pi_{1}(x),\pi_{2}(x),y^{*}),{\sf s}(\pi_{2}(x))\rangle
.
One checks by induction on
x\in T
that:

f^{\prime}(x,y^{*})=\langle f(x,y^{*}),x\rangle
,
and from
f^{\prime}
one obtains
f
by projection.

Exercise 13.3
(predecessor, equality)

1.

Give a primitive recursive definition of the predecessor function
p

where
p(0)=0
. Then transform the definition into an iterative
definition and derive a
\lambda
-term, typable in system F, to
compute the predecessor function on Church numerals
(cf. exercise
7.5
).

2.

Derive
\lambda
-terms that compute the subtraction and check the inequality and equality of two Church numerals.

Definition 13.1
(iterative functions)

Let
\Sigma
be a signature with
function symbols (constructors)
c_{i}
,
where
{\it ar}(c_{i})=n_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,k
.
Let
T=T_{\Sigma}(\emptyset)
be the closed first-order terms over the signature.
The collection of iterative functions
is the smallest set such that:

•

The functions induced by the constructors and the
projection functions are iterative functions.

•

The set is closed under composition, namely
if
g:T^{n}\rightarrow T
and
h_{i}:T^{m}\rightarrow T
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
are iterative functions then
g(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})
is an iterative function.

•

The set is closed under iteration, namely if

h_{i}:T^{n_{i}+m}\rightarrow T
, for
i=1,\ldots,k
,
are iterative functions then the function

f:T^{m+1}\rightarrow T
such that:

f(c_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{i}}),y^{*})=h_{i}(f(x_{1},y^{*}),\ldots,f(x_{n_{i}},y^{*}),y^{*})\qquad(\mbox{for }i=1,\ldots,k)~,

is an iterative function.

Table
13.5
explains how to associate:
(1) with a
signature

\Sigma
a
type

\underline{\Sigma}
of system F,
(2) with a
constructor
of the signature
\Sigma
a
closed
\lambda
-term
of system F of the appropriate type, and (3)
with a
ground term

a
over the signature
\Sigma
a
\lambda
-term

\underline{a}
of system F with type
\underline{\Sigma}
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\Sigma&=\{c_{i}^{n_{i}}:\underbrace{T\times\cdots\times T}_{n_{i}~{\it times}}\rightarrow T\mid i=1,\ldots,k\}\\
\underline{\Sigma}&\equiv\forall t.A_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{k}\rightarrow t,\hskip 8.50012pt\mbox{where: }A_{i}\equiv\underbrace{t\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow t}_{n_{i}~{\it times}}\rightarrow t~,\\
\underline{c_{i}^{n_{i}}}&\equiv\lambda y_{1}:\underline{\Sigma}\ldots\lambda y_{n_{i}}:\underline{\Sigma}.\hskip 8.50012pt\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:A_{1}\ldots\lambda x_{k}:A_{k}.\\
&\hskip 8.50012ptx_{i}(y_{1}tx_{1}\cdots x_{k})\cdots(y_{n_{i}}tx_{1}\cdots x_{k}):\underbrace{\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma}}_{n_{i}~{\it times}}\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma}\\
\underline{a}&\equiv\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:A_{1}\ldots\lambda x_{k}:A_{k}.[\![a]\!]~,\mbox{with: }[\![c_{i}^{n_{i}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{i}})]\!]\equiv x_{i}[\![a_{1}]\!]\cdots[\![a_{n_{i}}]\!]~.\par\end{array}

Table 13.5:
Encoding of signatures, constructors, and ground terms in system F

Example 13.1
(tally natural numbers)

If we apply the coding method to the signature

\Sigma=\{{\sf s}^{1},{\sf z}^{0}\}

of tally natural numbers we obtain the type:

\underline{\Sigma}\equiv\forall t.(t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow(t\rightarrow t)~.

Then we represent the constructors in the signature with the

\lambda
-terms:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\underline{{\sf s}}&\equiv\lambda y:\underline{\Sigma}.\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:t\rightarrow t.\lambda x_{2}:t.x_{1}(ytx_{1}x_{2})&:\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma}\\
\underline{{\sf z}}&\equiv\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:t\rightarrow t.\lambda x_{2}:t.x_{2}&:\underline{\Sigma}~.\par\end{array}

The term
n\equiv{\sf s}^{n}{\sf z}
,
n\geq 0
,
is represented (up to conversion) by the
\lambda
-term:

\underline{n}\equiv\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:t\rightarrow t.\lambda x_{2}:t.x_{1}^{n}x_{2}:\underline{\Sigma}~,

which is a typed version of the Church numeral presented in
section
7.3
.
We notice that:

\begin{array}[]{llll}\underline{s}\ \underline{n}&\rightarrow\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:t\rightarrow t.\lambda x_{2}:t.x_{1}(\underline{n}\ t\ x_{1}\ x_{2})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:t\rightarrow t.\lambda x_{2}:t.x_{1}(x_{1}^{n}\ x_{2})&\equiv\underline{n+1}~.\end{array}

Exercise 13.4

Make explicit the coding of the following signatures:
(1) The signature with no operation.
(2) The signature with two
0
-ary operations (the ‘booleans’).
(3) The signature of binary words.
(4) The signature of binary trees.

Proposition 13.2

There is a bijective correspondence between the ground terms over
a signature
\Sigma
and the closed
\lambda
-terms of system F of the
corresponding type
\underline{\Sigma}
modulo
\beta\eta
-conversion.

Proof
. Let
M
be a closed
\lambda
-term of system F in
\beta
-normal form of type
\underline{\Sigma}
,
where
\underline{\Sigma}
is defined according to the rules in Table
13.5
.
The existence of the
\beta
-normal form will be proved in section
13.3
. So the
\lambda
-term
M
has to have the shape:

M\equiv\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:A_{1}\ldots\lambda x_{i}:A_{i}.M^{\prime}~,~~i\leq k~.

If
i<k
and
M^{\prime}
is not a
\lambda
-abstraction then
M^{\prime}
has the
shape
(\cdots(x_{j}M_{1})\cdots M_{h})
and so we can
\eta
-expand

M^{\prime}
without introducing a
\beta
-redex.
By iterated
\eta
-expansions
we arrive at a
\lambda
-term in
\beta
normal form of the shape:

\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:A_{1}\ldots\lambda x_{k}:A_{k}.M^{\prime\prime},

where
M^{\prime\prime}
has type
t
, it is in
\beta
normal form,
and may include free variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.
We note that the types of the variables
x_{i}
do
not contain second order quantifications.
We claim that
M^{\prime\prime}
cannot contain a
\lambda
-abstraction:

•

A
\lambda
-abstraction on the left of an application
would contradict the hypothesis that
M
is in
\beta
normal form.

•

A
\lambda
-abstraction on the right of an application
is incompatible with the ‘first order’ types of
the variables
A_{i}
.

We have shown that a closed
\lambda
-term of type
\underline{\Sigma}
is
determined up to
\beta\eta
conversion by a
\lambda
-term

M^{\prime\prime}
which is a well-typed combination of the variables

x_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,k
.
Since each variable corresponds to
a constructor of the signature we can conclude that there is
a unique ground term over the signature which corresponds to
M^{\prime\prime}
.
{\Box}

Remark 13.1

The rule
(\eta)
is needed to have a bijection
between ground terms of the signature
\Sigma
and closed

\lambda
-terms of type
\underline{\Sigma}
. For instance,
with reference to example
13.1
(tally natural numbers),
there are two distinct
\lambda
-terms in
\beta
-normal form
corresponding to the numeral
1
, namely
\underline{1}
and

\lambda t.\lambda x_{1}:t\rightarrow t.x_{1}
.

Definition 13.2

A function
f:T^{n}\rightarrow T
over a signature
\Sigma
is representable
(with respect to the proposed coding)
if there is a closed
\lambda
-term
M:\underline{\Sigma}^{n}\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma}
, such that
for any vector of ground terms
a^{*}
:

M\underline{a^{*}}=_{\beta\eta}\underline{f(a^{*})}~.

Proposition 13.3

All iterative functions over a signature
\Sigma
are
representable.

Proof
. We proceed by induction on the definition of
iterative function. The interesting case
is iteration. Let
h_{i}:T^{n_{i}+m}\rightarrow T
be iterative
functions for
i=1,\ldots,k
, and the function
f:T^{m+1}\rightarrow T
be
defined by:

f(x^{*},c_{i}(y^{*}))=h_{i}(x^{*},f(x^{*},y_{1}),\ldots,f(x^{*},y_{n_{i}}))~~i=1,\ldots,k\;,

(13.2)

where
x^{*}\equiv x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
.
1
1

1

In this proof, it is convenient to write the additional parameters
x^{*}
before the main argument of the iteration.

We represent
f
with the function:

\underline{f}\equiv\lambda x_{1}:\underline{\Sigma}.\ldots\lambda x_{m}:\underline{\Sigma}.\lambda x:\underline{\Sigma}.x\underline{\Sigma}(\underline{h}_{1}x^{*})\cdots(\underline{h}_{k}x^{*})\;,

where we know inductively that
\underline{h}_{i}
represents
h_{i}
.
Note that iteration is already built into the representation
of the data. We prove by induction on the structure of a ground term
a
that for any
vector of ground terms
b^{*}
,
\underline{f}\;\underline{b^{*}}\;\underline{a}=_{\beta\eta}\underline{f(b^{*},a)}
.

\bullet\;
If
a\equiv c_{i}^{0}
then

\underline{f}\;\underline{b^{*}}\;\underline{c_{i}^{0}}\rightarrow^{*}\underline{c_{i}^{0}}\underline{\Sigma}(\underline{h}_{1}\underline{b^{*}})\cdots(\underline{h_{k}}\underline{b^{*}})\rightarrow^{*}\underline{h_{i}}\underline{b^{*}}=_{\beta\eta}\underline{h_{i}(b^{*})}~,

where the last step holds by induction hypothesis on
h_{i}
.

\bullet\;
If
a\equiv c_{i}^{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
then:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\underline{f(b^{*},c_{i}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))}&=\underline{h_{i}(b^{*},f(b^{*},a_{1}),\ldots,f(b^{*},a_{n}))}\\
&=_{\beta\eta}\underline{h_{i}}\;\underline{b^{*}}\;\underline{f(b^{*},a_{1})}\ldots\underline{f(b^{*},a_{n})}&\mbox{(by induction hypothesis on $h_{i}$).}\end{array}

On the other hand, we compute:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\underline{f}\;\underline{b^{*}}\;\underline{c_{i}^{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})}\\
\rightarrow\underline{c_{i}^{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})}\underline{\Sigma}(\underline{h_{1}}\;\underline{b^{*}})\cdots(\underline{h_{k}}\;\underline{b^{*}})\\
\rightarrow(\underline{h_{i}}\;\underline{b^{*}})(\underline{a_{1}}\underline{\Sigma}(\underline{h_{1}}\;\underline{b^{*}})\cdots(\underline{h_{k}}\;\underline{b^{*}}))\cdots(\underline{a_{n}}\underline{\Sigma}(\underline{h_{1}}\;\underline{b^{*}})\cdots(\underline{h_{k}}\;\underline{b^{*}}))~.\end{array}

Also, by induction hypothesis on
a
, we have for
i=1,\ldots,n
:

\underline{f(b^{*},a_{i})}=_{\beta\eta}\underline{f}\underline{b^{*}}\underline{a_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}\underline{a_{i}}\underline{\Sigma}(\underline{h_{1}}\underline{b^{*}})\cdots(\underline{h_{k}}\underline{b^{*}}))~.

Hence, by combining the computations above, we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\underline{f}\;\underline{b^{*}}\;\underline{c_{i}^{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})}&=_{\beta\eta}\underline{h_{i}}\;\underline{b^{*}}\;\underline{f(b^{*},a_{1})}\ldots\underline{f(b^{*},a_{n})}\\
&=_{\beta\eta}\underline{f(b^{*},c_{i}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))}~.\end{array}

{\Box}

Example 13.2

Suppose
T
is the set of tally natural numbers and
g:T\rightarrow T
and
h:T^{2}\rightarrow T
. The iteration
it(h,g)
of
h
and
g
must satisfy:

\begin{array}[]{cc}{\it it}(h,g)({\sf Z},y)=g(y)~,\par&{\it it}(h,g)({\sf S}(x),y)=h({\it it}(h,g)(x,y),y)~.\par\end{array}

In the pure
\lambda
-calculus, we would define:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\it it}&\equiv&\lambda h.\lambda g.\lambda x.\lambda y.\ x\ (\lambda z.h\ z\ y)\ (g\ y)~.\end{array}

For
\underline{\Sigma}\equiv\forall t.(t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow(t\rightarrow t)
, the
term it can be type decorated as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}\lambda h:\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow(\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma}).\lambda g:\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma}.\lambda x:\underline{\Sigma}.\lambda y:\underline{\Sigma}.x\ \underline{\Sigma}\ (\lambda z:\underline{\Sigma}.h\ z\ y)\ (g\ y)~,\end{array}

which has type

(\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow(\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma}))\rightarrow(\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma})\rightarrow(\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow(\underline{\Sigma}\rightarrow\underline{\Sigma}))
.
Notice that this would not work with a propositional type of the shape

(B\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(B\rightarrow B)
!

Example 13.3

One can also handle the case of signatures which are
defined parametrically with respect to a collection
of data. For instance
List(D)
is the signature
of lists whose elements belong to the
set
D
. This signature is equipped with
the constructors:

{\sf nil}:{\it List}(D),\qquad{\sf cons}:D\times{\it List}(D)\rightarrow{\it List}(D)~.

One can define iterative functions over
List(D)
and
show that these functions can be represented
in system F for a suitable embedding
of the closed
\lambda
-terms in system F.
The sort
List(D)
is coded by the type:

\forall t.t\rightarrow(r\rightarrow t\rightarrow t)\rightarrow t~,

where
r
is a type variable, and generic elements
in
D
are represented by (free) variables of type
r
.

13.3
Strong normalization (*)

We now move towards a proof of the announced strong normalization
result. The proof is based on a notion of
reducibility
candidate
which is an
abstraction
of the notion already
considered for the strong normalization of the propositionally typed

\lambda
-calculus (chapter
10
) and recursive path
ordering (chapter
4
).
In order to make
notation lighter
we shall work with untyped

\lambda
-terms obtained from the
erasure
of well-typed

\lambda
-terms.

Definition 13.3
(erasure)

The (type) erasure function
er
takes a typed
\lambda
-term and
returns an untyped
\lambda
-term.
It is defined by induction on the structure of the
\lambda
-term as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\it er}(x)=x,\qquad{\it er}(\lambda x:A.M)=\lambda x.{\it er}(M),\qquad{\it er}(MN)={\it er}(M){\it er}(N),\\
{\it er}(\lambda t.M)={\it er}(M),\qquad{\it er}(MA)={\it er}(M)~.\par\end{array}

In system F, we distinguish
two flavors of
\beta
-reduction
:
the one involving a redex
(\lambda x:A.M)N
which we call
simply
(\beta)
and the one involving a redex
(\lambda t.M)A
which
we call
(\beta_{t})
. Erasing type information we eliminate
the reductions
(\beta_{t})
.
However this does
not
affect the strong
normalization property as shown in the following.

Proposition 13.4
(erasure vs. typed)

Let
M
be a well-typed
\lambda
-term in system F. Then:

1.

If
M\rightarrow_{\beta}N
then
{\it er}(M)\rightarrow_{\beta}{\it er}(N)
.

2.

If
M\rightarrow_{\beta_{t}}N
then
{\it er}(M)\equiv{\it er}(N)
.

3.

If
M
may diverge then
{\it er}(M)
may diverge.

Proof
. Properties (1) and (2) are left to the reader.
For (3), we observe that sequences of
\beta_{t}
-reductions always terminate as the size of the
\lambda
-term
shrinks. Hence we can extract an infinite reduction of
{\it er}(M)
from an infinite reduction of
M
.
{\Box}

We can now address the
key
issue. Suppose we
want to adapt the
semantic
method already used in the propositional case.
What is the interpretation of
A\equiv\forall t.(t\rightarrow t)
?
We have to build first a
universe

{\cal U}
of type interpretations
where each type interpretation is a set of
\lambda
-terms.
Then we could require:

[\![A]\!]=\{M\mid\forall\,X\in{\cal U}\;\>\forall\,N\in X\;\>(MN\in X)\}~.

Technically, the type interpretations are the so-called
reducibility candidates and are defined as follows.
Let
{\it SN}
be the collection of untyped
\lambda
-terms
which are strongly normalizable with respect to the
(\beta)
rule.
We shall use
P,Q,\ldots
to denote the untyped
\lambda
-terms (as opposed
to the typed ones which are denoted with
M,N,\ldots
).

Definition 13.4
(candidates)

A set
X
of
\lambda
-terms is a reducibility candidate if:

1.

X\subseteq{\it SN}
.

2.

Q_{i}\in{\it SN}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
,
n\geq 0
implies

xQ_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}\in X
.

3.

[Q/x]PQ_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}\in X
and
Q\in{\it SN}
implies

(\lambda x.P)QQ_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}\in X
.

We denote with
{\it RC}
the collection of reducibility candidates.

Remark 13.2

We have made into a definition the properties stated in
proposition
10.6
of the interpretation of propositional types.

Proposition 13.5
(properties reducibility candidates)

The following properties hold.

1.

The set
{\it SN}
is a reducibility candidate.

2.

If
X\in{\it RC}
then
X\neq\emptyset
.

3.

The collection
{\it RC}
is closed under arbitrary intersections.

4.

If
X,Y\in{\it RC}
then the following
set is a reducibility candidate:

X\rightarrow Y=\{M\mid\forall\,N\in X\;\>(MN\in Y)\}~.

Proof
. We abbreviate
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}
with
Q^{*}
.
We recall (definition
10.8
) that if
P\in{\it SN}
then

{\it depth}(P)
is the length of its longest reduction.

(1)\;

As in the propositional case, we observe that
[Q/x]PQ^{*}\in{\it SN}
and
Q\in{\it SN}
implies
(\lambda x.P)QQ^{*}\in{\it SN}
.
This is an induction on
{\it depth}(P)+{\it depth}(Q)+{\it depth}(Q_{1})+\cdots+{\it depth}(Q_{n})
.

(2)\;
By definition,
x\in X
.

(3)\;
Immediate.

(4)\;
Here we see the use of the ‘saturation’
condition (3) in definition
13.4
.
{\Box}

Next we define a type interpretation.

Definition 13.5
(type interpretation)

Let
{\it Tvar}
be the set of type variables.
Given a type environment
\eta:{\it Tvar}\rightarrow{\it RC}
we interpret types
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}[\![t]\!]\eta&=&\eta(t)\\
[\![A\rightarrow B]\!]\eta&=&[\![A]\!]\eta\rightarrow[\![B]\!]\eta\\
[\![\forall t.A]\!]\eta&=&\bigcap_{X\in{\it RC}}[\![A]\!]\eta[X/t]~.\end{array}

We remark that the interpretations of a functional type and a
universal type are well-defined
because of propositions
13.5
(4) and
13.5
(3),
respectively.
Strong normalization follows from the

soundness of the interpretation
which is stated as follows.

Proposition 13.6
(soundness)

Let
\eta
be a type environment and

x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\vdash M:B

a derivable judgment.
If
P_{i}\in[\![A_{i}]\!]\eta
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
then

[P_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,P_{n}/x_{n}]{\it er}(M)\in[\![B]\!]\eta~.

Proof
. We abbreviate
[P_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,P_{n}/x_{n}]

with
[P^{*}/x^{*}]
.
We proceed by induction on the typing proof.

(asmp)

follows by definition.

(\rightarrow_{I})

We have to show:

\lambda x.[P^{*}/x^{*}]{\it er}(M)\in[\![A\rightarrow B]\!]\eta~.

By inductive hypothesis, we know:

[P^{*}/x^{*}][P/x]{\it er}(M)\in[\![B]\!]\eta
,
for all
P\in[\![A]\!]\eta
.
We conclude by using the properties of reducibility candidates.

(\rightarrow_{E})

By the definition of
\rightarrow
.

(\forall_{I})

We have to show:

[P^{*}/x^{*}]{\it er}(M)\in\bigcap_{X\in{\it RC}}[\![B]\!]\eta[X/t]~.

By the side condition on the typing rule, we know:

[\![A_{i}]\!]\eta=[\![A_{i}]\!]\eta[X/t]
,
for an arbitrary
X\in{\it RC}
.
By inductive hypothesis:

[P^{*}/x^{*}]{\it er}(M)\in[\![B]\!]\eta[X/t]
,
for an arbitrary
X\in{\it RC}
.

(\forall_{E})

We have to show:

[P^{*}/x^{*}]{\it er}(M)\in[\![B]\!]\eta[[\![A]\!]\eta/t]~.

By inductive hypothesis:

[P^{*}/x^{*}]{\it er}(M)\in\bigcap_{X\in{\it RC}}[\![B]\!]\eta[X/t]
.
Choose
X=[\![A]\!]\eta
.
{\Box}

Corollary 13.1
(strong normalization)

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A
in system F, then
M
is strongly
normalizing.

Proof
. We note that
\forall\,A,\eta,x\;\>(x\in[\![A]\!]\eta)
.
Then we apply proposition
13.6
with
P_{i}\equiv x_{i}
,
and derive that:

{\it er}(M)\in[\![A]\!]\eta\subseteq{\it SN}
.
By proposition
13.4
,
we conclude that
M
is strongly normalizing.
{\Box}

Exercise 13.5
(neutral
\lambda
-term)

Alternative definitions of reducibility candidates can
be found in the literature; one follows.
Say that a
\lambda
-term is neutral if
it does not start with a
\lambda
-abstraction.
Define
{\it Red}(M)=\{M^{\prime}\mid M\rightarrow_{\beta}M^{\prime}\}
.
The collection
{\it RC^{\prime}}
is given by the sets
X
of
strongly normalizing
\lambda
-terms satisfying the following conditions:

1.

M\in X
and
M\rightarrow_{\beta}M^{\prime}
implies
M^{\prime}\in X
.

2.

M
neutral and
{\it Red}(M)\subseteq X
implies
M\in X
.

Carry on the strong normalization proof using the collection
{\it RC^{\prime}}
.

13.4
Summary and references

The introduction of second-order quantification

preserves the standard properties
of the propositionally typed calculus:
subject reduction, strong normalization, confluence
\ldots

while
increasing the expressivity
in a very significant way as
one can encode inductive data types and iterative functions.
There is one catch however:
type inference becomes undecidable

which is one reason why
{\it ML}
-like programming languages adopt a
weaker/predicative form of polymorphism.
When extended with first-order quantification, system F is
the backbone of a higher-order constructive logic (the so called

calculus of constructions
on which the
Coq

proof assistant is built
[
CH88
]
).

The system F has been introduced by Girard in
[
Gir71
]

as a tool for the study
of the
cut-elimination procedure
in
second order
Peano arithmetic
(
{\it PA}_{2}
). More precisely the
normalization
of system F
implies the
termination of the cut-elimination
procedure
in
{\it PA}_{2}
(and thus the consistency of analysis!).
By relying on this strong connection between system F and
{\it PA}_{2}

it is proven that all functions that can be shown to be total in

{\it PA}_{2}
are
representable
in system F. This is a
huge
collection of total recursive functions that goes well beyond
the primitive recursive functions. The connections with the notion
of type polymorphism (or type parametricity) arising in programming
are noticed in
[
Rey74
]
and
the relationship between existential types and abstract data types
are pointed out in
[
MP88
]
.
The results on the representation of iterative
functions are based on
[
BB85
]
.
The type inference problem for a Curry-style system F (cf. chapter

12
) turns out to be undecidable

[
Wel99
]
.

Chapter 14
Program transformations

In this chapter, we introduce four program transformations.
Each transformation has its own interest. Moreover, when they are
put in pipeline they provide a compilation chain from a call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus to a register transfer level (RTL) language.
A RTL language can be regarded as a machine independent version
of assembly code. Functions correspond to assembly level routines and
the functions’ bodies correspond to sequences of vectors’ allocations and
vectors’ projections ended by a tail recursive call.
The compilation chain is summarized in the following diagram:

\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}\lambda&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\cal C}_{\it cps}}}{{\rightarrow}}&\lambda_{{\it cps}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\cal C}_{\it vn}}}{{\rightarrow}}&\lambda_{{\it cps},{\it vn}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\cal C}_{\it cc}}}{{\rightarrow}}&\lambda_{{\it cc},{\it vn}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\cal C}_{\it h}}}{{\rightarrow}}&\lambda_{h,{\it vn}}\end{array}

(14.1)

The source language is a call-by-value,

\lambda
-calculus (cf. chapter
8
).
The first transformation, called
continuation-passing style
(CPS),
internalizes the notion of evaluation context, the second,
called
value naming
, assigns a name to every value,
the third, called
closure conversion
, internalizes the notion of closure
and makes sure functions are closed,
i.e.
, they do not contain free variables, and the last, called
hoisting
, transforms a collection of closed
nested function definitions into a collection of possibly open, flat,

i.e.
, without nesting, function definitions.

Since we want to compose these transformations, we make sure the
target language of each transformation coincides with the source of
the following one. As a matter of fact, all the languages are subsets
of the initial source language though their evaluation mechanism is
refined along the way. In particular, one moves from an ordinary
substitution to a specialized one where variables can only be replaced
by other variables.

The approach to compiler correctness is similar
to the one considered for the toy compiler
of section
1.3
.
One proves that each transformation is correct in the sense that
the object code simulates the source code.
Then, by composition, one derives the correctness of the compilation chain.

14.1
Continuation passing style form

The origin of the CPS transformation goes back to
so called
double-negation
transformations from classical to
intuitionistic/constructive logic. In constructive logic,
the formula
\neg\neg t\rightarrow t
is not derivable but
the formula
\neg\neg\neg t\rightarrow\neg t
is
(cf. exercise
10.2
).
Then the idea
is to transform formulae in classical logic to
negated
formulae
in constructive logic so that negation is involutive on the image
of the transformation. For some fixed type variable
s
, let

\neg A=(A\rightarrow s)
, and define
a transformation of propositional types and type contexts as follows:

\begin{array}[]{llll}\underline{t}=t~,&\underline{A\rightarrow B}=\underline{A}\rightarrow\neg\neg\underline{B}~,&\underline{\emptyset}=\emptyset~,&\underline{\Gamma,x:A}=\underline{\Gamma},x:\underline{A}~.\end{array}

What the transformation shows is that for
for every formula
A
provable in classical logic, there
is a classicaly equivalent formula
\neg\neg\underline{A}
which is provable
in constructive logic.
Now suppose we start with a
\lambda
-term of type
A
,
say
\Gamma\vdash M:A
,
i.e.
, with
a constructive proof of
A
.
Can we build a
\lambda
-term
\underline{M}

such that
\underline{\Gamma}\vdash\underline{M}:\neg\neg\underline{A}
?
For variables and
\lambda
-abstractions, the typing suggests directly:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\underline{x}=\lambda k.kx~,&\underline{\lambda x.M}=\lambda k.k(\lambda x.\underline{M})~.\end{array}

The case for application is a bit more complex, but the reader may
easily check that the following does the job:

\begin{array}[]{c}\underline{MN}=\lambda k.\underline{M}(\lambda m.\underline{N}(\lambda n.mnk))~.\end{array}

Moreover, the transformed
\lambda
-term simulates the
original one as soon it is provided with
an additional argument which represents the initial evaluation
context. For instance, the reader may check that:

\underline{((\lambda x.x)y)}(\lambda z.z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\beta}y~,

(14.2)

where
\lambda z.z
stands for the initial evaluation context.
As a matter of fact, types are useless in proving the simulation
property and they will be omitted in the following formal treatment.
However, as we have seen, types shed light on
the CPS transformation and we shall come back to them in
chapter
15
.
The reader may have noticed that the reduction (
14.2
) above
performs many ‘useless’
\beta
-reductions.
For this reason, as well as for simplifying the proof strategy,
we shall study an
optimized version
of the CPS transformation.

Table
14.1
introduces the source language:
a type-free, left-to-right, call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus.
Notice that for technical reasons we include the variables among the values.
Also notice that the calculus is richer than the one studied
in chapter
8
in that it includes
let-definitions
,

polyadic abstraction
, and
tupling
, with the related
application and projection operators. Polyadic abstraction
grants a function the right to take several arguments at once while
tupling allows to build vectors of terms.
For the sake of readibility, we shall denote
explicitly

the polyadic application with the symbol
@
.
We stress that polyadic abstraction can be simulated
by iterated
\lambda
-abstraction
and tupling can be simulated by iterated pairing.
Still, it is worth to take them as primitive in order to
simplify the analysis of the following program transformations.

Working with polyadic abstraction and tuples, we need a compact
notation to represent sequences of symbols.
We shall write
X^{+}
(resp.
X^{*}
) for a non-empty (possibly empty)
finite sequence
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
of symbols.
By extension,
\lambda x^{+}.M
stands for
\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.M
,

[V^{+}/x^{+}]M
stands for
[V_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,V_{n}/x_{n}]M
, and

{\sf let}\ (x=V)^{+}\ {\sf in}\ M
stands for

{\sf let}\ x_{1}=V_{1}\ {\sf in}\ \cdots{\sf let}\ x_{n}=V_{n}\ {\sf in}\ M
.
By default, a
term
is a
\lambda
-term in the enriched
\lambda
-calculus
under consideration.

Syntax

\begin{array}[]{lll}V&::={\it id}\mid\!\!\mid\lambda{\it id}^{+}.M\mid\!\!\mid(V^{*})&\mbox{(values)}\\
M&::=V\mid\!\!\mid@(M,M^{+})\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=M\ {\sf in}\ M\mid\!\!\mid(M^{*})\mid\!\!\mid\pi_{i}(M)&\mbox{(terms)}\\
E&::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid@(V^{*},E,M^{*})\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=E\ {\sf in}\ M\mid\!\!\mid(V^{*},E,M^{*})\mid\!\!\mid\pi_{i}(E)&\mbox{(evaluation contexts)}\par\par\end{array}

Reduction Rules

\begin{array}[]{lll}E[@(\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.M,V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})]&\rightarrow&E[[V_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,V_{n}/x_{n}]M]\\
E[{\sf let}\ x=V\ {\sf in}\ M]&\rightarrow&E[[V/x]M]\\
E[\pi_{i}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})]&\rightarrow&E[V_{i}]\hskip 17.00024pt(1\leq i\leq n)\par\end{array}

Table 14.1:
A polyadic, call-by-value,
\lambda
-calculus:
\lambda

Table
14.2
introduces a fragment of the

\lambda
-calculus described in Table
14.1
and a related
CPS transformation.
An evaluation context
E
can be represented as a term

\lambda x.E[x]
; in a CPS transformation each
function takes its evaluation context, represented as a term,
as a fresh additional parameter.
The
initial
evaluation context is defined relatively to a
fresh variable named ’
{\it halt}
’.

The reduction rules we apply to CPS terms
are those of the
\lambda
-calculus (Table
14.1
).
The syntax of CPS terms is such that in an application
