or in a tuple all terms are values and this property
is preserved by reduction.
A corollary of this syntactic restriction is that
an evaluation context is either trivial or of
the shape
{\sf let}\ x=[]\ {\sf in}\ M
.

Notice that strictly speaking the CPS terms are not closed
under reduction because,
e.g.
,

{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{1}(V_{1},V_{2})\ {\sf in}\ M
reduces to

{\sf let}\ x=V_{1}\ {\sf in}\ M
which is not a CPS term.
However, the latter reduces to
[V_{1}/x]M
which is again
a CPS term.

There is a potential ambiguity concerning the CPS transformation of
tuples of values. We remove it, by assuming that

(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\mid K
is transformed according to the case for
values. But note that if we follow the general case for tuples we
obtain the same result.

Next, we state the properties enjoyed by the presented
CPS transformation, which is ‘optimized’ so as to
pre-compute many ‘administrative’ reductions.
In particular, thanks to this optimization,
we can show that the CPS transformation
of a term such as
E[@(\lambda x.M,V)]
is a term
of the shape
@(\psi(\lambda x.M),\psi(V),K_{E})
for a suitable
continuation
K_{E}
depending on the evaluation context
E
.

Proposition 14.1
(CPS simulation)

Let
M
be a term of the
\lambda
-calculus.
If
M\rightarrow N
then
{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(N)
.

Proof
. The proof takes the following steps.

1.

We show by induction on
M
that for all values
V
, terms
M
, and continuations
K\neq x
:

[V/x]M\mid[\psi(V)/x]K\equiv[\psi(V)/x](M\mid K)~.

(14.3)

2.

The evaluation contexts for the
\lambda
-calculus
described in Table
14.1

can also be specified ‘bottom up’ as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}E&::=&[~]\mid\!\!\mid E[@(V^{*},[~],M^{*})]\mid\!\!\mid E[{\sf let}\ {\it id}=[~]\ {\sf in}\ M]\mid\!\!\mid E[(V^{*},[~],M^{*})]\mid\!\!\mid E[\pi_{i}([~])]~.\end{array}

Following this specification, we associate with an evaluation context
E

a continuation
K_{E}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}K_{[~]}&=&\lambda x.@({\it halt},x)\\
K_{E[@(V^{*},[~],M^{*})]}&=&\lambda x.M^{*}\mid\lambda y^{*}.@(\psi(V)^{*},x,y^{*},K_{E})\\
K_{E[{\sf let}\ x=[~]\ {\sf in}\ N]}&=&\lambda x.N\mid K_{E}\\
K_{E[(V^{*},[~],M^{*})]}&=&(\lambda x.M^{*}\mid\lambda y^{*}.(\psi(V)^{*},x,y^{*}))\mid K_{E}\\
K_{E[\pi_{i}([~])}&=&\lambda x.{\sf let}\ y=\pi_{i}(x)\ {\sf in}\ (y\mid K_{E})~,\par\end{array}

where
M^{*}\mid\lambda x^{*}.N
stands for
M_{0}\mid\lambda x_{0}\ldots M_{n}\mid\lambda x_{n}.N
with
n\geq 0
.

3.

For all terms
M
and evaluation contexts
E,E^{\prime}
we prove by induction
on the evaluation context
E
that the following holds:

E[M]\mid K_{E^{\prime}}\equiv M\mid K_{E^{\prime}[E]}~.

(14.4)

4.

For all terms
M
, continuations
K,K^{\prime}
, and variable
x\notin{\sf fv}(M)

we prove by induction on
M
and case analysis that the following holds:

\begin{array}[]{c}[K/x](M\mid K^{\prime})\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\rightarrow M\mid K&\mbox{if }K\mbox{ abstraction},M\mbox{ value},K^{\prime}=x\\
\equiv(M\mid[K/x]K^{\prime})&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

(14.5)

5.

Finally, we prove the assertion by case analysis
on the reduction rule. We consider the case for application.
Suppose
E[@(\lambda x^{+}.M,V^{+})]\rightarrow E[[V^{+}/x^{+}]M]
.
We have:

\begin{array}[]{lll}E[@(\lambda x^{+}.M,V^{+})]\mid K_{[~]}&\equiv@(\lambda x^{+}.M,V^{+})\mid K_{E}&\mbox{(by (\ref{cps-step2}))}\\
&\equiv@(\lambda x^{+},k.M\mid k,\psi(V)^{+},K_{E})&\mbox{(by definition)}\\
&\rightarrow[K_{E}/k,\psi(V)^{+}/x^{+}](M\mid k)&\\
&\equiv[K_{E}/k]([V^{+}/x^{+}]M\mid k)&\mbox{(by (\ref{cps-step1}))}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}[V^{+}/x^{+}]M\mid K_{E}&\mbox{(by (\ref{cps-step3}))}\\
&\equiv E[[V^{+}/x^{+}]M]\mid K_{[~]}&\mbox{(by (\ref{cps-step2})).}\end{array}

{\Box}

We illustrate this result on the following example.

Example 14.1
(CPS)

Let
M\equiv@(\lambda x.@(x,@(x,x)),I)
, where
I\equiv\lambda x.x
. Then

{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(M)\equiv@(\lambda x,k.@(x,x,\lambda y.@(x,y,k)),I^{\prime},H)~,

where:
I^{\prime}\equiv\lambda x,k.@(k,x)
and
H\equiv\lambda x.@({\it halt},x)
.
The term
M
is simulated by
{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(M)
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}M&\rightarrow&@(I,@(I,I))&\rightarrow&@(I,I)&\rightarrow&I\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(M)&\rightarrow&@(I^{\prime},I^{\prime},\lambda y.@(I^{\prime},y,H))&\rightarrow^{+}&@(I^{\prime},I^{\prime},H)&\rightarrow^{+}&@({\it halt},I^{\prime})~.\par\end{array}

Syntax CPS terms

\begin{array}[]{lll}V&::={\it id}\mid\!\!\mid\lambda{\it id}^{+}.M\mid\!\!\mid(V^{*})&\mbox{(values)}\\
M&::=@(V,V^{+})\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=\pi_{i}(V)\ {\sf in}\ M&\mbox{(CPS terms)}\\
K&::={\it id}\mid\!\!\mid\lambda{\it id}.M&\mbox{(continuations)}\\
\end{array}

CPS transformation

\begin{array}[]{lll}\psi(x)&=&x\\
\psi(\lambda x^{+}.M)&=&\lambda x^{+},k.(M\mid k)\\
\psi((V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}))&=&(\psi(V_{1}),\ldots,\psi(V_{n}))\\
\\
V\mid k&=&@(k,\psi(V))\\
V\mid(\lambda x.M)&=&[\psi(V)/x]M\\
@(M_{0},\ldots,M_{n})\mid K&=&M_{0}\mid\lambda x_{0}.\ldots(M_{n}\mid\lambda x_{n}.@(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n},K))\\
{\sf let}\ x=M_{1}\ {\sf in}\ M_{2}\mid K&=&M_{1}\mid\lambda x.(M_{2}\mid K)\\
(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n})\mid K&=&M_{1}\mid\lambda x_{1}.\ldots(M_{n}\mid\lambda x_{n}.((x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mid K)\ )\\
\pi_{i}(M)\mid K&=&M\mid\lambda x.{\sf let}\ y=\pi_{i}(x)\ {\sf in}\ (y\mid K)\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(M)&=&M\mid\lambda x.@({\it halt},x),\hskip 17.00024pt{\it halt}\mbox{ fresh variable}\par\end{array}

Table 14.2:
CPS
\lambda
-calculus (
\lambda_{{\it cps}}
) and CPS transformation

Exercise 14.1

Prove the assertions (
14.3
), (
14.4
),
and (
14.5
).

Exercise 14.2

Write down a simplified CPS transformation for a monadic call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus without let-definitions and tuples.
Then apply the CPS transformation to show that it is possible to simulate
the call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus in the call-by-name
\lambda
-calculus
(cf. chapter
8
).

Exercise 14.3

So called control operators are programming instructions
that alter the execution flow.
For instance, consider the
continue
and
break

commands of exercise
1.2
and the control
{\cal C}
and
abort
{\cal A}
operators of exercise
8.3
.
CPS transformations allow to simulate such operators in a purely functional
setting.

1.

In chapter
1
, we have interpreted a statement
of the
{\sf Imp}
language as a function of type
({\it State}\rightarrow{\it State})
.
Define an alternative functional interpretation
where a command is regarded as a function of type:

{\it State}\rightarrow({\it State}\rightarrow{\it State})\rightarrow{\it State}~,

and show that such interpretation can be extended to interpret
a command
abort
which stops the computation and returns the current
state.

2.

Define a CPS transformation of the call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus
extended with the control operators
{\cal C}
and
{\cal A}
defined in exercise

8.3
.

14.2
Value named form (*)

Table
14.3
introduces a
value named

\lambda
-calculus
in CPS form:
\lambda_{{\it cps},{\it vn}}
.
In the ordinary
\lambda
-calculus, the application of a
\lambda
-abstraction
to an argument (which is a value) may duplicate the argument as in:

@(\lambda x.M,V)\rightarrow[V/x]M
.
In the value named
\lambda
-calculus, all values are named
and when we apply the name of a
\lambda
-abstraction to the name of a value
we create a new copy of the body of the function and replace its formal parameter name
with the name of the argument as in:

{\sf let}\ f=\lambda x.M\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ y=M\ {\sf in}\ @(f,y)\ \rightarrow\ {\sf let}\ f=\lambda x.M\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ y=V\ {\sf in}\ [y/x]M~.

Notice that the definition of
E(x)
in Table
14.3
makes sure
y

is not among the variables free in
\lambda x.M
.
Otherwise, one has to rename
y
in order to apply the rule.
We also remark that in the value named
\lambda
-calculus the
evaluation contexts are a sequence of let definitions associating
values to names. Thus, apart for the fact that the values are not
necessarily closed, the evaluation contexts are similar to the
environments of abstract machines for functional languages
(cf. chapter
8
).

Syntax

\begin{array}[]{lll}V&::=\lambda{\it id}^{+}.M\mid\!\!\mid({\it id}^{*})&\mbox{(values)}\\
C&::=V\mid\!\!\mid\pi_{i}({\it id})&\mbox{(let-bindable terms)}\\
M&::=@({\it id},{\it id}^{+})\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=C\ {\sf in}\ M&\mbox{(CPS terms)}\\
E&::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=V\ {\sf in}\ E&\mbox{(evaluation contexts)}\\
\end{array}

Reduction Rules

\begin{array}[]{cccl}E[@(x,z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})]&\rightarrow&E[[z_{1}/y_{1},\ldots,z_{n}/y_{n}]M]&\mbox{if }E(x)=\lambda y_{1}\ldots y_{n}.M\\
E[{\sf let}\ z=\pi_{i}(x)\ {\sf in}\ M]&\rightarrow&E[[y_{i}/z]M]]&\mbox{if }E(x)=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),1\leq i\leq n\par\end{array}

\mbox{where: }E(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}V&\mbox{if }E=E^{\prime}[{\sf let}\ x=V\ {\sf in}\ [~]]\\
E^{\prime}(x)&\mbox{if }E=E^{\prime}[{\sf let}\ y=V\ {\sf in}\ [~]],x\neq y,y\notin{\sf fv}(E^{\prime}(x))\\
\mbox{undefined}&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Table 14.3:
A value named CPS
\lambda
-calculus:
\lambda_{{\it cps,vn}}

Table
14.4
defines the compilation into value named
form along with a
readback
transformation.
The latter is useful to state the
simulation property. Indeed, it is not true that if
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
in

\lambda_{cps}
then
{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(M^{\prime})
in

\lambda_{{\it cps},{\it vn}}
. For instance, consider
M\equiv(\lambda x.xx)I
where
I\equiv(\lambda y.y)
. Then
M\rightarrow II
but
{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(M)

does not reduce to
{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(II)
but rather to a term where the ‘sharing’
of the duplicated value
I
is explicitly represented.

Example 14.2
(value named form)

Consider the term resulting from the CPS transformation in example

14.1
:

N\equiv@(\lambda x,k.@(x,x,\lambda y.@(x,y,k)),I^{\prime},H))~,

where:
I^{\prime}\equiv\lambda x,k.@(k,x)
and
H\equiv\lambda x.@({\it halt},x)
.
The corresponding term in value named form is:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\sf let}\ z_{1}=\lambda x,k.({\sf let}\ z_{11}=\lambda y.@(x,y,k)\ {\sf in}\ @(x,x,z_{11}))\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ z_{2}=I^{\prime}\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ z_{3}=H\ {\sf in}\\
@(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})~.\end{array}

Transformation in value named form (from
\lambda_{cps}
to
\lambda_{cps,vn}
)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(@(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}))&=&@(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})\\
{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(@(x^{*},V,V^{*}))&=&{\cal E}_{{\it vn}}(V,y)[{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(@(x^{*},y,V^{*}))]\hskip 8.50012ptV\neq{\it id},y\mbox{ fresh}\\
{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}({\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ M)&=&{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(M)\\
{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}({\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(V)\ {\sf in}\ M)&=&{\cal E}_{{\it vn}}(V,y)[{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(M)]\hskip 8.50012ptV\neq{\it id},y\mbox{ fresh}\\
{\cal E}_{{\it vn}}(\lambda x^{+}.M,y)&=&{\sf let}\ y=\lambda x^{+}.{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(M)\ {\sf in}\ [~]\\
{\cal E}_{{\it vn}}((x^{*}),y)&=&{\sf let}\ y=(x^{*})\ {\sf in}\ [~]\\
{\cal E}_{{\it vn}}((x^{*},V,V^{*}),y)&=&{\cal E}_{{\it vn}}(V,z)[{\cal E}_{{\it vn}}((x^{*},z,V^{*}),y)]\hskip 8.50012ptV\neq{\it id},z\mbox{ fresh}\par\end{array}

Readback transformation (from
\lambda_{{\it cps},{\it vn}}
to
\lambda_{{\it cps}}
)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\cal R}(\lambda x^{+}.M)&=&\lambda x^{+}.{\cal R}(M)\\
{\cal R}(x^{*})&=&(x^{*})\\
{\cal R}(@(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}))&=&@(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\\
{\cal R}({\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ M)&=&{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ {\cal R}(M)\\
{\cal R}({\sf let}\ x=V\ {\sf in}\ M)&=&[{\cal R}(V)/x]{\cal R}(M)\par\end{array}

Table 14.4:
Transformations in value named CPS form and readback

Proposition 14.2
(vn simulation)

Let
N
be a term in CPS value named form. If

{\cal R}(N)\equiv M
and
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
then there exists
N^{\prime}
such
that
N\rightarrow N^{\prime}
and
{\cal R}(N^{\prime})\equiv M^{\prime}
.

Proof
. First we fix some notation.
We associate a substitution
\sigma_{E}
with an evaluation context
E
of the
\lambda_{cps,{\it vn}}
-calculus
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma_{[~]}={\it Id}~,&\sigma_{{\sf let}\ x=V\ {\sf in}\ E}=[{\cal R}(V)/x]\circ\sigma_{E}~.\end{array}

Then we prove the property by case analysis. We look at the case:

{\cal R}(N)\equiv@(\lambda y^{+}.M,V^{+})\rightarrow[V^{+}/y^{+}]M~.

Then
N\equiv E[@(x,x^{+})]
,
\sigma_{E}(x)\equiv\lambda y^{+}.M
, and

\sigma_{E}(x^{+})\equiv V^{+}
.
Moreover,
E\equiv E_{1}[{\sf let}\ x=\lambda y^{+}.M^{\prime}\ {\sf in}\ E_{2}]
and

\sigma_{E_{1}}(\lambda y^{+}.M^{\prime})\equiv\lambda y^{+}.M
.
Therefore,
N\rightarrow E[[x^{+}/y^{+}]M^{\prime}]\equiv N^{\prime}
and we check that

{\cal R}(N^{\prime})\equiv\sigma_{E}([x^{+}/y^{+}]M^{\prime})\equiv[V^{+}/y^{+}]M
.
{\Box}

14.3
Closure conversion (*)

The next step is called
closure conversion
. It consists in
providing each functional value with an additional parameter that
accounts for the names free in the body of the function and in
representing functions using closures. Our closure conversion function
implements a closure using a pair whose first component is the code of
the transformed function and whose second component is a tuple containing the
values of the free variables.

It will be convenient to write “
{\sf let}\ (y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})=x\ {\sf in}\ M
” for
“
{\sf let}\ y_{1}=\pi_{1}(x)\ {\sf in}\ \cdots{\sf let}\ y_{n}=\pi_{n}(x)\ {\sf in}\ M
” and
“
{\sf let}\ x_{1}=C_{1}\ldots x_{n}=C_{n}\ {\sf in}\ M
” for “
{\sf let}\ x_{1}=C_{1}\ {\sf in}\ldots{\sf let}\ x_{n}=C_{n}\ {\sf in}\ M
”. The transformation
is described in Table
14.5
. The output of the
transformation is such that all functional values are closed.

Example 14.3
(closure conversion)

Let
M\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}({\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(\lambda x.y))
, namely:

M\equiv{\sf let}\ z_{1}=\lambda x,k.@(k,y)\ {\sf in}\ @({\it halt},z_{1})~.

Then
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)
is the following term:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\sf let}\ c=\lambda e,x,k.({\sf let}\ (y)=e,(c,e)=k\ {\sf in}\ @(c,e,y))\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ e=(y),z_{1}=(c,e),(c,e)={\it halt}\ {\sf in}\\
@(c,e,z_{1})~.\par\end{array}

Syntactic restrictions on
\lambda_{cps,{\it vn}}
after closure conversion

All functional values are closed.

Closure Conversion

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(@(x,y^{+}))&={\sf let}\ (c,e)=x\ {\sf in}\ @(c,e,y^{+})\\
\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}({\sf let}\ x=C\ {\sf in}\ M)&=\begin{array}[]{l}{\sf let}\ c=\lambda e,x^{+}.{\sf let}\ (z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})=e\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(N)\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ e=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ x=(c,e)\ {\sf in}\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt(\mbox{if }C=\lambda x^{+}.N,{\sf fv}(C)=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\})\end{array}\\
\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}({\sf let}\ x=C\ {\sf in}\ M)&={\sf let}\ x=C\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012pt(\mbox{if }C\mbox{ not a function})\par\end{array}

Table 14.5:
Closure conversion on value named CPS terms

Proposition 14.3
(CC simulation)

Let
M
be a CPS term in value named form.
If
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
then
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M^{\prime})
.

Proof
. As a first step we check that the closure conversion function commutes with name
substitution:

{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}([x/y]M)\equiv[x/y]{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)~.

This is a direct induction on the structure of the term
M
.
Then we extend the closure conversion function to contexts as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}([~])&=[~]\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}({\sf let}\ x=(y^{*})\ {\sf in}\ E)&={\sf let}\ x=(y^{*})\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}({\sf let}\ x=\lambda x^{+}.M\ {\sf in}\ E)&={\sf let}\ c=\lambda e,x^{+}.{\sf let}\ (z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})=e\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)\ {\sf in}\\
&\quad{\sf let}\ e=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}),x=(c,e)\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)\\
&\mbox{where: }{\sf fv}(\lambda x^{+}.M)=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\}~.\end{array}

We note that for any evaluation context
E
,
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)
is again an evaluation context, and
moreover for any term
M
we have:

{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E[M])\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)]~.

Finally we prove the simulation property by case analysis of the reduction rule being
applied.

•

Suppose
M\equiv E[@(x,y^{+})]\rightarrow E[[y^{+}/x^{+}]M]
where
E(x)=\lambda x^{+}.M

and
{\sf fv}(\lambda x^{+}.M)=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\}
.
Then:

{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E[@(x,y^{+})])\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[{\sf let}\ (c,e)=x\ {\sf in}\ @(c,e,y^{+})]~,

with
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)(x)=(c,e)
,

{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)(c)=\lambda e,x^{+}.{\sf let}\ (z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})=e\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)
and

{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)(e)=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
. Therefore:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[{\sf let}\ (c^{\prime},e^{\prime})=x\ {\sf in}\ @(c^{\prime},e^{\prime},y^{+})]\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[{\sf let}\ (z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})=e\ {\sf in}\ [y^{+}/x^{+}]{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)]\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[[y^{+}/x^{+}]{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)]\\
\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}([y^{+}/x^{+}]M)]\qquad\mbox{(by substitution commutation)}\\
\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E[[y^{+}/x^{+}]M])~.\end{array}

•

Suppose
M\equiv E[{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ M]\rightarrow E[[z_{i}/x]M]

where
E(y)=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
,
1\leq i\leq k
.
Then:

{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E[{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ M])\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)]

with
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)(y)=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
.
Therefore:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)]\\
\rightarrow{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[[z_{i}/x]{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)]\\
\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E)[{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}([z_{i}/x]M)]\qquad\mbox{(by substitution commutation)}\\
\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(E[[z_{i}/x]M])~.\end{array}

{\Box}

Exercise 14.4

Define a closure conversion transformation that applies directly to
the source language rather than to the CPS, value named form.

14.4
Hoisting (*)

The last compilation step consists in moving all function definitions at top level.
In Table
14.6
, we formalize this compilation step as the iteration of a
set of program transformations that commute with
the reduction relation. Denote with
\lambda z^{+}.T

a function that does
not
contain function definitions.
The transformations
(h_{1})
and
(h_{2})
consist in hoisting (moving up) the definition
of a function
\lambda z^{+}.T
.
In transformation
(h_{1})
, we commute the function definition with
a tuple or a projection definition. This is always possible on the terms
resulting from a closure conversion since in these terms the functions are closed
and therefore cannot depend on a tuple or a projection definition above them.
In transformation
(h_{2})
, we have a function definition, say
f_{1}
which contains a nested
function definition, say
f_{2}
. In this case we extract
f_{2}
putting
it at the same level, and above
f_{1}
. Notice that in doing this
f_{1}

is not closed anymore since it may depend on
f_{2}
.
It can be shown that the rewriting system induced by the rules
(h_{1})
and
(h_{2})

applied to the terms resulting from the closure conversion terminates and is confluent.
We omit this rather technical but not difficult development.
The proof that the hoisted program simulates the original one also requires some work because to
close the diagram we need to collapse repeated definitions, which may
occur, as illustrated in the example below. Again, we omit this development.

Syntax for
\lambda_{h}

Syntactic restrictions on
\lambda_{cps,{\it vn}}
after hoisting

All function definitions are at top level.

\begin{array}[]{lll}C&::=({\it id}^{*})\mid\!\!\mid\pi_{i}({\it id})&\mbox{(restricted let-bindable terms)}\\
T&::=@({\it id},{\it id}^{+})\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=C\ {\sf in}\ T&\mbox{(restricted terms)}\\
P&::=T\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=\lambda{\it id}^{+}.T\ {\sf in}\ P&\mbox{(programs)}\end{array}

Specification of the hoisting transformation

{\cal C}_{{\it h}}(M)=N\mbox{ if }M\leadsto\cdots\leadsto N\not\leadsto,\hskip 8.50012pt\mbox{where:}

\begin{array}[]{lll}D&::=&[~]\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=C\ {\sf in}\ D\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=\lambda{\it id}^{+}.D\ {\sf in}\ M\hskip 17.00024pt\mbox{(hoisting contexts)}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lll}\\
(h_{1})&D[{\sf let}\ x=C\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ y=\lambda z^{+}.T\ {\sf in}\ M]\leadsto\\
&D[{\sf let}\ y=\lambda z^{+}.T\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ x=C\ {\sf in}\ M]&\mbox{if }x\notin{\sf fv}(\lambda z^{+}.T)\\
\\
(h_{2})&D[{\sf let}\ x=(\lambda w^{+}.{\sf let}\ y=\lambda z^{+}.T\ {\sf in}\ M)\ {\sf in}\ N]\leadsto\\
&D[{\sf let}\ y=\lambda z^{+}.T\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ x=\lambda w^{+}.M\ {\sf in}\ N]&\mbox{if }\{w^{+}\}\cap{\sf fv}(\lambda z^{+}.T)=\emptyset\par\end{array}

Table 14.6:
Hoisting transformation

Example 14.4
(hoisting transformations and transitions)

Let

M\equiv{\sf let}\ x_{1}=\lambda y_{1}.N\ {\sf in}\ @(x_{1},z)~,

where
N\equiv{\sf let}\ x_{2}=\lambda y_{2}.T_{2}\ {\sf in}\ T_{1}

and
y_{1}\notin{\sf fv}(\lambda y_{2}.T_{2})
.
Then we either reduce and then hoist:

\begin{array}[]{ll}M&\rightarrow{\sf let}\ x_{1}=\lambda y_{1}.N\ {\sf in}\ [z/y_{1}]N\\
&\equiv{\sf let}\ x_{1}=\lambda y_{1}.N\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ x_{2}=\lambda y_{2}.T_{2}\ {\sf in}\ [z/y_{1}]T_{1}\\
&\leadsto{\sf let}\ x_{2}=\lambda y_{2}.T_{2}\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ x_{1}=\lambda y_{1}.N\ {\sf in}\ [z/y_{1}]T_{1}\end{array}

or hoist and then reduce:

\begin{array}[]{ll}M&\leadsto{\sf let}\ x_{2}=\lambda y_{2}.T_{2}\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ x_{1}=\lambda y_{1}.T_{1}\ {\sf in}\ @(x_{1},z)\\
&\rightarrow{\sf let}\ x_{2}=\lambda y_{2}.T_{2}\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ x_{1}=\lambda y_{1}.T_{1}\ {\sf in}\ [z/y_{1}]T_{1}\end{array}

In the first case, we end up duplicating the definition of
x_{2}
.

We conclude by sketching an alternative definition of the hoisting
transformation.
Let
h
be a function that takes a term
M
in CPS, value named form
where all functions are closed and produces a pair
(T,F)
, where

T
is a term without function definitions as specified in Table
14.6
,
and
F
is a one-hole context composed of a list of function definitions of the shape:

F::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid{\sf let}\ {\it id}=\lambda{\it id}^{+}.T\ {\sf in}\ F~.

The definition of the function
h
is given by induction on
M
as follows
where
C
is a tuple or a projection as in Table
14.6
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}h(@(x,y^{+}))&=(@(x,y^{+}),[~])\\
h({\sf let}\ x=C\ {\sf in}\ M)&={\sf let}\ (T,F)=h(M)\ {\sf in}\ ({\sf let}\ x=C\ {\sf in}\ T,F)\\
h({\sf let}\ x=\lambda y^{+}.M\ {\sf in}\ N)&={\sf let}\ (T,F)=h(M),(T^{\prime},F^{\prime})=h(N)\ {\sf in}\\
&\hskip 17.00024pt(T^{\prime},F[{\sf let}\ x=\lambda y^{+}.T\ {\sf in}\ F^{\prime}])~.\par\end{array}

The hoisting transformation of the term
M
then amounts to compute
(T,F)=h(M)
and then
build the term
F[T]
which is a program according to the syntax defined in Table
14.6
.

Exercise 14.5

Apply the hoisting transformation to the terms resulting from the closure
conversion of exercise
14.4
.

14.5
Summary and references

We have studied four program transformations:

continuation passing style
makes the evaluation context an additional parameter,

value naming
assigns a name to every value,
closure conversion

explicits the notion of closure, and
hoisting
removes
nested function definitions. By putting these transformations
in pipeline it is possible to transform a program written in a higher-order language
such as
{\it ML}
into a system of functions whose body includes operations
to build and project tuples of names and to perform tail-recursive
routine calls.
Thus we have an implementation technique for higher-order languages
which is alternative to the one based on the abstract machines presented
in chapter
8
.
A similar compilation chain has been analyzed in
[
Chl10
]

which provides machine certified simulation proofs.
A simpler compilation chain arises if we bypass the CPS transformation.
In this case, the function calls are not necessarily
tail-recursive and the target code can be described as
{\it C}
code
with function pointers (cf. exercises
14.4
and
14.5
).
An early analysis of the CPS transformation is in

[
Plo75
]
. The idea of value-naming transformation
is associated with various formalizations of
sharing
, see,
e.g.
,
[
Lau93
]
.
Closure conversion arises naturally when trying to define the interpreter of a higher-order
language in the language itself, see,
e.g.
,
[
Rey98
]
. Hoisting appears to be folklore.

Chapter 15
Typing the program transformations

We present a typing of the compilation
chain described in chapter
14
.
Specifically, each
\lambda
-calculus of the compilation chain is equipped
with a type system which enjoys
subject reduction
:
if a term has a type then all terms to which it reduces have the same type.
Then the compilation functions are extended to types and are shown
to be
type preserving
: if a term has a type then its compilation
has the corresponding compiled type.

The two main steps in typing the compilation chain
concern the CPS and the closure conversion transformations.
The typing of the CPS transformation has already been
sketched in chapter
14

where it has served as a guideline.
A basic idea is to type the continuation/the evaluation context of a
term of type
A
with its negated type
\neg A=(A\rightarrow R)
, where
R
is
traditionally taken as the type of ‘results’.
In typing closure conversion, one
relies on
existential types
(cf. chapter
13
)
to hide the details of the representation
of the ‘environment’ of a function,
i.e.
, the tuple of variables
occurring free in its body. Thus, to type the (abstract) assembly code
coming from the compilation of propositionally typed programs,
we need to go beyond propositional types.

To represent types we shall follow the notation introduced starting
from chapter
10
.
In particular, we denote with

{\it tid}
the syntactic category of
type variables

with generic elements
t,s,\ldots
and with
A
the
syntactic category of
types
with generic elements
A,B,\ldots

We write
x^{*}:A^{*}
for a possibly empty sequence
x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}
, and

\Gamma,x^{*}:A^{*}
for the context
resulting from
\Gamma
by adding the sequence

x^{*}:A^{*}
. Hence the variables in
x^{*}
must not be in the domain of
\Gamma
.
If
A
is a type, we write
{\sf ftv}(A)
for the set of
type variables occurring free
in it and, by extension, if
\Gamma
is a type context
then
{\sf ftv}(\Gamma)
is the union of the sets
{\sf ftv}(A)

where
A
is a type in the codomain of
\Gamma
.
A
typing judgment
is typically written as
\Gamma\vdash M:A
where
M
is some term.
We shall write
\Gamma\vdash M^{*}:A^{*}
for
\Gamma\vdash M_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,
\Gamma\vdash M_{n}:A_{n}
.
Similar conventions apply if we replace the symbol
`*^{\prime}
with the symbol
`+^{\prime}
except that in this case the sequence is assumed not-empty.
A type transformation, say
{\cal T}
, is
lifted to type contexts
by defining

{\cal T}(x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots x_{n}:A_{n})=x_{1}:{\cal T}(A_{1}),\ldots,x_{n}:{\cal T}(A_{n})
.
Whenever we write:

if
\Gamma\vdash^{S_{1}}M:A
then
{\cal T}(\Gamma)\vdash^{S_{2}}{\cal T}(M):{\cal T}(A)
,

what we actually mean is that if the judgment in the hypothesis is
derivable
in a certain ‘type system
S_{1}
’ then the transformed judgment in derivable in
the ‘type system
S_{2}
’.
Proofs are standard and are left as exercises.

15.1
Typing the CPS form

Table
15.1
describes the typing rules
for the polyadic, call-by-value,
\lambda
-calculus
defined in Table
14.1
.
These rules are a slight generalization of those
studied in chapter
10
and
they are preserved by reduction.
The typing rules described in Table
15.1

apply to the CPS
\lambda
-calculus too. Table
15.1

describes the restricted syntax of the CPS types
and the CPS type transformation. Then the CPS term transformation
defined in Table
14.2
preserves typing in the following
sense.

Proposition 15.1
(type CPS)

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A
then

{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(\Gamma),{\it halt}:\neg{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(A)\vdash{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(M):R
.

Exercise 15.1

Prove proposition
15.1
.

Syntax types

\begin{array}[]{lll}A&::={\it tid}\mid\!\!\mid A^{+}\rightarrow A\mid\!\!\mid\times(A^{*})&\mbox{(types)}\end{array}

Typing rules

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}x:A\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash x:A\end{array}\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A\vdash N:B\hskip 17.00024pt\Gamma\vdash M:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N:B\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x^{+}:A^{+}\vdash M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\lambda x^{+}.M:A^{+}\rightarrow B\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A^{+}\rightarrow B\hskip 17.00024pt\Gamma\vdash N^{+}:A^{+}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash@(M,N^{+}):B\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M^{*}:A^{*}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash(M^{*}):\times(A^{*})\end{array}\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:\times(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\hskip 8.50012pt1\leq i\leq n\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\pi_{i}(M):A_{i}\end{array}\par\par\par\par\par\end{array}

Restricted syntax CPS types,
R
type of results

\begin{array}[]{lll}A&::={\it tid}\mid\!\!\mid A^{+}\rightarrow R\mid\!\!\mid\times(A^{*})&\mbox{(CPS types)}\end{array}

CPS type compilation

(\neg A\equiv(A\rightarrow R))

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(t)&=t\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(\times(A^{*}))&=\times({\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(A)^{*})\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(A^{+}\rightarrow B)&=(({\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(A))^{+},\neg{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(B))\rightarrow R\end{array}

Table 15.1:
Type system for
\lambda
and
\lambda_{{\it cps}}

15.2
Typing value-named closures (*)

Table
15.2
describes the typing rules for the
value named calculi with functional, product, and existential types.
Notice that for the sake of brevity, we shall omit the type of a
term since this type is always the
type of results
R
and write
\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}M
rather than

\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}M:R
.
The first five typing rules are just a
specialization of the corresponding rules in Table
15.1
,
while the last two rules allow for the introduction and elimination of
existential types
. The need for existential types will be motivated
next. For the time being, let us notice that
in the proposed formalization we rely on the tuple constructor to
introduce an existential type and the first projection to eliminate
it. This has the advantage of leaving unchanged the syntax and the
reduction rules of the value named
\lambda
-calculus. An
alternative presentation (cf. chapter
13
) consists in
introducing specific operators to
introduce and eliminate existential types denoted with

pack
and
unpack
, respectively. Then one can
read
(x)
as
{\sf pack}(x)
and
\pi_{1}(x)

as
{\sf unpack}(x)
when
x
has an existential type. Notice that
the rewriting rule which allows to
unpack
a
packed
value is just a special case of the rule for projection.
As in the previous system, typing is preserved by reduction.

Proposition 15.2
(subject reduction, value named)

If
M
is a term of the
\lambda_{{\it cps},{\it vn}}
-calculus,

\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}M
and
M\rightarrow N
(definitions in Table
14.3
)
then
\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}N
.

Exercise 15.2

Prove proposition
15.2
.

Turning to the transformation from CPS to
value named
CPS form
specified in Table
14.4
, we notice that it
affects the terms but not the types. Therefore we have the following property.

Proposition 15.3
(type value named)

If
M
is a term of the
\lambda_{{\it cps}}
-calculus
and
\Gamma\vdash M:R
then
\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}{\cal C}_{{\it vn}}(M)
.

Exercise 15.3

Prove proposition
15.3
.

Next we discuss the typing of closure conversion via existential types
(Table
15.2
).
We recall that in closure conversion a function, say
\lambda x.M

with free variables
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
, becomes a pair (here we
ignore the details
of the CPS, value named form):

(\lambda e,x.{\sf let}\ (z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=e\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}(M),(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}))

(15.1)

whose first component is the function
itself, which is
closed
by taking the environment
e
as an additional
argument, and the second component is a tuple containg the values of
the free variables.
Now consider the functions
identity
and
successor

on the natural numbers coded as follows:

\lambda x.x~,\qquad{\sf let}\ y=1\ {\sf in}\ \lambda x.x+y~,

(15.2)

with a type, say,
\mathbf{N}\rightarrow\mathbf{N}
.
After closure conversion, we obtain the following pairs with the respective

different
types:

\begin{array}[]{ll}(\lambda e,x.{\sf let}\ ()=e\ {\sf in}\ x,())&:((1\times\mathbf{N})\rightarrow\mathbf{N})\times 1\\
(\lambda e,x.{\sf let}\ (y)=e\ {\sf in}\ x+y,(y))&:((\mathbf{N}\times\mathbf{N})\rightarrow\mathbf{N})\times\mathbf{N}~.\par\end{array}

Then take a function such as
F:(\mathbf{N}\rightarrow\mathbf{N})\rightarrow\mathbf{N}

which can operate both on the identity and the successor function.
This is no longer possible after closure conversion
if we stick to the typing outlined above. We can address this issue
by
abstracting
the types of the identity and successor functions
after closure conversion into the following
existential
type:

\exists t.((t\times\mathbf{N})\rightarrow\mathbf{N})\times t~.

(15.3)

To summarize, an environment is a tuple whose size depends
on the number of variables occurring free in the function.
This information should be abstracted in the type; otherwise,
we cannot type functions operating on arguments with environments
of different size.

Syntax types

\begin{array}[]{lll}A&::={\it tid}\mid\!\!\mid(A^{+}\rightarrow R)\mid\!\!\mid\times(A^{*})\mid\!\!\mid\exists{\it tid}.A\end{array}

Typing rules

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x^{+}:A^{+}\vdash^{{\it vn}}M\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}\lambda x^{+}.M:A^{+}\rightarrow R\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}x:A^{+}\rightarrow R,y^{+}:A^{+}\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}@(x,y^{+})\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}x^{*}:A^{*}\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}(x^{*}):\times(A^{*})\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}y:\times(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\in\Gamma\hskip 8.50012pt1\leq i\leq n\\
\Gamma,x:A_{i}\vdash^{{\it vn}}M\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{i}(y)\ {\sf in}\ M\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}V:A\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{{\it vn}}M\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}{\sf let}\ x=V\ {\sf in}\ M\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}x:[B/t]A\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}(x):\exists t.A\end{array}\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}y:\exists t.A\in\Gamma\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{{\it vn}}M\hskip 8.50012ptt\notin{\sf ftv}(\Gamma)\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{1}(y)\ {\sf in}\ M\end{array}\par\par\par\par\par\end{array}

Closure conversion type compilation

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(t)&=t\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(\times(A^{*}))&=\times({\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(A)^{*})\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(A^{+}\rightarrow R)&=\exists t.\times((t,{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(A)^{+}\rightarrow R),\ t)\par\end{array}

Table 15.2:
Type system for the value named calculi and closure conversion

In order to respect our conventions on the introduction and elimination
of existential types, the closure conversion transformation is slightly
modified in the way described in Table
15.3
.
This modified closure conversion still enjoys the simulation properties
stated in proposition
14.3
and moreover
it preserves typing as follows.

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(@(x,y^{+}))&=\begin{array}[]{l}{\sf let}\ x=\pi_{1}(x)\ {\sf in}\hskip 17.00024pt\mbox{($\leftarrow$ {\sc existential elimination})}\\
{\sf let}\ (c,e)=x\ {\sf in}\ @(c,e,y^{+})\end{array}\\
\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}({\sf let}\ x=C\ {\sf in}\ M)&=\begin{array}[]{l}{\sf let}\ c=\lambda e,x^{+}.{\sf let}\ (z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})=e\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(N)\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ e=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ x=(c,e)\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ x=(x)\ {\sf in}\hskip 17.00024pt\mbox{($\leftarrow$ {\sc existential introduction})}\\
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt(\mbox{if }C=\lambda x^{+}.N,{\sf fv}(C)=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\})\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 15.3:
Modified closure conversion

Proposition 15.4
(type closure conversion)

If
M
is a term in
\lambda_{{\it cps},{\it vn}}
and
\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}M

then
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(\Gamma)\vdash^{{\it vn}}{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(M)
.

Exercise 15.4

Prove proposition
15.4
.

The last step in the compilation chain is the hoisting transformation.
Similarly to the transformation in value named form,
the hoisting transformation affects the terms but
not
the types.

Proposition 15.5
(type hoisting)

If
M
is a term in
\lambda_{{\it cps},{\it vn}}
,

\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}M
, and
M\leadsto N
then
\Gamma\vdash^{{\it vn}}N
.

Exercise 15.5

Prove proposition
15.5
.

15.3
Typing the compiled code (*)

We can now extend the compilation function to types by defining:

{\cal C}(A)={\cal C}_{{\it cc}}({\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(A))

and by composing the previous results we derive the following type preservation
property of the compilation function.

Proposition 15.6
(type preserving compilation)

If
M
is a term of the
\lambda
-calculus
and
\Gamma\vdash M:A
then:

{\cal C}(\Gamma),{\it halt}:\exists t.\times(t,{\cal C}(A)\rightarrow R,t)\vdash^{{\it vn}}{\cal C}(M)~.

Exercise 15.6

Prove proposition
15.6
.

Remark 15.1

The ‘halt’ variable introduced by the CPS transformation
can occur only in a subterm of the shape
@({\it halt},x)
in the intermediate
code prior to closure conversion.
Then in the closure conversion transformation, we can set
{\cal C}_{{\it cc}}(@({\it halt},x))=@({\it halt},x)
, and give to
`{\it halt}^{\prime}

a functional rather than an existential type. With this proviso,
proposition
15.6
above can be restated as follows:

If
M
is a term of the
\lambda
-calculus and

\Gamma\vdash M:A
then
{\cal C}(\Gamma),{\it halt}:\neg{\cal C}(A)\vdash^{{\it vn}}{\cal C}(M)
.

Example 15.1
(typing the compiled code)

We consider again the compilation of the term
\lambda x.y
(cf. example
14.3
) which can be typed, e.g., as follows:

y:t_{1}\vdash\lambda x.y:(t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1})
.
Its CPS transformation is then typed as:

y:t_{1},{\it halt}:\neg{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1})\vdash@({\it halt},\lambda x,k.@(k,y)):R~.

The value named transformation does not affect the types:

y:t_{1},{\it halt}:\neg{\cal C}_{{\it cps}}(t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1})\vdash^{{\it vn}}{\sf let}\ z_{1}=\lambda x,k.@(k,y)\ {\sf in}\ @({\it halt},z_{1})~.

After closure conversion, we obtain the following term
M
:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\sf let}\ c=\lambda e,x,k.{\sf let}\ y=\pi_{1}(e),k=\pi_{1}(k),c=\pi_{1}(k),e=\pi_{2}(k)\ {\sf in}\ @(c,e,y)\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ e=(y),z_{1}=(c,e),z_{1}=(z_{1}),{\it halt}=\pi_{1}({\it halt}),c=\pi_{1}({\it halt}),e=\pi_{2}({\it halt})\ {\sf in}\\
@(c,e,z_{1})~,\end{array}

which is typed as follows:

y:t_{1},{\it halt}:\exists t.\times(t,\neg{\cal C}(t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1}),t)\vdash^{{\it vn}}M
.
In this case no further hoisting transformation applies.
If we adopt the optimized compilation strategy sketched in remark
15.1

then after closure conversion we obtain the following term
M^{\prime}
:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\sf let}\ c=\lambda e,x,k.{\sf let}\ y=\pi_{1}(e),k=\pi_{1}(k),c=\pi_{1}(k),e=\pi_{2}(k)\ {\sf in}\ @(c,e,y)\ {\sf in}\\
{\sf let}\ e=(y),z_{1}=(c,e),z_{1}=(z_{1}),\ {\sf in}\\
@({\it halt},z_{1})\end{array}

which is typed as follows:

y:t_{1},{\it halt}:\neg{\cal C}(t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1})\vdash^{{\it vn}}M^{\prime}
.

15.4
Summary and references

We have typed the compilation chain presented in chapter
14

which goes from a higher-order language
to an abstract assembly code. The typing of the CPS transformation
builds on the double negation translations from classical to
intuitionistic logic (see,
e.g.
,
[
TvD88
]
).
The typing of closure conversion relies on
existential types to hide the details of the representation

[
MMH96
]
.
The paper
[
MWCG99
]
shows that
the typing can be extend to the impredicative polymorphic types of
system F (cf. chapter
13
).

Chapter 16
Records, variants, and subtyping

Records and variants are common data types found in many programming
languages which allow to aggregate heterogeneous data.
Record (variant) types provide a user-friendly alternative
to product (sum) types where components can be manipulated by
labels rather than by projections (injections).

In this chapter, we start by discussing an extension of
the call-by-value, type-free,
\lambda
-calculus with records
and a possible encoding of records.
We then move on to consider a
typed
version of the language.
In order to gain in flexibility, we introduce
a
subtyping rule
for records and study
the properties of the derived type system. We conclude by
briefly discussing how the approach with subtyping
can be extended to variant types.

16.1
Records

A
record
is a notation to represent a function with a finite
domain over a set of
labels
which are defined as follows.

Definition 16.1
(labels)

We denote with
L
a countable and totally ordered
set of labels with generic elements

\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ldots

We rely on the notation:

\{\ell_{1}=V_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}=V_{n}\}~,

(16.1)

to denote the function that associates with the label
\ell_{i}
the value

V_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, and which is undefined otherwise. Whenever we write a record
we assume that the labels are all distinct:
\ell_{i}\neq\ell_{j}
if
i\neq j
.
Given a record
R
, we write
R.\ell
for the selection of
the value of
R
on the label
\ell
.
If
\ell
is not in the domain of definition of the record then
we are in an erroneous situation and the computation is stuck or
alternatively an error message is produced.

Table
16.1

describes an extension of the type-free, call-by-value,

\lambda
-calculus with records. In order to have a deterministic
evaluation strategy, we assume that records are always written
with labels in
growing order
and that the evaluation follows this order.

Syntax

\begin{array}[]{ll}M::={\it id}\mid\!\!\mid\lambda{\it id}.M\mid\!\!\mid MM\mid\!\!\mid\{\ell=M,\ldots,\ell=M\}\mid\!\!\mid M.\ell&\mbox{($\lambda$-terms)}\\
V::=\lambda{\it id}.M\mid\!\!\mid\{\ell=V,\ldots,\ell=V\}&\mbox{(values)}\end{array}

Call-by-value evaluation contexts and reduction rules

\begin{array}[]{c}E::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid EM\mid\!\!\mid VE\mid\!\!\mid\{(\ell=V)^{*},\ell=E,(\ell=M)^{*}\}\mid\!\!\mid E.\ell\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\lambda x.M)V&\rightarrow&[V/x]M\\
\{\ldots,\ell=V,\ldots\}.\ell&\rightarrow&V\end{array}

Table 16.1:
Type-free, call-by-value,
\lambda
-calculus with records

We pause to notice that in principle records could be represented
in the pure
\lambda
-calculus. For instance, we could
associate with each label a natural number and then associate
with it a Church numeral. Suppose: (i)
\underline{\ell}
denotes the
Church numeral that corresponds to the label
\ell
,
(ii)
E
is a
\lambda
-term that decides the equality of two Church numerals
(cf. exercise
13.3
),
(iii)
C
is the
\lambda
-term that represents the conditional,
(iv)
F
is a special
\lambda
-term to represent failure,
and (v) we write
{\sf let}\ x_{1}=M_{1},\ldots,x_{n}=M_{n}\ {\sf in}\ N
for

(\lambda x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}.N)M_{1}\cdots M_{n}
.
Then we could
compile
the call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus with records
into the call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus following the rules
in Table
16.2
(simple cases omitted).
A record is compiled into a function taking a label as
input and then performing a sequence of conditionals. Selecting
a record’s label just amounts to
apply
the compilation of the record
to the encoding of the label.

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal C}(\{\ell_{1}=M_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}=M_{n}\})&={\sf let}\ x_{1}={\cal C}(M_{1}),\ldots,x_{n}={\cal C}(M_{n})\ {\sf in}\\
&\hskip 8.50012pt\lambda l.C(E\ l\ \underline{\ell_{1}})\ x_{1}(\cdots\hskip 8.50012pt(C(E\ l\ \underline{\ell_{n}})\ x_{n}\ F)\cdots)\\
\\
{\cal C}(M.\ell)&={\sf let}\ x={\cal C}(M)\ {\sf in}\ (x\ \underline{\ell})~.\par\end{array}

Table 16.2:
Compilation of records in the pure
\lambda
-calculus

Exercise 16.1

Let
M=(\lambda x.\lambda y.\{\ell_{1}=x,\ell_{2}=y\}.\ell_{2})IK
.
Complete the definition of the compilation function
{\cal C}
of
Table
16.2
, compute
{\cal C}(M)
, and reduce the
result to normal form.

16.2
Subtyping

Next we turn to the issue of typing the extension of
the
\lambda
-calculus with records.
We take as starting point the type system in
Table
10.1
that assigns simple types
to
\lambda
-terms whose
\lambda
-abstractions are decorated with
types (Church style).
We extend the syntax of types by introducing a notion of
record
type
which is a notation for representing a finite function from
labels to types:

\begin{array}[]{ll}A::={\it tid}\mid\!\!\mid(A\rightarrow A)\mid\!\!\mid\{\ell:A,\ldots,\ell:A\}&\mbox{(types).}\\
\end{array}

And then we add two
typing rules
to introduce and eliminate record types
which are presented in Table
16.3
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}\hskip 8.50012pti=1,\ldots,n\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash\{\ell_{1}=M_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}=M_{n}\}:\{\ell_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{n}\}\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:\{\ell_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{n}\}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash M.\ell_{i}:A_{i}\end{array}~.\end{array}

Table 16.3:
Typing rules for records

The extended type system still has the property that in a given type context
each
\lambda
-term has at most one type. However, consider the record types:

A=\{\ell_{1}:A_{1},\ell_{2}:A_{2}\}~,\qquad B=\{\ell_{1}:A_{1}\}~.

If we have a value of type
A
then we could use it in any
context that waits for a value of type
B
. This simple remark
pleads for the introduction of a
subtyping
relation
A\leq B
.
Table
16.4
describes a possible definition
of the subtyping relation for records and functional types.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr t\leq t\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt\par\begin{array}[]{c}A^{\prime}\leq A\hskip 17.00024ptB\leq B^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr A\rightarrow B\leq A^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\{\ell^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{m}\}\subseteq\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}\hskip 8.50012ptA_{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}\leq B_{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip 8.50012pti=1,\ldots,m\\
\hline\cr\{\ell_{1}:A_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{\ell_{n}}\}\leq\{\ell^{\prime}_{1}:B_{\ell^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{m}:B_{\ell^{\prime}_{m}}\}\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 16.4:
Subtyping rules for records

We write
\vdash A\leq B
if the assertion
A\leq B
can be
derived according to the rules in Table
16.4
.
There are a couple of intriguing points in the definition of the rules.
First, notice that the rule for functional types is
anti-monotonic

in the first argument. To get an intuition, suppose we can use natural numbers
where integers are expected:
\mathbf{N}\leq{\bf Z}
.
Then a function
f
of type
{\bf Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{N}
can also be used whenever
a function of type
\mathbf{N}\rightarrow{\bf Z}
is expected. Indeed,
f
will
be able to handle any natural number since it is built to work on
integers and it will return an integer since it is expected to return
a natural number. On the other hand, if
g
has type
\mathbf{N}\rightarrow\mathbf{N}

then it cannot be used where a function of type
{\bf Z}\rightarrow{\bf Z}
is expected
as
g
may fail to handle a negative integer.
Second, the rules are completely
syntax directed
: for each pair
of types there is at most one rule that applies and in this case
there is only one way to apply it.

Proposition 16.1

The subtyping relation (Table
16.4
)
enjoys the following properties:

1.

It is reflexive and transitive.

2.

If
\vdash A\leq B
then there is a closed
\lambda
-term
C_{A,B}

(a coercion) such that
\emptyset\vdash C_{A,B}:A\rightarrow B
.

Proof
.
(1)\;

Reflexivity follows by induction on the structure of the type
A
.
For transitivity, we build a proof of
B\leq C
by induction on the
height of the proofs of
A\leq B
and
B\leq C
and case analysis
on the last rules applied.
For instance, suppose we have:

\begin{array}[]{c}B^{\prime}\leq A^{\prime}\hskip 17.00024ptA^{\prime\prime}\leq B^{\prime\prime}\\
\hline\cr A^{\prime}\rightarrow A^{\prime\prime}\leq B^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{array}[]{c}C^{\prime}\leq B^{\prime}\hskip 17.00024ptB^{\prime\prime}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\\
\hline\cr B^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime\prime}\leq C^{\prime}\rightarrow C^{\prime\prime}\end{array}~.

Then by inductive hypothesis we can prove
C^{\prime}\leq A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}\leq C^{\prime\prime}
and
we conclude as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}C^{\prime}\leq A^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012ptA^{\prime\prime}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\\
\hline\cr A^{\prime}\rightarrow A^{\prime\prime}\leq C^{\prime}\rightarrow C^{\prime\prime}\end{array}~.

(2)\;

We proceed by induction on the proof of
A\leq B
.
For the basic case, take the identity. For the functional case, take:

C_{A^{\prime}\rightarrow A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime\prime}}=\lambda f:A^{\prime}\rightarrow A^{\prime\prime}.\lambda x:B^{\prime}.c_{A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime}}(f(c_{B^{\prime},A^{\prime}}x))~.

For the record case, assume:

\begin{array}[]{c}A=\{\ell_{1}:A_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{\ell_{n}}\}~,\qquad B=\{\ell^{\prime}_{1}:B_{\ell^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{m}:B_{\ell^{\prime}_{m}}\}~,\end{array}

and the conditions specified in Table
16.4

are satisfied. Then define:

C_{A,B}=\lambda x:A.\{\ell^{\prime}_{1}=C_{A_{\ell^{\prime}_{1}},B_{\ell^{\prime}_{1}}}(x.\ell^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{m}=C_{A_{\ell^{\prime}_{m}},B_{\ell^{\prime}_{m}}}(x.\ell^{\prime}_{m})\}~.

{\Box}

Proposition
16.1
above guarantees that the subtyping
relation defined by the rules in Table
16.4
is indeed
a pre-order and moreover that whenever
A
is a subtype of
B
we can build
a well-typed
\lambda
-term of type
A\rightarrow B
that gives us a canonical way to
transform a
\lambda
-term of type
A
into a
\lambda
-term of type
B
.

Next we discuss the integration of the subtying rule to the type system
for the
\lambda
-calculus with records. One possibility would be to add
the following typing rule while leaving all the other typing rules unchanged:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A\qquad\vdash A\leq B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash M:B\end{array}~.

(16.2)

The problem with this approach is that typing is no more directed
by the syntax of the
\lambda
-term (we had a similar problem with the
rules
(\forall_{I})
and
(\forall_{E})
in Table
12.1
).
However, one can remark that the only
situation where types need to be matched arises in the application
of a
\lambda
-term to another one. Hence, we integrate subtyping to the rule
for application as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:A\rightarrow B\quad\Gamma\vdash N:A^{\prime}\quad\vdash A^{\prime}\leq A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash MN:B\end{array}~.

(16.3)

Notice that the resulting system maintains the property that each
\lambda
-term has
at most one type.
Let us write
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}M:A
for a judgment derivable in the
resulting type system and let us write
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:A
for a
judgment derivable in the ordinary type system extended with the subtyping rule
(
16.2
).

Proposition 16.2

The following properties hold:

1.

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}M:A
then

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:A
.

2.

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:A
then
there is a type
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}M:B
and
\vdash B\leq A
.

Proof
.
(1)\;

Rule (
16.3
) can be derived from
the rule (
16.2
) and the ordinary rule to
type application.

(2)\;
We proceed by induction on the
derivation of
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:A
. We
consider some significant cases.

•

Suppose we derive
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:A
from

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:A^{\prime}
and
\vdash A^{\prime}\leq A
.
Then by inductive hypothesis, we can derive

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}M:B
and
\vdash B\leq A^{\prime}
.
And by transitivity of subtyping (proposition
16.1
),
we conclude
\vdash B\leq A
.

•

Suppose we derive
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}MN:A
from

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
and

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}N:A^{\prime}
.
Then by inductive hypothesis, we can derive

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}M:B_{1}
,
\vdash B_{1}\leq A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
,

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}N:B_{2}
, and
\vdash B_{2}\leq A^{\prime}
.
Then we must have
B_{1}\equiv B^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow B^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,

\vdash A^{\prime}\leq B^{\prime}_{1}
, and
\vdash B^{\prime\prime}_{1}\leq A
.
By transitivity,
\vdash B_{2}\leq B^{\prime}_{1}
.
Therefore we can derive:

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}MN:B^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
\vdash B^{\prime\prime}_{1}\leq A
.

•

Suppose we derive

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}\lambda x:A.M:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
from

\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:A^{\prime}
.
Then by inductive hypothesis, we can derive

\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\leq}^{s}M:B
and
\vdash B\leq A^{\prime}
.
Hence
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}^{s}\lambda x:A.M:A\rightarrow B

and
\vdash A\rightarrow B\leq A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
.
{\Box}

Thus the syntax-directed system assigns to a typable
\lambda
-term
the least type among the types assignable to the
\lambda
-term in the more liberal
system where the subtyping rule can be freely applied.
The statement of the subject reduction property in the syntax-directed
system requires some care because the type of a
\lambda
-term may grow
after reduction. For instance, consider the reduction:

M\equiv(\lambda x:\{\ell_{1}:A_{1}\}.x)\{\ell_{1}=V_{1},\ell_{2}=V_{2}\}\rightarrow\{\ell_{1}=V_{1},\ell_{2}=V_{2}\}\equiv N~.

Then we may have
\emptyset\vdash_{\leq}M:\{\ell_{1}:A_{1}\}
and

\emptyset\vdash_{\leq}N:\{\ell_{1}:A_{1},\ell_{2}:A_{2}\}
.

Proposition 16.3

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}M:A
and
M\rightarrow N
then for some type
B
,

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}N:B
and
\vdash B\leq A
.

Proof
. As a preliminary remark, we show that if
\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\leq}M:B
,

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}N:A^{\prime}
, and
\vdash A^{\prime}\leq A
then

\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}[N/x]M:B^{\prime}
and
\vdash B^{\prime}\leq B
.
The preliminary remark is applied in the analysis of a
\beta
-reduction.
Suppose
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}(\lambda x:A.M)N:B
. Then we
must have
\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\leq}M:B
,
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}N:A^{\prime}
,
and
\vdash A^{\prime}\leq A
. Thus
\Gamma\vdash_{\leq}[N/x]M:B^{\prime}
and

\vdash B^{\prime}\leq B
.
{\Box}

The extension of the system with subtyping still guarantees that
a well-typed program cannot go wrong. In particular, it is not
possible to select a label
\ell
in a record where the label is
not defined.

Proposition 16.4

Suppose
\emptyset\vdash_{\leq}M:A
then either
M
is a value or

M\rightarrow N
.

Proof
. By induction on the structure of
M
.
Suppose
M
is not a value. It cannot be a variable because
the type context is empty.

If
M\equiv M_{1}M_{2}
then we must have
\emptyset\vdash_{\leq}M_{1}:A\rightarrow B
,

\emptyset\vdash_{\leq}M_{2}:A^{\prime}
and
\vdash A^{\prime}\leq A
.
By inductive hypothesis, if
M_{1}
or
M_{2}
are not values then
they reduce and so
M_{1}M_{2}
reduces too.
On the other hand, if
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are both values then

M_{1}
must be a
\lambda
-abstraction and therefore
M
reduces.

If
M\equiv M^{\prime}.\ell
then we must have

\emptyset\vdash_{\leq}M^{\prime}:\{\ldots\ell:A\ldots\}
.
By inductive hypothesis, if
M^{\prime}
is not a value then it reduces and
so
M
reduces too.
On the other hand, if
M^{\prime}
is a value then it must be a record
defined on the label
\ell
and therefore
M
reduces.
{\Box}

16.3
Variants (*)

Variants are data structures dual to records
just as sums are dual to products and
the subtyping theory developed for records can
be adapted to some extent to variants.
As for records, we start with a set of labels (cf. definition
16.1
).
Then a variant is a notation to represent an element of a finite disjoint sum
indexed over labels.

With reference to Table
16.1
, the collection of

\lambda
-terms
is extended as follows:

M::=\cdots\mid\!\!\mid[\ell=M]_{[\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}]}\mid\!\!\mid{\sf case}\ MN\cdots N

(16.4)

where we assume the labels
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}
are distinct.
A variant
value
is a
\lambda
-term of the shape
[\ell=V]_{[\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}]}
.
The collection of
evaluation contexts
of Table
16.1

is extended as follows:

E::=\cdots\mid\!\!\mid[\ell=E]_{[\ell^{*}]}\mid\!\!\mid{\sf case}\ V^{*}EM^{*}

(16.5)

The
reduction rule
for variants is:

{\sf case}\ [\ell=V]_{[\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}]}V_{1}\ldots V_{n}\rightarrow V_{i}V\qquad\mbox{if }\ell=\ell_{i}~.

(16.6)

Turning to the
typed
version,
we denote a
variant type
with the notation:

[\ell_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{n}]~.

(16.7)

The syntax of
\lambda
-terms is modified as follows so that each label is associated with a type:

M::=\cdots\mid\!\!\mid[\ell=M]_{[\ell_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{n}]}\mid\!\!\mid{\sf case}\ MN\cdots N

(16.8)

The typing and subtyping rules for introducing and eliminating variants
are given in
Table
16.5
.
We notice that the subtyping rule for variants is similar to the one for records
but
upside down
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{{\sc Typing rules}}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}A=[\ell_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{n}]\hskip 8.50012pt\ell=\ell_{i}\\
\Gamma\vdash M:A_{i}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash[\ell=M]_{A}:A\end{array}\par\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:[\ell_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{n}]\\
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B\hskip 8.50012pti=1,\ldots,n\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma\vdash{\sf case}\ MM_{1}\ldots M_{n}:B\end{array}\\
\\
\mbox{{\sc Subtyping rule}}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}\subseteq\{\ell^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{m}\}\hskip 8.50012ptA_{\ell_{i}}\leq B_{\ell_{i}}\hskip 8.50012pti=1,\ldots,n\\
\hline\cr[\ell_{1}:A_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\ell_{n}:A_{\ell_{n}}]\leq[\ell^{\prime}_{1}:B_{\ell^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{m}:B_{\ell^{\prime}_{m}}]\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 16.5:
Typing and subtyping rules for variant types

Exercise 16.2

Show that:
(1) the extension with variants (but without subtying) preserves
the property that each term has at most one type,
(2) the subtyping relation extended to variant types is still
reflexive and transitive,
(3) if
\vdash A\leq B
then there is a closed
\lambda
-term

C_{A,B}
such that
\emptyset\vdash C_{A,B}:A\rightarrow B
.

16.4
Summary and references

Records and variants are a user-friendly version of products and
disjoint unions.
The introduction of record and variant types suggests
a notion of
subtyping
with the following intuition:
if
A
is a subtype of
B
then we should be able to use a value
of type
A
whenever a value of type
B
is expected.
We have shown that the subtyping rule can be added to the type
system in such a way that typing is still syntax-directed and
a typable
\lambda
-term is assigned the least type with respect to the
sub-typing pre-order. The paper
[
Car88
]

is an early reference on the formalization of subtyping and its
semantics. Elaborations can be found,
e.g.
, in

[
Mit88
,
AC93
]
.
The book
[
Pie02
]
contains several
chapters dedicated to subtyping.

Chapter 17
References

In chapter
1
, we have
considered an elementary
imperative
programming
language whose programs can be understood as sequences
of commands acting on a global
state
.
In that context, the state was regarded as an abstraction
of the notion of computer memory and was simply modeled as a (total) function
from identifiers to (basic) values.

In this chapter, we reconsider the notion of
imperative

programming. We replace the
state
mentioned above with a notion
of
heap
. A heap can be regarded as an abstraction
of the notion of computer memory too and it is
modeled as a (partial) function from
references
to
(possibly complex) values. In turn, references can be regarded
as an abstraction of the notion of memory address. References
are first-class values. The value associated with a reference
can be read and modified. Moreover, during the computation,
it is possible to generate new references and associate values with them.

We formalize a higher-order functional language with references
which is inspired by the languages of the
{\it ML}
family.
Technically, we introduce the reduction rules of
a type-free, call-by-value,
\lambda
-calculus
with
references
extended with operations to generate, read,
and write references.
We then discuss a possible compilation of the
\lambda
-calculus
with ‘side effects’ on the heap into an ordinary

\lambda
-calculus. The compilation turns each expression into a function
that takes a heap as an argument and returns a pair composed
of a new heap and a value.
We conclude the chapter by introducing a propositional type system
for the
\lambda
-calculus with references which enjoys
a subject-reduction property and by discussing some
typing anomalies which arise with references.

17.1
References and heaps

References can be regarded as an abstraction of memory addresses and
a heap as an abstraction of a computer memory.

Definition 17.1
(references)

We denote with
R
a countable set of references with generic
elements
r,r^{\prime},\ldots
We assume
R
is equipped with a
function
{\cal N}:{\cal P}_{fin}(R)\rightarrow R
such that for all
X
,
finite subset of
R
, we have
{\cal N}(X)\notin X
(so
{\cal N}(X)
is a ‘fresh reference’
relatively to
X
).

Definition 17.2
(heap)

A heap
h
is a partial function over the set of
references
R
whose domain of definition is finite.

We manipulate heaps using the
standard notation for functions.
Thus if
h
is a heap then

{\it dom}(h)
is its domain of
definition,
h(r)
its image
at
r
, and
h[v/r]
is an ‘updated’ heap
defined as follows (this is a variant of the
state update defined in section
1.1
):

h[v/r](r^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h(r)&\mbox{if }r\neq r^{\prime}\\
v&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

(17.1)

Notice that we make no assumption on the nature
of the values in a heap and that in particular
a value can be a reference.
In Table
17.1
, we introduce an extension of
the type-free, call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus with a notation closely related to the
one found in the programming languages of the
{\it ML}
family:

{\sf ref}\ M
allocates a new reference which is associated with
the value of
M
,
!M
reads the value associated
with the reference resulting from the evaluation of

M
, and
M:=N
writes in the reference resulting from the evaluation
of
M
the value of
N
.
We also introduce a constant
*
which is used as the value resulting
from the evaluation of an assignment
M:=N
.
Ordinary programs are closed
\lambda
-terms where references
do not occur. However this property is not preserved by reduction
and for this reason we include references among the
\lambda
-terms and the
values of the language.
We rely on the following standard abbreviations:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\sf let}\ x=M\ {\sf in}\ N&=(\lambda x.N)M\\
M;N&=(\lambda x.N)M&\mbox{where: }x\notin{\sf fv}(N)~.\end{array}

Syntax

\begin{array}[]{ll}M::=V\mid\!\!\mid{\it id}\mid\!\!\mid MM\mid\!\!\mid{\sf ref}\ M\mid\!\!\mid!M\mid\!\!\mid M:=M&\mbox{($\lambda$-terms)}\\
V::=*\mid\!\!\mid r\mid\!\!\mid\lambda{\it id}.M&\mbox{(values)}\par\end{array}

Call by value evaluation contexts and Reduction rules

\begin{array}[]{l}E::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid EM\mid\!\!\mid VE\mid\!\!\mid{\sf ref}E\mid\!\!\mid!E\mid\!\!\mid E:=M\mid\!\!\mid V:=E\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{llll}(E[(\lambda x.M)V],h)&\rightarrow&([V/x]M,h)\\
(E[{\sf ref}\ V],h)&\rightarrow&(E[r],h[V/r])&\mbox{if }r={\cal N}({\it dom}(h))\\
(E[!r],h)&\rightarrow&(E[h(r)],h)&\mbox{if }r\in{\it dom}(h)\\
(E[r:=V],h)&\rightarrow&(E[*],h[V/r])&\mbox{if }r\in{\it dom}(h)\end{array}

Table 17.1:
A call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus with references

References and heaps can be simulated
in the pure
\lambda
-calculus. As for
records’ labels (cf. chapter
16
), we can use
Church numerals to represent references. The
reference
{\cal N}(X)
can be implemented by computing
the successor of the largest numeral in the finite set
X
.
A heap can then be represented
as a list of pairs composed of a reference and a value.
Computing the domain of a heap amounts to iterate the first
projection on the list. Reading a reference
r
in the heap means scanning the
list till a pair
(r,V)
is found. Updating a
reference means building a new heap where the value
corresponding to the reference is suitably modified.
Let us assume
\lambda
-terms
{\it New}
to create a new reference,

{\it Ext}
to extend a heap with a new pair,

{\it Read}
to read a reference, and
{\it Write}

to write a value in the heap.
In Table
17.2
, we describe the compilation of the
the
\lambda
-calculus with references into a
\lambda
-calculus
with
pairing
. We also use the following abbreviation for projections:

{\sf let}\ (x,y)=M\ {\sf in}\ N\equiv{\sf let}\ z=M,\ x=\pi_{1}z,\ y=\pi_{2}\ z\ {\sf in}\ N~.

We denote with
\underline{r}
the Church numeral which corresponds to the
reference
r
.
A
\lambda
-term
M
of the
\lambda
-calculus with references is compiled
into a function which takes a heap
h
as an argument and returns
a pair composed of the heap
h
modified according to the side-effects
of
M
and a value which corresponds to the outcome of the computation
of
M
.
There is some similarity between records (cf. chapter
16
)
and heaps in that a record is a finite function defined on a set
of labels and a heap is a finite function defined
on a set of references. However, references,
unlike labels, can be generated during the computation,
are treated as first class-values, and the value associated with a
reference can be updated.

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal C}(x)&=\lambda h.(h,x)\\
{\cal C}(\lambda x.M)&=\lambda h.(h,\lambda x.{\cal C}(M))\\
{\cal C}(r)&=\lambda h.(h,\underline{r})\\
{\cal C}(*)&=\lambda h.(h,*)\\
{\cal C}(MN)&=\lambda h.{\sf let}\ (h^{\prime},x)={\cal C}(M)h,\ (h^{\prime\prime},y)={\cal C}(N)h^{\prime}\ {\sf in}\ (xy)h^{\prime\prime}\\
{\cal C}({\sf ref}M)&=\lambda h.{\sf let}\ (h^{\prime},x)={\cal C}(M)h,\ r=({\it New}\ h^{\prime})\ {\sf in}\ ({\it Ext}\ h^{\prime}rx,r)\\
{\cal C}(!M)&=\lambda h.{\sf let}\ (h^{\prime},r)={\cal C}(M)h\ {\sf in}\ (h^{\prime},{\it Read}\ h^{\prime}r)\\
{\cal C}(M:=N)&=\lambda h.{\sf let}\ (h^{\prime},r)={\cal C}(M)h,\ (h^{\prime\prime},x)={\cal C}(N)h^{\prime}\ {\sf in}\ ({\it Write}\ h^{\prime\prime}rx,*)\par\end{array}

Table 17.2:
Simulating the heap in a functional language

Exercise 17.1

Consider the
\lambda
-term:

M\equiv{\sf let}\ x={\sf ref}(\lambda y.y)\ {\sf in}\ x:=\lambda y.(!x)y;(!x)*~.

Compute
{\cal C}(M)
and analyse its reduction.

17.2
Typing references

We consider the problem of extending the propositional type system
discussed in chapter
10
, Table
10.1
,
to the
\lambda
-calculus with references.
To type the value
*
we introduce a basic type
1
whose
only value is
*
.
Moreover, we introduce a new type constructor
{\sf Ref}
.
A value of type
{\sf Ref}\ A
is a reference which can contain
values of type
A
.
In order to type a
\lambda
-term we have to make hypotheses on the type
of its free variables and of the references that occur in it.
Consequently, we introduce a notion of
heap context

\Sigma
of the
shape
r_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,r_{n}:A_{n}
. If
r:A\in\Sigma
then
the reference
r
is associated with values of type
A
.
Table
17.3
gives the type system for
\lambda
-terms.

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}x:A\in\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash x:A\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}r:A\in\Sigma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash r:{\sf Ref}\ A\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,x:A;\Sigma\vdash M:B\\
\hline\cr\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\lambda x:A.M:A\rightarrow B\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash M:A\rightarrow B\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash N:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash MN:B\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash*:1\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash M:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash{\sf ref}\ M:{\sf Ref}\ A\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash M:{\sf Ref}\ A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash!M:A\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash M:{\sf Ref}\ A\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash N:A\\
\hline\cr\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash M:=N:1\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 17.3:
Typing rules for the
\lambda
-calculus with references

Besides
\lambda
-terms we need to type heaps too. Consider the

\lambda
-term without references:

{\sf let}\ x={\sf ref}\ (\lambda z:A.z)\ {\sf in}\ {\sf let}\ y={\sf ref}\ (\lambda z:A.!xz)\ {\sf in}\ x:=\lambda z:A.!yz~.

(17.2)

By reducing it, we can produce the following heap:

h_{0}=[\lambda x:A.(!r_{2})x/r_{1},\lambda x:A.(!r_{1})x/r_{2}]
.
Notice that the values associated with
r_{1}
and
r_{2}

depend on
r_{2}
and
r_{1}
respectively. Thus to type a heap
we have to find a heap context which assigns a type to
all the references of the heap which is coherent with
the type of the values associated with the references.
Also we require that all the references in the values of the
heap belong to the domain of definition of the heap.
This leads to the following rule for typing a heap with
respect to a heap context:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\it dom}(\Sigma)={\it dom}(h)\hskip 17.00024pt\emptyset;\Sigma\vdash h(r):A\hskip 8.50012pt\mbox{ for all }r\in{\it dom}(\Sigma),r:A\in\Sigma\\
\hline\cr\Sigma\vdash h\end{array}~.

(17.3)

We write
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash(M,h):A\
if

\ \Gamma;\Sigma\vdash M:A
and
\Sigma\vdash h
.

Example 17.1

The heap
h_{0}
produced by the
\lambda
-term (
17.2
) above
can be typed in the heap context:

\Sigma=r_{1}:A\rightarrow A,r_{2}:A\rightarrow A
.

Proposition 17.1

The typing system enjoys the following properties:

1.

If
\Gamma,x:A;\Sigma\vdash M:B
is derivable and

x\notin{\sf fv}(M)
then

\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash M:B
is derivable.

2.

If

\Gamma,x:A;\Sigma\vdash M:B
and
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash V:A

are derivable then
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash[V/x]M:B
is derivable.

Exercise 17.2

Prove by induction on the proof height proposition

17.1
.

We now discuss the way typing is preserved by reduction.
Notice that during reduction the domain of definition of the
heap can grow since the operator
ref
may dynamically generate
new references. Hence we also need to extend the heap context.
We write
\Sigma^{\prime}\supseteq\Sigma
if

\Sigma^{\prime}
is an extension of
\Sigma
. We notice
the following weakening property of the heap context.

Proposition 17.2

If
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash M:A
is derivable and

\Sigma^{\prime}\supseteq\Sigma
then
\Gamma;\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash M:A

is derivable.

Exercise 17.3

Prove proposition
17.2
.

Then we can state the following subject reduction property.

Proposition 17.3

If
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash(M,h):A
and
(M,h)\rightarrow(M^{\prime},h^{\prime})

then there is
\Sigma^{\prime}\supseteq\Sigma
such that

\Gamma;\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash(M^{\prime},h^{\prime}):A
.

Exercise 17.4

Prove proposition
17.3
.

Exercise 17.5

Suppose we have ‘abstract types’
R
and
H
and that we can assign
the following types to the heap-manipulating functions, where
A

can be any type:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\it New}:H\rightarrow R~,&{\it Ext}:H\rightarrow R\rightarrow A\rightarrow H~,\\
{\it Read}:H\rightarrow R\rightarrow A~,&{\it Write}:H\rightarrow R\rightarrow A\rightarrow H~.\end{array}

For every propositional type
A
and type context
\Gamma

define a type translation
\underline{A}
and context translation
\underline{\Gamma}
,
and show that the compilation function in Table
17.2

is type preserving in the sense that if
\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash M:A
according
to the rules in Table
17.3
then

\underline{\Gamma}\vdash{\cal C}(M):H\rightarrow H\times\underline{A}
.
On the other hand, find a closed
\lambda
-term with references
M
such that

{\cal C}(M)
is typable but
M
is not.

17.3
Typing anomalies (*)

As suggested by the
\lambda
-term (
17.2
) above,
simply typed
\lambda
-terms with references can produce
circular heaps. In fact it is possible to use
references to define general recursive functions.
First, let us consider a
minimal
example of typable and

looping
computation. Set:

\begin{array}[]{llll}M_{1}&\equiv{\sf ref}\ (\lambda x:1.x)~,&M_{2}&\equiv{\sf let}\ y=M_{1}\ {\sf in}\ y:=(\lambda x:1.(!y)x)\ ;\ (!y)~.\end{array}

Then
\vdash M_{1}:{\sf Ref}\ (1\rightarrow 1)
and
\vdash M_{2}:1\rightarrow 1
and
there is an infinite reduction starting with
M_{2}*
.
We can generalize this idea to define a function
f
of type

A\rightarrow B
which satisfies a recursive equation

f=\lambda x:A.M
where
M
may depend on
f
.
Let
\lambda x:A.N
be any
\lambda
-term of type
A\rightarrow B
.
Then we set:

\begin{array}[]{llll}M_{1}&\equiv{\sf ref}\ (\lambda x:A.N)~,&M_{2}&\equiv{\sf let}\ y=M_{1}\ {\sf in}\ y:=(\lambda x:A.[!y/f]M)\ ;\ (!y)~.\end{array}

Initially,
y
is a reference containing a fake function. Then
we replace the fake function with the real function where
each call to
f
is replaced by
!y
.
Then
y
is a reference which contains a value which refers
to the reference
y
. This circularity allows to simulate
recursion.

Another curious phenomenon arises when we try to mix
references and subtyping. Namely, from
A\leq B
we
cannot

infer
{\sf Ref}\ A\leq{\sf Ref}\ B
(or
{\sf Ref}\ A\leq{\sf Ref}\ B
).
The
{\sf Ref}\
type constructor is neither monotonic nor anti-monotonic
with respect to the subtyping pre-order.
In practice, this means that no proper subtyping is possible on reference types.
To see this, suppose
A\leq B
where for instance:

\begin{array}[]{lll}A=\{\ell_{1}:C,\ell_{2}:C\}&\leq&\{\ell_{1}:C\}=B~.\end{array}

Assume
{\sf Ref}\
is anti-monotonic and
x:{\sf Ref}\ B
then we should
also have
x:{\sf Ref}\ A
and
(!x.\ell_{2})
will produce an error.
On the other hand, if
{\sf Ref}\
is monotonic and
x:{\sf Ref}\ A
then
we should also have
x:{\sf Ref}\ B
and

x:=\{\ell_{1}=V\};\ !x.\ell_{2}
will produce an error.

As a third and final typing anomaly, let us notice that the

polymorphic generalization
(cf. chapter
12
)
of a
reference
may also lead to
errors
.
For instance, consider:

{\sf let}\ x={\sf ref}\ (\lambda x.x)\ {\sf in}\ x:=(\lambda x.x+1);(!x)\ {\sf true}~.

(17.4)

{\it ML}
-like languages avoid these problems by allowing polymorphic
generalization only on
values
. For instance, the programming language

ocaml

accepts
:

{\sf let}\ x=(\lambda x.x)\ {\sf in}\ x*;x\ {\sf true}~,

(17.5)

but
rejects
the dangerous expression (
17.4
) above
as well as the following innocuous one:

{\sf let}\ x=(\lambda y.(\lambda x.x)){\sf 2}\ {\sf in}\ x*;x\ {\sf true}~.

(17.6)

In practice, most programs seem to meet this restriction.

17.4
Summary and references

Heaps can be regarded as an abstraction of computer memory.
We have considered an extension of the
\lambda
-calculus with
operations to extend, read, and modify the heap.
Expressions in this extended
\lambda
-calculus may have side
effects and can be understood as functions that
take a heap and produce a new heap and a value.

References introduce the possibility to define recursive data
structures and functions. This power comes at a price in that
the ideas developed in the purely functional setting cannot
be readily lifted to the
\lambda
-calculus with side effects.
For instance, termination of typable programs fails, no proper subtyping is possible
on reference types, and polymorphic generalization is unsound
(but in practice it can be fixed
[
Wri95
]
).

One may argue that these failures are due to the fact that the usual
type systems neglect side effects completely. To address this issue,
so called
type and effect
systems

[
LG88
]
have been proposed. In these type
systems, references are abstracted into a finite set of
regions

and types become
dependent
on such regions. In particular, an
expression is now expected to produce both an effect and a value (this
is an
abstraction
of the idea mentioned above where an
expression with side effects is expected to produce a heap and a
value). Type and effect system have been applied to the design of
static mechanisms for safe memory deallocation

[
TT97
]
.
It has also been shown that a
stratified
version of
the system can guarantee the strong normalization of the typable

\lambda
-terms

[
Bou10
,
Ama09
]
.

Chapter 18
Objects

The programming paradigms discussed so far are built on the notion
of
function
. Indeed term rewriting and the
\lambda
-calculus
can be regarded as formalisms to define first-order and
higher-order functions, respectively and
imperative programs can also be regarded as functions operating
over the heap.
In this chapter, we discuss the situation for object-oriented
programs. We start with a minimalist object-oriented
language which is type-free and without side-effects.
We then gradually enrich this language
with
side-effects
and
types
to obtain a language which
corresponds to a (tiny) fragment of the
{\it Java}
programming language
(of which the reader is supposed to have a superficial knowledge).
We refer to this language as untyped/typed
{\sf J}
.
Along the way, we discuss the compilation of untyped
{\sf J}

to an extension of the
\lambda
-calculus with records, recursion,
and, possibly, references.
Thus objects can also be understood as functions. However,
typed
{\sf J}
differs from
the typed
\lambda
-calculi we have been considering in that it requires some degree
of type-checking at run time,
i.e.
, type errors at run-time
are possible.

18.1
An object-oriented language

In first approximation, an object is a
record

(cf. chapter
16
) whose labels
are traditionally partitioned into
fields

and
methods
. Usually, fields are mapped to (basic) values
describing the internal state of the object while methods are
mapped to functions that allow to manipulate this state.
As in records, the ‘dot-notation’ is used to access
fields and methods,
e.g.
, if
o
is an object and
f

a field then
o.f
is the value associated with the field
f

in the object
o
.

In object-oriented languages such as
{\it Java}
, the creation
of objects follows certain
patterns
known as
class declarations
.
So objects are
classified
according to the class declaration that
is used at the moment of their creation. Class declarations are designed
so that fields and methods are suitably initialized when the object
is created.
Unlike in the
\lambda
-calculus with
records

of chapter
16
,
recursion
is built into
object-oriented languages. First, class declarations may be mutually
recursive, and second there is a special variable
this
(
self
is also
used sometimes) which allows to refer to the object itself within, say,
the body of one of its methods. For instance,
an object
o
may consist of a field
val
which is mapped to an integer
and a method
inc
which is mapped to the function:

\lambda x.{\sf this}.{\sf val}={\sf this}.{\sf val}+x~.

Then the effect of invoking
o.{\sf inc}
on a value
v
is that of increasing by
v
the
value contained in the
val
field of the object
o
.

Class declarations

We reserve
C,C^{\prime},D,\ldots
for class names. Each class name corresponds
to a distinct class declaration. Usually, class declarations are built
incrementally. At the very beginning, there is a class

Object
without fields and methods. Then whenever we introduce
a new class declaration we say that it
extends
another class declaration.
For instance, one can declare a class

C
which extends the class
D
and includes
a field
f
and a method
m
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\sf class\ C\ {\sf extends}\ D=\{}&\mbox{(class declaration)}\\
{\sf\cdots C^{\prime}\ f\ \cdots}&\mbox{(field declarations)}\\
{\sf\cdots D^{\prime}\ m\ (D_{1}\ x_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\ x_{n})\{\ e\ \}\cdots\}}&\mbox{(method declarations).}\end{array}

We are using here a notation based on
{\it Java}
