and
local
, respectively, for reasons that we explain next.
Let us write
{x^{*}}
for the list of distinct variables in
{\sf fv}(P)
and
let us denote with
x^{*}_{r}
and
x^{*}_{l}
the corresponding lists of
fresh variables.
Rather than running
P
atomically we run
non-atomically
a
modified process
P^{\prime}=[x^{*}_{l}/{x^{*}}]P
where each
read/write operation to the variables
{x^{*}}
is replaced by a reference
to the fresh local variables
x^{*}_{l}
which are initialized with the
values of
{x^{*}}
.
Before running
P
, we also save the initial value of the variables
{x^{*}}

in the fresh local variables
x^{*}_{r}
.
If and when we are done with the execution of
P^{\prime}
we check
atomically

that the current value of
{x^{*}}
equals that of
x^{*}_{r}
. If
this is the case, in the same atomic step
we write
x^{*}_{l}
in
{x^{*}}
and we conclude
successfully the transaction, otherwise we try again. Notice that it may happen
that the variables
{x^{*}}
are modified during the computation above.
All that matters is that the value of
{x^{*}}
is the same as the value of

x^{*}_{r}
just before writing in
{x^{*}}
the variables
x^{*}_{l}
.
In particular, they can be modified while initializing the variables

x^{*}_{r}
.

The transformation can be described formally by a function
{\cal C}_{o}

(
o
for optimistic) on
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
processes. The key case concerns the

{\sf await}
and it is defined as follows assuming
{\sf fv}({\sf await~}b{\sf~do~}P)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
,

{x^{*}}=x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, and using vectorial notations such as
{x^{*}}:={v^{*}}

and
x^{*}_{r}={x^{*}}
as an abbreviation for
x_{1}:=v_{1};\cdots;x_{n}:=v_{n}

and
x_{r,1}=x_{1}\and\cdots\and x_{r,n}=x_{n}
, respectively.
We also assume that the variables
c,x^{*}_{r},x^{*}_{l}
do not appear free in
{\sf await~}b{\sf~do~}P
and that

P
does not contain
{\sf await}
statements.

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\cal C}_{{\it o}}({\sf await~}b{\sf~do~}P)&=&{\sf var}\ c=1,\ x^{*}_{r}={0^{*}},\ x^{*}_{l}={0^{*}}\\
&&{\sf while}\ c=1\ {\sf do}\\
&&(\ x^{*}_{r}:={x^{*}};\\
&&\ \ x^{*}_{l}:=x^{*}_{r};\\
&&\ \ {\sf if}\ [x^{*}_{l}/{x^{*}}]b\ {\sf then}\\
&&\ \qquad(\ [x^{*}_{l}/{x^{*}}]P;\\
&&\ \qquad\ \ {\sf atomic}({\sf if}\ x^{*}_{r}={x^{*}}\ {\sf then}\ {x^{*}}:=x^{*}_{l};c:=0;)\ )\ )~.\end{array}

(21.1)

There are a number of possible variations on this schema.
For instance, one can distinguish the variables which are
read from those that are written.
In another direction, instead of computing an over-approximation of
the collection of variables which are affected by the atomic statement,
we could determine this set at
run time
.
Also, it should be noticed that in the translation (
21.1
)
above
the computation of the process

[x^{*}_{l}/{x^{*}}]P
may operate on unexpected states which
in more complex programming settings may lead to exceptions
or diverging computations. Certain implementations of atomic transactions
ensure that the program always operates over consistent states,

i.e.
, states which could actually arise in the reference semantics.
The following exercise elaborates on this point.

Exercise 21.1

Suppose the
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
language is extended with a command

abort
which stops the computation and returns the current
state (such command was discussed in exercise
14.3
).
Extend the optimistic compilation function so that it handles

abort
commands.

21.2
A pessimistic strategy

A more pessimistic (or conservative) implementation strategy for
atomic transactions consists in gaining control of all the resources
relevant to the atomic process before running it.
For instance, suppose we associate a
lock variable

\ell_{x}

with every (shared) variable
x
. Recall that a
lock variable
is simply
a variable that is supposed to be used as a semaphore of capacity
1

(see example
19.1
).

As in the optimistic strategy, given a process
P
we can statically determine
an over-approximation of the variables the process
P
may read or write during its execution.
Let us denote these variables with
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
Then an implementation of
{\sf atomic}(P)
consists in a process that
acquires the locks for
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, then runs
P
, and eventually
releases the locks for
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. Such an implementation scheme
is known as
two phase locking
: the first phase is the one where
the process acquires the locks and the second the one where it releases them.
This locking scheme can be refined by distinguishing between reading
and writing accesses. Indeed a
write
access must be exclusive but a
read

access can be shared by an arbitrary number of processes.
The function
{\cal C}_{p}
formalizes this pessimistic transformation on
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
processes.
The key cases concern the variable declaration and the atomic statement:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal C}_{{\it p}}({\sf var}\ x=v\ {\sf in}\ P)&={\sf var}\ x=v,\ell_{x}=1\ {\sf in}\ {\cal C}_{{\it p}}(P)\\
{\cal C}_{{\it p}}({\sf atomic}(P))&={\it lock}(\ell_{x_{1}});\cdots;{\it lock}(\ell_{x_{n}});P;{\it unlock}(\ell_{x_{1}});\cdots;{\it unlock}(\ell_{x_{n}})~.\\
\end{array}

It should be noticed that parallel processes running a two phase
locking protocol may end up in a
deadlock
.
For instance, suppose
P_{1}
tries to acquire the locks
for
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
while
P_{2}
tries to acquire the locks for
x_{2}

and
x_{1}
. We can arrive at a deadlocked configuration where
P_{1}
has
acquired the lock for
x_{1}
and
P_{2}
the lock for
x_{2}
.
In general, one can represent a deadlock associated with locks as
a circular waiting situation where all parallel processes which are not
properly terminated are waiting to acquire a lock which is currently held
by another process.

An approach to
deadlock resolution
consists in introducing a
monitor process
that at appropriate times detects circular waiting
and breaks the circle by
aborting
one of the processes. This
means that the selected process must release all the acquired locks
and start again.

Rather than taking action after the deadlock has happened,
another approach consists in
preventing
it.
One basic approach that works if the locks can be
totally ordered

consists in acquiring the locks in growing order.
A more general approach not requiring a total order consists in introducing
an information on the
age
of the atomic transactions.
For instance, the so called
wait-die
scheme works as follows.
If an older transaction tries to acquire a lock held by a younger
transaction then it waits the lock is released, while if a
younger transaction tries to acquire a lock held by an older one
then it must release all the acquired locks and start again
(while keeping its age).

Exercise 21.2

Suppose the age of a transaction is a positive natural number.
Write pseudo-code for an
acquire
function that
takes as input a list of locks and an age and tries
to acquire the locks following the wait-die strategy sketched above.

21.3
A formal analysis of the optimistic strategy (*)

We conclude this chapter by sketching a formal analysis of the optimistic
strategy. With reference to the trace-environment interpretation
defined in section
20.3
, one would like to show that for any

{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
process
P
, we have
[\![P]\!]^{TE}=[\![{\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P)]\!]^{TE}
.
We shall approach this problem through the notion of

simulation
which we have already met in chapter
9
.
Recall that
\downarrow
is a predicate on programs/processes that defines immediate termination and
\rightarrow
is a binary relation that defines the
small-step reduction of
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
.
As usual, we denote with
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}
the
reflexive and transitive closure of
\rightarrow
.

Definition 21.1

A binary relation
{\cal R}
on
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
processes is a weak simulation if whenever
P\;{\cal R}\;Q
the following holds for any
state
s
:

•

if
P\downarrow
then
\exists\,Q^{\prime}\;\>(\ (Q,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q^{\prime},s)
and
Q^{\prime}\downarrow\ )
.

•

if
(P,s)\rightarrow(P^{\prime},s^{\prime})
then
\exists\,Q^{\prime}\;\>(\ (Q,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mbox{ and }P^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;Q^{\prime}\ )
.

We denote with
\leq
the union of all weak simulations. The reader
may check that this is again a weak simulation. Also we notice the following
properties.

Proposition 21.1

Let
P,Q
be
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
processes. Then:

(1)\;
If
P\leq Q
then
[\![P]\!]^{TE}\subseteq[\![Q]\!]^{TE}
.

(2)\;
The reverse implication does not hold.

(3)\;
If
P\leq Q
then for any process
R
,

(P\mid R)\leq(Q\mid R)
.

(4)\;
If
P\leq Q
then for any variable
x
and
integer value
n
,
{\sf var}\ x=n\ P\leq{\sf var}\ x=n\ Q
.

Proof
.
(1)\;

Suppose
P\leq Q
and
\alpha\in[\![P]\!]^{TE}
.
We proceed by induction on the length of the trace
\alpha
to show that

\alpha\in Q
.

\alpha=(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})

This means
(P,s_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(P_{2},s^{\prime}_{1})
and
P_{2}\downarrow
.
Then by repeatedly applying the second condition defining a simulation we have:

(Q,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q_{2},s^{\prime}_{1})\mbox{ and }P_{2}\leq Q_{2}~.

Also by the first condition
(Q_{2},s^{\prime}_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q^{\prime}_{2},s^{\prime}_{1})
and
Q^{\prime}_{2}\downarrow
.
Thus:

(Q,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q^{\prime}_{2},s^{\prime}_{1})\downarrow

which means
\alpha\in[\![Q]\!]^{TE}
.

\alpha=(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\neq\epsilon

This means
(P,s_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(P_{2},s^{\prime}_{1})
and
\alpha^{\prime}\in[\![P_{2}]\!]^{TE}
.
Then:

(Q,s_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q_{2},s^{\prime}_{1})\mbox{ and }P_{2}\leq Q_{2}~.

By inductive hypothesis,
\alpha^{\prime}\in[\![Q_{2}]\!]^{TE}
.
It follows
\alpha\in[\![Q]\!]^{TE}
.

(2)\;

Recall that a non-deterministic sum can be defined in
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}

(example
19.2
). Then we consider:

\begin{array}[]{lll}P&\equiv&(a=0)\rightarrow a:=1;[(b=0)\rightarrow b:=1+(c=0)\rightarrow c:=1]\\
Q&\equiv&[(a=0)\rightarrow a:=1;[(b=0)\rightarrow b:=1]\ +\ (a=0)\rightarrow a:=1;[(c=0)\rightarrow c:=1]]~.\end{array}

It is intended that only the first assignment after the test-for-zero is executed atomically.
Thus for instance:

(P,[0/a]s)\rightarrow\rightarrow([(b=0)\rightarrow b:=1+(c=0)\rightarrow c:=1],\ [1/a]s)

Moreover notice that:

([(b=0)\rightarrow b:=1+(c=0)\rightarrow c:=1],\ [0/b,0/c]s)\rightarrow(P^{\prime},[1/b,0/c]s),(P^{\prime\prime},[0/b,1/c]s)~,

where
P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}\downarrow
. On the other hand,
Q
cannot simulate the
first step of
P
. If it takes the first branch it cannot modify
c

and if it takes the second it cannot modify
b
.
Another possibility is to notice that in the trace-environment
semantics all looping processes are interpreted as the empty set while
the weak simulation semantics may distinguish two looping processes
such as
P={\sf while}\ {\sf true}\ {\sf do}\ x:=1
and
Q={\sf while}\ {\sf true}\ {\sf do}\ {\sf skip}
. Indeed, we have
P\not\leq Q
: the
move
(P,[0/x]s)\rightarrow(P,[1/x]s)
cannot be matched by
Q
.

(3)\;

We show that the following relation
{\cal R}
is a weak simulation:

{\cal R}=\leq\cup\{(P\mid R,Q\mid R)\mid P\leq Q,R\mbox{ process}\}~.

Suppose
P\leq Q
and
(P\mid R,s)\rightarrow(P^{\prime}\mid R^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.
We analyze the two possible cases:

(P,s)\rightarrow(P^{\prime},s^{\prime}),R^{\prime}=R

Then
(Q,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q^{\prime},s^{\prime})
and
P^{\prime}\leq Q^{\prime}
.
So
((Q\mid R),s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}((Q^{\prime}\mid R),s^{\prime})
and
(P^{\prime}\mid R)\;{\cal R}\;(Q^{\prime}\mid R)
.

(R,s)\rightarrow(R^{\prime},s^{\prime}),P^{\prime}=P

Then
((Q\mid R),s)\rightarrow((Q\mid R^{\prime}),s^{\prime})
and
(P\mid R^{\prime})\;{\cal R}\;(Q\mid R^{\prime})
.

(4)\;

We show that the following relation
{\cal R}
is a weak simulation:

{\cal R}=\leq\cup\{({\sf var}\ x=n\ P,{\sf var}\ x=n\ Q)\mid P\leq Q,n\in{\bf Z}\}~.

Suppose
P\leq Q
and

({\sf var}\ x=n\ P,s)\rightarrow({\sf var}\ x=m\ P^{\prime},s^{\prime}[s(x)/x])
because

(P,s[n/x])\rightarrow(P^{\prime},s^{\prime}[m/x])
.
Then
(Q,s[n/x])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q^{\prime},s^{\prime}[m/x])
and
P^{\prime}\leq Q^{\prime}
.
Thus
({\sf var}\ x=n\ Q,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}({\sf var}\ x=m\ Q^{\prime},s^{\prime}[s(x)/x])
and

({\sf var}\ x=m\ P^{\prime})\;{\cal R}\;({\sf var}\ x=m\ Q^{\prime})
.
{\Box}

Exercise 21.3

Show that if
P_{i}\leq Q_{i}
for
i=1,2
then
P_{1};P_{2}\leq Q_{1};Q_{2}
.

Proof techniques for simulation (and bisimulation) are
developed in the more abstract setting of labelled transition systems
in chapter
23
. For the time being, we recall
from chapter
9

that to show that
P\leq Q
it suffices to exhibit
a relation
{\cal R}
which contains the pair
(P,Q)
and which is
a weak simulation. As an application of this technique,
let us show the following.

Proposition 21.2

Let
P
be a
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
process then
P\leq{\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P)
.

Proof
. We consider the relation:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal R}=&\{(P,{\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P))\mid P\ {\sf Imp}_{\parallel}\ \mbox{ process}\}\cup\{(P,Q)\mid P\downarrow,Q\downarrow\}~.\end{array}

We have to check that whenever
(P,Q)\in{\cal R}
then the two conditions
specified in the definition
21.1
above hold.
For the first condition, we check that if
P\downarrow
then
{\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P)\downarrow

by induction on the definition of immediate termination.
For the second condition, we proceed by induction on the reduction

(P,s)\rightarrow(P^{\prime},s^{\prime})
according to the rules specified in table
19.2
of chapter
19
.
The only interesting case is when
P\equiv{\sf await~}b{\sf~do~}P_{1}
,
(b,s)\Downarrow{\sf true}
and
(P_{1},s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(P^{\prime},s^{\prime})
with
P^{\prime}\downarrow
.
First, we need a lemma that relates the reductions of
(P_{1},s)
to those
of
([x^{*}_{l}/{x^{*}}]P_{1},s[{s(x)^{*}}/x^{*}_{l}])
.
Then one exhibits a sequence of reductions such that

({\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P),s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q,s^{\prime})
and
Q\downarrow
.
Now in general it is not true
that
Q\equiv{\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P^{\prime})
, and this is precisely the reason we enlarged
the definition of
{\cal R}
to include all the pairs of immediately
terminated processes.
{\Box}

It follows from propositions
21.1
and

21.2
that
[\![P]\!]^{TE}\subseteq[\![{\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P)]\!]^{TE}
.
For the sake of simplicity, we discuss the reverse inclusion
in a particular case.

Proposition 21.3

Suppose
P\equiv{\sf await~}b{\sf~do~}P_{1}
is a
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
process and
P_{1}

does not contain parallel composition,
while
and

await
statements. Then
{\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P)\leq P
.

Proof
. By the hypotheses on
P_{1}
, for any state
s
,
the reduction of
(P_{1},s)
is deterministic and terminates.
So there exist unique
P^{\prime}_{1}
and
s^{\prime}
such that

(P_{1},s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(P^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime})
and
P^{\prime}_{1}\downarrow
.

We write
Q\Downarrow
if for all states
s
,
all reductions starting from
(Q,s)

terminate to a configuration
(Q^{\prime},s^{\prime})
such that

Q^{\prime}\downarrow
and
s=s^{\prime}
.

The pair
(P,s)
can either loop on itself if
(b,s)\Downarrow{\sf false}
or
move to
(P^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime})
if
(b,s)\Downarrow{\sf true}
.
On the other hand, we claim that if
({\cal C}_{{\it o}}(P),s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q,s^{\prime\prime})
then
either
Q
has the shape
{\sf var}\ c=1\ Q^{\prime}
and
s=s^{\prime\prime}
or

Q
has the shape
{\sf var}\ c=0\ Q^{\prime}
,
{\sf var}\ c=0\ Q^{\prime}\Downarrow
,
and
s^{\prime}=s^{\prime\prime}
. Then the rough idea is to define a simulation that
relates the processes of the first type to
P
and those
of the second type to
P^{\prime}_{1}
.
{\Box}

Exercise 21.4

Complete the proof of proposition
21.3
.

21.4
Summary and references

Atomicity is a major issue in concurrency theory
starting from early work on the implementation of atomic transactions
in databases

[
Pap79
,
BHG87
,
LMWF94
]
.
Later, related concepts have been developed in the framework of
concurrent programming. In particular, let us mention the notion of

concurrent object
and
linearizability

[
HW90
]
and the related results that
classify the synchronization power of various concurrent objects

[
Her91
]
(see also chapter
31
).
Nowadays, the various strategies to implement atomicity we
have discussed are applied to standard programming languages
(
C++
,
{\it Java}
,
Haskell
,
{\it ML}
,
\ldots
).
In particular, the work on so called hardware/software
transactional memories

[
HM93
,
ST95
]

is mainly concerned with the problem of finding
an efficient implementation of the
atomic
operator.
Amdahl’s law is presented in
[
Amd67
]
.
An early and quite readable description of the optimistic strategy
in the framework of database systems can be found in

[
KR81
]
.

Chapter 22
Rely-guarantee reasoning

We have seen in chapter
20
that
the
semantics
of concurrent processes calls for new techniques.
Not surprisingly, a similar and related phenomenon arises
in the
specification
of concurrent processes.
In chapter
1
, we have introduced the notion
of partial correctness assertion (pca).
Table
1.4
gives the rules to reason on a
sequential fragment of the
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
language.
These rules are
sound
(proposition
1.4
) and can be

inverted
(proposition
1.3
)
thus providing a syntax-directed method to reduce a pca
to an ordinary logical statement.
Is it possible to extend these results to the
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
language?

22.1
Rely-guarantee assertions

We recall and extend some of the notation introduced in section

1.2
to reason on pca.
We associate with a program
P
the input-output
relation on states:

(s,s^{\prime})\in[\![P]\!]^{IO}\ \mbox{if }\ (P,s)\Downarrow s^{\prime}~.

For example, in the case
P
is an assignment
x:=e
, we have:

[\![P]\!]^{IO}=\{(s,s[v/x])\mid(e,s)\Downarrow v\}~,

which turns out to be (the graph of) a total function.

In the assertions, we identify a boolean predicate
b

with the set of states that satisfy it, thus
b
stands for

\{s\mid s\models b\}
.
We denote the set of states with
{\it St}
,
unary relations on
{\it St}
with
A,B,\ldots
and
binary relations on
{\it St}
with
R,G,\ldots
.
To manipulate relations on states,
we use the following notation:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\it Id}&=\{(s,s)\mid s\in{\it St}\}&\mbox{(identity relation)}\\
{\it Top}&=\{(s,s^{\prime})\mid s,s^{\prime}\in{\it St}\}&\mbox{(top relation)}\\
A;R&=\{s^{\prime}\mid\exists\,s\;\>s\in A\mbox{ and }(s,s^{\prime})\in R\}&\mbox{(image)}\\
R;A&=\{s\mid\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s^{\prime}\in A\mbox{ and }(s,s^{\prime})\in R\}&\mbox{(pre-image)}\\
R;R^{\prime}&=\{(s,s^{\prime\prime})\mid\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>(s,s^{\prime})\in R\mbox{ and }(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})\in R^{\prime}\}&\mbox{(composition).}\end{array}

As usual, if
R
is a relation then
R^{*}
is its reflexive and transitive closure.

As mentioned above, the generation
of the logical conditions follows the structure of the program
(proposition
1.3
).
One would like to follow this pattern for the concurrent programs
of the
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
language too.
So let us focus on parallel composition, which is the core of the matter,
and let us try to formulate a rule of the shape:

\begin{array}[]{c}\{A_{i}\}\ P_{i}\ \{B_{i}\}\quad i=1,2\\
\hline\cr\{f(A_{1},A_{2})\}\ P_{1}\mid P_{2}\ \{g(B_{1},B_{2})\}\end{array}~,

where
f
and
g
are two ways of combining predicates.
Take:

P_{1}\equiv x:=1;x:=x+1
and
P_{2}\equiv x:=2
.
We already know from example
20.1

that:

[\![P_{1}]\!]^{pca}=[\![P_{2}]\!]^{pca}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad[\![P_{1}\mid P_{2}]\!]^{pca}\neq[\![P_{2}\mid P_{2}]\!]^{pca}~.

In particular, this means that any derivation that would
end with a proof of the shape:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\{A_{1}\}\ P_{2}\ \{B_{1}\}\quad\{A_{2}\}\ P_{2}\ \{B_{2}\}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\{{\sf true}\}\ P_{2}\mid P_{2}\ \{x\leq 2\}\end{array}~,

where

\models g(B_{1},B_{2})\subseteq\{s\mid s(x)\leq 2\}
could be turned
into a derivation of the triple

\{{\sf true}\}\ P_{1}\mid P_{2}\ \{x\leq 2\}

which is obviously
not
valid!
An early approach to this problem goes back to Owicki and Gries.
Their rule has the shape:

\begin{array}[]{c}\{A_{1}\}\ P_{1}\ \{B_{1}\}\quad\{A_{2}\}\ P_{2}\ \{B_{2}\}\\
\hline\cr\{A_{1}\cap A_{2}\}\ P_{1}\mid P_{2}\ \{B_{1}\cap B_{2}\}\end{array}

(22.1)

provided the proofs of the premises do not ‘interfere’.
Having to look at the internal structure of processes is not
very satisfying and it is clearly at odd with one basic principle
of module composition:
to compose proofs (modules) one should just know
what is proved (the interface) without
depending on the details of the proof (the implementation).
A way to tackle these limitations
is to consider a richer specification
language whose judgments have the shape:

{\@index{\@indexphrase{rely-guarantee assertion}}}P:(A,R,G,B)

(22.2)

where:
(1)
A
and
B
are a pre-condition and a post-condition, respectively
as in Floyd-Hoare rules, hence sets of states,
(2)
R
is a
relation on states
that describes the
environment transitions that are admitted (thus
R
is part of the
pre-conditions), and
(3)
G
is
relation on states
that describes
the program transitions that are guaranteed (thus
G
is part of the
post-condition).
We refer to assertions of the shape (
22.2
) as

rely-guarantee assertions
, or rga for short.
To define their validity, we recall
from chapter
20
that

{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
programs may perform the following
labelled transitions
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}(P,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}(P^{\prime},s^{\prime})&\mbox{(program transition, cf. Table \ref{parimp-smallstep})}\\
(P,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e}}{{\rightarrow}}(P,s^{\prime})&\mbox{(environment transition, cf. rule (\ref{env-step-rule})).}\end{array}

Definition 22.1
(computation)

A computation of a program
P_{0}
is a (finite or infinite) sequence:

(P_{0},s_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}(P_{1},s_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}(P_{2},s_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots

(22.3)

where
s_{i}
are states and
\lambda_{i}\in\{p,e\}
.

Definition 22.2
(validity rely-guarantee)

The rely-guarantee assertion
P:(A,R,G,B)
is valid if for all
computations of
P=P_{0}
of the shape (
22.3
)
such that the following pre-condition holds:

s_{0}\in A\mbox{ and }\forall\,i\;\>(\lambda_{i}=e\mbox{ implies }(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in R)~,

it follows that the following post-condition holds:

\forall\,i\;\>(P_{i}\downarrow\mbox{ implies }s_{i}\in B)\mbox{ and }(\lambda_{i}=p\mbox{ implies }(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in G)~.

Thus the pre-condition concerns the initial configuration and all
transitions performed by the environment (including those after termination)
while the post-condition concerns the final configurations (if any) and all the transitions
performed by the program.

Exercise 22.1

The validity of a given pca is equivalent to the validity of a derived
rga. Specifically, show that the pca
\{A\}\ P\ \{B\}
is valid iff the rga

P:(A,{\it Id},{\it Top},B)
is valid.

Remark 22.1

Rga’s can discriminate programs which are trace-environment equivalent.
For instance, consider the programs
{\sf loop}\equiv{\sf while~}{\sf true}{\sf~do~}{\sf skip}
and

P\equiv x:=0;{\sf loop}
. We know that

[\![{\sf loop}]\!]^{TE}=[\![P]\!]^{TE}
, since all the diverging computations
receive the empty interpretation.
On the other hand, the rga
({\sf true},{\it Id},{\it Id},{\sf false})
is satisfied by

{\sf loop}
but not by
P
because the assignment
x:=0
does not
respect the guarantee condition
{\it Id}
.

Definition 22.3
(stability)

Let
A
be a unary relation and
R
a binary relation on some set.
Then we write
{{\cal S}}(A,R)
if
s\in A
and
(s,s^{\prime})\in R
implies

s^{\prime}\in A
and say that
A
is stable with respect to
R
.

Exercise 22.2

Let
A,B
be unary relations and
R
be a binary relation. Show that:

1.

{{\cal S}}(A,R)
iff
A;R^{*}\subseteq A
.

2.

A;R^{*}\subseteq B
iff
\ \exists\,A^{\prime}\;\>(A\subseteq A^{\prime},{{\cal S}}(A^{\prime},R),A^{\prime}\subseteq B)
.

Table
22.1

provides a collection of rules to derive
valid rely-guarantee assertions, with the proviso that
the guarantee relation
G
in the conclusion of the rules
contains the identity relation
{\it Id}
. Without this hypothesis,
the rules are unsound. For instance, one can derive

{\sf skip};{\sf skip}:({\sf true},{\it Top},\emptyset,{\sf true})
from

{\sf skip}:({\sf true},{\it Top},\emptyset,{\sf true})
.
Also notice that we do not include a rule for the local variables.
This can be done at the price of some technicalities which are not
essential for the following discussion.

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}A\subseteq A^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012ptR\subseteq R^{\prime}\\
B^{\prime}\subseteq B\hskip 8.50012ptG^{\prime}\subseteq G\\
P:(A^{\prime},R^{\prime},G^{\prime},B^{\prime})\end{array}\\
\hline\cr P:(A,R,G,B)\end{array}\par\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}P:(A,R,G,C)\\
Q:(C,R,G,B)\end{array}\\
\hline\cr P;Q:(A,R,G,B)\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}P:(A\cap b,R,G,B)\\
Q:(A\cap\neg b,R,G,B)\end{array}\\
\hline\cr{\sf if~}b{\sf~then~}P{\sf~else~}Q:(A,R,G,B)\end{array}\par\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}A\subseteq B\hskip 8.50012pt{{\cal S}}(A,R)\\
\hline\cr{\sf skip}:(A,R,G,B)\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{{\cal S}}(A,R)\hskip 8.50012pt{{\cal S}}(B,R)\\
A;[\![x:=e]\!]^{IO}\subseteq B\\
[\![x:=e]\!]^{IO}\subseteq G\end{array}\\
\hline\cr x:=e:(A,R,G,B)\end{array}\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{{\cal S}}(A,R)\hskip 8.50012pt{{\cal S}}(B,R)\\
(A\cap\neg b)\subseteq B\\
P:(A\cap b,R,G,A)\end{array}\\
\hline\cr{\sf while~}b{\sf~do~}P:(A,R,G,B)\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{{\cal S}}(A,R)\hskip 8.50012pt{{\cal S}}(B,R)\\
b;[\![P]\!]^{IO}\subseteq G\\
P:(A\cap b,{\it Id},{\it Top},B)\end{array}\\
\hline\cr{\sf await~}b{\sf~do~}P:(A,R,G,B)\end{array}\par\par\par&\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}(R\cup G_{1})\subseteq R_{2}\hskip 8.50012pt(R\cup G_{2})\subseteq R_{1}\\
(G_{1}\cup G_{2})\subseteq G\hskip 8.50012pt(B_{1}\cap B_{2})\subseteq B\\
P:(A,R_{1},G_{1},B_{1})\\
Q:(A,R_{2},G_{2},B_{2})\end{array}\\
\hline\cr P\mid Q:(A,R,G,B)\end{array}~.\par\end{array}

Table 22.1:
Rely-guarantee rules for
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
(assume
G\supseteq{\it Id}
)

Proposition 22.1
(soundness rga rules)

The rely-guarantee assertions derivable in the system in Table
22.1
are valid.

Proof
. We present the argument for the rule for parallel composition.
A computation of the shape:

(P\mid Q,s_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}(P_{1}\mid Q_{1},s_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots

can be turned into a computation of
P
where the moves played
by
Q
are actually attributed to the environment:

(P,s_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda^{\prime}_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}(P_{1},s_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots

(22.4)

with
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=e
if
\lambda_{i}=e
or
\lambda_{i}=p
and the
i^{{\it th}}
move
is due to
Q
, and
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=p
otherwise.
By a symmetric argument, we derive a computation for
Q
too:

(Q,s_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}(Q_{1},s_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots

(22.5)

We claim that:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=p&\mbox{implies}&(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in G_{1}\\
\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{i}=p&\mbox{implies}&(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in G_{2}~.\end{array}

We reason by contradiction and take
j
to be the least natural number
where the property above fails. For instance, suppose:

\lambda^{\prime}_{j}=p
and
(s_{j},s_{j+1})\notin G_{1}
.
Now for a transition
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
with
i<j
we have
3
cases:
either
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=p
and
(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in G_{1}
,
or
\lambda_{i}=e
,
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=e
and
(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in R
,
or
\lambda_{i}=p
,
\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{i}=p
and
(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in G_{2}

because
i<j
.
In particular, if
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=e
we have that

(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in R\cup G_{2}\subseteq R_{1}
.
Then the following computation of
P
:

(P,s_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda^{\prime}_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots(P_{j},s_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}(P_{j+1},s_{j+1})

contradicts the hypothesis
P:(A,R_{1},G_{1},B)
.
It follows that if
\lambda_{i}=p
then
(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in G_{1}\cup G_{2}\subseteq G
.
Finally, we notice that
(P_{i}\mid Q_{i})\downarrow
entails
P_{i}\downarrow
and

Q_{i}\downarrow
. Thus
s_{i}\in B_{1}\cap B_{2}\subseteq B
.
{\Box}

Exercise 22.3

Prove the pca
\{x=0\}\ x:=1;x:=x+1\mid x:=2\ \{x\in\{2,3\}\}
using the
rely-guarantee system.

Exercise 22.4

Complete the proof of proposition
22.1
.

Unfortunately, the move from pca’s to rga’s is not quite sufficient
to reason about concurrent programs. For instance, it is problematic
to prove simple assertions such as:

\{x=0\}\ x:=x+1\mid x:=x+1\ \{x=2\}~.

(22.6)

The problem is that in the rule for parallel composition we need
to
abstract
the possible state transformations of the parallel processes
into a relation on states. In doing this, we lose information. For instance,
we can record the fact that the variable
x
can be incremented by one,
but we lose the information that this state transition can occur
at most
once
. Further, in slightly more complicated programs such as

x:=x+1;x:=x+2
, one loses information on the
order

of the state transformations too.

A ‘solution’ which goes back to the Owicki-Gries system is to allow for an
instrumentation
of the program. This means enriching the program
with auxiliary variables and assignments which allow to record
(essential parts of) the history of the computation without affecting
it. The ‘auxiliary variable’ rule states that if we can prove a
rely-guarantee assertion of the instrumented program then we can
transfer this property to the original program where the
instrumentation is removed. For instance, with reference to the pca
(
22.6
) above we can prove:

\{x=0,p=0,q=0\}\ {\sf atomic}(x:=x+1;p:=1)\mid{\sf atomic}(x:=x+1;q:=1)\ \{x=2\}

(22.7)

and then derive the desired pca (
22.6
)
by erasing the auxiliary variables
p
and
q
along
with the related assignments
and atomic statements. In practice, one can insert
program counters

in all processes and describe exactly in the assertions
the way the computation progresses.

It turns out that when adding an
auxiliary variable rule
, it is
possible to invert the rules presented in Table
22.1

along the spirit of proposition
1.3
.
However, as for
the non-interference rule (
22.1
), this is not very satisfying
and really points to a weakness of the specification language.
A more
powerful and flexible approach consists in building a full fledged
modal logic that allows to describe the transitions of the program and
the environment.
Examples of such modal logics are discussed later in
chapter
24
in the more abstract framework of labelled
transition systems.

22.2
A coarse grained concurrent garbage collector (*)

We suppose the reader is familiar with the idea that a
program may need to allocate memory at run time and that
such memory should be collected and reused whenever possible
so that the program can carry on its execution within
certain given memory bounds.

In general, it is hard to predict when a
memory block becomes useless to the rest of the computation
and can be collected. One approach to this issue consists
in designing a specific program called
garbage collector

which periodically analyzes the state of the memory and
collects the blocks which are useless. Specifically,
the memory is modelled as a directed graph with a collection
of root nodes which are the entry points of the program to the memory.
All nodes which are not
accessible
from the roots are considered
as
garbage
and can be collected. Thus, at least from a
logical point of view, the activity of the garbage collector can
be decomposed in two phases: a
marking
phase where
the accessible nodes are determined and
a
collecting
phase where the inaccessible nodes
become again available for future usage. In practice,
it is convenient to relax a bit this specification. Namely,
one determines an
over-approximation
of the accessible nodes and
consequently one collects a
subset
of the inaccessible ones.

Going towards a formalization, let us assume a fixed set of
nodes
N
and a fixed subset of roots
{\it Roots}\subseteq N
.
We also use
E\subseteq N\times N
to denote the collection of directed edges
which varies over time. For any given collection of edges
E
,
we have a collection of nodes which are accessible from the roots:

{\it Acc}(E)={\it Roots};E^{*}
.

The activity of the program, henceforth called
Mutator
,
on the memory graph can
be summarized as follows: it selects two accessible nodes
i,j

and redirects an outgoing edge of the first node, say
(i,k)
,
towards
j
. In other terms, the edge
(i,k)
is replaced by the
edge
(i,j)
, where
i,j,k
are not necessarily distinct.
The fundamental property which is
guaranteed by the
Mutator
is that the collection of accessible nodes
can only decrease. Thus if we denote with
E
and
E^{\prime}

the collection of edges before and
after a
Mutator
’s action we have that:

{\it Acc}(E^{\prime})\subseteq{\it Acc}(E)~.

(22.8)

This representation of the
Mutator
seems very simple but it is
actually reasonable provided we assume
that among the root nodes there is: (i) a special node
called
{\sf nil}
without outgoing edges and (ii) a special node
called
{\sf fl}
(free list) that points to the list of free nodes
that can be used to allocate memory.
With these hypotheses, expected operations of the
Mutator

such as setting a pointer to
nil
or redirecting a pointer
towards a newly allocated node, fall within the scope of the model.

The activity of the garbage collector is a bit more complex.
The task of the marking phase is to determine a set
M\subseteq N
which
over-approximates the collection of reachable nodes. A natural way
to approach the task is to compute iteratively the least fixed point
of the function associating to a set of nodes
M
the
set of nodes
{\it Roots}\cup M;E
. This can be expressed
in an imperative programming notation as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\it Marker}\equiv&M:=\emptyset\\
&M_{1}:={\it Roots}\\
&{\sf while}\ M_{1}\not\subseteq M\ {\sf do}\\
&\hskip 17.00024ptM:=(M\cup M_{1})\\
&\hskip 17.00024ptM_{1}:=(M;E)\end{array}

The
Marker
program satisfies the pca:

\{{\sf true}\}\ {\it Marker}\ \{({\it Roots}\subseteq M)\ \mbox{ and }\ (M;E\subseteq M)\}~.

(22.9)

It follows by induction that:
{\it Acc}(E)\subseteq M
.
Once the marking phase is completed, the collecting phase consists in
inserting all the nodes which are not in the set
M
in the

free list
pointed by
{\sf fl}
.

Our goal in the following is to reason on the properties of the

Mutator
and
Marker
when they are run in parallel.
The property we want to check is that once the
Marker

terminates the set
M
is indeed an over-approximation of
the set
{\it Acc}(E)
. In order to express the specification
we introduce an auxiliary variable
{\it done}
which is
initially set to
{\sf false}
and becomes
{\sf true}
when

{\it Marker}
terminates.

Formally, we regard a
memory state

s
of our parallel program
as a function from the variables
E
,
M
,
M_{1}
,
{\it done}

to the appropriate value domains. When defining a relation
R
on memory states
we say that a variable
x
is stable if
(s,s^{\prime})\in R
implies

s(x)=s^{\prime}(x)
. We will also say that
{\it Acc}(E)
decreases if

(s,s^{\prime})\in R
implies
{\it Acc}(s(E))\supseteq{\it Acc}(s^{\prime}(E))
.
We define:

{\it Marker^{\prime}}\equiv({\it done}:={\sf false};\ {\it Marker};\ {\it done}:={\sf true})~.

Then we want to show that:

{\it Mutator}\mid{\it Marker^{\prime}}:(\emptyset,\emptyset,G,{\sf true})

(22.10)

where
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
and:

\begin{array}[]{ll}G_{1}&=\{(s,s^{\prime})\mid M,M_{1},{\it done}\ \mbox{stable},{\it Acc}(E)\ \mbox{decreasing}\}\\
G_{2}&=\{(s,s^{\prime})\mid E\ \mbox{stable},\\
&\qquad s({\it done})={\sf true}\mbox{ implies }({\it Acc}(s(E))\subseteq s(M),\ {\it M},{\it M_{1}},{\it done}\ \mbox{stable})\}~.\end{array}

By the rule for parallel composition in Table
22.1
,
the assertion (
22.10
) is reduced to:

\begin{array}[]{ll}(1)&{\it Mutator}:(\emptyset,R_{1},G_{1},{\sf true})\\
(2)&{\it Marker^{\prime}}:(\emptyset,R_{2},G_{2},{\sf true})\\
(3)&R_{1}=\{(s,s^{\prime})\mid E\mbox{ stable}\}\\
(4)&R_{2}=G_{1}~.\par\end{array}

The related inclusions are easily checked.

Having outlined a formal analysis of the garbage collector, let us reconsider our
description of the
Marker
to notice that we are assuming
that the computation of the set
M;E
of nodes reachable in one step from
the set
M
is performed
atomically
. This is a potentially
long operation and one would like to split it in smaller pieces.
The difficulty that arises in this case is that while visiting
the nodes in
M
the set
E
may be modified by the
Mutator

in a non-monotonic way. For instance,
we can have
{\it Roots}=\{i,j\}
and the collection of edges
oscillating between
E_{1}=\{(i,k)\}
and
E_{2}=\{(j,k)\}
.
If the
Marker
visits
i
(
j
) while the collection of edges is

E_{2}
(
E_{1}
, respectively) then it will never notice that the
node
k
is accessible! For this reason, it is usually
assumed that in finer grained concurrent garbage collectors
the mutator must
help
the marker. At our abstract level, that
could mean that the mutator is also allowed to add elements to the
set
M
.

22.3
Summary and references

Rely-guarantee assertions to reason about concurrent programs are
based on both unary and binary relations on states. The latter
describe the transitions of the environment we can rely upon and the
transitions of the program that are guaranteed.
Owicki-Gries system is presented in

[
OG76
]
.
Rely-guarantee
assertions for reasoning about
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
programs were put forward in

[
Jon83
]
and then developed by
Stirling
[
Sti88
]
.
The presentation above is close
to
[
Nie03
]
which in turn is based on

[
XdRH97
]
.

The compact modeling of the garbage collector is introduced in

[
DLM+78
]
.
More refined solutions to the concurrent garbage collection problem
are described and analyzed,
e.g.
, in

[
DLM+78
,
Gri77
,
BA84
,
vdS87
]
.
The number of proof obligations to be checked for fine grained
garbage collectors becomes quickly overwhelming and
a machine-assisted proof is instrumental to
raise the confidence in the proposed solution. Examples of
such developments can be found in

[
NE00
,
DG94
]
.
Also it seems useful to cast the problem of concurrent garbage collection
in the more general framework of lock-free concurrent data
structures
[
HM92
]

which is discussed in chapter
31
.

Chapter 23
Labelled transition systems and bisimulation

So far we have considered a rather concrete model of concurrent
computation, namely parallel imperative programs with shared
variables. To analyze the design spectrum which is available in the
semantics of concurrency, it is convenient to move to a more abstract
framework where systems perform some set of
actions
.
Such a system is called
labelled transition system

(lts). Concretely, an action could consist in changing the contents of
a shared variable or sending a message.
In this chapter, we formalize the notion of (bi-)simulation over a lts,
consider a way to abstract away internal computation steps
(
weak
(bi-)simulation), and present some proof techniques for
(bi-)simulation.

23.1
Labelled transition systems

A labelled transition system (lts)
can be regarded as an
automaton
where we do not
specify the set of initial and final states.

Definition 23.1
(labelled transition system)

A labelled transition system is a ternary relation
\rightarrow
such that

\ \rightarrow\subseteq S\times{\it Act}\times S\
,

S
is a set of states, and
{\it Act}
is a set of actions.
We also write
s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}
for
(s,\alpha,s^{\prime})\in\rightarrow
.

Example 23.1

In section
19.1
, we have presented a transition system
for the
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
language.
It is possible to regard this system
as a lts by taking the set of states
S
as the collection of
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
processes and

{\it Act}
as the collection of pairs of memory states (not to be confused with
the states of the lts we are defining). Then we would write

(P,(s,s^{\prime}),Q)\in\rightarrow
if
(P,s)\rightarrow(Q,s^{\prime})
according to the
rules in Table
19.2
.

Inspired by definition
20.1
of trace for
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
processes,
we introduce a notion of
trace and trace equivalence on lts.

Definition 23.2
(traces)

We define the set of traces of a state
s
in a lts as:

T(s)=\{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}\mid s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{n}}}{{\rightarrow}}\}~.

We say that two states
s
and
t
are trace equivalent if
T(s)=T(t)
.

There are a few points to be noticed concerning the definition

23.2
above. First, it neglects
termination
since
this notion is not even present in the definition
23.1
of lts
(but a termination predicate on states could be added).
And since termination is neglected, the set of traces is closed
under prefix.
Second, there
is no notion of closure of the traces under reflexivity and
transitivity. This point is treated later in section

23.3
once the notion of
internal action
is
introduced. Third, the
environment
seems to play no role in the
behavior of the lts and the related definition of trace
equivalence. We shall see in chapter
25
that it is possible to
enrich lts with a notion of synchronization and parallel composition
and then prove that the notion of trace equivalence in definition

23.2
is indeed preserved by parallel composition.

23.2
Simulation and bisimulation

In chapter
19
, we have motivated the interest of
accounting for the
branching behavior
of a system
(example
19.6
of the vending machine).
The notion of
(bi-)simulation
is a very popular approach to this issue.
We have already met this notion in chapter
9

in the framework of the
\lambda
-calculus and in chapter

21
in the framework of the
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
concurrent language.
Next, we reconsider this notion in the setting of labelled transition systems.
The proposed definition ignores certain observables such as termination
and deadlock. However,
it is quite possible to enrich the notion of lts with
predicates that represent termination and/or deadlock and then
to formulate a notion of (bi-)simulation which depends on these
predicates.

Definition 23.3
((bi-)simulation)

Let
\rightarrow\subseteq S\times{\it Act}\times S
be a labelled transition system.
A binary relation
{\cal R}
on
S
is a simulation if:

\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t,\quad s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,t^{\prime}\;\>t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime},\quad s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\end{array}~.

(23.1)

Moreover we say that
{\cal R}
is a bisimulation if:

\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t,\quad t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime},\quad s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\end{array}~.

(23.2)

Remark 23.1

In definition
23.3
as well as in the following ones there is an implicit
universal quantification on the states which are not existentially quantified.

Proposition 23.1
(on bisimulation)

The following properties hold for the collection of bisimulations
over a labelled transitions system:

1.

The empty and identity relations are bisimulations.

2.

The collection of bisimulations is closed under
inverse, composition, and arbitrary unions.

3.

There is a greatest bisimulation which is defined
as the union of all bisimulations and that we represent with
\sim

(some authors call it bisimilarity).

4.

Bisimulations are not closed under (finite) intersection.

Proof
. Properties (1-2) follow by a simple unravelling of the definitions.
Property (3) follows by the closure under arbitrary unions.
For property (4), consider the lts
1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}2
,
1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}3
,
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}y
,
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}z
and
the relations
{\cal R}_{1}=\{(1,x),(2,y),(3,z)\}
,
{\cal R}_{2}=\{(1,x),(2,z),(3,y)\}
.
{\Box}

Exercise 23.1
(on simulation)

Show that the properties above
are true of simulations too but for closure under inverse.
Further, denote with

\leq
the greatest simulation. Find a lts with states

s,t
such that
s\leq t
,
t\leq s
, and
s\not\sim t
.

Exercise 23.2
(trace vs. simulation)

For
s,t
states of a lts show that

s\leq t
implies
T(s)\subseteq T(t)
, while the
converse may fail.

Exercise 23.3
(backward simulation)

Let
\rightarrow\subseteq S\times{\it Act}\times S
be a lts.
A binary relation
{\cal R}
on
S
is a backward simulation if:

\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t,\quad s^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s\\
\hline\cr\exists\,t^{\prime}\;\>t^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t,\quad s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\end{array}~.

(23.3)

We write
{\cal R}[s]
for the set
\{t\mid s\;{\cal R}\;t\}
and say that
{\cal R}
is
total if for all
s\in S
,
{\cal R}[s]\neq\emptyset
.
Show that if
{\cal R}
is total then
T(s)\subseteq\bigcup_{t\in{\cal R}[s]}T(t)
.

Remark 23.2
(alternative definition of bisimulation)

Sometimes a bisimulation is defined as a symmetric relation
{\cal R}
such
that:

\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t,\quad s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,t^{\prime}\;\>t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime},\quad s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\end{array}~.

The advantage of this definition is that
one can omit the second condition (
23.2
).
The inconvenience is that by forcing a bisimulation to be
symmetric we make it larger than really needed.
However, notice that given a bisimulation
{\cal R}
one can
always derive a symmetric relation which is a bisimulation by taking
{\cal R}\cup{\cal R}^{-1}
.

Let
\rightarrow\subseteq S\times{\it Act}\times S
be a
labelled
transition system
. Notice that
L=2^{S\times S}
is a
complete lattice
with respect to inclusion (cf. definition

9.4
).
Bisimulation can be characterized as the greatest fixed point of
a certain monotonic function
{\cal F}
on binary relations
which we introduce below.

Definition 23.4
(function
{\cal F}
)

We define
{\cal F}:L\rightarrow L
as:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\cal F}({\cal R})=&\{(s,t)\mid&s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }\exists\,t^{\prime}\;\>t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}\mbox{ and }s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\mbox{ and }\\
&&t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\mbox{ and }s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\}~.\end{array}

We notice the following properties of the function
{\cal F}

(cf. proposition
9.1
).

Proposition 23.2

The following properties hold:

1.

{\cal R}
is a bisimulation iff
{\cal R}\subseteq{\cal F}({\cal R})
.

2.

{\cal F}
is monotonic on
L
.

3.

The greatest bisimulation
\sim
is the greatest fixed point of
{\cal F}
.

Exercise 23.4

Prove proposition
23.2
.

Remark 23.3
(transfinite definition of bisimulation)

The bisimulation
\sim
being the greatest fixed point of the monotonic function
{\cal F}
,
it can be approximated from above as follows (cf. chapter
9
):

\begin{array}[]{lll}\sim_{0}\ =\ S\times S&\sim_{\kappa+1}\ =\ {\cal F}(\sim_{\kappa})~,&\sim_{\kappa}\ =\ \bigcap_{\kappa^{\prime}<\kappa}\sim_{\kappa^{\prime}}\qquad\mbox{($\kappa$ limit ordinal).}\end{array}

Thus to show
s\not\sim t
it suffices to find an ordinal
\kappa
such that
s\not\sim_{\kappa}t
.

Definition 23.5
(image finite lts)

A lts
\rightarrow\subseteq S\times{\it Act}\times S
is image finite if for all
P,\alpha

the set
\{s^{\prime}\mid s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\}
is finite.

For finite (image finite) lts the greatest fixed point is reached
in a finite (countable) number of iterations.

Proposition 23.3
(bisimulation for (image) finite lts)

The following properties hold:

1.

If the support
S
of the lts is finite
then there is a natural number
n
such that the greatest
bisimulation coincides with
\sim_{n}
.

2.

If the lts is image finite
then the greatest bisimulation coincides with
\sim_{\omega}

(a form of co-continuity).

Proof
. For the first property, see exercise
9.5
.
For the second property, suppose
s\sim_{\omega}t
and
s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}
.
Then:

\forall\,n\;\>\exists\,t_{n}\;\>t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t_{n}\mbox{ and }s^{\prime}\sim_{n}t_{n}~.

Since the set
\{t^{\prime}\mid t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}\}
is finite there
must be a
t^{\prime}
in this set such that
\{n\mid t_{n}=t^{\prime}\}
is infinite.
1
1

1

This is a version of the so called
pigeonhole principle
which states
that if infinitely many pigeons are put in finitely many boxes then at least one
box must contain infinitely many pigeons.

Thus there is an infinite sequence
n_{1}<n_{2}<n_{3}<\cdots
such that

s^{\prime}\sim_{n_{j}}t_{n_{j}}=t^{\prime}
. Then for any
n
we can find a
n_{j}\geq n

such that
s^{\prime}\sim_{n_{j}}t^{\prime}
; and this entails
s^{\prime}\sim_{n}t^{\prime}
.
Hence
s^{\prime}\sim_{\omega}t^{\prime}
.
{\Box}

We conclude this section by introducing a notation to denote lts
which will be extended in chapter
25

to a full language of processes known as
{\it CCS}
.
The notation is generated by the following grammar:

P::=0\mid\!\!\mid\alpha.P\mid\!\!\mid P+P~,\qquad\alpha\in{\it Act}~.

(23.4)

Here
0
denotes the empty lts, also called
nil
,

\alpha.P
is the lts denoted by
P

prefixed
by the action
\alpha
, and

P+Q
is the non-deterministic sum of the lts denoted by
P
and
Q
.
In this notation, the states’ identities are immaterial; what matters
of a state is not its name but the actions it can do.
Using this notation, the lts version
of the two vending machines in example
19.6

can be represented as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}a.(b.0+c.0)&\mbox{ and }&a.b.0+a.c.0~.\end{array}

Notice that identifying the two machines amounts to
distribute

the prefix over the non-deterministic sum.
We use the following abbreviations:

b
for
b.0
,

b^{n}
for
b.\ldots.b.0
(
b
prefixed
n
times), and

b^{\omega}
for the infinite lts
b.b.\cdots
.
If
I
is a (possibly infinite) set then
\Sigma_{i\in I}\ P_{i}

denotes the non-deterministic sum of the lts denoted by
P_{i}
.
We apply the notation in the following exercise.

Exercise 23.5
(non-bisimilar lts)

Consider the lts
P_{i},Q_{i}
defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{llllll}P_{0}&=&b.0&Q_{0}&=&c.0\\
P_{i+1}&=&a.(P_{i}+Q_{i})&Q_{i+1}&=&a.P_{i}+a.Q_{i}~.\end{array}

1.

Show that for all
i
natural number:
(i)
P_{i}\sim_{i}P_{i}+Q_{i}\sim_{i}Q_{i}
,
(ii)
P_{i}\not\sim_{i+1}P_{i}+Q_{i}\not\sim_{i+1}Q_{i}\not\sim_{i+1}P_{i}
.

2.

Show that:
(i)
\forall\,i\leq n\;\>b^{n}\sim_{i}b^{\omega}
,
(ii)
\Sigma_{i\geq 0}\ b^{i}+b^{\omega}\sim_{\omega}\Sigma_{i\geq 0}\ b^{i}
,
(iii)
\forall\,i\;\>b^{i}\not\sim_{\omega}b^{\omega}
,
(iv)

\Sigma_{i\geq 0}\ b^{i}+b^{\omega}\not\sim_{\omega+1}\Sigma_{i\geq 0}\ b^{i}
.

23.3
Weak transitions

Certain computation steps should not be directly observable.
For instance, in sequential programs usually one is just
interested in the input-output behavior and not in the
way the output is computed.
To model this situation in lts,
we enrich the collection of actions with a
distinct internal action

\tau
. For instance,
\tau^{\omega}

is a diverging system which never interacts with the environment.
As another example, we could regard a system such as

a.\tau.b.0
equivalent to
a.b.0
.
Though the internal action is not directly observable,
it may make a difference. For instance, consider the lts

a.0+b.0
and
\tau.a.0+\tau.b.0

with the interpretation:

a=\mbox{ `accepts to deliver coffee'}
and

b=\mbox{ `accepts to deliver tea'}
.
The second system decides ‘internally’ whether to deliver
coffee or tea while the first
will take a decision that may be controlled
by the environment.

Given a lts with
\tau
transitions, we derive a related
lts with the same states but where an observable transition
may be preceded and followed by an arbitrary number of internal
transitions (think of
\epsilon
transitions in
automata theory). As usual, if
{\cal R}
is a binary relation then
we denote with
{\cal R}^{*}
its reflexive and transitive closure.

Definition 23.6
(derived weak lts)

Let a lts
\rightarrow\subseteq S\times({\it Act}\cup\{\tau\})\times S

be given where
\tau\notin{\it Act}
is a distinct internal action.
We derive from this lts another weak lts

\Rightarrow\ \subseteq\ S\times({\it Act}\cup\{\tau\})\times S

where:

\begin{array}[]{l}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}\ =\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}})^{*}&\mbox{if }\alpha=\tau\\
(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}})^{*}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}})(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}})^{*}&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

Remark 23.4

When working with weak transitions, lts tend to be image infinite,
and therefore proposition
23.5
cannot be applied.

The notion of bisimulation for lts with internal actions is simply the
standard notion of bisimulation on the derived weak lts (a similar
convention applies to the notion of simulation).

Definition 23.7
(weak bisimulation)

Let
\rightarrow\subseteq S\times({\it Act}\cup\{\tau\})\times S
be
a lts with a distinct internal action
\tau
.
A binary relation
{\cal R}
on
S
is a weak bisimulation if it is a bisimulation with respect
to the weak transition system
\Rightarrow
.
We denote with
\approx
the largest weak bisimulation.

The following definition of weak bisimulation is the one which is used
in practice
.

Definition 23.8
(one step weak bisimulation)

A relation
{\cal R}
is a one step weak bisimulation if:

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t\quad s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,t^{\prime}\;\>t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}t^{\prime},\quad s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\end{array}~,&\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t\quad t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}s^{\prime},\quad s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\end{array}~.\par\end{array}

Proposition 23.4

A relation
{\cal R}
on a lts is a weak bisimulation iff
it is a one step weak bisimulation.

Exercise 23.6

Prove proposition
23.4
by diagram chasing.

Henceforth we just speak of
weak bisimulation
and use
the more convenient definition
23.8
.

Definition 23.9
(weak up to strong)

We say that a relation
{\cal R}
on a lts is a
weak bisimulation up to strong bisimulation if:

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t,\quad s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,t^{\prime}\;\>t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}t^{\prime},\quad s^{\prime}\sim\;{\cal R}\;\sim t^{\prime}\end{array}~,\par&\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t,\quad t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}s^{\prime},\quad s^{\prime}\sim\;{\cal R}\;\sim t^{\prime}\end{array}~.\par\end{array}

Exercise 23.7

Show that if
{\cal R}
is a weak bisimulation up to strong bisimulation
then
{\cal R}\subseteq\approx
.

23.4
Proof techniques for bisimulation (*)

The standard method to prove
s\sim t
is
to
exhibit a relation

{\cal R}
such that
s\;{\cal R}\;t
and

{\cal R}\subseteq{\cal F}({\cal R})
, where
{\cal F}
is as in definition
23.4
.
Exercise
23.7
suggests that it is possible to refine
this proof technique by exhibiting a relation

\;{\cal R}\;
which is a bisimulation
up to a relation
‘with suitable properties’.
In the following, we provide a rather general treatment of what
‘with suitable properties’ means.
First, some preliminary remarks.
Let
(L,\leq)
be a complete lattice and
f:L\rightarrow L
be a
monotonic function on
L
. The function
f
induces a
transitive
relation

<_{f}
which refines
\leq
:

x<_{f}y\mbox{ if }x\leq y\mbox{ and }x\leq f(y)~.

Notice that
<_{f}
is
anti-symmetric
but
not necessarily reflexive
.
In the case we are interested in,
L
is the power-set

2^{S\times S}
,
f
is
{\cal F}
, and

{\cal R}
is a bisimulation iff
{\cal R}<_{{\cal F}}{\cal R}
.

Definition 23.10

We say that a function
h:L\rightarrow L
preserves
<_{f}
if:

x<_{f}y\mbox{ implies }h(x)<_{f}h(y)~.

Exercise 23.8

Show that the set of functions preserving the order
<_{f}

is closed under composition and supremum.

Proposition 23.5
(key property)

Let
(L,\leq)
be a complete lattice,
f:L\rightarrow L
be a monotonic
function (with greatest fixed point
{\it gfp}(f)
), and

h:L\rightarrow L
be a function that preserves
<_{f}
.
Then:

x\leq f(h(x))~~\mbox{ implies }~~x\leq{\it gfp}(f)~.

Proof
. Given
x
, we build a bigger element
y
such that
y\leq f(y)
.
To this end, we define a sequence
x_{0}=x
,
x_{n+1}=x_{n}\vee h(x_{n})
.
Let
y=\bigvee_{n\geq 0}x_{n}
; obviously
x_{n}\leq x_{n+1}
and
x\leq y
.
We show that
x_{n}<_{f}x_{n+1}
, by induction on
n
.

n=0

x_{0}=x\leq f(h(x))\leq f(x\vee h(x))
, since
x\leq f(h(x))

by hypothesis and
f
is monotonic.

n>0

We have to show:

x_{n}=x_{n-1}\vee h(x_{n-1})\leq f(x_{n}\vee h(x_{n}))=f(x_{n+1})~.

Since
f
is monotonic, we have

f(x_{n})\vee f(h(x_{n}))\leq f(x_{n}\vee h(x_{n}))
.
By inductive hypothesis, we know
x_{n-1}\leq f(x_{n})
.
Moreover, since
h
preserves
<_{f}
, we have

h(x_{n-1})\leq f(h(x_{n}))
.

Finally, we remark that
y\leq f(y)
, as:

y=\bigvee_{n\geq 0}x_{n}\leq\bigvee_{n\geq 0}f(x_{n+1})\leq f(\bigvee_{n\geq 1}x_{n})=f(y)~.

Since
y\leq f(y)
implies
y\leq{\it gfp}(f)
, we conclude

x\leq{\it gfp}(f)
.
{\Box}

Exercise 23.9
(when
h
is a closure)

We say that a function
h
on a lattice
L

is a closure if
{\it id}\leq h=h\circ h
.
Show that if
h
is a closure then
y=h(x)
in the previous
construction.

In our
application scenario
, this means that to prove that
s
and
t
are
bisimilar it suffices to find:
(1) a function
{\cal H}
that preserves
<_{{\cal F}}
and
(2) a relation
{\cal R}
such that
(s,t)\in{\cal R}
and

{\cal R}\subseteq{\cal F}({\cal H}({\cal R}))
.

Exercise 23.10

Let
{\cal H}({\cal R})=\sim\circ{\cal R}\circ\sim
.
Check that
{\cal H}
preserves
<_{{\cal F}}
.

We introduce a notion of weak bisimulation up to expansion
which is often used in applications.

Definition 23.11
(expansion)

A binary relation
{\cal R}
on a lts is an expansion if:

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t,\quad s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,t^{\prime}\;\>t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}t^{\prime}\quad s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\end{array}~,\qquad\par\begin{array}[]{c}s\;{\cal R}\;t\quad t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;t^{\prime}\mbox{ and }(s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\mbox{ or }(\alpha=\tau,s^{\prime}=s))\end{array}~.\par\end{array}

We denote with
\preceq
be the largest expansion
and with
\succeq
its inverse. Also we read
s\preceq t
as

s
expands to
t
.

Note that an expansion is a hybrid relation which is
weak
on one
side and
almost strong
on the other side.
The intuition is that the state on the right is a kind of implementation of the
one on the left,
i.e.
, the state on the right may take more
internal steps to perform the ‘same task’.

Exercise 23.11
(weak bisimulation up to expansion)

Define:

{\cal H}({\cal R})\ =\ \succeq\circ{\cal R}\circ\preceq
.
Let
{\cal F}
be the monotonic function induced by the definition of
(one step) weak bisimulation.
Show that:

1.

s\sim t
implies strictly
s\preceq t
(and
s\succeq t
).

2.

\preceq\cup\succeq
implies strictly
\approx
.

3.

{\cal H}
preserves
<_{{\cal F}}
. And
explicit the condition that needs to be checked to
ensure that a relation
{\cal R}
is a weak bisimulation up to expansion.

The following exercise highlights two possible pitfalls in the
usage of up-to techniques.

Exercise 23.12
(pitfalls)

Define:

{\cal H^{\prime}}({\cal R})\ =\ \approx\circ{\cal R}\circ\approx
and

{\cal H^{\prime\prime}}({\cal R})\ =\ \preceq\circ{\cal R}\circ\succeq
.
As in the previous exercise, let

{\cal F}
be the monotonic function induced by the definition of
(one step) weak bisimulation. Show that:

1.

{\cal H^{\prime}}
does not preserve
<_{{\cal F}}
.
Suggestion: consider

{\cal R}=\{(\tau.a,0)\}
and check that

{\cal R}\subseteq{\cal F}({\cal H^{\prime}}({\cal R}))
while obviously

{\cal R}\not\subseteq\approx
.

2.

{\cal H^{\prime\prime}}
does not preserve
<_{{\cal F}}
,
by an argument similar to the one used for
{\cal H^{\prime}}
.

23.5
Summary and references

The notion of
labelled transition system
provides an abstract
setting to explore the variety of possible semantics of concurrent
systems. In particular, we have developed the notion of
bisimulation
which corresponds to the greatest fixed point of a
certain monotonic function on lts. Bisimulation is a
natural
notion
and Park
[
Par81
]

seems the first to have
used it in the semantics of programming languages. In order to
abstract the internal behavior of a system, we have introduced the
notion of
internal action
and the related notions of
weak
transition
and
weak bisimulation
. Finally, we have discussed
an
up to proof technique
which allows to reduce the size of the
relation to be exhibited to show that two states are bisimilar.

Chapter 24
Modal logics

In chapter
22
, we have considered
partial correctness and rely-guarantee assertions as means
to specify the behaviour of concurrent processes and
in doing this we have faced some problems due to the
limited expressive power of the specification language.
In this chapter, we take a bold step in that for a given
notion of equivalence on lts we aim at a specification
language which captures exactly the equivalence.
The presented languages build on the notion of (propositional)
modal logic

which is an extension of usual logic with
modalities

that qualify the validity of the assertions: possibly true,
necessarily true,
\ldots

In particular, we introduce a
diamond modality
indexed over
the actions of the lts and stipulate:

s\models\langle\alpha\rangle A\mbox{ if }\exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\mbox{ and }s^{\prime}\models A~,

which is read as follows: a state
s
satisfies the formula
\langle\alpha\rangle A
if
there is a state
s^{\prime}
such that
s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
satisifies
A
.
It turns out that the full
infinitary
specifications generated by this
extension characterize bisimulation, while
restricted versions correspond to coarser equivalences such as
simulation or trace equivalences.

In practice, one needs finite means to describe ‘infinitary’ specifications.
An elegant way to achieve this, is to define (monotonic) formulae by
least and greatest fixed points (in the spirit of proposition
9.1
).
The resulting modal language is called the
\mu
-calculus.
For finite state lts, we present a simple algorithm to decide whether a state
satisfies a formula of the
\mu
-calculus.

24.1
Modal logics vs. equivalences

We introduce a modal logic which consists of
a classical propositional logic enriched with
a so called
diamond
modality describing
the ability to perform an action.

Definition 24.1
(formulae)

The collection of formulae of a propositional modal logic is defined as:

A::=\bigwedge_{i\in I}A_{i}\mid\!\!\mid\neg A\mid\!\!\mid\langle\alpha\rangle A\qquad\alpha\in{\it Act}

(24.1)

where the set
I
can also be empty or infinite.
By convention, we write

{\sf true}
for
\bigwedge\emptyset
and

[\alpha]A
for
\neg\langle\alpha\rangle\neg A
.

Definition 24.2
(formulae satisfaction)

We define when a state in a lts satisfies a formula, written
s\models A
, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}s\models\bigwedge_{i\in I}A_{i}&\mbox{if }\ \forall\,i\in I\;\>s\models A_{i}\\
s\models\neg A&\mbox{if }\ s\not\models A\\
s\models\langle\alpha\rangle A&\mbox{if }\ \exists\,s^{\prime}\;\>s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\mbox{ and }s^{\prime}\models A~.\par\end{array}

We also write:

\begin{array}[]{lll}[\![A]\!]&=\{s\mid s\models A\}&\mbox{(formula interpretation)}\\
[\![s]\!]&=\{A\mid s\models A\}&\mbox{(state interpretation)}\\
s\sim_{L}s^{\prime}&\mbox{if }[\![s]\!]=[\![s^{\prime}]\!]&\mbox{(logical equivalence)}~.\end{array}

Exercise 24.1
(on modal formulae)

Spell out what it means to satisfy
[\alpha]A
.
Find a formula showing that
a.(b.0+c.0)\not\sim_{L}a.b.0+a.c.0
.

It is easily checked that two bisimilar states are logically equivalent.

Proposition 24.1

Let
s,s^{\prime}
be states in a lts.
If
s\sim s^{\prime}
then
s\sim_{L}s^{\prime}
.

Exercise 24.2

Prove proposition
24.1
.

To show the converse of proposition
24.1
,
we introduce the (possibly infinite) so called

characteristic formulae
.

Definition 24.3
(characteristic formula)

Given a state
s
in a lts and an ordinal
\kappa
the characteristic formula

C^{\kappa}(s)
is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}C^{0}(P)&=&{\sf true}\\
C^{\kappa+1}(s)&=&\bigwedge_{s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}}\ \langle\alpha\rangle C^{\kappa}(s^{\prime})~\and~\bigwedge_{\alpha\in{\it Act}}\ [\alpha](\ \bigvee_{s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}}C^{\kappa}(s^{\prime})\ )\\
C^{\kappa}(s)&=&\bigwedge_{\kappa^{\prime}<\kappa}C^{\kappa^{\prime}}(s)\qquad\mbox{($\kappa$ limit ordinal).}\end{array}

(24.2)

Proposition 24.2

For any state
s
and ordinal
\kappa
:

1.

s\models C^{\kappa}(s)
.

2.

s^{\prime}\models C^{\kappa}(s)
iff
s\sim_{\kappa}s^{\prime}
, where

\sim_{\kappa}
is the approximation of bisimulation defined in remark
23.3
.

Proof
. To prove a property for all ordinals one relies on the principle of transfinite induction. Namely one shows that if a property is true of all ordinals less than
\kappa
then it is true of
\kappa
.
{\Box}

Exercise 24.3

Suppose that the set of actions
{\it Act}
is finite.
Then show for image finite lts the following property: if two processes
are not bisimilar then there is a finite formula that distinguishes them.

Given that full modal logic characterizes bisimulation, one may look
for fragments of the logic that characterize coarser equivalences or
pre-orders.
We consider the cases of trace inclusion (definition
23.2
)
and simulation pre-order (definition
23.3
).

Proposition 24.3

The modal formulae
A
of the following shape characterize
trace inclusion:

A::=B\mid\!\!\mid\bigwedge_{i\in I}A_{i}~,

where:
\ B::={\sf true}\mid\!\!\mid\langle\alpha\rangle B\
.

Proof
. If
s
is a state then let

T(s)
be the collection of its traces.
Associate to a state
s
the following formula
C(s)
:

C(s)=\bigwedge_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}\in{\it tr}(s)}\langle\alpha_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle\alpha_{n}\rangle{\sf true}~.

Then for any state
s^{\prime}
,
s^{\prime}\models C(s)
iff
T(s)\subseteq T(s^{\prime})
.
{\Box}

Proposition 24.4

The modal formulae
A
of the following shape characterize the simulation
pre-order:

A::=\bigwedge_{i\in I}A_{i}\mid\!\!\mid\langle\alpha\rangle A~.

Proof
. We build the formula
C^{\kappa}(s)
taking the left hand side of the formula (
24.2
) that works for bisimulation.
Then for any state
s^{\prime}
,

s^{\prime}\models C^{\kappa}(s)
iff
s\leq_{\kappa}s^{\prime}
,
where
\leq_{\kappa}
is the
\kappa
-approximation of simulation.
{\Box}

24.2
A modal logic with fixed points: the
\mu
-calculus (*)

In the presented modal language, to express,
e.g.
, that a process can do infinitely many actions
\alpha
we
need an
infinite formula
. It is possible to increase the expressive power of formulae while keeping the
syntax
finite
. An elegant
extension known as
\mu
-calculus
consists in adding to the logical
formulae
least fixed points
. Then the syntax of modal formulae given in definition
24.1

is revisited as follows.

Definition 24.4
(formulae with fixed points)

The modal formulae with fixed points have the following syntax:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\it id}::=x\mid\!\!\mid y\mid\!\!\mid\ldots&\mbox{(formula identifiers)}\\
A::=\bigwedge_{i\in I}A_{i}\mid\!\!\mid\neg A\mid\!\!\mid\langle\alpha\rangle A\mid\!\!\mid{\it id}\mid\!\!\mid\mu{\it id}.A&\mbox{(formulae).}\end{array}

In a formula
\mu x.A
, the identifier
x
is bound in
A
by the least fixed point operator
\mu
.
Also we assume that each free occurrence of
x
in
A
is
positive
,
i.e.
,
under an even number of negations. This positivity condition is essential to show that
the function induced by the formula is monotonic and therefore has a least (and a greatest) fixed point (cf. exercise
24.4
below).

Since a formula may contain free identifiers, its interpretation is given
relatively to an assignment
\rho:{\it id}\rightarrow 2^{S}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}[\![\bigwedge_{i\in I}A_{i}]\!]\rho&=\bigcap_{i\in I}[\![A_{i}]\!]\rho\\
[\![\neg A]\!]\rho&=([\![A]\!]\rho)^{c}\\
[\![\langle\alpha\rangle A]\!]\rho&=\{s\mid s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\mbox{ and }s^{\prime}\in[\![A]\!]\rho\}\\
[\![x]\!]\rho&=\rho(x)\\
[\![\mu x.A]\!]\rho&=\bigcap\{X\subseteq S\mid[\![A]\!]\rho[X/x]\subseteq X\}~.\end{array}

Of course, if
A
is a closed formula its interpretation does
not
depend on the assignment
and we can write
s\models A
if
s\in[\![A]\!]\rho
, for some
\rho
.

Exercise 24.4
(positivity)

Check that for all well-formed formulae
A
, identifier
x
, and assignments
\rho
, the function

X\mapsto[\![A]\!]\rho[X/x]
is monotonic on
2^{S}
. Conclude that the semantics of a formula

\mu x.A
does indeed correspond to a least fixed point.

An intuitive way to understand the meaning of a formula
\mu x.A
is
to unfold it as an infinite
disjunction
\bigvee_{\kappa}A^{\kappa}
where:

A^{0}={\sf false}
,
A^{\kappa+1}=[A^{\kappa}/x]A
and

A^{\kappa}=\bigvee_{\kappa^{\prime}<\kappa}A^{\kappa^{\prime}}
, for
\kappa
limit ordinal.
This viewpoint is based on the
iterated definition of the least fixed point mentioned in chapter

9
.

Greatest fixed points
are derived by duality from least fixed points by defining:

\nu x.A=\neg\mu x.\neg([\neg x/x]A)~.

For instance:
\nu x.\langle\alpha\rangle x=\neg\mu x.\neg\langle\alpha\rangle\neg x
.

Exercise 24.5
(greatest fixed points)

Check that the interpretation of
\nu
does indeed correspond
to a greatest fixed point, namely:

[\![\nu x.A]\!]\rho=\bigcup\{X\subseteq S\mid X\subseteq[\![A]\!]\rho[X/x]\}~.

We have seen that disjunction and greatest fixed points can be derived from
conjunction, least fixed points, and negation. An alternative approach
consists in dropping negation and taking conjunction, disjunction,

\mu
and
\nu
operators as primitive. This way we have to deal with
an additional operator but we can drop the positivity condition on the
fixed points since conjunction and disjunction are guaranteed to induce
monotonic functions.

Exercise 24.6
(deriving negation)

Show that the negation operator can be defined (on closed formulas).
Hint: Consider the following equations:

\begin{array}[]{llll}\neg\langle\alpha\rangle A=[\alpha]\neg A,\par&\neg[\alpha]A=\langle\alpha\rangle\neg A,\par&\neg\mu x.A=\nu x.\neg A,\quad\par\neg\nu x.A=\mu x.\neg A~.\par\end{array}

It turns out that for
finite lts
, the modal logic with fixed points
can express the characteristic formula of a state by
a
finite
formula.

Proposition 24.5

Let
s
be a state in a finite lts.
Then there is a closed finite characteristic formula
C(s)
involving only greatest fixed points such that
for any state
s^{\prime}
,
s^{\prime}\models C(s)
iff
s\sim s^{\prime}
.

Proof
. For every state
s
introduce a propositional variable
x_{s}
and
an equation based on the characteristic formula in definition

24.3
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}x_{s}&=&\bigwedge_{s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}}\ \langle\alpha\rangle x_{s^{\prime}}~\and~\bigwedge_{\alpha\in{\it Act}}\ [\alpha](\ \bigvee_{s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}}x_{s^{\prime}}\ )\par\end{array}

Then the general idea is to take the
greatest
fixed point of
this system of equations and project on
the component which corresponds to the state
s
.
For instance, suppose
S=\{1,2\}
and
1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}2
,
1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}1
,

2\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}1
,
2\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}2
.
Then define:

\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}=\langle a\rangle x_{1}\and\langle b\rangle x_{2}\and[a]x_{2}\and[b]x_{1}~,&A_{2}=\langle b\rangle x_{1}\and\langle b\rangle x_{2}\and[a]{\sf false}\and[b](x_{1}\vee x_{2})~.\end{array}

The characteristic formula for,
e.g.
,
the state
1
can be written as
\nu x_{1}.[\nu x_{2}.A/x_{2}]A_{1}
.
{\Box}

Another interesting property of the
\mu
-calculus on finite lts is
that the model-checking problem is
decidable
. We spend the rest
of the section to present a proof of this fact that relies on the
following elementary property of fixed points.

Proposition 24.6
(reduction)

Let
f
be a monotonic function over
2^{S}
and
s\in S
be a state.
Consider the following monotonic functions over
2^{S}
:

(f\cup s)(x)=f(x)\cup\{s\}
and

(f\backslash s)(x)=f(x)\backslash\{x\}
.
Also if
g
is a monotonic function denote by

\nu(g)
and
\mu(g)
its greatest and least fixed point.
Then:

1.

s\in\nu(f)\mbox{~~iff~~}s\in f(\nu(f\cup s))
.

2.

s\in\mu(f)\mbox{~~iff~~}s\in f(\mu(f\backslash s))
.

3.

s\in\nu(f\cup s)
.

4.

s\notin\nu(f\backslash s)
.

Proof
.
(1)\;

Suppose
s\in\nu(f)
. Then:

f(\nu(f))\cup\{s\}=\nu(f)\cup\{s\}=\nu(f)~.

By definition of
\nu(f\cup s)
this implies
\nu(f)\subseteq\nu(f\cup s)
.
By monotonicity,
\nu(f)=f(\nu(f))\subseteq f(\nu(f\cup s))
,
and therefore
s\in f(\nu(f\cup s))
.
On the other hand, suppose
s\in f(\nu(f\cup s))
. It follows:

\nu(f\cup s)=f(\nu(f\cup s))\cup\{s\}=f(\nu(f\cup s))~.

By definition of
\nu(f)
this implies
\nu(f\cup s)\leq\nu(f)
.
By monotonicity,
f(\nu(f\cup s))\leq f(\nu(f))=\nu(f)
, and therefore

s\in\nu(f)
.

(2)\;
Prove by a dual argument:

s\notin\mu(f)\mbox{~~iff~~}s\notin f(\mu(f\backslash s))
.

(3-4)\;
Immediate by unfolding the fixed point.
{\Box}

This proposition suggests a strategy to unfold recursive formulae. The starting idea is to tag each fixed point
with a set of states. Then properties (1-2) of proposition
24.6
when read from left to right
suggest to record in the tag the states that are crossed when unfolding a fixed point while
properties (3-4) of proposition

24.6
provide the halting conditions.
To formalize this idea, we begin by introducing the syntax of
tagged
formulae.

Definition 24.5
(formulae with tagged fixed points)

The modal formulae with tagged fixed points have the following syntax:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\it id}::=x\mid\!\!\mid y\mid\!\!\mid\ldots&\mbox{(formula identifiers)}\\
T::=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\}&\mbox{(tags, finite sets of states)}\\
A::=\bigwedge_{i\in I}A_{i}\mid\!\!\mid\bigvee_{i\in I}A_{i}\mid\!\!\mid\langle\alpha\rangle A\mid\!\!\mid[\alpha]A\mid\!\!\mid{\it id}\mid\!\!\mid\mu{\it id}:T.A\mid\!\!\mid\nu{\it id}:T.A&\mbox{(tagged formulae).}\end{array}

The interpretation of tagged fixed points is as follows while the interpretation of the logical
and modal operators is left unchanged:

\begin{array}[]{ll}[\![\mu x:T.A]\!]\rho&=\bigcap\{X\subseteq S\mid([\![A]\!]\rho[X/x])\backslash T\subseteq X\}~,\\
[\![\nu x:T.A]\!]\rho&=\bigcup\{X\subseteq S\mid X\subseteq([\![A]\!]\rho[X/x])\cup T\}~.\par\end{array}

Based on this interpretation and proposition
24.6
, we introduce in
Table
24.1

the collection of rules to model-check states against
finite formulae of the
\mu
-calculus.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}s:A~~s:B\\
\hline\cr s:A\and B\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}s:A\\
\hline\cr s:A\vee B\end{array}\par\par\hskip 17.00024pt\par\begin{array}[]{c}s:B\\
\hline\cr s:A\vee B\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}s^{\prime}:A\\
\hline\cr s:\langle\alpha\rangle A\end{array}\mbox{ for some }s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime}\par\par\par\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{array}[]{c}s^{\prime}:A\\
\hline\cr s:[\alpha]A\end{array}\mbox{ whenever }s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}s^{\prime\prime}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}s\notin T~~s:[\mu x:T\cup\{s\}.A/x]A\\
\hline\cr s:\mu x:T.A\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt\par\begin{array}[]{c}s\notin T~~s:[\nu x:T\cup\{s\}.A/x]A\\
\hline\cr s:\nu x:T.A\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}s\in T\\
\hline\cr s:\nu x:T.A\end{array}\par\par\par\end{array}

Table 24.1:
A model checker for the
\mu
-calculus

Proposition 24.7
(soundness)

Let
A
be a closed formula of the modal, tagged
\mu
-calculus.
If we can derive the assertion
s:A
according to the rules
in Table
24.1
then
s\in[\![A]\!]
.

Proof
. By induction on the height of the proof, relying on the reduction
proposition
24.6
for the rules that fold the
fixed points.
{\Box}

Proving completeness of the method for finite state lts amounts to prove
the termination of the unfolding process. Suppose we look at the rules
in Table
24.1
bottom up.
All rules but those that unfold fixed points either entail termination or shrink the size of the formula to be proved.
Hence any infinite backward development must include
an infinite number of applications of the rules unfolding fixed points.
Now we remark that these rules add new
elements to the tags. Since
T\subseteq S
and
S
is finite,
we might conjecture that this process eventually terminates.
We prove this property in two steps.
First, we present a simple rewriting system whose termination proof
exposes the kernel of the combinatorial problem.
Second, we show termination of the bottom up proof development
by exhibiting a reduction preserving translation from judgments to terms
of the simple rewriting system.

Definition 24.6

We define a collection of
\sigma
-terms as follows where
n
is a natural number:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\it id}::=x\mid\!\!\mid y\mid\!\!\mid\cdots&\mbox{(identifiers)}\\
\theta::={\it id}\mid\!\!\mid 1\mid\!\!\mid\theta*\theta\mid\!\!\mid\bullet\theta\mid\!\!\mid\sigma^{n}{\it id}.\theta&\mbox{($\sigma$-terms)}\end{array}

Definition 24.7

A term
\theta
can be reduced according to the following rules
where the rules can be applied at top level only:

\begin{array}[]{llll}\sigma^{n+1}x.\theta\rightarrow[\sigma^{n}x.\theta/x]\theta,\par&\bullet\theta\rightarrow\theta,\par&\theta*\theta^{\prime}\rightarrow\theta,\par&\theta*\theta^{\prime}\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}~.\par\par\end{array}

Proposition 24.8

The rewriting system defined in
24.7
terminates.

Proof
. Let
{\it WF}
be the collection of terminating

\sigma
-terms. If
\theta\in{\it WF}
let
d(\theta)
be the length of the longest
reduction sequence (this is well defined because the reduction
tree is finitely branching). We want to prove:

\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in{\it WF}~~\mbox{ implies }~~[\theta^{\prime}/x]\theta\in{\it WF}~.

(24.3)

We prove (
24.3
) by induction on
d(\theta)
.
The only interesting case is when
\theta
has the
shape
\sigma^{n+1}y.\theta
. Then we observe:
