\begin{array}[]{llll}[\theta^{\prime}/x](\sigma^{n+1}y.\theta)&\equiv\sigma^{n+1}y.[\theta^{\prime}/x]\theta&\rightarrow[\sigma^{n}y.[\theta^{\prime}/x]\theta/y]([\theta^{\prime}/x]\theta)&\equiv[\theta^{\prime}/x][\sigma^{n}y.\theta/y]\theta~.\end{array}

We note that
(\sigma^{n+1}y.\theta)\rightarrow[\sigma^{n}y.\theta/y]\theta\in{\it WF}
.
Hence, we can apply the inductive hypothesis on
d([\sigma^{n}y.\theta/y]\theta)
,
and we conclude that
[\theta^{\prime}/x][\sigma^{n}y.\theta/y]\theta\in{\it WF}
.

Next we prove that all
\sigma
-terms terminate.
We proceed by induction on a relation
\succ
which is
the least transitive relation such that:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\sigma^{n+1}x.\theta\succ\sigma^{n}{x}.\theta~,\par&\sigma^{n+1}x.\theta\succ\theta~,\par&\bullet\theta\succ\theta~,\par&\theta*\theta^{\prime}\succ\theta~,\par&\theta*\theta^{\prime}\succ\theta^{\prime}~.\par\end{array}

Clearly
\succ
is a well founded relation.
Again the only interesting case is when
the term has the shape
\sigma y^{n+1}.\theta
.
By the inductive hypothesis
\sigma y^{n}.\theta\in{\it WF},\theta\in{\it WF}
,
and by (
24.3
)

[\sigma y^{n}.\theta/y]\theta\in{\it WF}
.

{\Box}

Definition 24.8

Given a finite lts with a set of states
S
,
we associate a
\sigma
-term to a modal formula as follows,
where
n=\sharp S+1
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\langle{\sf true}\rangle=\langle{\sf false}\rangle=1~,\par&\langle x\rangle=x~,\\
\langle A\and B\rangle=\langle A\rangle*\langle B\rangle~,\par&\langle A\vee B\rangle=\langle A\rangle*\langle B\rangle~,\\
\langle\langle\alpha\rangle A\rangle=\bullet\langle A\rangle~,\par&\langle[\alpha]A\rangle=\bullet\langle A\rangle~,\\
\langle\mu x:T.A\rangle=\sigma x^{(n-\sharp{T})}.\langle A\rangle~,\par&\langle\nu x:T.A\rangle=\sigma x^{(n-\sharp{T})}.\langle A\rangle~.\par\end{array}

Suppose
s^{\prime}:A^{\prime}
is a premise of
s:A
in the
proof development.
We show
\langle A\rangle\rightarrow\langle A^{\prime}\rangle
by inspection of the proof rules.
The only interesting case is when we unfold a fixed point.
Since in the translation we have picked

n=\sharp S+1
bigger than
\sharp{T}
we can compute,
e.g.
,
in the case of the least fixed point:

\begin{array}[]{llll}\langle\mu x:T.A\rangle&=\sigma x^{(n-\sharp{T})}.\langle A\rangle&\rightarrow[\sigma x^{(n-\sharp{T}-1)}.\langle A\rangle/x]\langle A\rangle&=\langle[\mu x:T\cup\{s\}.A/X]A\rangle~.\end{array}

Proposition 24.9

The model checker is complete on finite structures.

Proof
. We show by induction on
A
that
s\models A
iff
a proof rule applies. We can bound the depth of
a path in a bottom up proof development. Hence, if
s\models A

by developing the proof bottom up we eventually obtain a proof
of
s:A
.
{\Box}

24.3
Summary and references

We have described a family of
modal logical languages
which can
be used to characterize bisimulation as well as coarser equivalences.
We have also presented a few basic results on a fixed point extension of
modal logic known as
\mu
-calculus.
The
\mu
-calculus is a kind of basic modal
logical language to which more user-friendly logical languages
can be compiled. It was introduced by Kozen
[
Koz83
]
, following previous work by V. Pratt.
The simple proof of decidability of the model checking
problem for finite lts we have presented is based on
[
Win89
]
.
The model-checking problem for the
\mu
-calculus is
known to be in
\mbox{{\sc NP}}\cap\mbox{{\sc co-NP}}
(like the graph
isomorphism problem). Upper bounds on the time complexity are
polynomial in the size of the lts and exponential in the so called

alternation depth
of the formula. This is a measure that counts the
number of alternations of nested greatest and least fixed points.
It is also known
[
Bra96
,
Len96
]

that bounding the alternation depth limits the expressivity of the logic,
i.e.
, the hierarchy of formulae obtained by measuring the
alternation depth is
strict
. The basic theory of the
\mu
-calculus
is developed systematically in
[
AN01
]
.

Chapter 25
Labelled transition systems with synchronization

One can make the basic model of labelled transition systems
a bit more interesting by adding some
parallelism and synchronization

mechanisms. One elegant way to provide a synchronization mechanism is
to introduce a
notion of co-action
and suppose that synchronization
happens when a process can perform an action and another parallel process can
perform the corresponding co-action. Thus, given a set
A
, take the set of
actions to be:

{\it Act}=\{a,\overline{a}\mid a\in A\}\cup\{\tau\}~.

(25.1)

It is convenient to extend the co-action definition to the whole
set
{\it Act}
by assuming:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\overline{\tau}=\tau~,&\overline{\overline{a}}=a~.\end{array}

{\it CCS}
(Calculus of Communicating Systems) is a minimal set of
operators to represent such labelled transition systems enriched with
the co-action mechanism; we introduce this formalism and discuss two
ways to define its bisimulation semantics which turn out to be
equivalent.
{\it CCS}
is a
simple
model of concurrent systems and
we shall build on it to discuss the notions of
deterministic

(chapter
27
),
timed
(chapter
28
), and

probabilistic
concurrent system (chapter
29
). We
shall also consider an extension of
{\it CCS}
, known as
\pi
-calculus
(chapter
30
), that allows for a rather direct embedding of
higher-order functional programs.

25.1
{\it CCS}

Actions in
{\it CCS}
are defined according to the equation
(
25.1
) above. Besides the nil, prefix and
non-deterministic choice operators introduced in chapter

23
,
{\it CCS}
includes operators to declare a
local action (cf. local variable in
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
), to put processes in
parallel, and to define recursive behaviors:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P&::=0\mid\!\!\mid\alpha.P\mid\!\!\mid(P+P)\mid\!\!\mid(P\mid P)\mid\!\!\mid\nu a\ P\mid\!\!\mid A(a^{*})\end{array}

where
\alpha\in{\it Act}
and
A,B,\ldots
are process identifiers.
An action name is free if it is not in the scope of a local
action declaration (a
\nu
).
We write
a^{*}
for a possibly empty list of action names
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
.
Similarly,
\nu a^{*}\ P
stands for
\nu a_{1}\cdots\nu a_{n}\ P
,
and
[b^{*}/a^{*}]
for
[b_{1}/a_{1},\ldots,b_{n}/a_{n}]
.
It is assumed that each process identifier
A
is defined
by a unique equation
A(b^{*})=P
where the free names in
P

are contained in the set of parameters
\{b^{*}\}
.
For instance,
A
could be a process identifier defined by the
equation:

A(a,b)=a.\nu c\ (A(a,c)\mid\overline{b}.A(c,b))~.

(25.2)

Here the set of variables occurring free in

a.\nu c\ (A(a,c)\mid\overline{b}.A(c,b))
is
\{a,b\}
which happens
to be included (actually equal) to the set of parameters of the process identifier
A
. Also notice that an action name, say
b
, may appear in a prefix as such or in its dual form
\overline{b}
.

Moving towards semantics, the main design decision consists in regarding

a,b,\ldots
as channel names on which parallel processes synchronize.
More precisely, a synchronization may only happen when a process is ready to
perform an action and another parallel
process is ready to perform its co-action as,

e.g.
, in the process

(a.P\mid\overline{a}.Q)
. Following the synchronization, the process moves
to
(P\mid Q)
.
{\it CCS}
is an asynchronous model of concurrency where
interaction is possible through
rendez-vous
synchronization on

pure
channels. A
rendez-vous
channel is a channel of
null capacity where the sender must always wait for a receiver.
A channel is
pure
if no message value is exchanged; all that
matters is the synchronization.

An important consequence of assuming a synchronization by

rendez-vous
is to offer a better control on the role of the environment.
In the
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
model, the environment can modify the (visible part
of the) state and these modifications may affect the future
computation of the process. In
{\it CCS}
, the only way the environment
may affect the computation of the process is to perform an action
which is dual to an action that the process is
ready
to perform.

Starting from this intuition, we follow
two paths
to define
a compositional semantics of
{\it CCS}
. The first path consists in
associating a labelled transition system with each
{\it CCS}
process.
Then the equivalences on lts defined in the previous chapter
23
,
apply to
{\it CCS}
processes too and lead to a compositional semantics.
The second path consists in looking at
{\it CCS}
as a (rudimentary)
programming language and define its possible reductions
similarly to what we have done for the
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
language
in chapter
19
. Then what needs to be done is to fix
a notion of observable and to derive a notion of compositional equivalence.
We work with the notion of (weak) bisimulation introduced
in chapter
23
and in the end, we show that the two
paths outlined above actually lead to the same compositional
equivalence.

As a concrete example illustrating the difference between
the two approaches, consider the
{\it CCS}
process
P\equiv(a.0\mid\overline{a}.0)
.
In the first approach, we have to consider the labelled transitions:

\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}(0\mid\overline{a}.0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{a}}}{{\rightarrow}}(0\mid 0)~,\quad P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{a}}}{{\rightarrow}}(a.0\mid 0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}(0\mid 0)~,\quad P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}(0\mid 0)~.\end{array}

While in the second, we just have have the reduction:

P\rightarrow(0\mid 0)~.

We shall see that the
\tau
transitions correspond to the reductions
while the other labelled transitions correspond to interactions
with the environment.

25.2
Labelled transition system for
{\it CCS}

Table
25.1
describes a lts for
{\it CCS}
processes where
the symmetric rules for
\mid
and
+
are omitted.

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr\alpha.P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\alpha\notin\{a,\overline{a}\}\\
\hline\cr\nu a\ P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}\nu a\ P^{\prime}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr P\mid Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mid Q\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\hskip 17.00024ptQ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{a}}}{{\rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr(P\mid Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}(P^{\prime}\mid Q^{\prime})\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime\prime}\\
\hline\cr P+P^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}B(a^{*})=P\hskip 8.50012pt[b^{*}/a^{*}]P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr B(b^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 25.1:
Lts for
{\it CCS}
(symmetric rules omitted)

Exercise 25.1
(labelled transitions)

Check that:

\nu b\ (a.P\mid P^{\prime})\mid\nu c\ \overline{a}.Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\nu b\ (P\mid P^{\prime})\mid\nu c\ Q~.

Example 25.1
(an unbounded buffer in
{\it CCS}
)

In
{\it CCS}
, the communication is by rendez-vous (or handshake, or synchronous).
What if we want channels with buffers?
One approach is to enrich the model.
Another approach is to show that buffers can be expressed in
{\it CCS}
.
An unbounded buffer taking inputs on
a
and producing outputs on
b

can be written as (up to renaming, this is the same as equation (
25.2
)):

{\it Buf}(a,b)=a.\nu c\ ({\it Buf}(a,c)\mid\overline{b}.{\it Buf}(c,b))~.

We write more suggestively
a\mapsto b
for
{\it Buf}(a,b)
,
assuming
a\neq b
.
We would like to show that
a\mapsto b
works indeed as an unbounded buffer.
Let
\overline{c}^{n}=\overline{c}\ldots\overline{c}.0
,
n
times,
n\geq 0
.
We should have:

P(n)=\nu a\ (\overline{a}^{n}\mid a\mapsto b)\mbox{ `equivalent to' }\overline{b}^{n}

An interesting exercise because

P(n)
has a non trivial dynamics.
For the time being we just analyze some of
the labelled transitions of
P(n)
.

•

For
n=0
,
P(n)
cannot reduce.

•

For
n>0
, we need to generalize a bit the form of the process

P(n)
. Let
Q(n,m)
be a process of the form:

Q(n,m)=\nu a,c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}\ (\overline{a}^{n}\mid a\mapsto c_{1}\mid\cdots\mid c_{m}\mapsto b)~,

for
m\geq 0
. Note that
P(n)=Q(n,0)
and
Q(0,k)
cannot reduce for any

k
. Moreover, the message can traverse the whole chain so that for
n>0
:

Q(n,m)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{b}}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q(n-1,2m+1)~.

Thus:

P(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{b}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{b}}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q(0,2^{n}-1)\mbox{ `equivalent to' }0~,

where we recall that:
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{b}}}{{\Rightarrow}}=(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}})^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{b}}}{{\rightarrow}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}})^{*}
.

Note that there are plenty of reductions we did not consider!
Yet, in chapter
27
we shall be able to conclude that
this analysis suffices to derive that

P(n)
is ‘equivalent to’
\overline{b}^{n}
.

We can regard
{\it CCS}
as a
labelled transition system
where
states are processes. We say that
P
and
Q
are
strongly
bisimilar
(written
P\sim Q
) if they are bisimilar with respect to
the lts we have just defined. We say that they are
weakly
bisimilar
(written
P\approx Q
) if they are bisimilar with respect
to the derived lts
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}
where internal actions are
‘abstracted’. Obviously
P\sim Q
implies
P\approx Q
.
Next, we consider the issue of compositionality.

Definition 25.1
(
{\it CCS}
context)

A context
C
is a process with a hole
[~]
(here and in the following
we omit the symmetric cases when listing contexts):

C::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid\alpha.C\mid\!\!\mid C+P\mid\!\!\mid C\mid P\mid\!\!\mid\nu a\ C~.

Proposition 25.1

If
P\sim Q
then
C[P]\sim C[Q]
.

Proof
. We apply the standard technique which amounts to define a relation
{\cal R}

which includes the processes of interest and show that it is a
bisimulation.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{Prefix}&{\cal R}=\{(\alpha.P_{1},\alpha.P_{2})\mid P_{1}\sim P_{2}\}\cup\sim\\
\mbox{Sum}&{\cal R}=\{(P_{1}+Q,P_{2}+Q)\mid P_{1}\sim P_{2}\}\cup\sim\\
\mbox{Parallel}&{\cal R}=\{(P_{1}\mid Q,P_{2}\mid Q)\mid P_{1}\sim P_{2}\}\cup\sim\\
\mbox{Restriction}&{\cal R}=\{(\nu a\ P_{1},\nu a\ P_{2})\mid P_{1}\sim P_{2}\}\cup\sim~.\end{array}

{\Box}

Remark 25.1

In general, the non-deterministic sum does not preserve weak
bisimulation as:

\tau.a\approx a\qquad\mbox{ but }\qquad\tau.a+b\not\approx a+b~.

However, a
guarded version
of the non-deterministic sum has this property.
Denote with
D
the following contexts:

D::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid\alpha.D\mid\!\!\mid\alpha^{\prime}.D+P\mid\!\!\mid(D\mid P)\mid\!\!\mid\nu a\ D\qquad\alpha^{\prime}\neq\tau~.

Proposition 25.2

If
P\approx Q
then
D[P]\approx D[Q]
.

Proof
. Similar to the strong case.
{\Box}

Remark 25.2
(on unguarded sum)

There are two viewpoints on the non-preservation of weak-bisimulation
by the sum:

1.

One should take the largest congruence which refines
\approx
.
Then, e.g., we should distinguish
\tau.a
from
a
.

2.

In most applications one just needs a guarded sum and so it
is enough to have a notion of equivalence which is preserved by guarded sums.

The second viewpoint tends to prevail at least in formalisms
like
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
(chapter
19
)
or the
\pi
-calculus (chapter
30
)
where a guarded form of sum is
easily derived from parallel composition.

Exercise 25.2
(sequentialization in
{\it CCS}
)

We consider a fragment of
{\it CCS}
(we drop sum and recursive definitions)
extended with
an operator ‘;’ for process sequentialization. Thus the process syntax is as follows:

P::=0\mid\!\!\mid\alpha.P\mid\!\!\mid(P\mid P)\mid\!\!\mid P;P\mid\!\!\mid\nu a\ P\qquad\alpha\in\{a,\overline{a}\mid a\in A\}\cup\{\tau\}~.

(25.3)

The labelled transition system for
{\it CCS}
is extended with the following rules for process sequentialization:

\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr P;Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime};Q\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{c}P\searrow\qquad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr P;Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}\end{array}

Here the predicate
\searrow
denotes proper termination and it is defined as the least set of processes
such that:
1
1

1

This is related to (but slightly different from) the
immediate termination
predicate
introduced for the language
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
(chapter
19
).

\begin{array}[]{llll}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr 0\searrow\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}P\searrow\quad Q\searrow\\
\hline\cr(P\mid Q)\searrow\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}P\searrow\quad Q\searrow\\
\hline\cr(P;Q)\searrow\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}P\searrow\\
\hline\cr\nu a\ P\searrow\end{array}~.\end{array}

If
P
is a process in the extended language (
25.3
)
and
c
is a name not occurring
in
P
then
[\![P]\!]c
is a
{\it CCS}
process (without process sequentialization)
defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}[\![0]\!]c&=\overline{c}.0\\
[\![\alpha.P]\!]c&=\alpha.[\![P]\!]c\\
[\![P_{1}\mid P_{2}]\!]c&=\nu c_{1},c_{2}\ (([\![P_{1}]\!]c_{1}\mid[\![P_{2}]\!]c_{2})\mid c_{1}.c_{2}.\overline{c}.0)&(c_{1},c_{2}\notin{\sf fv}(P_{1})\cup{\sf fv}(P_{2})\cup\{c\})\\
[\![P_{1};P_{2}]\!]c&=\nu c^{\prime}\ ([\![P_{1}]\!]c^{\prime}\mid c^{\prime}.[\![P_{2}]\!]c)&(c^{\prime}\notin{\sf fv}(P_{1})\cup{\sf fv}(P_{2})\cup\{c\})\\
[\![\nu a\ P]\!]c&=\nu a\ [\![P]\!]c&(c\neq a)\\
\end{array}

(1) Show that if
P\searrow
then
[\![P]\!]c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{c}}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q
and
Q\sim 0
, where
\sim
is the largest strong bisimulation.
(2) Define the notion of weak simulation up to strong bisimulation and show that this is a sound technique
to prove weak simulation.
(3) Show that for all processes
P
,
P
is weakly simulated by
[\![P]\!]c
up to strong bisimulation.

Exercise 25.3
(bisimulation up-to context)

We introduce a notion of bisimulation up to the contexts.
For simplicity let us assume:

D::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid\alpha.D\mid\!\!\mid(D\mid P)\mid\!\!\mid\nu a\ D
.
Suppose:

{\cal H}({\cal R})=\{(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\mid\exists\,D\;\>P^{\prime}\equiv D[P],P\;{\cal R}\;Q,D[Q]\equiv Q^{\prime}\}~.

Let
{\cal F}
be the monotonic function associated with (one step) weak
bisimulation.

1.

Analyze the one-step transitions of a process
D[P]
as a function
of the transitions of
P
and
D[0]
.

2.

Show that
{\cal H}
preserves
<_{\cal F}
.

3.

Explicit the associated notion of bisimulation up to context.

Exercise 25.4
(prime factorization)

Let
A
be a set of actions with generic elements
a,b,\ldots

and let
P
denote a process in the following fragment of
{\it CCS}
:

P::={\bf 0}\mid\!\!\mid a.P\mid\!\!\mid(P+P)\mid\!\!\mid(P\mid P)\qquad a\in A~.

Notice that there is no notion of co-action
and therefore no possibility of synchronization among parallel processes.
In this exercise when we speak of a process, we refer to a process
in this fragment. We define the size of a process, say
|P|
, by induction on the structure
of
P
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{llll}|{\bf 0}|=0~,&|a.P|=1+|P|~,&|P+Q|={\it max}(|P|,|Q|)~,&|P\mid Q|=|P|+|Q|~.\end{array}

We say that a process
P
is irreducible if
P\sim P_{1}\mid P_{2}

implies that
P_{1}\sim 0
or
P_{2}\sim 0
and we say that

P
is prime if
P
is irreducible and moreover
P\not\sim 0
.
Prove the following assertions.

1.

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}Q
then
|P|>|Q|
.

2.

If
P\sim Q
then
|P|=|Q|
(but the converse fails).

3.

For all processes
P,Q,R
the following properties hold:

•

If
(P\mid R)\sim(Q\mid R)
then
P\sim Q
(this is a kind of
cancellation property).

•

If
(P\mid R)\sim(Q\mid R^{\prime})
and
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}R^{\prime}
then there exists

Q^{\prime}
such that
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
and
P\sim Q^{\prime}
.

4.

Every process
P
such that
P\not\sim 0
can
be expressed up to strong bisimulation as the parallel composition of
prime processes.

5.

Every process
P
such that
P\not\sim 0
has a unique
decomposition as the parallel composition of prime processes.
Unicity here has to be understood in the same sense as the
unicity of the prime factorization of a natural number.

25.3
A reduction semantics for
{\it CCS}

We want to define a reduction semantics for
{\it CCS}
.
A technical problem is that in the
syntax
,
the synchronizing processes can be far away as in:

\ \nu b\ (a.P\mid P^{\prime})\mid\nu c\ \overline{a}.Q
.
In the lts presented in Table
25.1
we have
tackled this problem by keeping track of the
potential

transitions of every sub-process.
An
alternative approach
consists in introducing a notion
of
structural equivalence
on processes which is strong enough
to bring two synchronizing processes in contiguous positions and weak
enough to identify only processes that are intuitively equivalent.
To simplify the formalization we drop the non-deterministic sum.
In this context, we assume a structural equivalence
\equiv
which
is the least congruence such that: (i) it includes renaming,
(ii) parallel composition is associative and commutative, and (iii):

\begin{array}[]{ll}\nu a\ (P\mid Q)\equiv\nu a\ P\mid Q&\mbox{if }a\notin{\sf fv}(Q)\\
A(b^{*})\equiv[b^{*}/a^{*}]P&\mbox{if }A(a^{*})=P~.\end{array}

An
evaluation context

E
is defined by:

E::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid\nu a\ E\mid\!\!\mid E\mid P\qquad\mbox{(evaluation contexts).}

(25.4)

Then the
reduction relation
is:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P\rightarrow Q&\mbox{if }\quad P\equiv E[a.P^{\prime}+Q^{\prime}\mid\overline{a}.P^{\prime\prime}+Q^{\prime\prime}]\mbox{ and }Q\equiv E[P^{\prime}\mid P^{\prime\prime}]\\
P\rightarrow Q&\mbox{if }\quad P\equiv E[\tau.P+Q^{\prime}]\mbox{ and }Q\equiv E[P]~.\end{array}

Exercise 25.5
(reduction up to structural equivalence)

Check that:

\nu b\ (a.P\mid P^{\prime})\mid\nu c\ \overline{a}.Q\rightarrow\nu b\ (P\mid P^{\prime})\mid\nu c\ Q~.

Internal transitions and reductions can be related as follows.

Proposition 25.3

Let
P
be a
{\it CCS}
process (without non-deterministic sum).

1.

The structural equivalence
\equiv
is a strong bisimulation.

2.

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then
P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
.

3.

If
P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
then
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\equiv P^{\prime\prime}
.

Exercise 25.6

Prove proposition
25.3
.

The next step is to introduce some candidates for the notion of

basic observable
.
We write

P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}

if the process
P
is ‘ready to perform’ a visible communication action on channel
a
.
This is also called a
strong commitment
(or
barb
).
It is a simple exercise to define
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}
by induction on the
structure of
P
.
We also write
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}
if for some
Q
,
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q
and
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}
.
This is also called a
weak
commitment.

It is also possible to abstract the polarity of the commitement (input
or output) and the name of the channel on which the process commits.
So we write:

\begin{array}[]{llllll}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\rightarrow}}\mbox{ if }\exists\,a\;\>P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}~,&P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\Rightarrow}}\mbox{ if }\exists\,a\;\>P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}~.\end{array}

Finally, we use
\Downarrow
for weak normalisation:

P\Downarrow
if
\exists\,P^{\prime}\;\>P\Rightarrow P^{\prime}\mbox{ and }P^{\prime}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
.

Definition 25.2
(static contexts)

We define the static contexts as the contexts of the following shape:

C::=[~]\mid\!\!\mid(C\mid P)\mid\!\!\mid\nu a\ C~.

Intuitively, they are called
static
because
they persist after a transition
(unlike a prefix or a sum). It is generally held that a useful equivalence
should be preserved at least by static contexts.
Incidentally, in the simple case considered,
static contexts coincide with evaluation
contexts.
Next we introduce a notion of compositional equivalence which is
based on the notion of bisimulation.

Definition 25.3
(contextual bisimulation)

A binary relation
{\cal R}
on processes is a strong
contextual bisimulation if whenever
P\;{\cal R}\;Q
the following
conditions hold (and reciprocally for
Q
):

(cxt)

For all static contexts
C
,
C[P]\;{\cal R}\;C[Q]
.

(red)

If
P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
then for some
Q^{\prime}
,
Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;Q^{\prime}
.

(cmt)

For all observable action
a
, if
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}
then
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}
.

For the weak version replace
\rightarrow
by
\Rightarrow
and

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}
by
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}
. For the one-step weak version the
replacement only takes place on the right of the implication.
Denote with
\sim_{C}
(
\approx_{C}
) the largest contextual (weak) bisimulation.

Informally, we can say that a contextual bisimulation is a relation
that is preserved by static contexts and by reduction, and that is
compatible with commitments. If we drop the preservation
by static contexts we obtain the following notion.

Definition 25.4
(barbed bisimulation)

A binary relation
{\cal R}
on processes is a strong
barbed bisimulation if whenever
P\;{\cal R}\;Q
the following
conditions hold (and reciprocally for
Q
):

(red)

If
P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
then for some
Q^{\prime}
,
Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\;{\cal R}\;Q^{\prime}
.

(cmt)

For all observable action
a
, if
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}
then
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}
.

We denote with
\sim_{BB}
the largest such equivalence, and
with
\approx_{BB}
its weak variant.

Barbed bisimulation distinguishes less processes than contextual bisimulation
and it is not preserved by parallel composition.

Exercise 25.7
(on barbed bisimulation)

In the framework of
{\it CCS}
, show that barbed bisimulation is not preserved
by parallel composition.

Because preservation by the operators of the language is essential
for compositional reasoning, the notion of barbed bisimulation
can be refined as follows.

Definition 25.5
(barbed equivalence)

We say that two processes are barbed equivalent if
put in any static context they are barbed bisimilar.
We denote such equivalence with
\sim_{BE}
.
The weak variant based on weak barbed bisimulation is denoted with

\approx_{BE}
.

Then the comparison of contextual bisimulation and barbed equivalence
arises as an obvious question.

Exercise 25.8
(on barbed equivalence)

Show that if two processes are contextually bisimilar then
they are barbed equivalent.

The converse can be quite tricky to prove.
The characterization of labelled
bisimulation we are aiming at is more direct/natural when working with

contextual bisimulation
than with
barbed equivalence
.

Exercise 25.9
(variations on commitment)

Show that we get an
equivalent notion of contextual bisimulation if the condition
[cmt] is replaced by:

P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\Rightarrow}}\mbox{ implies }Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\Rightarrow}}
. On the other hand, show that we get an incomparable
notion of contextual bisimulation if the condition
[cmt] is replaced by:

P\Downarrow\mbox{ implies }Q\Downarrow
.

The labelled bisimulation introduced in section
25.2

is an example of contextual bisimulation.

Proposition 25.4

The largest labelled bisimulation is a contextual
bisimulation (both in the strong and weak case).

Proof
. Denote with
\sim
(
\approx
) the labelled (weak) bisimulation.
It has been proved that
\sim
and
\approx
are preserved by static contexts.
Incidentally, note that an
arbitrary
labelled bisimulation
does not need to be saturated by static contexts.
Moreover the conditions [red] and [commit] of contextual bisimulation are
particular cases of the bisimulation game in the labelled case.
{\Box}

The previous proposition shows that every labelled bisimulation is
contained in contextual bisimulation. The converse is given
by the following.

Proposition 25.5

The largest contextual bisimulation
\sim_{C}
(or
\approx_{C}
in the weak case)
is a labelled (weak) bisimulation.

Proof
. We consider directly the
weak case
.
An
internal choice
2
2

2

It is called internal
because the environment has no way of controlling it; by opposition, a choice such as

a.P+b.Q
is called external.

in
{\it CCS}
can be defined as follows:

P\oplus Q=\nu a\ (a.P\mid a.Q\mid\overline{a})=\tau.P+\tau.Q~.

If
P\approx_{C}Q
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\approx_{C}Q^{\prime}
, by condition [red]
of contextual bisimulation.

So suppose
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
.
Let
o_{1},o_{2}
be two distinct fresh names (not in
P
and
Q
)
and define the static context:

C=[~]\mid\overline{a}.(o_{1}\oplus(o_{2}\oplus 0))~.

(25.5)

By hypothesis,
C[P]\approx_{C}C[Q]
.
Clearly,
C[P]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mid(o_{2}\oplus 0)
and again
by hypothesis (condition [red])

C[Q]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime\prime}
and
P^{\prime\prime}\equiv P^{\prime}\mid(o_{2}\oplus 0)\approx_{C}Q^{\prime\prime}
.

Now we argue that
Q^{\prime\prime}
must be of the shape

Q^{\prime}\mid(o_{2}\oplus 0)
where
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
.
The case
Q^{\prime\prime}=C[Q^{\prime}]
and
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
is impossible
because
P^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle o_{2}}}{{\Rightarrow}}
entails
Q^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle o_{2}}}{{\Rightarrow}}
, and
the latter entails
Q^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle o_{1}}}{{\Rightarrow}}
which cannot be matched by
P^{\prime\prime}
.
The cases
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}=Q^{\prime}\mid R^{\prime}
where

R^{\prime}\in\{o_{1}\oplus(o_{2}\oplus 0),o_{1},o_{2},0\}
are also impossible
for similar reasons.
Thus we must have
Q^{\prime\prime}=Q^{\prime}\mid(o_{2}\oplus 0)
and

P^{\prime}\mid(o_{2}\oplus 0)\approx_{C}Q^{\prime\prime}
.

It is easy to argue that since

P^{\prime}\mid(o_{2}\oplus 0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mid 0
we must have

Q^{\prime}\mid(o_{2}\oplus 0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}\mid 0
and

Q_{1}\mid 0\approx_{C}P^{\prime}\mid 0
.
Thus
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}
and

P^{\prime}\equiv(P^{\prime}\mid 0)\approx_{C}(Q_{1}\mid 0)\equiv Q_{1}
.
Strictly speaking, we use an
up to technique
.
{\Box}

Exercise 25.10

1.

Show that in the strong case it is possible
to simplify the context
C
(
25.5
) in the proof above.

2.

Show that (weak) labelled bisimulation implies
(weak) barbed bisimulation.

3.

Show that (weak) labelled bisimulation implies
(weak) barbed equivalence.

25.4
Value-passing
{\it CCS}
(*)

As already mentioned, in
{\it CCS}
communication is pure synchronization.
We now consider an extension where
values
can be sent
along channels. In the following, values are just basic
atomic objects such as booleans or integers which can be tested
for equality:

v::=v_{0}\mid\!\!\mid v_{1}\mid\!\!\mid v_{2}\ldots\qquad\mbox{(values)}

In chapter
30
, we shall consider the more complex case
where the values are actually channels.
Let
{\it Val}
be the set of values. Then
the collection of actions given by equation (
25.1
)
is revised as follows:

{\it Act}=\{av,\overline{a}v\mid a\in A,v\in{\it Val}\}\cup\{\tau\}\qquad\mbox{(actions with value passing)}

(25.6)

Input and output actions are now pairs composed of a channel name
and a value.
To write value-passing
{\it CCS}
processes, we need a notion of
variable

ranging over values which stands for the value read upon communication.

{\it id}::=x\mid\!\!\mid y\mid\!\!\mid\ldots\qquad\mbox{(variables)}

We also need a notion of
term
which is either a value or a variable
(we call this
term
by analogy with first-order logic):

t::=v\mid\!\!\mid{\it id}\qquad\mbox{(term)}

Then the syntax of
{\it CCS}
value-passing processes is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P&::=0\mid\!\!\mid a({\it id}).P\mid\!\!\mid\overline{a}t.P\mid\!\!\mid[t=t]P,P\mid\!\!\mid(P+P)\mid\!\!\mid(P\mid P)\mid\!\!\mid\nu a\ P\mid\!\!\mid A(a^{*})\end{array}

where
a(x).P
is the process that receives a value
v
on the channel

a
and becomes
[v/x]P
,
\overline{a}v.P
is the process that sends
v
on

a
and becomes
P
, and
[v=v^{\prime}]P,Q
is the process that compares the
values
v
and
v^{\prime}
and runs
P
if they are equal and
Q
otherwise.

The reduction semantics for
{\it CCS}
is easily extended to value passing

{\it CCS}
. Omitting the details concerning the evaluation context and
the structural equivalence, the synchronization rule with
exchange of values is:

\overline{a}v.P\mid a(x).Q\rightarrow P\mid[v/x]Q~,

and we add the usual rules for the conditional (cf. chapter

19
):

[v=v]P,Q\rightarrow P~,\qquad[v=v^{\prime}]P,Q\rightarrow Q\quad(v\neq v^{\prime})~.

Notice that the resulting reduction semantics is supposed
to operate on terms
without
free value variables.
As a matter of fact, reducing processes with free variables would be a form
of
symbolic execution
and requires carrying along with
the process a set of constraints which describe the
possible values of its free variables.

The labelled semantics of
{\it CCS}
with value passing rises some subtle
issues concerning the treatment of the input prefix.
Consider a process
a(x).P
. The action structure we have given
above in equation (
25.6
) suggests
a rule of the shape:

{\@index{\@indexphrase{binding, early}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr a(x).P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle av}}{{\rightarrow}}[v/x]P\end{array}\qquad\mbox{(early binding)}~.

(25.7)

However, by changing a little bit the action structure (
25.6
),
we could also think of a rule that maps a process
to a
function
from values to processes:

{\@index{\@indexphrase{binding, late}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr a(x).P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}\lambda x.P\end{array}\qquad\mbox{(late binding)}~.

(25.8)

This in turn requires defining an obvious notion of bisimulation on
functions: two functions
\lambda x.P
and
\lambda x.Q
from values
to processes are bisimilar if for all values
v\in{\it Val}
,
[v/x]P

and
[v/x]Q
are bisimilar (cf. chapter
9
).
The first rule is called
early binding
and formalizes a situation
where the communication channel and the value received are
selected at the same time. By opposition,
the second rule is called
late binding
.
It turns out that the late binding approach leads to
a labelled bisimulations which is more discriminating than
the one based on early binding.
For instance, consider the processes:

\begin{array}[]{lll}P&\equiv&a(x).([x=v_{0}]\overline{b}.0,0+[x=v_{1}]\overline{c}.0,0)~,\\
Q&\equiv&a(x).[x=v_{0}]\overline{b}.0,0+a(x).[x=v_{1}]\overline{c}.0,0~.\end{array}

The processes
P
and
Q
are ‘early-binding bisimilar’ but not ‘late-binding
bisimilar’. Specifically, by a late-binding input the process
P
goes
to a function that cannot be matched by
Q
.
In this case, the comparison with contextual bisimulation suggests
that the early binding semantics is the ‘right’ one.

We conclude this quick review of value passing
{\it CCS}
by mentioning
that at the price of an
infinitary
syntax, it is quite simple to
reduce it to ordinary
{\it CCS}
. This is similar
in spirit to transformations from predicate logic
to propositional logic where universal and existential quantifications
are replaced by infinitary conjunctions and disjunctions, respectively.
In our case, the basic idea is to replace the input of a value by
the non-deterministic sum of infinitely many inputs:

[\![a(x).P]\!]=\Sigma_{v\in{\it Val}}a_{v}.[\![[v/x]P]\!]~.

(25.9)

Incidentally, for a finite and small set of values this gives an effective
way of programming value passing in basic
{\it CCS}
.

25.5
Summary and references

Labelled bisimulation requires: labels, labelled transitions, and
labelled bisimulation. The choice of the labels and the rules of the
bisimulation game may be hard to justify. On the other hand,
contextual bisimulation requires reduction, static contexts, and
commitments. This approach is more natural but it may be harder to
prove that two processes are contextual bisimilar. For
{\it CCS}
, labelled
bisimulation coincides with contextual bisimulation. In general this
kind of result is a guideline when we are confronted to more
complicated models (such as the
\pi
-calculus in chapter

30
).

{\it CCS}
is a model of message passing based on redez-vous communication
among two processes. Another popular interaction mechanism consists
in allowing several parallel processes to synchronize on the same
label. This mechanism does not scale so well when we want to add more
structure to the actions as,
e.g.
, in value passing
synchronization.

{\it CCS}
has been introduced by Milner in

[
Mil80
]
; a revised
presentation is in
[
Mil95
]
.
The reduction semantics of concurrent systems is put forward
in
[
BB92
]
.
The notion of
contextual
bisimulation
is studied by
[
HY95
]
. The
earlier definition of
barbed equivalence
can be found in

[
MS92
]
.
Exercise
25.4
is based on
[
MM93
]
.

Chapter 26
Testing processes

In this chapter, we discuss an alternative approach to the
notion of process (in-)equivalence which is based on a notion
test
.
The basic ingredients of the approach are as follows:

•

a process
P
is run in parallel with a testing
process
Q
which is of a similar nature and able to interact with
P
,

•

an (internal) computation of
(P\mid Q)
is deemed
successful

if it reaches a configuration where a
certain (simple) predicate
is valid.

We shall cast the technical development in the setting of the
labelled transition systems with synchronisation described in
chapter
25
.
Since the processes we consider are
non-deterministic
, each given test can
produce several computations and one is naturally led to distinguish
two basic situations: the one where at least one computation
is successful and the one where all computations are successful.
In the first case, one says that
P

may
pass the test
Q
while
in the second one says that
P

must
pass the test
Q
.
Once this framework is fixed, it is immediate to define two pre-orders
based on the
may
and the
must
interpretation of the test.
Namely, if
P_{1},P_{2}
are processes then we say that
P_{1}
is
less or equal than

P_{2}
if
every time
P_{1}
may (respectively, must) pass a test,
P_{2}
may
(respectively, must) pass the same test.

Historically, the theory of testing has been developed around
a predicate that asserts a
strong commitment
(in the
sense of section
25.3
) on
a distinguished action
w
. In this case,

P

may
pass the test
Q
means that
(P\mid Q)
may
reduce to a process that strongly commits on
w
while

P

must
pass the test
Q
means that every computation
of
(P\mid Q)
reaches a point where it strongly commits on
w
.
Section
26.2
, characterizes the
induced may and must pre-orders in this case.

Another basic predicate that comes to mind is
termination

intended as the impossibility to perform an internal action.
In this case, may and must have a familiar interpretation:

P

may
pass the test
Q
means that
(P\mid Q)

is
weakly normalizing
and
P

must
pass the test
Q
means
that
(P\mid Q)
is
strongly normalizing
. Section
26.3
,
characterizes the induced may and must pre-orders in this case
and compares them with the traditional ones.

26.1
Testing notation

We recall some standard notations discussed in the previous chapters and
introduce a few more which turn out to be handy in the testing framework.

Actions, transitions, and commitments

We work on the labelled transition systems with synchronisation
described in chapter
25
.
We write
a,b,\ldots
to denote observable
actions (not the internal action
\tau
) varying on a set

{\it Act}
. We also write
s,s^{\prime},\ldots
to denote
finite traces (or words) of observable actions. If
s
is such a trace then

\overline{s}
is the trace obtained by taking the co-actions of
the actions in
s
.
We have the usual
weak transition
relations:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}=(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}})^{*}~,&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}=(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}})\circ(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}})\circ(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}})~,\\
\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon}}{{\Rightarrow}}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}~,&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}}{{\Rightarrow}}=(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{n}}}{{\Rightarrow}})\circ\cdots\circ(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{1}}}{{\Rightarrow}})~.\end{array}

We say that a process
P
is
stable
if it cannot perform an internal reduction.
Given a process
P
, we define:

\begin{array}[]{lll}S(P)&=\{a\in{\it Act}\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\}&\mbox{(strong commitments),}\\
W(P)&=\{a\in{\it Act}\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\}&\mbox{(weak commitments).}\end{array}

In general,
S(P)\subseteq W(P)
and
S(P)=W(P)
if
P
is stable.

We assume the labelled transition systems have a
nil
element that
performs no action and are
closed
under the operations of
action prefix, sum, parallel composition,
and name restriction described in chapter
25
.
In particular, we shall use the CCS notation to describe
certain families of tests and suppose
\Omega
is
a process that loops such as:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Omega&=\nu a\ (\overline{a}\mid{\sf rec}X.a.(\overline{a}\mid X))&\mbox{(definitely looping process)}~.\end{array}

Finite and infinite traces

The set
T(p)
of
finite traces
of a process
P

is defined as:

T(P)=\{s\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\}\qquad\mbox{(set of finite traces).}

We denote with
u,v,\ldots
countably
infinite traces (or words) of observable actions.
If
u
is an infinite trace then let
u[k]
be the action
in position
k
counting from position
1

and
u(k)
be the finite prefix of
u
up
to the
k^{{\it th}}
position included (thus
u(0)
is the empty trace).
If
P
is a process then we write
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
if

P
has an infinite reduction which corresponds to the trace
u

and we define the set
T^{\omega}(P)
of
infinite traces
of the process

P
as:

T^{\omega}(P)=\{u\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}\}\qquad\mbox{(set of infinite traces).}

Hereditary termination

If
P
is a process, we write:

\begin{array}[]{l}P\downarrow\mbox{ if all internal reduction sequences terminate,}\\
P\Downarrow\mbox{ if an internal reduction sequence terminates.}\end{array}

So
\downarrow
corresponds to
must-termination
(or strong normalization)
and
\Downarrow
to
may-termination
(or weak normalization).

We generalize the definition of strong normalization as follows.
If
s
is a sequence of observable actions and
a
an action then:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P\downarrow\epsilon&\mbox{if }P\downarrow\\
P\downarrow as&\mbox{if }P\downarrow\mbox{ and }(P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }P^{\prime}\downarrow s)~.\end{array}

Thus if
P\downarrow s
then all processes that may be reached from
P
performing a prefix of the sequence of (weak) actions
s
terminate.
Notice that
P\downarrow s
does
not
imply that
P
can actually
perform
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}
. For instance,
0\downarrow s
for all
s
.
The hereditary termination predicate is extended to infinite
traces by stating that
P\downarrow u
if for all
k\geq 0
,
P\downarrow u(k)
.

We collect in the sets
C(P)
and
C^{\omega}(P)
the collection of
finite and infinite traces, respectively, along which the process

P
terminates:

\begin{array}[]{llll}C(P)&=\{s\mid P\downarrow s\}~,\qquad\par C^{\omega}(P)&=\{u\mid P\downarrow u\}~.\end{array}

We say that a process
P
is
reactive
if for all sequences of actions

s
we have
P\downarrow s
.

Image finiteness

In certain situations, we shall assume the labelled
transition system is
image finite
(definition

23.5
),
i.e.
,
for all processes
P
and actions
\alpha
we have:

\{P^{\prime}\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\}\mbox{ is finite}\qquad\mbox{(image finiteness).}

By extension, we say that a
process
is
image finite

if the portion of the lts reachable from the process is image finite.
Image finite lts are a
sweet spot
for certain testing
pre-orders as they are the lts on which they can be
characterized in a
finitary
sense
(recall that in section
24.1

we already relied on image finiteness to obtain a finitary logical
description of bisimulation).
Notice that we do
not
assume the lts is finitely branching,
i.e.
,
the set
\{\alpha\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\}
can be infinite.

26.2
Testing strong commitment

We suppose the collection of actions contains a
special
observable action

w
. There is no co-action for this action. We
now call
tests
(all) the elements of the lts and call
processes
those tests whose derivatives cannot perform the special
action
w
. In this section, we shall reserve the
letters
P,Q
for processes and the
letter
R
for the (more general) tests,
i.e.
, the processes
that may strongly commit on the special action
w
.

Testing pre-orders

A
computation
of a test
R_{0}
is a possibly infinite sequence of internal reductions:

R_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}R_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}R_{n}\cdots

A computation of a test
R_{0}
is
successful

if there is an
i\in\mathbf{N}
such that
R_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
.
We write:

\begin{array}[]{ll}R\ {\sf may}&\mbox{if some computation starting from }R\mbox{ is successful.}\\
R\ {\sf must}&\mbox{if every computation starting from }R\mbox{ is successful.}\end{array}

Thus tests are partitioned in three sets: (i) those that must,
(ii) those that may but must not, and (iii) those that may not
(this last set contains all (ordinary) processes).
The relevance of a test depends very much on the
the way it is interpreted (may or must).
For instance, consider the test
R=\tau.w
. Then

(P\mid R){\sf may}
is always true while
(P\mid R){\sf must}

is true if and only if
P
terminates.

Definition 26.1
(
{\sf may}
,
{\sf must}
pre-orders)

If
P,Q
are processes (not tests) then we define:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P\leq_{{\sf may}}Q&\mbox{if for all tests }R\ ((P\mid R){\sf may}\mbox{ implies }(Q\mid R){\sf may})~,\\
P\leq_{{\sf must}}Q&\mbox{if for all tests }R\ ((P\mid R){\sf must}\mbox{ implies }(Q\mid R){\sf must})~.\end{array}

Proposition 26.1

May and must pre-orders are preserved by parallel composition.

Proof
. Suppose
P_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}P_{2}
and
Q_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}Q_{2}
.
Then for all tests
R
,
(Q_{1}\mid R)
is also a test
and parallel composition is associative. So

((P_{1}\mid Q_{1})\mid R){\sf may}
implies

((P_{2}\mid Q_{1})\mid R){\sf may}
, and
we derive
(P_{1}\mid Q_{1})\leq_{{\sf may}}(P_{2}\mid Q_{1})
.
In the same way, we prove that

(Q_{1}\mid P_{2})\leq_{{\sf may}}(Q_{2}\mid P_{2})
.
Then using commutativity of parallel composition and
transitivity, we conclude
(P_{1}\mid Q_{1})\leq_{{\sf may}}(P_{2}\mid Q_{2})
.
The same argument applies to the must pre-order.
{\Box}

A remark on specification

Suppose
S
represents a specification process and
I
a corresponding
implementation. When using
{\sf may}
testing we typically aim to prove:

I\leq_{{\sf may}}S\qquad\mbox{(${\sf may}$ requirement)}

namely all tests that may be passed by the implementation are passed by
the specification too.
When using
{\sf must}
testing we aim to prove:

S\leq_{{\sf must}}I\qquad\mbox{(${\sf must}$ requirement)}

namely if the specification must pass a test then the implementation must pass
the test too.
Informally, one could say that the
{\sf may}
requirement is fixing the

rights
of the implementation while the
{\sf must}
requirement
is fixing its
duties
.

In general, may and must testing are incomparable. For instance,
consider:

\begin{array}[]{llll}P=a.(b+c)~,&Q=a.b+a.c~,&P\leq_{{\sf may}}Q~,&P\not\leq_{{\sf must}}Q~,\\
P=a+\Omega~,&Q=b+\Omega~,&P\not\leq_{{\sf may}}Q~,&P\leq_{{\sf must}}Q~.\end{array}

However, we shall see that if the specification
S
satisfies
an hereditary termination predicate then the must requirement

S\leq_{{\sf must}}I
implies the may requirement
I\leq_{{\sf may}}S
(notice
the inversion of the order).

It is also worth noting that in general, the must testing pre-order is incomparable with simulation too (cf. definition
23.3
).
For instance, consider:

\begin{array}[]{llll}P_{1}=a.(b.c+b.d),&Q_{1}=a.b.c+a.b.d,&P_{1}=_{\sf must}Q_{1},&P_{1}\not\leq_{{\sf sim}}Q_{1},\\
P_{2}=a.b,&Q_{2}=a.b+a,&P_{2}\not\leq_{{\sf must}}Q_{2},&P_{2}=_{{\sf sim}}Q_{2}~.\end{array}

May testing and trace inclusion

The characterization of may testing as trace inclusion is quite direct.

Definition 26.2
(test for
{\sf may}
)

Suppose
s
is a finite sequence of observable actions.
We define the family of tests
R_{0}(s)
as:

R_{0}(s)=\overline{s}.w
.

Proposition 26.2

Suppose
P,Q
are processes and

s
is a finite sequence of actions. Then:

1.

P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot
iff
(P\mid R_{0}(s)){\sf may}
.

2.

P\leq_{{\sf may}}Q
iff
T(P)\subseteq T(Q)
.

Proof
.

1.

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}
then we have a successful computation:

(P\mid R_{0}(s))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
.
On the other hand, if we have a successful computation of
(P\mid R_{0}(s))

then necessarily
R_{0}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\rightarrow}}w
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.

2.

Suppose
P\leq_{{\sf may}}Q
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}
. Then, by the previous assertion:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}(P\mid R_{0}(s)){\sf may}&\mbox{ implies}&(Q\mid R_{0}(s)){\sf may}&\mbox{ implies}&Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot~.\end{array}

On the other hand, suppose
T(P)\subseteq T(Q)
,

R
is a test, and
(P\mid R){\sf may}
.
This means that for some
s
,
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}
,
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}R_{1}
and

R_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
.
Since
s\in T(P)
we also have
s\in T(Q)
.
Hence
(Q\mid R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}(Q_{1}\mid R_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
,
that is
(Q\mid R){\sf may}
.
{\Box}

Testing hereditary termination

A process
P
terminates iff
(P\mid\tau.w){\sf must}
.
Thus in a sense must testing is a generalization of termination.

Definition 26.3
(hereditary termination test)

If
s
is a finite sequence of actions then we define
an hereditary termination test
R_{1}(s)
as:

R_{1}(s)=(\overline{s}.0\mid\tau.w)~.

Proposition 26.3

Suppose
P,Q
are processes and
s
is a finite sequence of actions.
Then:

1.

P\downarrow s
iff
(P\mid(\overline{s}.0\mid\tau.w)){\sf must}
.

2.

If
P\leq_{{\sf must}}Q
and
P\downarrow s
then
Q\downarrow s
.

3.

If
P\leq_{{\sf must}}Q
and
P
is reactive then
Q
is reactive.

Proof
. We prove the first assertion
by induction on
|s|
. If
s=\epsilon
, we verify:

P\downarrow\epsilon\mbox{ iff }(P\mid\tau.w){\sf must}~.

If
s=a\cdot s_{1}
then by definition:

P\downarrow s\mbox{ iff }P\downarrow\mbox{ and }\forall P_{1}\ (P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}\mbox{ implies }P_{1}\downarrow s_{1})~.

By induction hypothesis on
s_{1}
, this can be rewritten as:

P\downarrow\mbox{ and }\forall P_{1}\ (P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}\mbox{ implies }(P_{1}\mid R_{1}(s_{1})){\sf must})~,

and this is equivalent to:

(P\mid R_{1}(s)){\sf must}~.

Assertions 2 and 3 follow immediately by definition of
must-testing.
{\Box}

Must set predicates

Hereditary termination is essential but not quite enough to characterize the
must pre-order. For instance, it identifies all reactive processes.
With this motivation, we introduce a second family of tests
that we call must-set
tests
since they relate to a must-set

predicate
we introduce next.

Definition 26.4
(must-set tests)

Suppose
s
is a finite sequence of actions
and
B
a
finite set of actions. We define a family
of tests
R_{2}(s,B)
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{llll}R_{2}(\epsilon,B)&=\Sigma_{a\in B}\ \overline{a}.w~,&R_{2}(b\cdot s,B)&=\tau.w+\overline{b}.R_{2}(s,B)~.\par\end{array}

Remark 26.1

If
B\cap\overline{B}=\emptyset
then we can also define:

\begin{array}[]{ll}R_{2}(s,B)&=\nu d\ \overline{d}.0\mid d.w\mid\overline{s}.d.\Pi_{a\in B}\ \overline{a}.w~.\end{array}

To characterize the processes that pass the must-set
tests
,
we introduce a family of must-set
predicates
.
The predicate
M(P,s,B)
holds
if whenever
P
performs the sequence of actions
s
it
must

perform some action in
B
(
B
is the
must-set
that justifies
the name of the test and the predicate).

Definition 26.5
(must-set predicates)

Let
P
be a process,

s\in{\it Act}^{*}
, and
B\subseteq_{{\it fin}}{\it Act}
. We write:

M(P,s,B)\mbox{ if }\forall P^{\prime}\ (P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }W(P^{\prime})\cap B\neq\emptyset)~.

Proposition 26.4

Let
P
be a process,

s\in{\it Act}^{*}
, and
B\subseteq_{{\it fin}}{\it Act}
. Then:

P\downarrow s
and
M(P,s,B)
iff
(P\mid R_{2}(s,b)){\sf must}
.

Proof
. By induction on the length of
s
.
If
s=\epsilon
we have:

\begin{array}[]{lll}(P\mid R_{2}(\epsilon,B)){\sf must}&\mbox{iff}&P\downarrow\mbox{ and }\forall P^{\prime}\ (P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }W(P^{\prime})\cap B\neq\emptyset)\\
&\mbox{iff}&P\downarrow\epsilon\mbox{ and }M(P,\epsilon,B)~.\end{array}

If
s=as_{1}
we have:

\begin{array}[]{lll}(P\mid R_{2}(s,B)){\sf must}&\mbox{iff}&P\downarrow\mbox{ and }\forall P^{\prime}\ (P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }(P^{\prime}\mid R_{2}(s_{1},B)){\sf must})\\
&\mbox{iff}&P\downarrow\mbox{ and }\forall P^{\prime}\ (P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }(P^{\prime}\downarrow s_{1}\mbox{ and }M(P^{\prime},s_{1},B))\\
&\mbox{iff}&P\downarrow s\mbox{ and }M(P,s,B)~.\end{array}

{\Box}

By combining the tests
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
we see that whenever

P\leq_{{\sf must}}Q
we have the following properties:

1.

For all
s
, if
P\downarrow s
then
Q\downarrow s
.

2.

For all
s,B
, if
P\downarrow s
and
M(P,s,B)
then

Q\downarrow s
and
M(Q,s,B)
.

By logical manipulation of the conjunction of the two implications,
we can eliminate
two occurrences of the hereditary termination predicate and arrive at
the following definition and proposition.

Definition 26.6
(must-set pre-order)

Let
P,Q
be processes. We write
P\leq_{M}Q
if
for all
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
such that
P\downarrow s

the following two properties hold:

1.

Q\downarrow s
.

2.

for all
B\subseteq_{{\it fin}}{\it Act}
if
M(P,s,B)

then
M(Q,s,B)
.

Proposition 26.5

Let
P,Q
be processes. If
P\leq_{{\sf must}}Q
then
P\leq_{M}Q
.

Proof
. By the definition of must pre-order and propositions
26.3

and
26.4
.
{\Box}

Remark 26.2

Consider the reactive processes:

P=\Sigma_{n\geq 0}\ a.b_{n}
and
Q=a.0
.
Then for all finite sets
B
, we have
\neg M(P,a,B)

since
P
after doing
a
can do one out of infinitely
many actions and therefore no finite
B
can cover all of them.
We conclude that
P=_{M}Q
and we have an example
of how the hypothesis that the must-set
B
is finite
limits the discriminating
power of these tests on lts which are not image finite.

It is possible to give an alternative definition of the must-set pre-order
in which we require that the process reaches a
stable
configuration and
then its
strong
commitments intersect the actions in
B
.
In general, this is a weaker condition but equivalent to the one
we have given when combined with hereditary termination.

Definition 26.7
(strong-stable must-set predicate)

Let
P
be a process,

s\in{\it Act}^{*}
, and
B\subseteq_{{\it fin}}{\it Act}
. We write:

M_{S}(P,s,B)
if
\forall P^{\prime}\ (P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
implies

S(P^{\prime})\cap B\neq\emptyset)
.

Proposition 26.6

1.

If
M(P,s,B)
then
M_{S}(P,s,B)
.

2.

If
M_{S}(P,s,X)
and
P\downarrow s
then
M(P,s,X)
.

Exercise 26.1

Prove proposition
26.6
.

Remark 26.3

Consider the recursive process
P={\sf rec}X.\tau.X+a.X
and
the empty trace
s=\epsilon
. Then
M_{S}(P,s,B)
for any
B
,
since
P
cannot reduce to a stable process. On the other hand,
if
a\notin B
then
M(P,s,B)
is false.

A modal logic view

We take a modal logic view at the predicates we have been
defining to characterize the may and must testing pre-orders.
Consider the modal logic on actions with box and diamond modalities
introduced in chapter
24
.
If
a\in{\it Act}
and
A
is a modal formula then recall that:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P\models\langle a\rangle A\mbox{ if }\exists P^{\prime}(P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mbox{ and }P^{\prime}\models A)~,&P\models[a]A\mbox{ if }\forall P^{\prime}(P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }P^{\prime}\models A)~.\end{array}

Notice that the modalities are interpreted
weakly
.
Weak modalities can be defined starting from strong modalities and fixed
points.
Suppose
F
is a collection of formulae. If
P
is a process
its interpretation relative to
F
is the set of formulae in
F

that satisfy it:

[\![P]\!]^{F}=\{A\in F\mid P\models A\}~,

and the induced pre-order on processes is:

P\leq_{F}Q
if
[\![P]\!]^{F}\subseteq[\![Q]\!]^{F}
.
If one regards the formulae as ‘tests’, then we are again saying that
all the tests passed by
P
are passed by
Q
too.
We look for collections of modal formulas
F_{{\sf trace}}
and
F_{{\sf must-set}}

that correspond to the trace inclusion and must-set pre-orders.
For trace inclusion, we can just take:

F_{{\sf trace}}=\{\langle s\rangle{\sf true}\mid s\in{\it Act}^{*}\}~,

with the obvious extension of the diamond modality to sequences of
actions.
The situation for the must-set pre-order is a bit more complex.
First, to express termination we need a least fixed point:

\downarrow=\mu X.([\tau]{\sf false}\vee[\tau]X)~.

Then hereditary termination becomes for
s=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}\in{\it Act}^{*}
:

\begin{array}[]{llll}\downarrow\epsilon&=\downarrow~,\qquad\downarrow as&=\downarrow\and\ [a]\downarrow s~.\end{array}

We can write this in more compact form by introducing a box modality,
say
[~]_{\downarrow}
,
on sequences of actions which is sensitive to termination. So define:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P\models[\epsilon]_{\downarrow}A&\mbox{if }P\downarrow\mbox{ and }P\models A~,\\
P\models[as]_{\downarrow}A&\mbox{if }P\downarrow\mbox{ and }\forall P^{\prime}(P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\mbox{ implies }P^{\prime}\models[s]_{\downarrow}A)~.\end{array}

Then hereditary termination becomes:

\downarrow s=[s]_{\downarrow}{\sf true}
.
The second basic condition we have to express is:

P\downarrow s\and M(P,s,B)~,

which can be written as:
[s]_{\downarrow}\langle B\rangle{\sf true}
,
where
\langle B\rangle{\sf true}=\bigvee_{a\in B}\langle a\rangle{\sf true}
.
So we define:

F_{{\sf must-set}}=\{[s]_{\downarrow}A\mid A\in\{{\sf true},\langle B\rangle{\sf true}\},s\in{\it Act}^{*},B\subseteq_{{\it fin}}{\it Act}\}~.

When processes are
reactive
,
[s]_{\downarrow}A
can be simply written
as
[s]A
and since
[s]{\sf true}
is equivalent to
{\sf true}
,
the collection of relevant formulae boils down to:

\{[s]\langle B\rangle{\sf true}\mid s\in{\it Act}^{*},B\subseteq_{{\it fin}}{\it Act}\}~,

and we see that the must-set predicate can be regarded as a kind of
box-diamond modal formula (box on a sequence of actions and diamond on a finite
set of actions). Notice that taking
B=\emptyset
, we have:

[s]\langle\emptyset\rangle{\sf true}=[s]{\sf false}=\neg\langle s\rangle{\sf true}~.

Hence, in the reactive case,

P\leq_{F_{{\sf must-set}}}Q
implies
Q\leq_{F_{{\sf trace}}}P

(the following proposition
26.11
, gives another
presentation of this result).

Acceptance sets

Acceptance sets provide an alternative to the must-set approach.

Definition 26.8
(acceptance set)

Let
P
be a process and
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
a sequence of actions.
The acceptance set
A(P,s)
is a set of sets of
observable actions:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}A(P,s)&=\{W(P^{\prime})\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\}~.\par\end{array}

To determine
A(P,s)
, we consider all processes
P^{\prime}
such that

P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
and
then we compute the set of weak commitments
W(P^{\prime})
.
Since we do
not
assume finite branching,
in general the sets of commitments
S(P^{\prime})
and
W(P^{\prime})
can be infinite.
On the other hand, assuming
image finiteness
,
if
P\downarrow s
then the set

\{P^{\prime}\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\}

is finite.

To compare acceptance sets, we introduce the following
pre-order
.

Definition 26.9
(pre-order on acceptance sets)

Let
A_{1},A_{2}
be acceptance sets. Then:

A_{1}\leq_{A}A_{2}\mbox{ if }\forall Y\in A_{2}\ \exists X\in A_{1}\ (X\subseteq Y)~.

As for the must-set predicate, there is an alternative definition
of acceptance sets that relies on stability and strong commitment.

Definition 26.10
(stable-strong acceptance sets)

Let
P
be a process and
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
a sequence of actions.
The stable strong acceptance set
A_{S}(P,s)
is a set of sets of
observable actions:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}A_{S}(P,s)&=\{S(P^{\prime})\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\}~.\par\end{array}

We use the pre-order on acceptance sets to compare the two definitions
which are equivalent when combined with hereditary termination.

Proposition 26.7

Let
P
be a process and
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
a trace. Then:

1.

A(P,s)\leq_{A}A_{S}(P,s)
.

2.

If
P\downarrow s
then
A_{S}(P,s)\leq_{A}A(P,s)
.

Exercise 26.2

Prove proposition
26.7
.

Remark 26.4

Consider again the recursive process
P={\sf rec}X.\tau.X+a.X
and the
empty trace
s=\epsilon
. Then

A_{S}(P,s)=\emptyset
,
A(P,s)=\{\{a\}\}
, and

\{\{a\}\}\leq_{A}\emptyset
but

\emptyset\not\leq_{A}\{\{a\}\}
.

It remains to relate the must-set predicate with the
acceptance sets. First, we notice that the pre-order on
acceptance sets implies the inclusion of the must-set predicates.

Proposition 26.8

Let
P,Q
be processes and
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
a sequence of
observable actions. Then
A(P,s)\leq_{A}A(Q,s)

implies that for all finite set of actions
B
,

M(P,s,B)
implies
M(Q,s,B)
.

Exercise 26.3

Prove proposition
26.8
.

The other direction is a bit more delicate as it appeals to the
hereditary termination predicate and image finiteness.

Proposition 26.9

Let
P
be an image-finite processes,
Q
a process, and
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
.
Further suppose
P\downarrow s
and
for all
B
finite set of actions,
M(P,s,B)
implies
M(Q,s,B)
.
Then
A(P,s)\leq_{A}A(Q,s)
.

Exercise 26.4

Prove proposition
26.9
.

The example in remark
26.2
shows
that the hypothesis that
P
is image finite is needed.
In view of propositions
26.8
and
26.9
,
we can rephrase definition
26.6

using acceptance sets and derive the following corollary.

Definition 26.11
(acceptance set pre-order)

Let
P,Q
be processes. We write
P\leq_{A}Q
if
for all
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
such that
P\downarrow s

the following two properties hold:

1.

Q\downarrow s
.

2.

A(P,s)\leq_{A}A(Q,s)
.

Corollary 26.1

Let
P,Q
be processes such that
P
is image-finite. Then

P\leq_{M}Q
iff
P\leq_{A}Q
.

Hereditary termination and infinite traces

Trace inclusion, combined with reactivity and image finiteness,
suffices to guarantee
infinite
trace inclusion.

Proposition 26.10

Let
P,Q
be processes such that
T(P)\subseteq T(Q)

and
Q
is image finite.

1.

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
and
Q\downarrow u
then
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.

2.

If
Q
is reactive then
T^{\omega}(P)\subseteq T^{\omega}(Q)
.

Proof
.

1.

From
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
,
u
infinite trace, we derive:

\forall k\ (u(k)\in T(P)\subseteq T(Q))
.
Build the following rooted tree.

•

Put the process
Q
at the root. This is the only node at level
0
.

•

For each node
n
at level
i
associated with say process
Q^{\prime}
,
compute
{{\cal P}}=\{Q^{\prime\prime}\mid Q^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u[i+1]}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime\prime}\}
.
For each process in
Q^{\prime\prime}
in
{{\cal P}}
, introduce a new node
n^{\prime}
at level
i+1

associated with
Q^{\prime\prime}
and an edge from
n
to
n^{\prime}
.

Paths in the tree from the root to a node at level
i
,
i\geq 0
,
correspond to a reduction of process
Q
labelled with
u[1]\cdots u[i]
.
The tree has infinitely many nodes but it is finitely branching because

Q
is image finite and
Q\downarrow u
.
By proposition
2.6
(König’s lemma), there must be an infinite path which
corresponds to an infinite reduction
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.

2.

If
Q
is reactive then
Q\downarrow u
and the previous
assertion applies.
{\Box}

Next we consider the relationship between
the must-set pre-order and (infinite) trace inclusion.

Proposition 26.11

1.

If
P\leq_{M}Q
,
P\downarrow s
, and
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot
then

P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot
.

2.

If
P\leq_{M}Q
,
P\downarrow u
,
P
is image finite,
and
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
then
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.

3.

If
P
is reactive and image finite and
P\leq_{M}Q
then

T(Q)\subseteq T(P)
and
T^{\omega}(Q)\subseteq T^{\omega}(P)
.

Exercise 26.5

Prove proposition
26.11
.

Remark 26.5

Proposition
26.11
may fail without the
image-finiteness assumption. Consider:

\begin{array}[]{lll}P=\Sigma_{n\geq 0}\ a^{n}.0~,&Q=a^{\omega}~,&R=\overline{a}^{\omega}\mid\tau.w~.\end{array}

Then
P,Q
are reactive,
P\leq_{M}Q
, and
T(P)=T(Q)
.
However,
T^{\omega}(Q)\not\subseteq T^{\omega}(P)
and

(P\mid R){\sf must}
while
(Q\mid R){\sf mustnot}
.

We have noticed (proposition
26.11
(1))
that if
P
is a reactive process
then
P\leq_{M}Q
implies
T(Q)\subseteq T(P)
.
What about the other trace inclusion? In general, this fails;
for instance, consider
P=a.b+a.c
and
Q=a.b
.
However, if
P
behaves in a rather
determinate way

(in chapter
27
, definition
27.1
will propose a stronger definition of determinate process)
then we have the following proposition.

Proposition 26.12

Suppose
P,Q
are processes,
P
is reactive, and for all traces
s
,

P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1},P_{2}
implies
W(P_{1})=W(P_{2})
. Then
P\leq_{M}Q
implies

T(P)=T(Q)
.

Exercise 26.6

Prove proposition
26.12
.

Remark 26.6

Notice that we may well have reactive processes

P,Q
such that
T(P)=T(Q)
,
P
is determinate, and

P\not\leq_{M}Q
. For instance, consider again
P=a.b
and
Q=a.b+a
.
On the other hand, it is easy to check that
if both
P
and
Q
are reactive and determinate
then
T(P)=T(Q)
implies
P=_{M}Q
.

Hereditary termination and must set tests are enough

In image finite lts, the following
key property
holds:
the simple
finite
tests for hereditary termination (definition

26.3
) and must-sets (definition
26.4
)
suffice to must-test processes.
Before proving this result, we notice that
the property of must-passing a test is
not
stable under reduction.
For instance, consider
R=0\mid w.0+\tau.0
. Then

R\ {\sf must}
but
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}R^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}=(0\mid 0){\sf mustnot}
.
The following predicate is helpful in describing this situation
and is used in the proof of proposition

26.14
.

Definition 26.12
(unsuccessful predicate)

Given a test
R
and a finite trace
s
,
we write
U(R,s,R^{\prime})
if there is some sequence
of labelled transitions such that
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}R^{\prime}

and all the tests traversed (including
R
and
R^{\prime}
)
cannot perform immediately the
w
action.
If
u
is an infinite trace we write
U(R,u)
if there is
an infinite reduction starting from
R
and labelled with
u

which never crosses a test (including
R
)
that can perform immediately the
w
action.

In other terms,
U(R,s,R^{\prime})
can be read as:
there is an
unsuccessful
transition labelled

s
from
R
to
R^{\prime}
(and similarly for
U(R,u)
).

Proposition 26.13

Suppose
P
is a process and
R
is a test. If
(P\mid R){\sf must}

and
U(R,\overline{s},R^{\prime})
then (i)
P\downarrow s
and (ii)
if
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then
(P^{\prime}\mid R^{\prime}){\sf must}
.

Proof
. By induction on the length of the sequence
s
.
If
s=\epsilon
then
U(R,\epsilon,R^{\prime})
implies
R\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
.
Therefore
(P\mid R){\sf must}
implies
P\downarrow\epsilon
and
if
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then
(P^{\prime}\mid R^{\prime}){\sf must}
.

If
s=as_{1}
then
U(R,\overline{as_{1}},R^{\prime})
implies there is some
R_{1}
such
that
U(R,\overline{a},R_{1})
and
U(R_{1},\overline{s_{1}},R^{\prime})
.
As in the basic case,
R\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
implies
P\downarrow\epsilon
.
Suppose
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}
. Then
(P\mid R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}(P_{1}\mid R_{1})

via a sequence of reductions where the test is
not
successful.
Therefore
(P\mid R){\sf must}
implies
(P_{1}\mid R_{1}){\sf must}

and by inductive hypothesis we conclude
P_{1}\downarrow s_{1}

and if
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then
(P^{\prime}\mid R^{\prime}){\sf must}
.
{\Box}

Proposition 26.14
(soundness)

Let
P,Q
be processes such that
P
is image finite.
If
P\leq_{M}Q
then
P\leq_{{\sf must}}Q
.

Proof
. Let
R
be a test such that
(Q\mid R){\sf mustnot}
,
that is there is an unsuccessful computation of
(Q\mid R)
.
We show how to derive an unsuccessful computation of
(P\mid R)
.
We distinguish two cases.

•

The computation is finite and the process
Q
performs a
finite sequence of observable actions
s
such that:

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1},R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}R_{1},(Q\mid R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}(Q_{1}\mid R_{1})\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}~.

From
(P\mid R){\sf must}
and
U(R,\overline{s},R_{1})
we derive using
proposition
26.13
that
P\downarrow s
.
By definition of the must-set pre-order, it follows that

Q\downarrow s
.
By proposition
26.9
, it follows that there exists
P_{1}
such
that
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}
and
W(P_{1})\subseteq W(Q_{1})
.
Hence
(P\mid R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}(P_{1}\mid R_{1})
and
(P_{1}\mid R_{1}){\sf mustnot}

since
R_{1}
is stuck and
P_{1}
cannot synchronize with it.

•

The computation is infinite. We distinguish three cases.

–

In the unsuccessful computation of
(Q\mid R)
, the test
R

performs a finite number of steps and then stabilizes.
So for some
s
, we have
U(R,\overline{s},R_{1})
and
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}
.
Now we show that
(P\mid R){\sf must}
leads to a contradiction.
Indeed, from
(P\mid R){\sf must}
we derive
P\downarrow s
.
Since
P\leq_{M}Q
we must have
Q\downarrow s
. But this
contradicts the hypothesis that the process
Q_{1}
has a diverging
internal computation.

–

In the unsuccessful computation of
(Q\mid R)
, the test
R
performs a finite
number of interactions with
Q
and then infinitely many internal steps.
Again for some
s
, we have
U(R,\overline{s},R_{1})
,
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}
,
and starting from
R_{1}
we have an infinite internal reduction.
As before, assuming
(P\mid R){\sf must}
produces a contradiction
as it implies that
P\downarrow s
. Then, since

P\leq_{M}Q
, we have
Q\downarrow s
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}
.
But then
(P\mid R){\sf mustnot}
.

–

It remains the interesting case where
Q
and
R
engage in infinitely
many interactions. So there is an infinite trace
u
of observable
actions such that
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
and
U(R,\overline{u})
.
If for some
k
,
P\not\downarrow u(k)
we can conclude directly
that
(P\mid R){\sf mustnot}
.
Otherwise, we have
P\downarrow u
. Also since
P\leq_{M}Q
,
by proposition
26.11
(1), we have

\forall k(P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u(k)}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot)
.
So we are in a situation where
P\downarrow u
and

\forall k(P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u(k)}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot)
and
proposition
26.11
(2) applies to conclude that

P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
and
(P\mid R){\sf mustnot}
.
{\Box}

26.3
Testing termination (*)

We consider a variant of the may and must pre-orders where
we rely on the termination predicate rather than on the
strong commitment predicate.
To stress this change of predicate, we
shall denote the resulting may and must pre-orders
with
\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}
and
\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}
(
t
for termination)
while keeping the notation

\leq_{{\sf may}}
and
\leq_{{\sf must}}
for those introduced in the
previous section.

Definition 26.13
(may and must termination pre-orders)

Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be processes. We write:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}P_{2}&\mbox{if }\forall Q\ ((P_{1}\mid Q)\Downarrow\mbox{ implies }(P_{2}\mid Q)\Downarrow)~,\\
P_{1}\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}P_{2}&\mbox{if }\forall Q\ ((P_{1}\mid Q)\downarrow\mbox{ implies }(P_{2}\mid Q)\downarrow)~.\end{array}

Notice that in this section there is no distinction between processes
and tests and they are both denoted with
P,Q,\ldots
.

May termination testing

First, we look at the may termination testing pre-order. This
is incomparable with the ordinary may testing pre-order as it is actually
sensitive to both termination and deadlock.

Definition 26.14
(tests for may termination)

Let
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
be a finite trace and
B
a finite set of
observable actions.
We define the following two families of tests where
a
is a fresh name:

\begin{array}[]{llll}R_{1}(s)&=\nu a\ \overline{s}.a\mid\overline{a}\mid a.\Omega~,&R_{2}(s,B)&=\nu a\ \overline{s}.a.\Sigma_{b\in B}\ \overline{b}.\Omega\mid\overline{a}\mid a.\Omega~.\end{array}

Intuitively to avoid divergence,
the test
R_{1}(s)
has to run completely
\overline{s}.a
,
while the test
R_{2}(s,B)
has to run completely
\overline{s}.a

and then it must
not
run actions in
\overline{B}
.

Proposition 26.15

Let
P
be a process,
s
a trace, and
B
a finite set of
observable actions. Then:

1.

(P\mid R_{1}(s))\Downarrow
iff
\exists P^{\prime}(P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}})
.

2.

(P\mid R_{2}(s,B))\Downarrow
iff
\exists P^{\prime}(P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\mbox{ and }S(P^{\prime})\cap B=\emptyset)
.

Proof
.

1.

(\Leftarrow)
We have:

(P\mid R_{1}(s))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}(P^{\prime}\mid\nu a\ a.\Omega)\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}~.

(\Rightarrow)
We must have
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}
so
that:

(P\mid R_{1}(s))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}(P_{1}\mid\nu a\ a.\Omega)~,

and then we must have
P_{1}\Downarrow
.

2.

(\Leftarrow)
We have:

(P\mid R_{2}(s,B))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}(P^{\prime}\mid\Sigma_{b\in B}\ \overline{b}.\Omega\mid\nu a\ a.\Omega)\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}~.

(\Rightarrow)
We must have
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}
so that:

(P\mid R_{2}(s,B))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}(P_{1}\mid\Sigma_{b\in B}\ \overline{b}.\Omega\mid\nu a\ a.\Omega)~,

and
P_{1}
must reduce to a
P^{\prime}
such that
P^{\prime}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
and

S(P^{\prime})\cap B=\emptyset
.
{\Box}

The definition
26.10
of
strong stable acceptance sets turns out to be
relevant to characterize the may termination pre-order.
We recall that if
P_{1},P_{2}
are processes and
s
is a finite trace
then
A_{S}(P_{2},s)\leq_{A}A_{S}(P_{1},s)

means that for all
P^{\prime}_{1}
such that
P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{1}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
,
there exists
P^{\prime}_{2}
such that
P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{2}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}

and
S(P^{\prime}_{2})\subseteq S(P^{\prime}_{1})
.

Proposition 26.16

Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be processes such that

P_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}P_{2}
and
P_{2}
is image finite.
Then for all
s\in{\it Act}^{*}
,

A_{S}(P_{2},s)\leq_{A}A_{S}(P_{1},s)
.

Proof
. If
P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{1}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
then by proposition

26.15
(1), there is some
P^{\prime}_{2}
such that

P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{2}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
.
Reasoning by contradiction, suppose
for all such
P^{\prime}_{2}
we have
S(P^{\prime}_{2})\not\subseteq S(P^{\prime}_{1})
.
Then using image finiteness, let
B
be a finite set built by choosing
an action in each
S(P^{\prime}_{2})\backslash S(P^{\prime}_{1})
. Then

(P_{1}\mid R_{2}(s,B))\Downarrow
while
(P_{2}\mid R_{2}(s,B))\not\Downarrow
.
{\Box}

Proposition 26.17

Suppose for all
s
,
A_{S}(P_{2},s)\leq_{A}A_{S}(P_{1},s)
. Then

P_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}P_{2}
.

Proof
. Suppose
(P_{1}\mid Q)\Downarrow
. Then, for some
s
we have

P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{1}
,
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
and
(P^{\prime}_{1}\mid Q^{\prime})\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
.
By hypothesis, there must be a
P^{\prime}_{2}
such that
P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{2}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
and
S(P^{\prime}_{2})\subseteq S(P^{\prime}_{1})
. Hence
P^{\prime}_{2}\mid Q^{\prime}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}
and

(P_{2}\mid Q)\Downarrow
.
{\Box}

In general, the pre-orders
\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}
and
\leq_{{\sf must}}
are
incomparable since the former requires weak normalization while the
latter requires the strong one
(concrete example available in table
26.1
). However, if
we consider image finite and reactive processes then
may termination is the inverse of
ordinary
must testing.

Proposition 26.18

Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be reactive processes such that
P_{2}
is image finite.
Then:

P_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}P_{2}\mbox{ iff }P_{2}\leq_{{\sf must}}P_{1}~.

Proof
. By propositions
26.16
and
26.17
, we know (assuming
P_{2}
is image finite):

P_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}P_{2}\mbox{ iff }\forall s\ (A_{S}(P_{2},s)\leq_{A}A_{S}(P_{1},s))~.

On the other hand, if
P_{1},P_{2}
are reactive and
P_{2}
is image finite
then the ordinary must pre-order coincides with the
pre-order on the strong, stable acceptance sets:

\begin{array}[]{lll}P_{2}\leq_{{\sf must}}P_{1}&\mbox{iff }P_{2}\leq_{M}P_{1}&\mbox{(propositions \ref{must-implies-must-set-prop} and \ref{soundness-acceptance-prop})}\\
&\mbox{iff }P_{2}\leq_{A}P_{1}&\mbox{(corollary \ref{must-set-acc-set-prop})}\\
&\mbox{iff }\forall s\ (A_{S}(P_{2},s)\leq_{A}A_{S}(P_{1},s))&\mbox{(proposition \ref{acc-set-strong-stable-prop}).}\end{array}

Notice that the reactivity of
P_{2}
is required; for instance,

0\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}\tau+\Omega
while
0\not\leq_{{\sf must}}\tau+\Omega
.
{\Box}

Must termination testing

We turn to the must termination pre-order. This is strictly weaker
than the ordinary must pre-order.

Proposition 26.19

Let
P_{1},P_{2}
