be processes. If

P_{1}\leq_{{\sf must}}P_{2}
then
P_{1}\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}P_{2}
.

Proof
. Suppose
(P_{1}\mid Q)\downarrow
. This is equivalent to

(P_{1}\mid Q\mid\tau.w){\sf must}
, which by hypothesis implies

(P_{2}\mid Q\mid\tau.w){\sf must}
, which is equivalent
to
(P_{2}\mid Q)\downarrow
.
{\Box}

Remark 26.7

In general, the

4
pre-orders
\leq_{{\sf may}}
,
\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}
,
\leq_{{\sf must}}
,
and
\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}
are all pairwise incomparable
except in the case considered
in the proposition
26.19
above.
Counter-examples to the remaining
11
inclusions are given in table

26.1
.
For instance, read the first line as stating: (i) in the
\not\subset
column,
that
a.b\leq_{{\sf may}}a.b+a
while
a.b\not\leq_{{\sf must}}a.b+a
and (ii) in the

\not\supset
column, that
a+\Omega\leq_{{\sf must}}b+\Omega
while

a+\Omega\not\leq_{{\sf may}}b+\Omega
.

\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}&&\not\subset&\not\supset\\
\hline\cr\leq_{{\sf may}}&\leq_{{\sf must}}&a.b,a.b+a&a+\Omega,b+\Omega\\
\leq_{{\sf may}}&\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}&a.b+a,a.b&a\mid\Omega,b\mid\Omega\\
\leq_{{\sf may}}&\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}&0,\tau+\Omega&a+\Omega,b+\Omega\\
\leq_{{\sf must}}&\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}&\tau+\Omega,\Omega&0,\tau+\Omega\\
\leq_{{\sf must}}&\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}&\mbox{(holds!)}&a.b,a.b+a\\
\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}&\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}&0,\tau+\Omega&\tau+\Omega,\Omega\end{array}

Table 26.1:
Incomparable testing pre-orders

Towards a characterization of must termination, we notice the
following properties.

Proposition 26.20

Let
P
be a process and
s
a trace of observable actions.
Then
P\downarrow s
iff
(P\mid\overline{s})\downarrow
.

Proof
. By induction on
|s|
, proof similar to the one of proposition
26.3
(1).
{\Box}

Proposition 26.21

Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be processes such that
P_{1}\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}P_{2}
. Then:

1.

C(P_{1})\subseteq C(P_{2})
,

2.

C(P_{1})\cap T(P_{2})\subseteq T(P_{1})
.

Proof
.

1.

By proposition
26.20
,

P_{1}\downarrow s
is equivalent to
(P_{1}\mid\overline{s})\downarrow
.
By hypothesis, the latter implies
(P_{2}\mid\overline{s})\downarrow
which is
equivalent to
P_{2}\downarrow s
.

2.

Suppose
P_{1}\downarrow s
and
P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.
Let
R(s)=\overline{s}.\Omega
.
Then
(P_{2}\mid R(s))\not\downarrow
implies

(P_{1}\mid R(s))\not\downarrow
. But since
P_{1}\downarrow s

we must have
P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.
{\Box}

Proposition 26.22

Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be processes such that
C(P_{1})\subseteq C(P_{2})

and
C(P_{1})\cap T(P_{2})\subseteq T(P_{1})
. Further suppose

P_{1}
is image finite. Then:

C^{\omega}(P_{1})\cap T^{\omega}(P_{2})\subseteq T^{\omega}(P_{1})
.

Proof
. Suppose
P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
and
P_{1}\downarrow u
.
So for all
k
:

u(k)\in C(P_{1})\cap T(P_{2})\subseteq T(P_{1})
.
Since
P_{1}
is image finite, we conclude
u\in T^{\omega}(P_{1})
.
{\Box}

Proposition 26.23

Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be processes such that
P_{1}
is image finite.
If
C(P_{1})\subseteq C(P_{2})
and
C(P_{1})\cap T(P_{2})\subseteq T(P_{1})

then
P_{1}\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}P_{2}
.

Proof
. From
(P_{2}\mid Q)\not\downarrow
we derive that

(P_{1}\mid Q)\not\downarrow
. We distinguish
2
cases.

•

Divergence is due to an infinite interaction between
P_{2}
and

Q
. So suppose
P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
and
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{u}}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.
If for some
k
,
P_{1}\not\downarrow u(k)
then

(P_{1}\mid Q)\not\downarrow
.
Otherwise,
P_{1}\downarrow u
and, by proposition
26.22
,
we conclude
P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\Rightarrow}}
and therefore
(P_{1}\mid Q)\not\downarrow
.

•

After a finite interaction, either
P_{2}
or
Q
do not terminate.
So suppose
P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{2}
and
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
.

–

If
P^{\prime}_{2}\not\downarrow
then
P_{2}\not\downarrow s
and therefore

P_{1}\not\downarrow s
which implies
(P_{1}\mid Q)\not\downarrow
.

–

If
P^{\prime}_{2}\downarrow
and
Q^{\prime}\not\downarrow
then
either
P_{1}\not\downarrow s
or
P_{1}\downarrow s
, and in both
cases
(P_{1}\mid Q)\not\downarrow
.
{\Box}

Proposition 26.24

Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be reactive processes such that
P_{1}
is image finite. Then:

P_{1}\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}P_{2}\mbox{ iff }P_{2}\leq_{{\sf may}}P_{1}~.

Proof
. By propositions
26.21
,
26.22
, and
26.23
we have:

P_{1}\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}P_{2}\mbox{ iff }T(P_{2})\subseteq T(P_{1})~,

and we have shown in proposition
26.2
(2)
that trace inclusion characterizes the may testing pre-order.
{\Box}

To summarize the contents of propositions
26.18
and

26.24
,
assuming
P_{1},P_{2}
are reactive processes and
P_{2}
is image finite,
we have the following situation (again, notice the inversion of the order when switching from may to must):

P_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}^{t}P_{2}\mbox{ iff }P_{2}\leq_{{\sf must}}P_{1}\mbox{ implies strictly }P_{2}\leq_{{\sf must}}^{t}P_{1}\mbox{ iff }P_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}P_{2}~.

Going back to our discussion on the may and must requirements,
it should be noticed that the may
termination
requirements
are equivalent to the must requirements on reactive processes.

26.4
Summary and references

Section
26.2
is a rephrasing of results in

[
NH84
,
Hen88
]
in
the context of the CCS action structure.
A closely related notion of
failure equivalence
has been
developed at about the same time
in the framework of the CSP process calculus

[
BR84
]
.

If one focuses on reactive processes, then the must-testing

equivalence
can be regarded as a refinement of trace equivalence that is
still sensitive to deadlock. As observed at the end of section
26.2
,
the must-testing equivalence is incomparable
with the (weak) simulation equivalence but it is definitely coarser than
the (weak) bisimulation equivalence.

On non-reactive processes, the comparison between must-testing
equivalence and (weak) bisimulation is more delicate as must-testing
identifies all processes that may diverge. It is possible to revise
the definition of bisimulation to take this requirement into account
and then prove that the modified bisimulation is strictly
included in the must-testing
equivalence.

However, in certain application contexts the treatment of divergence
imposed by must-testing appears to be too severe and attempts
have been made to relax the requirements so as to allow for some
form of
benign
divergence. For instance, see the notions of
fair testing developed in
[
BRV95
,
NC95
]
.
The may termination testing pre-order discussed
in section
26.3
gives yet another angle on this issue.

The testing semantics approach has been applied to a number of concurrent
programming models; we refer to
[
BCLS25
]
for an
extensive bibliography.

Chapter 27
Determinacy and confluence

In automata theory, one can envisage various definitions of
determinacy. For instance, in the framework of
finite
automata,
consider the following ones.

1.

There is no
word

w
that admits two computation paths in the
graph such that one leads to an accepting state and the other to a non-accepting state.

2.

Each
reachable configuration
admits at most one successor.

3.

For each
state
, either there is exactly one outgoing transition labelled with

\epsilon
, or all outgoing transitions are labelled
with distinct symbols of the input alphabet.

Thus one can go from
‘extensional’ conditions
(intuitive but hard
to verify) to
‘syntactic’ conditions
(verifiable but not as general).
In the following, we propose a definition of determinate lts and
show that all the equivalences included between trace equivalence
and bisimulation collapse on such lts.
1
1

1

A note on terminology: we
speak of
determinacy
and
determinate
lts rather than

determinism
and
deterministic
lts to emphasize the fact that
we are interested in lts which on a given label
can choose among several transitions
but whose observable behavior is insensitive to this choice.

We also introduce a notion
of
confluence
on labelled transition systems. This is a stronger
property than determinacy which allows for a restricted form
of parallel composition and for the representation of determinate
models of parallel computation such as
Kahn networks
.
Finally, we consider
reactive
systems,
i.e.
, systems which
enjoy a kind of generalized termination property.
It turns out that for such system, it is enough to check a
local

form of confluence.

27.1
Determinacy in lts

In the first place, it is useful to recall why non-determinacy is needed.
First, it arises naturally in
race conditions
where
two ‘clients’ request the same service such as:

\nu a\ (\overline{a}.P_{1}\mid\overline{a}.P_{2}\mid a)~.

Second, it is a tool for general specification and portability.
It is often the case that we do not want to commit on
a particular behavior. For instance, consider:

\nu a,b\ (\overline{a}.\overline{b}.\overline{c}\ \mid\ a.\overline{b}.\overline{d}\ \mid\ b)~.

Depending on the compilation, the design of the virtual machine,
the processors timing,
\ldots
we might always run
\overline{d}

rather than
\overline{c}
(or the other way around).

On the other hand, determinate systems are easier to
test, debug, and possibly prove correct.
Notice that often the implementation seems ‘determinate’ because
the scheduler
determinizes the program’s behavior. However this kind of
determinacy is
not portable
: running the program in another
environment may produce different results.

We now move towards a definition of determinacy. Here are some reasonable
requirements:

•

If
P
and
P^{\prime}
are
‘equivalent’
then one is determinate
if and only if the other is.

•

If we run an
‘experiment’
twice we always get the same ‘result’.

•

If
P
is determinate and we run an experiment then
the
residual of
P
after the experiment should still be determinate.

If we place ourselves in the context of a simple model such as
{\it CCS}
, we can
interpret
equivalent
as
weak bisimilar
and
experiment

as a finite sequence of
labelled transitions
.

As in the previous chapters, let us denote with
{\cal L}
the set of
visible actions and co-actions
with
generic elements
a,b,\ldots
and let us
denote with
{\it Act}={\cal L}\cup\{\tau\}

the set of
actions
, with generic elements
\alpha,\beta,\ldots

Let
s\in{\cal L}^{*}
denote a finite word over
{\cal L}
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}&\mbox{if }P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\\
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{1}\ldots a_{n}}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime},\ n\geq 1&\mbox{if }P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{1}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{n}}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}~.\par\end{array}

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q
we say that
Q
is a
derivative
of
P
.
As usual we write
|s|
for the length of the word
s
.

Definition 27.1
(determinate)

A process
P
is determinate if for any
s\in{\cal L}^{*}
,
if
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{i}
for
i=1,2
then
P_{1}\approx P_{2}
.

Remark 27.1

This definition relies on the notion of labelled
transition system.
Indeed, in the transition
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
,
a
represents a minimal
interaction with the environment and
P^{\prime}
is
the residual after the interaction.

Exercise 27.1

Are the following
{\it CCS}
processes determinate?
(1)
a.(b+c)
.
(2)
a.b+ac
.
(3)
a+a.\tau
.
(4)
a+\tau.a
.
(5)
a+\tau
.

Proposition 27.1

The following properties hold:

1.

If
P
is determinate and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then
P^{\prime}
is determinate.

2.

If
P
is determinate and
P\approx P^{\prime}
then
P^{\prime}
is determinate.

Proof
. Like most of the following proofs, the argument is by
diagram chasing
.

1.

Suppose
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

•

If
\alpha=\tau
then
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
Hence
P_{1}\approx P_{2}
.

•

If
\alpha=a
then
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a\cdot s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
Hence
P_{1}\approx P_{2}
.

2.

Suppose
P\approx P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

•

By definition of
weak bisimulation
:

P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{i}
and
P_{i}\approx P^{\prime}_{i}
,
for
i=1,2
.

•

Since
P
is
determinate
, we have
P_{1}\approx P_{2}
.

•

Therefore, we conclude by
transitivity
of
\approx
:

P^{\prime}_{1}\approx P_{1}\approx P_{2}\approx P^{\prime}_{2}
.
{\Box}

Definition 27.2
(
\tau
-inertness)

We say that a process
P
is
\tau
-inert if for all its derivatives

Q
, if
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
then
Q\approx Q^{\prime}
.

Proposition 27.2

If a process is determinate then it is
\tau
-inert.

Proof
. Suppose
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q
and
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
.
Then
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
.
Thus by determinacy,
Q\approx Q^{\prime}
.
{\Box}

Next we recall a
weak version
of the
notion of trace equivalence for lts
presented in definition
23.2
(this is
the same notion of trace considered in chapter
26
).
We define the
traces
of a process
P
as:

T(P)=\{s\in{\cal L}^{*}\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\}~,

and say that two processes
P,Q
are
trace equivalent
if

T(P)=T(Q)
.
Notice that the traces of a process form a
non-empty
,
prefix-closed

set of
finite words
over
{\cal L}
.

Exercise 27.2

Are the following equations valid for
trace equivalence and/or weak bisimulation?

\begin{array}[]{llll}a+\tau=a,&\alpha.(P+Q)=\alpha.P+\alpha.Q,&(P+Q)\mid R=P\mid R+Q\mid R,&P=\tau.P~.\end{array}

Exercise 27.3
(compositionality of trace semantics)

Show that if
P,Q,R
are
{\it CCS}
processes and

T(P)=T(Q)
then
T(P\mid R)=T(Q\mid R)
.

The following result entails that on determinate processes
most equivalences (trace, simulation-induced equivalence,
bisimulation,
\ldots
) collapse.

Proposition 27.3

Let
P,Q
be processes.

1.

If
P\approx Q
then
T(P)=T(Q)
.

2.

Moreover, if
P,Q
are determinate then

T(P)=T(Q)
implies
P\approx Q
.

Proof
.
(1)\;
Suppose
P\approx Q
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot
.
Then
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot
by induction on
|s|
using the properties
of weak bisimulation.

(2)\;
Suppose
P,Q
determinate and
T(P)=T(Q)
.
We show that:

\{(P,Q)\mid T(P)=T(Q)\}

is a bisimulation.

•

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then
P\approx P^{\prime}
by
determinacy
.
Thus taking
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q
we have:

P^{\prime}\approx P\qquad T(P)=T(Q)~.

By (1), we conclude:

T(P^{\prime})=T(P)=T(Q)
.

•

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then we note that:

T(P)=\{\epsilon\}\cup\{a\}\cdot T(P^{\prime})\cup\bigcup_{a\neq a^{\prime},P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a^{\prime}}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime\prime}}\{a^{\prime}\}\cdot T(P^{\prime\prime})~.

This is because all the processes
P^{\prime}
such that
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}

are bisimilar, hence trace equivalent.
A similar reasoning applies to
T(Q)
.
Thus there must be a
Q^{\prime}
such that

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
and
T(P^{\prime})=T(Q^{\prime})
.
{\Box}

Example 27.1
(the unbounded buffer reconsidered)

Recall the unbounded buffer in example
25.1
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\it Buf}(a,b)&=a.\nu c\ ({\it Buf}(a,c)\mid\overline{b}.{\it Buf}(c,b))\\
P(n)&=\nu a\ (\overline{a}^{n}\mid{\it Buf}(a,b))\end{array}

We shall also use the more suggestive notation f
a\mapsto b
for

{\it Buf}(a,b)
. One can show that these processes are determinate. In fact one can show that they actually enjoy a
stronger property known as confluence which is introduced next
in section
27.2
.

27.2
Confluence in lts

We introduce a notion of
confluence

that strengthens determinacy and is preserved by some form of
communication (parallel composition + restriction).
For instance,

\nu a\ ((a+b)\mid\overline{a})

will be
rejected
because
a+b
is
not
confluent (while
being
determinate
).

The notion of confluence we consider is reminiscent of
confluence
in
rewriting systems (cf. definition
2.4
).
By analogy, one calls confluence the related theory
in process calculi but bear in mind that:
(1) confluence is relative to a
labelled transition system

and (2) we close diagrams
up to equivalence
.

Before introducing formally the notion of confluence for lts we need to define a notion
of action difference.

Definition 27.3
(action difference)

Suppose
\alpha,\beta\in{\it Act}
. Their action difference
\alpha\backslash\beta
is defined as:

\alpha\backslash\beta=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha&\mbox{if }\alpha\neq\beta\\
\tau&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

We can generalize the notion of action difference to sequences of
visible actions
r,s\in{\cal L}^{*}
. To compute the
difference

r\backslash s
of

r
by
s
we scan
r
from left to right deleting
each label which occurs in
s
taking into account the multiplicities
(cf. difference of
multi-sets
). We abuse notation by writing

a\notin s
to mean that
a
does not occur in the word
s
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}(\epsilon\backslash s)&=\epsilon\\
(ar\backslash s)&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a\cdot(r\backslash s)&\mbox{if }a\notin s\\
r\backslash(s_{1}\cdot s_{2})&\mbox{if }s=s_{1}as_{2},a\notin s_{1}~.\end{array}\right.\end{array}

For instance:

aba\backslash ca=ba
and
ca\backslash aba=c
.

Exercise 27.4

Let
r,s,t\in{\cal L}^{*}
. Show that:

1.

(rs)\backslash(rt)=s\backslash t
.

2.

r\backslash(st)=(r\backslash s)\backslash t
.

3.

(rs)\backslash t=(r\backslash t)(s\backslash(t\backslash r))
.

We now introduce a notion of confluent process.

Definition 27.4
(confluence)

A process
P
is confluent if for every derivative
Q

of
P
we have:

\begin{array}[]{c}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}\quad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,Q^{\prime}_{1},Q^{\prime}_{2}\;\>(Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta\backslash\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{1},\quad Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\backslash\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{2},\mbox{ and }\quad Q^{\prime}_{1}\approx Q^{\prime}_{2})\end{array}\quad\mbox{[conf 0]}

(27.1)

The condition in definition
27.4
is labelled
as [conf 0] to distinguish it from two equivalent
conditions that we state below and that are labelled
[conf 1] and [conf 2].

•

A process
P
is
confluent 1
if if
for every derivative

Q

of
P
we have:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{1}\quad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,Q^{\prime}_{1},Q^{\prime}_{2}\;\>(\ Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta\backslash\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{1},\quad Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\backslash\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{2},\mbox{ and }\quad Q^{\prime}_{1}\approx Q^{\prime}_{2}\ )\end{array}\quad\mbox{[conf 1]}\par\end{array}

(27.2)

•

A process
P
is
confluent 2
if for all
r,s\in{\cal L}^{*}
we have:

\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}\qquad P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2}\;\>(\ P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\backslash r}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{1},\quad P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r\backslash s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{2},\mbox{ and }\quad P^{\prime}_{1}\approx P^{\prime}_{2}\ )\end{array}\quad\mbox{[conf 2]}

(27.3)

Remark 27.2

In conditions [conf 0] and [conf 1] if
\alpha=\beta
then we close the diagram with
\tau
actions only.

A first
sanity check
is to verify that the confluent processes
are invariant under
transitions
and
equivalence

(cf. proposition
27.1
).

Proposition 27.4

The following properties hold:

1.

If
P
is confluent and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
then
P^{\prime}
is confluent.

2.

If
P
is confluent and
P\approx P^{\prime}
then
P^{\prime}
is confluent.

Proof
.
(1)\;
If
Q
is a derivative of
P^{\prime}
then it is also a derivative of

P
.

(2)\;
It is enough to apply the fact that:

(P\approx P^{\prime}\mbox{ and }P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1})\ \mbox{ implies }\ \exists\,P^{\prime}_{1}\;\>(P^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{1}\mbox{ and }P_{1}\approx P^{\prime}_{1})

and the transitivity of
\approx
.
{\Box}

Confluence implies
\tau
-inertness, and from this we can
show that it implies determinacy too.

Proposition 27.5

Suppose
P
is confluent. Then
P
is:
(1)
\tau
-inert and (2) determinate.

Proof
. First a reminder.
A relation
R
is a
weak bisimulation up to
\approx
if:

\begin{array}[]{c}P\ R\ Q\quad P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,Q^{\prime}\;\>Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}\mbox{ and }P^{\prime}(\approx\circ R\circ\approx)Q^{\prime}\end{array}

(and symmetrically for
Q
).
It is important that we work with the
weak moves

on both sides, otherwise the relation
R
is

not
guaranteed to be contained in

\approx
(cf. exercise
23.11
).
Now we move to the proof.

1.

We want to show that
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q
implies
P\approx Q
.
We show that:

R=\{(P,Q)\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q\}

is a weak bisimulation up to
\approx
.
It is clear that whatever
Q
does,
P
can do too with some
extra moves.
In the other direction, suppose,
e.g.
,
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{1}
with
\alpha\neq\tau

(case
\alpha=\tau
left as exercise).
By [conf 0],

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}
,

P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{2}
, and

Q_{1}\approx P_{2}
.
That is:
P_{1}(R\circ\approx)Q_{1}
.

2.

We want to show that if
P
is confluent then it is determinate.
Suppose
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{i}
for
i=1,2
and
s\in{\cal L}^{*}
.
We proceed by
induction
on
|s|
.
If
|s|=0
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{i}
for
i=1,2
then

by
\tau
-inertness

P_{1}\approx P\approx P_{2}
.
For the inductive case, suppose
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
By confluence and
\tau
-inertness, we derive that
P^{\prime}_{1}\approx P^{\prime}_{2}
.
By
weak bisimulation
,
P^{\prime}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime\prime}_{2}
and
P^{\prime\prime}_{2}\approx P_{1}
.
By
inductive hypothesis
,
P_{2}\approx P^{\prime\prime}_{2}
.
Thus
P_{2}\approx P^{\prime\prime}_{2}\approx P_{1}
as required.
{\Box}

Exercise 27.5

We have seen that confluence implies determinacy which implies

\tau
-inertness. Give examples that show that these implications
cannot be reversed.

We now turn to the condition (
27.2
) labelled [conf 1] which is
‘asymmetric’ in that
the move from
Q
to
Q_{1}
just concerns a
single
action.

Proposition 27.6

A process
P
is confluent iff for every derivative
Q
of
P
,
it satisfies condition [conf 1].

Exercise 27.6

Prove proposition
27.6
.

A similar result holds for condition (
27.3
) labelled [conf 2].

Proposition 27.7

A process
P
is confluent iff it satisfies [conf 2].

Exercise 27.7

Prove proposition
27.7
.

Next, we return to the issue of building confluent (and therefore
determinate) processes.

Proposition 27.8
(building confluent processes)

If
P,Q
are confluent processes then so are:
(1)
0
,
\alpha.P
,
(2)
\nu a\ P
, and
(3)
\sigma P
where
\sigma
is an injective substitution
on the free names of
P
.

Proof
. Routine analysis of transitions (cf. similar statement for determinacy).
{\Box}

Remark 27.3
(on sum)

In general,
a+b
is determinate but it is not confluent for

a\neq b
.

Definition 27.5
(sorting)

Let
P
be a process. We define its sorting
{\cal L}(P)
as the
set:

\{a\in{\cal L}\mid\exists\,s\in{\cal L}^{*}\;\>P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\}~.

Exercise 27.8

With reference to exercise
27.1
, show that

{\cal L}(a\mapsto b)=\{a,\overline{b}\}
.

Definition 27.6
(restricted composition)

A restricted composition is a process of the shape:

\nu a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\ (P\mid Q)

where:

1.

P
and
Q
do not share visible actions:

{\cal L}(P)\cap{\cal L}(Q)=\emptyset
.

2.

P
and
Q
may interact only on the restricted names:

{\cal L}(P)\cap\overline{{\cal L}(Q)}\subseteq\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}\cup\{\overline{a}_{1},\ldots,\overline{a}_{n}\}~.

Proposition 27.9

Confluence is preserved by restricted composition.

Proof
. We abbreviate
\nu a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\ (P\mid Q)
as

\nu a^{*}\ (P\mid Q)
.
First we observe that any derivative of
\nu a^{*}\ (P\mid Q)

will have the shape
\nu a^{*}\ (P^{\prime}\mid Q^{\prime})
where
P^{\prime}
is a derivative
of
P
and
Q^{\prime}
is a derivative of
Q
.

Since sorting is preserved by transitions, the two conditions
on sorting in definition
27.5
will be satisfied.
Therefore, it is enough to show that the diagrams in [conf 1]
commute for processes of the shape
R=\nu a^{*}\ (P\mid Q)
under the given
hypotheses.

We consider one case. Suppose:

R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}\nu a^{*}\ (P_{1}\mid Q)

because
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}P_{1}
.
Also assume:

R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\Rightarrow}}\nu a^{*}\ (P_{2}\mid Q_{2})

because
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle sar}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{2}
and

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}\cdot\overline{r}}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}

with
s\cdot r\in\{a^{*},\overline{a^{*}}\}^{*}

and
a\notin\{a^{*},\overline{a^{*}}\}
.

Since
P
is confluent we have:

\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}P_{1}\quad P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle sar}}{{\Rightarrow}}P_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2}\;\>P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle sr}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{1},\quad P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{2},\mbox{ and }\quad P^{\prime}_{1}\approx P^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}~.

Then we have:

\nu a^{*}\ (P_{1}\mid Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}\nu a^{*}\ (P^{\prime}_{1}\mid Q_{2})\approx\nu a^{*}\ (P^{\prime}_{2}\mid Q_{2})~,

thus closing the diagram (note that we use the congruence properties of
\approx
).
{\Box}

Exercise 27.9

Consider other cases of the proof, for instance:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\nu a\ (P\mid Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\nu a\ (P\mid Q)&\mbox{as }P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}P_{1},\quad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{a}}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{1}~,\\
\nu a\ (P\mid Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}\nu a\ (P_{2}\mid Q_{2})&\mbox{as }P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}P_{2},\quad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}~.\\
\end{array}

27.3
Kahn networks (*)

Kahn networks
are a determinate model of parallel computation where
communication is point-to-point,
i.e.
, for every channel there
is at most one sender and one receiver, and channels are order preserving
buffers of unbounded capacity,
i.e.
, sending is non blocking
and the order of emission is preserved at the reception.

In this model, each (sequential) process may:

1.

perform arbitrary
sequential deterministic computation
,

2.

insert a message in a buffer
,

3.

receive a message from a buffer
. If the buffer is empty then
the process
must
suspend,

However, a process
cannot
try to receive a message from
several channels at once.
In a nutshell Kahn’s approach to the semantics of such systems is as follows.
First, we regard the unbounded buffers as finite or infinite words over
some data domain and
second, we model the nodes of the network as functions over words.
Kahn observes that the associated system of equations has
a least fixed point which defines the semantics of the whole system.

Kahn networks are an important (practical)
case where
parallelism
does
not
induce
race conditions and it is compatible with
determinacy
.
For instance, they are frequently used
in the
signal processing
community.
Our modest goal is to
formalize Kahn networks as a fragment of
{\it CCS}
and to
apply the developed theory to show that the fragment is
confluent and therefore determinate.

We will work with a ‘data domain’ that contains just
one element. The generalization to arbitrary data domains is not difficult,
but we would need to formalize determinacy and confluence in the framework
of an extended
{\it CCS}
where messages carry values (as,
e.g.
, in
the value passing
{\it CCS}
described in chapter
25
).
First, let us conclude the analysis of the
unbounded buffers
in
{\it CCS}
.

Exercise 27.10

With reference to examples
25.1
,
27.1
, and exercise

27.8
:

1.

Apply proposition
27.9
to derive that
the process
a\mapsto b
is confluent.

2.

Derive from proposition
27.3
that to prove

P(n)=\nu a\ (\overline{a}^{n}\mid a\mapsto b)\approx\overline{b}^{n}

it is enough to check the trace equivalence

T(P(n))=T(\overline{b}^{n})
.

3.

Prove the trace equivalence above.

We define a class of
{\it CCS}
processes sufficient to
represent Kahn networks.

Definition 27.7
(restricted processes)

Let
{\it KP}
be the least set of processes such that

0\in{\it KP}
and if
P,Q\in{\it KP}
and
\alpha
is an action then:

1.

\alpha.P\in{\it KP}
,

2.

A(b^{*})\in{\it KP}
provided the names
b^{*}
are all distinct,

A
is defined by an equation
A(a^{*})=P
, and
P\in{\it KP}
.

3.

\nu a^{*}\ (P\mid Q)\in{\it KP}
provided

{\cal L}(P)\cap{\cal L}(Q)=\emptyset
and

{\cal L}(P)\cap\overline{{\cal L}(Q)}\subseteq\{a^{*},\overline{a^{*}}\}
,

Exercise 27.11

Check that: (1)
a\mapsto b
is a
{\it KP}
process and (2)
Kahn processes are confluent.

Example 27.2

Suppose we have a Kahn network with three nodes, and
the following ports and behaviors where we use

!
for output and
?
for input.

\begin{array}[]{l|ll}\mbox{Node}&\mbox{Ports}&\mbox{Behaviors}\\
\hline\cr 1&?a,?b,?c,!d,!e,!f&A_{1}=?a.!d.!e.?b.?c.!f.A_{1}\\
2&!b,?d&A_{2}=?d.!b.A_{2}\\
3&!c,?e&A_{3}=?e.!c.A_{3}~.\par\end{array}

The corresponding
{\it CCS}
system relies on the equations
for the buffer process plus:

\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}(a,b,c,d,e,f)&=a.\overline{d}.\overline{e}.b.c.\overline{f}.A_{1}(a,b,c,d,e,f)\\
A_{2}(b,d)&=d.\overline{b}.A_{2}(b,d)\\
A_{3}(c,e)&=e.\overline{c}.A_{3}(c,e)~.\par\end{array}

The sorting is easily derived:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal L}(A_{1}(a,b,c,d,e,f)&=\{a,b,c,\overline{d},\overline{e},\overline{f}\}\\
{\cal L}(A_{2}(b,d))&=\{\overline{b},d\}\\
{\cal L}(A_{3}(c,e))&=\{\overline{c},e\}~.\par\end{array}

To build the system, we have to introduce a buffer
before every input channel.
Thus the initial configuration is:

\begin{array}[]{l}\nu a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime},c,c^{\prime},d,d^{\prime},e,e^{\prime}\\
(a\mapsto a^{\prime}\mid b\mapsto b^{\prime}\mid c\mapsto c^{\prime}\mid d\mapsto d^{\prime}\mid e\mapsto e^{\prime}\mid\\
A_{1}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d,e,f)\mid A_{2}(b,d^{\prime})\mid A_{3}(c,e^{\prime})\ )\par\end{array}

It is easily checked that the resulting process belongs to the
class KP.

To summarize,
to build confluent processes we can use:
(i) nil and input prefix,
(ii) restricted composition,
(iii) injective recursive calls, and
(iv) recursive equations
A(a^{*})=P
, where
P
is built according
to the rules above.
This class of processes is enough to represent Kahn networks.
Notice that, via recursion, we can also represent
Kahn networks with a dynamically changing number of nodes
(see example
25.1
).

27.4
Reactivity and local confluence in lts (*)

We know that a terminating and locally confluent rewriting system is
confluent (proposition
2.8
).
We present a suitable generalization of this result
to confluent lts. First, we recall from section
26.1

that a process is

reactive
if all its derivatives are strongly normalizing.

Definition 27.8
(local confluence)

Let
P
be a process.
We say that it is locally confluent if for all its derivatives
Q
:

\begin{array}[]{c}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{1}\quad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,Q^{\prime}_{1},Q^{\prime}_{2}\;\>(Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta\backslash\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{1},\quad Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\backslash\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{2},\mbox{ and }\quad Q^{\prime}_{1}\approx Q^{\prime}_{2})\end{array}~.

Exercise 27.12

Consider again the process:

A(a,b)=a.\nu c\ (A(a,c)\mid\overline{b}.A(c,b))~.

Is the process
A(a,b)
reactive?
Consider the cases
a\neq b
and
a=b
.

Exercise 27.13

Consider the process:

A=a.b+\tau.(a.c+\tau.A)
.
Check whether
A
is:
(1)
\tau
-inert,
(2) locally confluent,
(3) terminating,
(4) reactive,
(5) determinate,
and (6) confluent.

Suppose
P
is a
reactive
process and let

W
be the set of its derivatives.
For
Q,Q^{\prime}\in W
write
Q>Q^{\prime}
if
Q

rewrites to
Q^{\prime}
by a positive number of

\tau
-actions.
Then
(W,>)
is a well founded set.

Proposition 27.10

If a process is reactive and locally confluent
then it is confluent.

Proof
. Let
B
be the relation
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\cup(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}})^{-1}\cup\approx

(restricted to
W
) and
B^{*}
its reflexive and transitive closure.
Note that
B^{*}
is symmetric too.
We take the following steps.

1.

For every derivative
Q
of
P
it holds:

\begin{array}[]{c}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1},\quad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,Q_{3}\;\>(\ Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{3}\mbox{ and }Q_{2}B^{*}Q_{3}\ )\end{array}~.

2.

The relation
B^{*}
is a weak-bisimulation.

3.

The process
P
is
\tau
-inert.

4.

The process
P
is confluent.

Note that
B^{*}
is a binary relation on
W
(the derivatives of
P
).

Step 1

The argument is
by induction
(cf. proposition
2.3
)
on the well founded order
(W,>)
.

•

If
Q=Q_{1}
then the statement holds trivially.

•

So assume
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}
and
consider 2 cases.

1.

If
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}
.

–

By local confluence,
Q_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{5}
,
Q_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{6}
, and

Q_{5}\approx Q_{6}
.

–

By inductive hypothesis,
Q_{6}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{7}
and
Q_{2}B^{*}Q_{7}
.

–

By definition of bisimulation,
Q_{5}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{8}
and
Q_{7}\approx Q_{8}
.

–

By inductive hypothesis,
Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{9}
and
Q_{8}B^{*}Q_{9}
.

So
Q_{2}B^{*}Q_{7}\approx Q_{8}B^{*}Q_{9}
, and by definition of
B
,
Q_{2}B^{*}Q_{9}
.

2.

If
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}
with
\alpha\neq\tau
.

–

By local confluence,
Q_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{5}
,
Q_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{6}
,

Q_{5}\approx Q_{6}
.

–

By inductive hypothesis,
Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{7}
and
Q_{5}B^{*}Q_{7}
.

So
Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Leftarrow}}Q_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{6}\approx Q_{5}B^{*}Q_{7}
.
Hence
Q_{2}B^{*}Q_{7}
.

Step 2

The relation
B^{*}
is a weak-bisimulation.

Suppose
Q_{0}BQ_{1}\cdots BQ_{n}BQ_{n+1}
and
Q_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{0}
.
Proceed by induction on
n
and case analysis on
Q_{n}BQ_{n+1}
.
By inductive hypothesis, we know that
Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{n}
and

Q^{\prime}_{0}B^{*}Q^{\prime}_{n}
.

1.

If
Q_{n}\approx Q_{n+1}
then

Q_{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{n+1}
and
Q^{\prime}_{n}\approx Q^{\prime}_{n+1}
.
So
Q^{\prime}_{0}B^{*}Q^{\prime}_{n}\approx Q^{\prime}_{n+1}
and we use

B^{*}\circ\approx\subseteq B^{*}
.

2.

If
Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\leftarrow}}Q_{n+1}
then

Q_{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{n}
.

3.

If
Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{n+1}
then by Step (1),

Q_{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{n+1}
and
Q^{\prime}_{n}B^{*}Q^{\prime}_{n+1}
.

So
Q^{\prime}_{0}B^{*}Q^{\prime}_{n}B^{*}Q^{\prime}_{n+1}
and we use

B^{*}\circ B^{*}\subseteq B^{*}
.

Step 3

The process
P
is
\tau
-inert.

By definition,
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\subseteq B^{*}
and
by Step (2),
B^{*}\subseteq\approx
.

Step 4

The process
P
is confluent.

By induction on the well-founded order
W
. We distinguish two cases.

1.

Suppose
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}
and

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}
, with

\alpha,\beta\neq\tau
.

•

By local confluence,
Q_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta\backslash\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{5}
,

Q_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\backslash\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{6}
, and
Q_{5}\approx Q_{6}
.

•

By Step (3),
Q_{4}\approx Q_{2}
, and by weak bisimulation,

Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\backslash\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{8}
,
Q_{6}\approx Q_{8}
.

•

By Step (3),
Q_{3}\approx Q_{1}
, and by weak bisimulation,

Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta\backslash\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{7}
,
Q_{5}\approx Q_{7}
.

So we have
Q_{8}\approx Q_{6}\approx Q_{5}\approx Q_{7}
as required.

2.

Suppose
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}
and

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}
.

•

By Step (3),
Q\approx Q_{3}
, and by weak bisimulation,

Q_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{5}
,
Q_{2}\approx Q_{5}
.

•

By inductive hypothesis,
Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta\backslash\alpha}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{6}
,

Q_{5}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\backslash\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{7}
, and
Q_{6}\approx Q_{7}
.

•

By weak bisimulation,
Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\backslash\beta}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{4}
and

Q_{4}\approx Q_{7}
.

So
Q_{4}\approx Q_{7}\approx Q_{6}
as required.
{\Box}

Exercise 27.14

Suppose
P
is a
{\it CCS}
process that is reactive and such
that for every derivative
Q
of
P
we have:

\begin{array}[]{c}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{1}\qquad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{2}\\
\hline\cr Q_{1}\approx Q_{2}\end{array}~.

Show that this implies that for every derivative
Q
of
P
we have:

\begin{array}[]{c}Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}\quad Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{2}\\
\hline\cr\exists\,Q^{\prime}_{1},Q^{\prime}_{2}\;\>(\ Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{1},\quad Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}_{2},\quad\mbox{ and }\quad Q^{\prime}_{1}\approx Q^{\prime}_{2}\ )\end{array}~.

27.5
Summary and references

A process is determinate if it always reacts in the same
way to the stimuli coming from the environment.
Confluence is a stronger property than determinacy
that is preserved by a restricted form of parallel composition.
Following
[
Mil95
]
[chapter 11],
we have presented
3
alternative characterizations of confluence.
We have seen that a restricted form of parallel
composition preserves confluence and
as a case study we have shown that this fragment of
{\it CCS}

is enough to represent Kahn networks
[
Kah74
]
.
Synchronous data flow languages such as
Lustre
[
CPHP87
]
can be
regarded as a refinement of this model where buffers have size
0
.
A rather complete study of the notion of confluence in the more
general framework of the
\pi
-calculus is in

[
PW97
]
, which builds
on previous work on confluence for

{\it CCS}
with value passing.
For reactive processes, local confluence entails confluence.
This is a generalization of Newman’s proposition
2.8

described in
[
GS96
]
.

Chapter 28
Synchronous/Timed models

As mentioned in chapter
19
, an important
classification criterion in concurrent systems is
the
relative speed
of the processes.
In particular, in chapter
19
we have contrasted
asynchronous and synchronous systems.
So far we have considered models (
{\sf Imp}_{\parallel}
,
{\it CCS}
)
where processes are
asynchronous
,
i.e.
, proceed at
independent speeds. In particular, processes can only
synchronize
through
an
await
statement or an
input/output
communication.
In the following we are going to discuss an enrichment of the
{\it CCS}
model where
processes are
synchronous
(or
timed
).
In first approximation, in a synchronous concurrent
system all processes
proceed in lockstep
(at the same speed).
In other words, the computation is regulated by a notion of

instant
(or
round
, or
phase
, or
pulse
,
\ldots
).

Though synchronous circuits are typical examples of
synchronous systems, one should
not
conclude that
synchronous systems are
hardware
.
Notions of synchrony are quite useful in the design
of
software systems
too.
The programming of many problems in a distributed setting
can be ‘simplified’ or even ‘made possible’ by a
synchronous assumption. Examples include: leader election, minimum spanning tree, and consensus in the presence of failures.
In general, the notion of synchrony is a useful
logical
concept
that can make
programming easier
.

The formalization of a synchronous model depends on the way the
notion of instant is considered. One possibility is to assume that at
each instant each (sequential) process performs a locally defined amount of work. For
instance, a popular definition found in books on distributed algorithms
requires that at each instant
each process (1) writes in the output communication channels, (2) reads
the contents of the input communication channels, and (3) computes its
next state. However, a less constrained viewpoint is possible which
consists in assuming that at each instant, each process performs an
arbitrary, but hopefully finite, number of actions. The instant ends
when each process has either terminated its task for the current
instant or it is suspended waiting for events that cannot arise.
This is the viewpoint taken by synchronous languages such as
{\it Esterel}

and we shall describe next its formalization in the framework of
{\it CCS}
.
The reader should keep in mind that we select
{\it CCS}
because of its simplicity but
that the approach can be easily ported to other models of concurrent systems.
In particular, in section
28.2
we shall sketch a synchronous
model where processes interact through
signals
rather than

{\it CCS}

channels
.

28.1
Timed
{\it CCS}

We discuss the definition of a synchronous/timed model on top of
{\it CCS}
.
Following the terminology in the literature, we call this model
timed

{\it CCS}
(
{\it TCCS}
).
As usual, we write
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\ldots

for the
{\it CCS}
actions and we reserve
a,b,\ldots

for the
{\it CCS}
actions but the
\tau
action.
We denote with
\mu,\mu^{\prime},\ldots
the
{\it TCCS}
actions.

They are obtained
by extending the
{\it CCS}
actions (chapter
25
)
with a new
{\sf tick}\
action which represents the move to the following instant:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mu&::=\alpha\mid\!\!\mid{\sf tick}&\mbox{(${\it TCCS}$ actions).}\end{array}

We also extend the syntax of
{\it CCS}
processes with a new operator ‘else-next’
which allows to program processes which are
time dependent and are able to react to the absence of an event.
Intuitively, the process
(P\triangleright Q)
tries to run
P
in
the current instant and if it cannot it runs
Q
in the following.

\begin{array}[]{lll}P&::=\cdots\mid\!\!\mid(P\triangleright P)&\mbox{(${\it TCCS}$ processes).}\par\end{array}

The labelled transition system for
{\it TCCS}
includes the
usual rules for the
\alpha
actions (Table
25.1
) plus:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr(P\triangleright Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\end{array}\par&\mbox{(a rule for else-next).}\end{array}

Moreover, we introduce in Table
28.1

special rules for the
{\sf tick}\
action describing
the passage of time. The intuition is the following:

\begin{array}[]{|c|}\hline\cr\mbox{A process can ${\sf tick}\ $ if and only if it cannot perform $\tau$ actions.}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Incidentally, this is in perfect agreement with the usual feeling that we
do not see time passing when we have something to do!

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{c}P\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\\
\hline\cr(P\triangleright Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}Q\end{array}\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr 0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}0\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\hline\cr a.P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}a.P\end{array}\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}P_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.50012pti=1,2\hskip 8.50012pt(P_{1}\mid P_{2})\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\\
\hline\cr(P_{1}\mid P_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}(P^{\prime}_{1}\mid P^{\prime}_{2})\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}P_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.50012pti=1,2\\
\hline\cr(P_{1}+P_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}(P^{\prime}_{1}+P^{\prime}_{2})\end{array}\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\nu a\ P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}\nu a\ P^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}

Table 28.1:
Labelled transition system for the
{\sf tick}\
action

Exercise 28.1
(on formalising
tick
actions)

Check that
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot
if and only if
P\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot

The lts in Table
28.1
uses the negative condition
P\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot
.
Show that this condition can be formalized in a positive way by
defining a formal system to derive judgments of the shape

P\downarrow L
where
L
is a set of observable actions and

P\downarrow L
if and only if
P\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot
and

L=\{a\mid P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\}
.

The following exercise identifies two important choices in
the design of
{\it TCCS}
.

Exercise 28.2
(continuations of
tick
action)

We say that
P
is a ‘
{\it CCS}
process’ if it does not contain the
else_next operator. Show that:

1.

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{1}
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}Q_{2}
then
Q_{1}=Q_{2}
.
So the passage of time is deterministic.

2.

If
P
is a
{\it CCS}
process and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}Q
then
P=Q
.
So
{\it CCS}
processes are insensitive to the passage of time.

Exercise 28.3
(programming a switch)

Let
{\sf tick}.P=(0\triangleright P)
and
{\sf tick}^{n}.P={\sf tick}\cdots{\sf tick}.P
,

n
times.

1.

Program a light switch

{\it Switch}({\it press},{\it off},{\it on},{\it brighter})

that behaves as follows:
(i)
initially the switch is off,
(ii) if the switch is off and it is pressed then the light turns
on,
(iii) if the switch is pressed again in the following 2 instants then
the light becomes brighter while if it is pressed at a later instant
it turns off again,
(iv)
if the light is brighter and the switch is pressed then it
becomes off.

2.

Program a fast user
{\it Fast}({\it press})

that presses the switch every 2 instants
and a slow user
{\it Slow}({\it press})

that presses the switch every 4 instants.

3.

Consider the systems:

\begin{array}[]{l}\nu{\it press}\ (\ {\it Switch}({\it press},{\it off},{\it on},{\it brighter})\mid{\it Fast}({\it press})\ )\\
\nu{\it press}\ (\ {\it Switch}({\it press},{\it off},{\it on},{\it brighter})\mid{\it Slow}({\it press})\ )\end{array}

and determine when the light is going to be off, on, and bright.

Definition 28.1
(bisimulation for
{\it TCCS}
)

The notion of weak transition is extended to the
{\sf tick}\
action by
defining:

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\Rightarrow}}\ =\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}\qquad\mbox{(weak ${\sf tick}\ $ action)}~.

Then we denote with
\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }
the related largest weak bisimulation.

Exercise 28.4

Show that
\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }
is preserved by parallel composition.
Also show that
((P_{1}\triangleright P_{2})\triangleright P_{3})\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }(P_{1}\triangleright P_{3})
.
Thus the nesting of else-next operators
on the left is useless!

Exercise 28.5
(more on congruence of
\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }
)

Suppose
P_{1}\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }P_{1}
and
Q_{1}\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }Q_{2}
.
Prove or give a counterexample to the following equivalences.

1.

P_{1}+Q_{1}\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }P_{2}+Q_{2}
.

2.

((a.P_{1})\triangleright Q_{1})\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }((a.P_{2})\triangleright Q_{2})
.

3.

(P_{1}\triangleright Q_{1})\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }(P_{2}\triangleright Q_{2})
.

We have identified the
{\it CCS}
processes with the
{\it TCCS}
processes that do
not contain an else-next operator. A natural question is whether the
equivalences we have on
{\it CCS}
are still valid when the
{\it CCS}
processes are
placed in a timed environment. A basic observation is that a
diverging computation does not allow time to pass. Thus if we denote
with
\Omega
the diverging process
\tau.\tau.\tau\cdots
we have
0\not\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }\Omega
while in the ordinary (termination
insensitive) bisimulation for
{\it CCS}
we have
0\approx\Omega
.
The situation is more pleasant for
reactive
processes
cf. chapter
27
).

Proposition 28.1
(
{\it CCS}
vs.
{\it TCCS}
)

Suppose
P,Q
are
{\it CCS}
processes.

1.

P\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }Q
implies
P\approx Q
.

2.

If moreover,
P,Q
are reactive then
P\approx Q
implies

P\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }Q
.

Proof
. (1)
{\it TCCS}
bisimulation is stronger than
{\it CCS}
bisimulation and
\alpha
-derivatives of
{\it CCS}
are again
{\it CCS}
processes.

(2)\;

First notice that for a
{\it CCS}
process being reactive w.r.t.
{\it CCS}
actions is the same as being reactive w.r.t.
{\it TCCS}
actions.
For
\alpha
actions, the condition
P\approx Q
suffices.
Otherwise, suppose
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
.
By exercises
28.1
and
28.2
(2),
this means
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}P^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
.
By definition of
{\it CCS}
bisimulation,
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q_{1}
,
P^{\prime}\approx Q_{1}
.
By reactivity,
Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\rightarrow}}
.
Again by definition of
{\it CCS}
bisimulation,
P^{\prime}\approx Q^{\prime}
,
Hence
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf tick}\ }}{{\Rightarrow}}Q^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\approx Q^{\prime}
.
{\Box}

Exercise 28.6
(termination sensitive bisimulation)

Rather than restricting the attention to reactive processes, another possibility
is to consider a bisimulation for
{\it CCS}
which is sensitive to termination.
We write
P\downarrow
if
P\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot
and
P\Downarrow
if
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}Q
and
Q\downarrow
.
Show that on
{\it CCS}
processes the bisimulation
\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }
can be characterized
as the largest relation
{\cal R}
which is a weak labelled bisimulation
(in the usual
{\it CCS}
sense) and such that if
P\;{\cal R}\;Q
and
P\downarrow
then
Q\Downarrow
.

28.2
A determinate calculus based on signals (*)

As a case study, we consider a variant of the
{\it TCCS}
model where
processes interact through
signals
(rather than channels).
A signal is either emitted or not. Once it is emitted
it
persists
during the instant and it is
reset
at the end of it.
Thus the collection of emitted signals grows
monotonically

during each instant.

The presented calculus is named
{\it SL}

(
synchronous language
).
We describe it as a fragment of timed
{\it CCS}
where
we write
s,s^{\prime},\ldots
for
signal
names.
The syntax of
{\it SL}
processes is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}P::=0\mid\!\!\mid s.P,P\mid\!\!\mid({\sf emit}\ s)\mid\!\!\mid(P\mid P)\mid\!\!\mid\nu s\ P\mid\!\!\mid A(s^{*})\par&\mbox{(${\it SL}$ processes).}\par\end{array}

The newly introduced operators can be understood in terms of those of
{\it TCCS}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}s.P,Q&=(s.P\triangleright Q)\\
({\sf emit}\ s)&=(\overline{s}.{\it Emit}(s)\triangleright 0)\\
&\mbox{where: }{\it Emit}(s)=(\overline{s}.{\it Emit}(s)\triangleright 0)~.\\
\end{array}

Notice that in
{\it SL}
there is no sum and no prefix for emission (cf. asynchronous

\pi
-calculus, chapter
30
).
The input is a specialized form of the input prefix and
the else-next operator.
The derived synchronization rule is:

({\sf emit}\ s)\mid s.P,Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}({\sf emit}\ s)\mid P~.

The second
\tau
transition is just recursion unfolding and we will ignore it in the following.
Notice that:

({\sf emit}\ s)\mid s.P_{1},Q_{1}\mid s.P_{2},Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\Rightarrow}}({\sf emit}\ s)\mid P_{1}\mid P_{2}~.

The
{\sf tick}\
action can be expressed as:

{\sf tick}\ .P=\nu s\ s.0,P\qquad s\notin{\sf fv}(P)~.

A
persistent input
(as in
{\it TCCS}
) is expressed as:

{\sf await}\ s.P=A(s^{*}),\quad\mbox{where: }A(s^{*})=s.P,A(s^{*}),\ {\sf fv}(P)\cup\{s\}=\{s^{*}\}~.

Exercise 28.7

Re-program in
{\it SL}
the light switch seen in exercise
28.3
.
Compare the solution with the one based on
{\it TCCS}
.

The
{\it SL}
calculus enjoys a
strong
form of confluence where
one can close the diagram in
at most one step
and
up to

\alpha
-renaming
.

Proposition 28.2
(strong confluence)

For all
{\it SL}
programs
P
the following holds:

\begin{array}[]{c}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}P_{1}\qquad P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}P_{2}\\
\hline\cr P_{1}\equiv P_{2}\mbox{ or }\exists\,Q\;\>(P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}Q,P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}Q)\end{array}

Proof
. Internal reductions are due either to
unfolding
or to

synchronization
.
The only possibility for a
superposition
of the redexes is:

({\sf emit}\ s)\mid s.P_{1},Q_{1}\mid s.P_{2},Q_{2}~.

And we exploit the fact that emission is
persistent
.
{\Box}

The bisimulation
\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }
developed for

{\it TCCS}
can be applied to
{\it SL}
too. However, because of the
restricted form of
{\it SL}
processes, one can expect additional equations to hold.
For instance:

s.({\sf emit}\ s),0\mbox{ should be `equivalent' to }0~.

(28.1)

A similar phenomenon arises with asynchronous communication in the
\pi
-calculus (cf. chapter
30
).
More generally, because
{\it SL}
is determinate (cf. proposition
28.2
) one can expect
a collapse of the
bisimulation
and
trace
semantics
(cf. proposition
27.3
).

Exercise 28.8
(on
{\it SL}
equivalence)

Check that the equation (
28.1
) does not hold in the
{\it TCCS}
embedding.
Also, prove or disprove the following equivalences:

1.

s.(s.P,Q),Q\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }s.P,Q
.

2.

({\sf emit}\ s)\mid s.P,Q\approx_{{\sf tick}\ }({\sf emit}\ s)\mid P
.

28.3
Summary and references

Time, in the sense we have described it here, is derived
from the notion of computation and as such it is a
logical

notion rather than a concept we attach on top of the computational model.
Time passes when no computation is possible.
Moving from an asynchronous to a synchronous model means
enriching the language with the possibility to react
to the absence of computation,
i.e.
, to the passage
of time.
The distinction between synchronous and asynchronous models
is standard in the analysis of distributed algorithms (see,
e.g.
,

[
Lyn96
]
).
In the framework of process calculi,
a notion of ‘timed’
{\it CCS}
is introduced in

[
Yi91
]
.
This calculus has a
{\sf tick}(x)
operator that describes the passage
of
x
time units where
x
is a non-negative real.
A kind of
else_next
operator is proposed in

[
NS94
]
.
A so called
testing semantics
of a process calculus very close to the one
presented here is given in
[
HR95
]
.
However, it seems fair to say that all these works generalize to
{\it CCS}

ideas that were presented for the
{\it Esterel}
programming language

[
BC84
,
BG92
]
.
Two basic differences in the
{\it Esterel}
approach are that
processes interact through
signals
and
that the resulting calculus is
determinate
.

Another important difference is that in the
{\it Esterel}
model it is actually possible
to
react immediately
(rather than at the end of the instant)
to the absence of a signal. This requires some semantic care,
to avoid writing
paradoxical programs
such as

\ s.0,({\sf emit}\ s)\

which are supposed to emit
s
when
s
is not there
(cf. stabilization problems in the design of synchronous circuits).
It also requires some clever
compilation techniques
to determine
whether a signal is not emitted. In fact these techniques (so far!) are
specific to
finite state models
.

The
{\it SL}
model
[
BdS96
]

we have described is a
relaxation
of the
{\it Esterel}
model
where the
absence of a signal
can only be detected at the
end of the instant. If we forget about
name generation
,
then the
{\it SL}
model
essentially defines a kind of
monotonic Mealy machine
.
Monotonic in the sense that output signals can only depend

positively
on input signals (within the same instant).
The monotonicity restriction allows to
avoid the
paradoxical programs
as monotonic boolean equations do
have a least fixed point!
The
{\it SL}
model has a natural and
efficient implementation model

that works well for
general programs
(not just finite state
machines). The model has been adapted to several

programming environments
(
{\it C}

[
Bou91
]
,

Scheme

[
SBS04
]
,
{\it ML}

[
MP05
]
) and
it has been used to program significant applications.

The
{\it Esterel}
/
{\it TCCS}
/
{\it SL}
models described here actually follow an earlier
attempt at describing synchronous/timed systems in the framework
of
{\it CCS}
known as
SCCS
/
Meije
model
[
Mil83
,
AB84
]
.
The basic idea of these models is that the actions of the system
live in an abelian (commutative) group freely generated from a
collection of basic actions. At each instant, each (sequential) process must perform
exactly one action and the observable result of the computation
is the group composition of the actions performed by each process.
This gives rise to a model with pleasant algebraic properties
but whose implementation and generalization to a full scale programming
languages appear to be problematic.

Finally, let us mention
timed automata

as another popular formalism for describing ‘timed’ systems

[
AD94
]
.
This is an enrichment of finite state automata with
timing
constraints
which still enjoys decidable model-checking properties.
This is more a
specification language
for finite control systems
than a
programming language
.

Chapter 29
Probability and non-determinism

Probabilities arise in several areas of system design and analysis.
For instance, one may want to analyze programs or protocols that
toss coins at some point in the computation,
e.g.
, to compute the
probability that a test for number primality returns the correct
answer. In another direction, one may want to evaluate the
reliability of a system given some probability of failure of its
components. And yet in another direction, one may be interested in
evaluating the performance of a system in terms of, say, the average
waiting time of its users. As already mentioned, in concurrent
systems, non-determinism arises to account for
race conditions

and also as a
specification device
. It is then natural to

lift
methods for (deterministic) probabilistic systems to
non-deterministic ones. This is not a simple task and still the
subject of ongoing research.
In this chapter, we focus on the notion of
probabilistic
rewriting
system which is a rewriting system where a state can reduce to
a distribution over states. For such systems, we consider the
notion of
almost sure
termination which intuitively means that
under any reduction strategy the system is guaranteed to terminate
with probability
1
. We consider a sufficient criteria to
establish almost sure termination and apply it
to the analysis of the so called dining philosophers protocol.

29.1
Probabilistic rewriting and termination

Let
S
be a finite or countable set.
A (probability) distribution over
S
is a function
\Delta:S\rightarrow[0,1]

such that
\Sigma_{s\in S}\Delta(s)=1
.
We say that a distribution
\Delta
is
Dirac
if all the probability is concentrated
in one point, namely there exists
s\in S
such
\Delta(s)=1
.
We denote with
D(S)
the set of distributions over
S
.
If
s_{1},s_{2}\in S
and
p\in[0,1]
then we denote with
s_{1}+_{p}s_{2}
the
distribution
\Delta\in D(S)
such that
\Delta(s_{1})=p
and
\Delta(s_{2})=(1-p)
.

Definition 29.1
(probabilistic rewriting system)

A probabilistic rewriting system is a pair
(S,\rightarrow)
such that

S
is finite or countable and
\rightarrow\subseteq S\times D(S)
.

An ordinary rewriting system on
S
(definition
2.1
)
can be viewed as a probabilistic
rewriting system where if
s\rightarrow\Delta
then
\Delta
is Dirac.
We say that the system is
not non-deterministic
, shortened as NND,
if for every
s\in S
,
there is at most one distribution
\Delta
such that
s\rightarrow\Delta
.
A NND system is not quite deterministic as it can exhibit
probabilistic behavior (here is a situation where double negation is not involutive).
In particular,
Markov chains

are a special case of NND systems
where for every
s\in S
there is
exactly

one distribution
\Delta
such that
s\rightarrow\Delta
.

Let
(S,\rightarrow)
be a probabilistic rewriting system. A (finite)
computation

is a sequence
\gamma
with the following shape and properties:

\gamma=s_{0}\Delta_{1}s_{1}\cdots\Delta_{n}s_{n}~,\qquad n\geq 0~,s_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{i+1}~,\mbox{ and }\Delta_{i+1}(s_{i+1})>0~\mbox{ for }i=0,\ldots,n-1.

(29.1)

The computation
\gamma
has
length

n
which we denote with

\ell(\gamma)
.
We say that
\gamma
is
terminated
if

\{\Delta\mid s_{n}\rightarrow\Delta\}=\emptyset
.
We attach to the computation
\gamma
(terminated or not)
the following
weight
(which can be seen as a
probability
in
an appropriate setting, hence the notation):

\mathbb{P}(\gamma)=\Delta_{1}(s_{1})\cdots\Delta_{n}(s_{n})~\qquad\mbox{(weight of a computation).}

A
scheduler

{\cal S}
is a partial function that takes as input an arbitrary computation and produces a possible
distribution among those that can extend the computation. So if
\gamma
is the computation above we must
have that
{\cal S}(\gamma)
is undefined if
\gamma
is terminated and otherwise

{\cal S}(\gamma)\in\{\Delta\mid s_{n}\rightarrow\Delta\}
.
The scheduler can be regarded as a device that resolves the non-determinism and brings us back
in the realm of NND systems. Once we have a NND system it makes sense to speak
of the probability of certain events and in particular we shall focus in this section on the probability of termination.

We say that the computation
\gamma
as displayed in (
29.1
)
agrees with the scheduler
{\cal S}
if

\Delta_{i+1}={\cal S}(s_{0}\Delta_{1}s_{1}\cdots\Delta_{i}s_{i})
for
i=0,\ldots,n-1
.
We denote with
T(s,{\cal S})
the collection of terminated computations
\gamma

which start with
s
and agree with the scheduler
{\cal S}
.

The probability that
s
terminates relatively to a scheduler
{\cal S}
is defined as:

\Sigma_{\gamma\in T(s,{\cal S})}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma)\qquad\mbox{(probability of termination, relative to a scheduler).}

Definition 29.2
(almost sure termination)

We say that a probabilistic rewriting system
(S,\rightarrow)
terminates almost
surely (or with probability
1
), if for any scheduler
{\cal S}
and any
s\in S
the
probability that
s
terminates relatively to
{\cal S}
is
1
.

Example 29.1

Consider the probabilistic rewriting system
(S,\rightarrow)
where

S=\{a,b\}
,
a\rightarrow\Delta_{2}
,
a\rightarrow\Delta_{3}
, and for

n\in\{2,3\}
:

\Delta_{n}(a)=\frac{1}{n},\qquad\Delta_{n}(b)=1-\frac{1}{n}~.

At each rewriting step the system reaches the normal form
b
with
probability at least
1/3
. Then the probability of a non-terminated,

n
steps computation is at most
(1-1/3)^{n}
and since this quantity
goes to
0
as
n
goes to infinity we can conclude that for any
scheduler the probability of a non-terminating computation is null.

Remark 29.1

The reader should keep in mind that the
definition of almost sure termination depends on the class of
schedulers. It is possible to enlarge this class by
considering randomized
schedulers. A randomized scheduler is not forced to choose one reduction
but can choose a distribution
over the set of possible reductions.
For instance if
s\rightarrow\Delta_{1}
and
s\rightarrow\Delta_{2}
then
the scheduler may select the distribution
\Delta_{1}
with probability

1/3
and the distribution
\Delta_{2}
with probability
2/3
.
In another direction, one may just focus on memoryless schedulers that
select the next reduction as a function of the last state of the computation.

We introduce next a notion of
finite
probabilistic rewriting system
and provide a simple criteria that guarantees almost sure termination
for such systems.

Definition 29.3
(finite probabilistic rewriting system)

We say that the probabilistic rewriting system
(S,\rightarrow)

is finite if
S
is finite and the relation
\rightarrow
is finite.

Notice that even if
S
is finite, there still can be uncountably many
distributions; hence the additional condition on the finiteness of
the rewriting relation. For finite systems, it is possible to give a
simple sufficient criteria for probabilistic termination.

Proposition 29.1

Let
(S,\rightarrow)
be a finite probabilistic rewriting system.
Let
(W,>)
be a well-founded order and
\mu:S\rightarrow W
a function
such that:

s\rightarrow\Delta\mbox{ implies }\exists s^{\prime}(\Delta(s^{\prime})>0\mbox{ and }\mu(s)>\mu(s^{\prime}))~.

(29.2)

Then
(S,\rightarrow)
terminates almost surely.

Proof
. Let
n=\sharp S
be the number of elements in
S
.
Notice that for any computation
\gamma=s_{0}\Delta_{1}s_{1}\cdots\Delta_{m}s_{m}

such that
\mu(s_{0})>\cdots>\mu(s_{m})
we must have
m=\ell(\gamma)\leq n-1
.
This is because the
s_{0},\ldots,s_{m}
must be all distinct,
as the order
>
is well-founded, and
S

contains
n
elements.

Let
q
be the least positive probability occurring
in a distribution; it exists because there are finitely
many distributions in the system and
S
is finite. Then let
p=q^{n-1}
.
For any execution
\gamma
such that
\ell(\gamma)\leq(n-1)
,
we have
\mathbb{P}(\gamma)\leq p
.

We are going to generalize the argument in example
29.1
.
We denote with
{\it NT}(s,{\cal S},m)
the set of
not
terminated executions
starting with
s
, having length
m
,
and agreeing with the scheduler

{\cal S}
. We show that for every
starting state
s
, for every scheduler
{\cal S}
, and for every
integer
k\geq 1
:

\Sigma_{\gamma\in{\it NT}(s,{\cal S},k\cdot(n-1))}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma)\leq(1-p)^{k}~.

(29.3)

If
k=1
then we know that there is at least one terminating
execution with length at most
n-1
and probability at least
p
.
Hence the probability of the non-terminating executions of length

(n-1)
is at most
(1-p)
.
Next, suppose the assertion for
k
. We are going to decompose
a non-terminating execution of length
(k+1)\cdot(n-1)

as a non-terminating execution of length
k\cdot(n-1)

concatenated with a non-terminating execution of length
(n-1)
.
Let
N
abbreviate
{\it NT}(s,{\cal S},(k+1)\cdot(n-1))
. Define the
set of computations
I
as the prefixes of the computations in
N

of length
k\cdot(n-1)
:

I=\{\gamma\mid\ell(\gamma)=k\cdot(n-1),\exists\gamma^{\prime}(\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\in N)\}~.

Notice that
I\subseteq{\it NT}(s,{\cal S},k\cdot(n-1))
, hence by inductive
hypothesis we have:

\Sigma_{\gamma\in I}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma)\leq(1-p)^{k}~.

(29.4)

For each
\gamma\in I
, we define
J_{\gamma}
as the set of computations
that concatenated with
\gamma
produce a computation in
N
:

J_{\gamma}=\{\gamma^{\prime}\mid\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\in N\}~.

By an argument similar to the one for the base case, we derive that:

\Sigma_{\gamma^{\prime}\in J_{\gamma}}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma^{\prime})\leq(1-p)~.

(29.5)

Then we can conclude by the following computation:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Sigma_{\gamma\in N}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma)&=\Sigma_{\gamma^{\prime}\in I,\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in J_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\gamma^{\prime\prime})\\
&=\Sigma_{\gamma^{\prime}\in I,\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in J_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma^{\prime})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\\
&=\Sigma_{\gamma^{\prime}\in I}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma^{\prime})\cdot(\Sigma_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in J_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}))\\
&\leq\Sigma_{\gamma^{\prime}\in I}\ \mathbb{P}(\gamma^{\prime})\cdot(1-p)&\mbox{(by \ref{base-case-prob})}\\
&\leq(1-p)^{(k+1)}&\mbox{(by \ref{ind-case-prob})}~.\end{array}

{\Box}

Example 29.2

Let
S=\{a,b,c,d\}
with rewriting:

\begin{array}[]{llll}a\rightarrow b,&a\rightarrow c&b\rightarrow a+_{1/2}d&c\rightarrow a+_{1/3}d\end{array}

Taking the order
a>b,c>d
and
\mu
as the identity,
we satisfy condition (
29.2
). Hence the
system terminates almost surely.

Exercise 29.1

Consider the probabilistic rewriting system
(S,\rightarrow)
with

S=\{a,b\}
and
a\rightarrow\Delta_{n}
for
n\geq 1
, where:

\begin{array}[]{lll}p_{n}=\frac{1}{2^{n}},&\Delta_{n}(a)=(1-p_{n}),&\Delta_{n}(b)=p_{n}~.\end{array}

Show that this (non-finite) system satisfies the
condition (
29.2
)
but it does not terminate almost surely.
On the other hand, the system does terminate almost surely
if we just consider memoryless schedulers (remark
29.2
).

29.2
Dining philosophers (*)

As a substantial case study for probabilistic termination,
we introduce a family of systems freely inspired by the classical
problem of the dining philosophers which we describe briefly
(the problem was proposed in 1965
by E. Dijkstra at a final examination).

A system is composed of
n\geq 2
dining philosophers, alternating with

n
chopsticks, with the
2n
elements disposed in a ring.
The goal of each philosopher
is to grab repeatedly the two chopsticks on his left and on his right
and eat. The problem is that two philosophers may compete for the
same chopstick and that each philosopher can eat only once it
has grabbed the two chopsticks next to him.
A symmetric, distributed, and probabilistic strategy to
coordinate the philosophers’
activities proposed by
[
LR81
]
goes as follows.
Each philosopher is repeatedly engaged in the following tasks:

1.

It decides probabilistically whether to look for the left or right chopstick,

2.

Say, it decides to go for the left chopstick first (the case where it goes for the right is completely symmetric). Then it waits till he can grab it.

3.

Then it looks whether the right chopstick is available: if it is then it grabs it, eats, and releases the two
chopsticks, otherwise he releases the chopstick already grabbed.

Our goal is to show that for any possible scheduler and initial state,
at least one philosopher will manage to eat (but still leaving the possibility
that one or more philosophers will starve).
We rephrase this goal as
a termination problem as follows: we remove the transitions in step 3.
that move a philosopher into eating state
and show that: (i) the remaining transitions must
terminate almost surely and (ii) in each normal form
at least one philosopher can grab the second chopstick and eat.

Let
X=\{\ell,L,r,R\}
be a set describing the
4
possible
states of each philosopher (before eating). Intuitively,
\ell
(
r
)
is the state where the philosopher is trying to grab the left (right)
chopstick and
L
(
R
) is the state where the philosopher has grabbed
the left (right) chopstick.

A state of a system of
n
philosophers is a vector

(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})
such
x_{i}\in X
and for no
i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
we have
x_{i}=R
and
x_{i+1}=L
, where addition is
always intended modulo
n
. By forbidding the configuration
x_{i}=R

and
x_{i+1}=L
, we exclude an odd situation where a chopstick is
grabbed at the same time by two philosophers.

A state of a system rewrites to another state if one of its
components, say
x_{i}
rewrites into
y_{i}
according to the following
rules, for
i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
:

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l}x_{i}=&y_{i}=&\mbox{side condition}\\
\hline\cr\ell&L&\mbox{if }x_{i-1}\neq R\\
r&R&\mbox{if }x_{i+1}\neq L\\
L&\ell+_{1/2}r&\mbox{if }x_{i+1}=L\\
R&\ell+_{1/2}r&\mbox{if }x_{i-1}=R\end{array}

The last two rewriting rules produce non-Dirac distributions; the notation

\ell+_{1/2}r
means that
x_{i}
becomes
\ell
with probability
1/2
and

r
with probability
1/2
.

Example 29.3

Let us consider some reductions for the case
n=3
. Starting from the
state
s_{0}=(r,\ell,\ell)
we can have, for instance the following reductions:

\begin{array}[]{l}s_{0}\rightarrow(r,L,\ell)\rightarrow(r,L,L)\rightarrow(r,\ell,L)+_{1/2}(r,r,L)~,\\
(r,\ell,L)\rightarrow(r,L,L)~,\\
(r,r,L)\rightarrow(R,r,L)\not\rightarrow~.\end{array}

So for some scheduler, it is possible to loop back with
probability
1/2
or to go to a normal form with probability
1/2
.

It is useful to identify certain special configurations arising during
the reduction.

Definition 29.4
(separation and conflict points)

Let
s=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})
be a state.
The integer
i\in{\bf Z}_{n}
is a:

•

separation point for
s

if
x_{i}\in\{\ell,L\}
and
x_{i+1}\in\{r,R\}
.

•

conflict point for
s
if

x_{i}\in\{r,R\}
and
x_{i+1}\in\{\ell,L\}

(with the usual proviso that
x_{i}=R
and
x_{i+1}=L
is
impossible).

Proposition 29.2

The following properties hold.

1.

In every state, there are as many separation points as conflict points.

2.

Separation and conflict points alternate and there are at most
n/2
of them.

3.

Rewriting cannot decrease the number of separation and conflict points.

4.

Rewriting cannot change the position of a separation point.

Exercise 29.2

Prove proposition
29.2
.

By proposition
29.2
(4),
separation points once created keep a fixed position.
On the other hand, each conflict point
is framed by two separation points on the left and on the right
but its exact position between these two separation points
may change. Then we have to make sure that a
conflict point cannot oscillate between the two framing
separation points indefinitely.
To this end, we introduce a notion of
potential
of a conflict point.

Definition 29.5
(potential)

Let
s=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})
be a state and

i\in{\bf Z}_{n}
a conflict point for
s
. Its potential is determined as follows:

•

if
x_{i}=r,x_{i+1}=\ell
take the maximum distance from the
separation points framing
i
.

•

if
x_{i}=r,x_{i+1}=L
take the distance from the separation point
framing
i
on the right.

•

if
x_{i}=R,x_{i+1}=\ell
take the distance from the separation point
framing
i
on the left.

Definition 29.6
(well-founded measure)

The measure we associate to a state is the lexicographic order from left to right of:

1.

n/2
minus the number of separation points (if
n
is odd take
the integer part of
n/2
).

2.

sum of the potentials of the conflict points.

3.

number of states which are
\ell
or
r
.

Example 29.4

Continuing example
29.3
, consider the following (probabilistic)
reduction and the corresponding strictly decreasing sequence in the
left to right lexicographic order:

\begin{array}[]{llll}(r,\ell,\ell)&\rightarrow(r,L,\ell)&\rightarrow(r,L,L)&\rightarrow_{1/2}(r,r,L)~,\\
(0,2,3)&>_{{\it lex}}(0,2,2)&>_{{\it lex}}(0,2,1)&>_{{\it lex}}(0,1,2)~.\end{array}

Proposition 29.3

The rewriting system of the dining philosophers terminates
almost surely.

Proof
. It is enough to check that condition (
29.2
)
of proposition
29.1
is satisfied.
The first two (non-probabilistic) rules do not affect the
number and the position of the separation (and conflict) points.
The potential of the conflict points does not increase since the
potential of a conflict point of the shape
r\ell
is the maximum
of the potential of the conflict points of the shape
R\ell
or
rL

that can be obtained as a result of a reduction of this type.
On the other hand, the number of states
\ell
or
r
is decreased by
1
.

The following two probabilistic rule have a symmetric treatment.
Let us consider the situation where
x_{i}=L
is replaced by

\ell+_{1/2}r
where
x_{i+1}=L
. If we transform
L
to
\ell
we are
in a loop. So let us consider the case where we transform

x_{i}x_{i+1}=LL
into
rL
and distinguish two cases.

•

If
x_{i-1}\in\{\ell,L\}
then we increase by one the number
of separation points and the first component of the measure decrease.

•

If
x_{i-1}=r
(here
x_{i-1}
cannot be
R
)
then the conflict point at position
i-1
has moved to position
i

and therefore its potential,
i.e.
, its distance from the
separation point on the right, has decreased by
1
. So the second
component decreases while the third increases and the first stays the same.
{\Box}

Every normal form contains a conflict point, and around a conflict
point there is a philosopher that can eat by grabbing a second chopstick.
For instance, if the conflict point
i
is such that
x_{i},x_{i+1}=R,\ell

then
x_{i-1}\notin\{r,R\}
by the hypothesis that we have a normal
form. Hence
x_{i-1}\in\{\ell,L\}
and the philosopher in position
i

can grab the left chopstick. Therefore for any initial configuration,
almost surely
the system goes to a configuration where at least one philosopher is going to eat.

Remark 29.2

In the second step of the original probabilistic algorithm
for the dining philosophers

[
LR81
]
,
each philosopher actively tries to get the first chopstick. This
induces a potential looping computation that is ruled out by assuming
a fair scheduler. In the presented modeling, we
suppose that the philosopher is suspended and
may resume the computation when the chopstick
becomes available. In this case, as noted by

[
DFP04
]
, it is possible
to prove probabilistic termination without making fairness assumptions.

29.3
Summary and references

Probabilistic rewriting systems are rewriting systems where
a state can reduce to a distribution over states.
Markov chains (see,
e.g.
,
[
Nor98
]
)
are a typical example of
purely
probabilistic rewriting
systems. In this chapter, we have considered more general systems which
can have both probabilistic and non-deterministic reductions.
To speak of probabilities in the presence of non-determinism,
one introduces a class of
schedulers
.
Each scheduler resolves the non-determinism and brings us back to the realm
of purely probabilistic rewriting.
A probabilistic rewriting system
terminates almost surely if for every scheduler, the system terminates with
probability
1
.
For
finite
probabilistic rewriting systems, it is possible
to give a simple criteria that guarantees almost sure termination
(see,
e.g.
,
[
DFP01
]
) and
as an application of the criteria, we have analyzed a variant of
the dining philosophers protocol

[
LR81
,
DFP04
]
.
There is a growing body of results on
probabilistic
labelled
transition systems which we have entirely omitted. See,
e.g.
,

[
JLY01
]
for a preliminary overview and
some references.

Chapter 30
\pi
-calculus

{\it CCS}
provides a basic model of
communication
and
concurrency
while ignoring the mechanisms of
procedural
and

data abstraction
which are at the heart of sequential
programming (in this respect,
{\it CCS}
is close to Turing machines).
We can contrast
{\it CCS}

with a basic model of sequential programming
such as the (typed)
\lambda
-calculus.
A basic question is: how can we
integrate
the
