31.3
Introduction to the semantics of concurrent objects

In this section and the following,
we analyze the semantics of
concurrent objects
such as
the Treiber’s stack described in Table
31.5
at the level
of labelled transition systems equipped
with a CCS like action structure (see chapter
25
).

We think of a
process
as a kind of CCS process, possibly the
result of the compilation of a more complex process involving value
passing as described in section
25.4
.
An
object
is a kind of passive process
that receives
requests
and may provide
answers
to them.
Think of a request as a method invocation and of an answer as the result
of an invocation. What happens between a request and the corresponding
answer is described by internal reductions. The analysis of
these internal reductions can
be quite challenging but it is
not
relevant to the following discussion.
A
client
is a process that may send requests to an object
and receive the respective answers. Moreover, a client may also
engage in observable activities
as schematized in Table
31.9
.
An object and a client agree on an
interface
to exchange requests and
answers and these exchanges are private (not directly observable).
Moreover, the interaction between an object and a client
complies with the following rules:

•

each request comes with a unique number and this number is recalled
in the corresponding answer ;
an object can answer at most once to any given request.

•

when a client sends a request it can decide either to
wait immediately for the corresponding answer
or to disregard forever any answer to the request.

\begin{array}[]{lc}\left.\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{$n$=interaction number}\\
\mbox{req=request}\\
\mbox{ans=answer}\end{array}\right.\par&\begin{array}[]{ccc}\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mbox{{\sc object}}&\left.\begin{array}[]{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\it req},n)}}{{\longleftarrow}}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\it ans},n)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\end{array}\right.&\mbox{{\sc client}}\end{array}\right]&\leftrightarrow&{\mbox{\sc observer}}\end{array}\par\end{array}

Table 31.9:
Request/answer interaction between object and client

Formalization

To represent formally the expected behaviour of objects and clients,
we shall introduce:

•

a kind of
enriched CCS action structure
to represent the
unique number associated with each pair of request and answer.

•

a
co-inductive definition
of the behaviour expected by objects
and clients.

Beyond the correct handling of requests and answers, the co-inductive
definition has to capture the intended client’s behaviour.
It turns out that this can be done abstractly at the level of the
client’s labelled transition system as follows:

•

answer anticipation: an answer can anticipate any
other action which is not the corresponding request,

•

request postponement: a request can be postponed after
any action which is not the answer to the request.

Comparing objects

Assuming this framework,
let us now move towards the problem of comparing objects’ behaviours.
As we already mentioned, requests and answers are
protected from a direct observation.
Given this, how do we compare two objects, say
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
?
We shall assume a
may-testing framework
as described in section

26.2
. Specifically, we assume clients may commit
on a special action
w
and write
p_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}p_{2}
if
for every client
q
,
(p_{1}\mid q){\sf may}
implies
(p_{2}\mid q){\sf may}
.
The main technical result characterizes this pre-order as
a pre-order based on
trace inclusion up to trace rewriting
.

An example

We illustrate this preliminary discussion with a concrete example.
Suppose we want to analyze objects that are supposed to
implement a multiple readers, multiple writers, boolean variable.
We fix the interface between object and client as follows:

•

the labels
r,w_{0},w_{1}
represent the
requests : read, write
0
, and write
1
, respectively.

•

the labels
r_{0},r_{1},w
represent the answers:
value read
0
, value read
1
, and write completed, respectively.

We recall that in standard CCS, an action over some set of labels
A

is either a distinct internal action
\tau
or an action
a\in A

or a co-action
\overline{a}
. We shall introduce an
enriched
framework,
where an action distinct
by the internal action is actually a pair
(a,n)
where
a\in A
and

n\in\mathbf{N}
is a natural number. Each action
(a,n)
has a co-action

\overline{(a,n)}
. We use the enrichment to describe the expected properties
of objects and their clients.

Returning to the boolean variable example and taking the object’s
viewpoint, a request is an action
(a,n)
where

a\in\{r,w_{0},w_{1}\}
,
and an answer is a co-action
\overline{(a,n)}

where
a\in\{r_{0},r_{1},w\}
. So in the modelling,
the action-co-action mechanism
is instrumental to the distinction between object and client.

In Table
31.10
, we describe the labelled
transitions of a first object that answers
immediately each request. An object that exhibits this
behaviour is often called
sequential
and it is taken
as the specification to which
concurrent

implementations should comply.

Let
N\subseteq\mathbf{N}
be a set which contains
the numbers still available for an interaction
with the client.
The rules describe a labelled transition system over
the enriched action structure. One can think of the
A_{j}(\ldots)

as parametric processes; once the parameters are given we have a process
and the rules in Table
31.10
describe its labelled transitions.

\begin{array}[]{llll}A_{1}(x,N)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(r,n)}}{{\rightarrow}}&A_{2}(x,N\backslash\{n\},n)&(n\in N)\\
A_{1}(x,N)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(w_{x},n)}}{{\rightarrow}}&A_{3}(x,N\backslash\{n\},n)&(n\in N)\\
A_{1}(x,N)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(w_{\overline{x}},n)}}{{\rightarrow}}&A_{3}(\overline{x},N\backslash\{n\},n)&(n\in N)\\
A_{2}(x,N,n)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(r_{x},n)}}}{{\rightarrow}}&A_{1}(x,N)&(n\notin N)\\
A_{3}(x,N,n)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(w,n)}}}{{\rightarrow}}&A_{1}(x,N)&(n\notin N)\par\end{array}

Table 31.10:
A sequential object for a boolean variable

The description of the sequential object is rigorous but not
very readable. A more compact and readable notation is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}A(x)&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.A(x)+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(\overline{x})\end{array}

(31.3)

Here the sequential object is described as a recursive process that
is ready to receive three types of requests with the associated number
n
,
answers immediately the selected request, and updates the value
of the variable if needed (if
x
is a boolean value then
\overline{x}
is its complement). We rely on this handy notation to describe
in Table
31.11
three variants of the sequential object.
In the first variant
(V_{1})
, the object answers immediately each request
but it may lazily update the value of the variable (updating is presumably a costly operation).
In the second variant
(V_{2})
, the object delays the answer to a request to complement the value of the variable.
Finally, the third variant
(V_{3})
combines the strategies of the first and second variant. We shall consider the relationships among these variants in example
31.5
once the semantic framework is in place.

\begin{array}[]{lll}A(x)&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.A(x)+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(\overline{x})+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.B(x)&(V_{1})\\
B(x)&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.B(x)+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(\overline{x})\\
\\
A(x)&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.A(x)+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).B(x,n)&(V_{2})\\
B(x,n^{\prime})&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.B(x,n^{\prime})+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n^{\prime})}.\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n^{\prime})}.\overline{(w,n)}.A(\overline{x})\\
\\
A(x)&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.A(x)+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(\overline{x})+w_{\overline{x}}(n).B(x,n)&(V_{3})\\
B(x,n^{\prime})&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.B(x,n^{\prime})+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n^{\prime})}.\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n^{\prime})}.\overline{(w,n)}.A(\overline{x})\end{array}

Table 31.11:
Three variants of the boolean variable

We stress that there is no need to describe the
internal

object’s behaviour using a CCS notation as we have done for
the boolean variable. For instance, in the case of the stack
object described in section
31.1
, the object’s interface

I=I_{R}\cup I_{A}
could be described as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}I_{R}=\{{\sf empty},{\sf pop},{\sf push_{v}}\mid v\in V\}~,&I_{A}=\{{\sf true},{\sf false},{\sf pop_{null}},{\sf pop_{v}},{\sf push}\mid v\in V\}~,\end{array}

where
V
is the set of values that can be stored in the stack
not containing the special element
null
.
The description of the internal state of the object would typically
rely on a heap memory similar to the one described in
section
31.2
.
Moreover, for each kind of request there should be a formal description of
the legal sequences of atomic heap transformations possibly
leading to an answer.

Exercise 31.2

Prove or disprove: every trace of the sequential object in Table
31.10

is a trace of the object variant

V_{i}
in Table
31.11
, for
i=1,2,3
.

31.4
Co-inductive definitions of objects and clients (*)

In this section, we formalize the hypotheses on objects and clients
and derive certain commutations properties of the clients’ traces.
Let
A
be a set of labels and
\mathbf{N}
be the set of natural numbers.
The collection of
actions

Act
is defined as:

{\it Act}=\{\tau\}\cup\{(a,n)\mid a\in A,n\in\mathbf{N}\}\cup\{\overline{(a,n)}\mid a\in A,n\in\mathbf{N}\}\qquad\mbox{(actions)}

Let
P
be a set whose elements we call
processes
.
We suppose a labelled transition system
(P,\rightarrow,{\it Act})
where

\rightarrow\subseteq P\times{\it Act}\times P
.
We suppose that the labelled transition system (lts) has a neutral element

0
which has no transitions at all and it
is closed under action prefix,
parallel composition, and action restriction.
Namely, if
p,q\in P
, and
a
is an action (not an internal action)
then
there are processes strongly equivalent to
a.p
,
(p\mid q)
,
and
\nu a\ p
,
where
a.\_
,
\_\mid\_
, and
\nu a\ \_
are the usual
action prefix, parallel composition, and action restriction
as described in chapter
25
.
As usual, synchronization is the combination of an action with a co-action;
the label
and
the number in the action and the co-action have to be the
same.
Here
strong equivalence
can be taken to mean,
e.g.
,
strong bisimulation (definition
23.3
).

Among all processes, we want to define those that are objects and those
that are clients. These definitions are parametric on an interface which is
defined as a pair of disjoint sets of labels
I_{R},I_{A}
:

I_{R},I_{A}\subseteq A,I_{R}\cap I_{A}=\emptyset\qquad{\mbox{(client-object interface)}}

with the convention that the labels in
I_{R}
are used to formulate requests
and the labels in
I_{A}
to provide answers.
We also denote with
{\it Obs}
the set of observable actions in a system,

i.e.
, those which do not use the labels in the interface:

{\it Obs}=\{(a,n),\overline{(a,n)}\mid n\in\mathbf{N},a\notin I_{R}\cup I_{A}\}\qquad\mbox{(observable actions)}.

Given an interface, we want to define the collection of objects and
the collection of clients which are compatible with this interface.
However, these definitions depend on the collection of numbers that
can be used to formulate requests and the collection of numbers that
have been used to formulate a request and can still be used to provide an
answer. So rather than defining a set of processes we actually define
a
family of sets
of processes indexed on a pair of disjoint
sets of natural numbers. We start with the definition of the family of
objects which is simpler. As expected, families of sets are partially
ordered by pointwise set inclusion.

Definition 31.1
(objects)

We define co-inductively a family of objects:

O:2^{\mathbf{N}}\times 2^{\mathbf{N}}\rightarrow 2^{P}

as the largest family
such that for all
N_{1},N_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{N}
:
(i) if
O(N_{1},N_{2})\neq\emptyset
then
N_{1}\cap N_{2}=\emptyset
and
(ii) if
p\in O(N_{1},N_{2})
and
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
then exactly one of
the following conditions holds:

•

\alpha=\tau
and
p^{\prime}\in O(N_{1},N_{2})
,

•

\alpha=(a,n)
,
a\in I_{R}
,
n\in N_{1}
,
and
p^{\prime}\in O(N_{1}\backslash\{n\},N_{2}\cup\{n\})
,

•

\alpha=\overline{(a,n)}
,
a\in I_{A}
,
n\in N_{2}

and
p^{\prime}\in O(N_{1},N_{2}\backslash\{n\})
.

Notice that an object cannot perform observable actions outside its
interface.
As usual with co-inductive definitions, to prove, say, that
p\in O(N_{1},N_{2})

it suffices to exhibit a family
F
that satisfies the conditions
in definition
31.1
and such that
p\in F(N_{1},N_{2})
.
We practice on the following example.

Example 31.1

Let us reconsider the sequential object of Table
31.10
.
Recall that in this case the interface is:

I_{R}=\{r,w_{0},w_{1}\}
and
I_{A}=\{r_{0},r_{1},w\}
.
We define for
N_{1},N_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{N}
the family
F
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}F(N_{1},N_{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{A_{1}(x,N_{1})\mid x\in\{0,1\}\}&\mbox{if }N_{2}=\emptyset\\
\{A_{2}(x,N_{1},n),A_{3}(x,N_{1},n)\mid x\in\{0,1\}\}&\mbox{if }N_{2}=\{n\},n\notin N_{1}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

It remains to check that the
5
transition schema in Table
31.10

respect the conditions in definition
31.1
.

Next we turn to the co-inductive definition of a family of
clients. It is not difficult to imagine a concrete syntax in some
value passing process calculus where a client generates a fresh number
to formulate a request and then spawns a thread that immediately waits
for an answer to the request. However, here the name of the game is
to describe axiomatically at the level of the labelled transition
system what the expected behaviour of a client is. The good news is
that the conditions turn out to be rather simple.

Definition 31.2
(clients)

We define co-inductively a family of clients:

C:2^{\mathbf{N}}\times 2^{\mathbf{N}}\rightarrow 2^{P}

as the largest family such that for all
N_{1},N_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{N}
:
(i) if
C(N_{1},N_{2})\neq\emptyset
then
N_{1}\cap N_{2}=\emptyset
and
(ii) if
p\in C(N_{1},N_{2})
and
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
then the following conditions hold:

•

if
\alpha\in\{\tau\}\cup{\it Obs}
then
p^{\prime}\in C(N_{1},N_{2})
.

•

if
\alpha=(a,n)\notin{\it Obs}
then
a\in I_{A}
,
n\in N_{2}
,
and
p^{\prime}\in C(N_{1},N_{2}\backslash\{n\})
.

•

if
p^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a,n)}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime\prime}
,
a\in I_{A}
,
n\in N_{2}
and

\alpha\neq\overline{(b,n)}
for
b\in I_{R}
then:

p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a,n)}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime\prime}\qquad\mbox{(answer anticipation)}

•

if
\alpha=\overline{(a,n)}\notin{\it Obs}
then
a\in I_{R}
,
n\in N_{1}

and
p^{\prime}\in C(N_{1}\backslash\{n\},N_{2}\cup\{n\})
; moreover, we require
that if
p^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime\prime}
and
\beta\neq(b,n)
for
b\in I_{R}
then:

p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(a,n)}}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime\prime}\qquad\mbox{(request postponement).}

If we denote with
r_{i}
a request with number
i
and with
a_{i}
the
corresponding answer then the two most important conditions
for a client
p
are described by the following inference rules:

\begin{array}[]{cc}\mbox{(answer anticipation)}&\mbox{(request postponement)}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{i}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot p^{\prime}\qquad\alpha\neq r_{i}\\
\hline\cr p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{i}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}\end{array}\par&\begin{array}[]{c}p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r_{i}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}\qquad\alpha\neq a_{i}\\
\hline\cr p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r_{i}}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}\end{array}\par\end{array}

which can be rephrased in plain English as follows:
(i) an answer can
anticipate any action which is not the corresponding request and
(ii)
a request can be postponed after
any action which is not the answer to the request.
The remaining conditions just ensure that the numbers associated with
requests and answers are used as expected.

Example 31.2

In Table
31.12
, we describe a simple client for the objects introduced in the Tables
31.10

and
31.11
. Informally, the client repeats the following
behaviour: (i)
it chooses internally whether to read or write a boolean variable,
(ii) it sends the corresponding request to the object, (iii) then it waits for
an answer, and (iv) finally, it performs some observable action
(a,0)
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}(N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{2}(N)\hskip 17.00024ptA_{1}(N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{3}(N)\hskip 17.00024ptA_{1}(N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{4}(N)\\
A_{2}(N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(r,n)}}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{5}(N\backslash\{n\},n)&n\in N\\
A_{3}(N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(w_{0},n)}}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{6}(N\backslash\{n\},n)&n\in N\\
A_{4}(N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(w_{0},n)}}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{6}(N\backslash\{n\},n)&n\in N\\
A_{5}(N,n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(r_{0},n)}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{7}(N)\hskip 17.00024ptA_{5}(N,n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(r_{1},n)}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{7}(N)&n\notin N\\
A_{6}(N,n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(w,n)}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{7}(N)&n\notin N\\
A_{7}(N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(a,0)}}}{{\rightarrow}}A_{1}(N)\end{array}

Table 31.12
:
An example of a client

As in the example
31.1
, we define for
N_{1},N_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{N}
a family
F
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}F(N_{1},N_{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{A_{i}(N_{1})\mid i\in\{1,2,3,4,7\}\}&\mbox{if }N_{2}=\emptyset\\
\{A_{i}(N_{1},n)\mid i\in\{5,6\},\}&\mbox{if }N_{2}=\{n\},n\notin N_{1}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

It remains to check that the transitions
respect the conditions in definition
31.2
.
In a less formal notation that complements the one introduced
for objects, the client can be described by the
following recursive equation:

A=\tau.\nu n\ \overline{r}n.(r,n).A+\tau.\nu n\ \overline{w_{0}}n.(w,n).A+\tau.\nu n\ \overline{w_{1}}n.(w,n).A~.

Here, e.g.,
\nu n\ \overline{r}n.(r,n)
denotes the action of issuing
the request
r
along with a fresh number
n
and
(r,n)
denotes
the action of receiving the corresponding answer.

We can now state some consequences of the answer
anticipation and request postponement hypotheses on the clients’ behaviours
and on the way clients can observe objects.
Suppose
s=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}

is a sequence of actions.
We write
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
as an abbreviation
for
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
.
We also write
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
to mean
that there is some
p^{\prime\prime}
such that

p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime\prime}
and
p^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
.

Proposition 31.1

Suppose
p\in C(N_{1},N_{2})
is a client and
s=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}

is a sequence of actions in
\{\tau\}\cup{\it Obs}
.

1.

If
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(a_{1},n_{1})}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(a_{2},n_{2})}}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
then

p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(a_{2},n_{2})}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(a_{1},n_{1})}}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
, where
a_{1},a_{2}\in I_{R}
.

2.

If
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a_{1},n_{1})}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a_{2},n_{2})}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
then

p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a_{2},n_{2})}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a_{1},n_{1})}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
, where

a_{1},a_{2}\in I_{A}
.

3.

If
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(a_{1},n_{1})}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a_{2},n_{2})}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
then

p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a_{2},n_{2})}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{(a_{1},n_{1})}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}p^{\prime}
, where

a_{1}\in I_{R}
,
a_{2}\in I_{A}
, and
n_{1}\neq n_{2}
.

Exercise 31.3

Prove proposition
31.1
.

An informal way to state the proposition is as follows.
Say that a request/answer is adjacent to another request/answer
in a client’s trace if all actions performed between the two are in

\{\tau\}\cup{\it Obs}
.

1.

two adjacent requests can be commuted.

2.

two adjacent answers can be commuted.

3.

a request followed by an unrelated answer can be commuted.

Notice however that in general an answer followed by a
request cannot be commuted as the answer may block the execution
of the request.
The trace of an object is composed just
of requests and answers (it does not contain observable actions).
The commutation properties of the traces of
a client induce legal commutations in the traces of an object.
We illustrate this point with an example.

Example 31.3

We abbreviate with
r_{i}
a request action
and with
a_{i}
the corresponding answer actions.
Consider the following
5
very simple objects (one trace suffices to
describe their behaviour):

\begin{array}[]{lllll}p_{1}=r_{1}.r_{2}.\overline{a}_{1}.\overline{a}_{2},&p_{2}=r_{2}.r_{1}.\overline{a}_{1}.\overline{a}_{2},&p_{3}=r_{1}.r_{2}.\overline{a}_{2}.\overline{a}_{1},&p_{4}=r_{1}.r_{2}.\overline{a}_{1}.\overline{a}_{2}.r_{3},&p_{5}=r_{1}.\overline{a}_{1}.r_{2}.\overline{a}_{2}.\par\end{array}

Let us call system the parallel composition
of the object with a client where the names in the
interface are suitably restricted.
Because on the client side requests can be commuted,
any trace the system can generate relying on object

p_{1}
can also be generated relying on object
p_{2}
(and vice versa).
Similarly, because on the client side answers can be commuted,
any trace the system can generate relying on object
p_{1}

can also be generated relying on object
p_{3}
(and vice versa).
Because on the client side requests can be postponed,
any trace the system can generate relying on object
p_{4}

can also be generated relying on object
p_{1}
(and vice versa).
Because an answer can anticipate an unrelated request, any
trace the system can generate relying on object
p_{1}
can
also be generated relying on object
p_{5}
. However, this time
the converse fails. Suppose the client
q
has the shape:

q=\overline{r}_{1}.a_{1}.\alpha.\overline{r}_{2}.a_{2}~,

where
\alpha
is some observable action. The client
q

can produce the trace
\alpha
while interacting with
p_{5}

but it fails to do so while interacting with
p_{1}
.

31.5
May testing and trace pre-orders (*)

We adapt the may strong commitment testing
described in section
26.2
.
We assume there is a special observable action
w
.
There is no co-action for this action. This action is
the device by which a client marks the success of a test
on the object. In this section, we show that trace
inclusion up to a certain trace rewriting relation
is included in the may-testing pre-order.

Definition 31.3
(
{\sf may}
pre-order)

If
p_{1},p_{2}
are objects in
O(N_{1},N_{2})
then we write

p_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}p
if for all clients
q\in C(N_{1},N_{2})
,

(p_{1}\mid q){\sf may}
implies
(p_{2}\mid q){\sf may}
.

Rewriting traces

For a given interface
I=I_{R}\cup I_{A}
,
let
t
be an object’s (finite) trace.
This is a word over the following (infinite) alphabet:

\{(a,n)\mid a\in I_{R},n\in\mathbf{N}\}\cup\{\overline{(a,n)}\mid a\in I_{A},n\in\mathbf{N}\}~.

Because of definition
31.1
, for each
n\in\mathbf{N}
there is
at most one request and one answer associated with
n
and if both
are present then the request must precede the answer.
As in the example
31.3
above,
let us abbreviate a request and an answer depending on the number
n

with
r_{n}
and
a_{n}
, respectively.
We define rules to rewrite traces. The transitive and reflexive
closure of the rewriting system defines a pre-order on traces.

Definition 31.4
(trace pre-order)

A rewriting system on traces is defined by
the following (word) rewriting rules where
i\neq j
and
t,t^{\prime}

are arbitrary:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}(R_{1})&tr_{i}r_{j}t^{\prime}&\rightarrow&tr_{j}r_{i}t^{\prime}&\mbox{(request commutation)}\\
(R_{2})&ta_{i}a_{j}t^{\prime}&\rightarrow&ta_{j}a_{i}t^{\prime}&\mbox{(answer commutation)}\\
(R_{3})&tr_{i}a_{j}t^{\prime}&\rightarrow&ta_{j}r_{i}t^{\prime}&\mbox{(request-answer commutation)}\\
(R_{4})&tr_{i}&\rightarrow&t&\mbox{(erasure of terminal request)}\end{array}

We write
t\leq t^{\prime}
if there is a possibly empty sequence of reductions
from
t
to
t^{\prime}
. In other terms, the pre-order

\leq
is the reflexive and transitive closure
of the relation
\rightarrow
.

The rewriting system in definition
31.4
allows for
infinite reductions, however it is easy to see that in every such
reduction the last two rules (
R_{3}
and
R_{4}
) can only be
applied a finite number of times. First, notice that the erasure rule

(R_{4})

reduces the size of the trace while all the others keep it unchanged.
Second, define the
potential
of a trace as the sum of
the
distances
between each request and the corresponding
answer. The distance is just the number of times one has to move right
to go from a request to the corresponding answer. If a request has no
answer count the distance from a
virtual
answer located
rightmost in the trace. Similarly, if an answer has no request count
the distance from a virtual request located leftmost in the trace.
Then the first two rules (
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
)
keep the potential unchanged while the last two (
R_{3}
and
R_{4}
) decrease the
potential.

Trace inclusion up to rewriting

The pre-order on traces induces a pre-order on
objects as follows.

Definition 31.5
(trace object pre-order)

Let
p_{1},p_{2}\in O(N_{1},N_{2})
be two objects. Then we write
p_{1}\leq_{T}^{o}p_{2}
as:

\forall t_{1}\in T(p_{1})\ \exists t_{2}\in T(p_{2})\ (t_{1}\leq t_{2})~.

Remark 31.1

One may regard a trace as a particular simple object
(as in example
31.3
). Beware that the pre-orders on traces
and on objects are incomparable. For instance, consider

t_{1}=r_{1}a_{1}
,
t_{2}=r_{1}a_{1}r_{2}a_{2}
, and
t_{3}=r_{1}r_{2}a_{2}a_{1}
. Then:

t_{1}\not\leq t_{2}
and
t_{1}\leq_{T}^{o}t_{2}
.
On the other hand, we have:

t_{3}\leq t_{2}
and
t_{3}\not\leq_{T}^{o}t_{2}
.
This is because for any prefix
t
of
t_{2}
,
r_{1}r_{2}a_{2}\not\leq t
.

We notice the following property of clients.

Proposition 31.2

Let
p\in O(N_{1},N_{2})
be an object and
t\in T(p)
.
If
q\in C(N_{1},N_{2})
is a client such that
q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{t}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
and

t\leq s
then
q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
.

Exercise 31.4

Prove proposition
31.2

Trace inclusion up to rewriting is a
sound
criteria to infer
the may testing pre-order.

Proposition 31.3

If
p_{1},p_{2}\in O(N_{1},N_{2})
and
p_{1}\leq_{T}^{o}p_{2}
then
p_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}p_{2}
.

Proof
. Suppose
q\in C(N_{1},N_{2})
is a client and

(p_{1}\mid q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot
. Then there is a trace
t
such that

p_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot
and
q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{t}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
.
Since
p_{1}\leq_{T}^{o}p_{2}
, there is a trace
s
such that

t\leq s
and
p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot
.
By proposition
31.2
, we derive

q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
, and we conclude that
(p_{2}\mid q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.
{\Box}

31.6
Clients to test objects (*)

The goal of this section is to show that the converse of proposition

31.3
holds: the may testing pre-order is included in
trace inclusion up to rewriting. Thus, given proposition
31.3
,
trace inclusion up to rewriting is a sound and
complete
criteria
for the may testing pre-order.
To this end, we introduce a family of clients to test objects.
Each client depends on an object’s trace
t
which can be
regarded as an alternating sequences of
(distinct) requests and answers:

t=A_{0}R_{1}A_{1}\cdots R_{n}A_{n}R_{n+1}\qquad\mbox{(alternating structure object's trace).}

(31.4)

All these sequences are supposed non-empty with the exception of the
sequences
A_{0}
and
R_{n+1}
which can be empty. In particular, if the trace
t
is empty then we have
n=0
and
A_{0}=R_{1}=\emptyset
.
The definition of the tests is invariant under permutation of requests
(or answers) in the same sequence. For this reason, we can regard the sequences

A_{i},R_{i}
as sets too.

For
j=1,\ldots,n+1
, we denote with
R_{j}^{+}
the set composed
of the requests in
R_{j}
for which there is an answer in the trace
t

and we set
R_{j}^{-}=R_{j}\backslash R_{j}^{+}
; these are the requests in
R_{j}
for
which there is no answer in the trace
t
.
Recalling that requests must precede answers, notice that
R_{n+1}^{+}=\emptyset
and
R_{n+1}^{-}=R_{n+1}
.

Similarly, for
j=0,\ldots,n
, we denote with
A_{j}^{+}
the
set composed of the answers in
A_{j}
for which there is a request in the trace

t
and we set
A_{j}^{-}=A_{j}\backslash A_{j}^{+}
; these are the answers in
A_{j}
for
which there is no request in the trace
t
. Notice that

A_{0}^{-}=A_{0}
and
A_{0}^{+}=\emptyset
.

We denote with
D
a set composed of
n+1
fresh action names
d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}

which are not in the interface
I_{R}\cup I_{A}
. If
d
is one of these names
and
n
is a natural number then
d^{n}.P
stands for
(d,0).\cdots.(d,0).P

where the prefixed action
(d,0)
is repeated
n
times.

Finally, if
r\in R^{+}_{j}
is a request then we denote with

a(r)
the corresponding answer and
with
i(a(r))
the unique number such that
a(r)\in A_{i(a(r))}^{+}
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}C(A_{0}t)&=\Pi_{a\in\bigcup_{j=0,\ldots,n}A^{-}_{n}}\ a.\overline{d}_{i(a)}.0\mid d_{0}^{\sharp A_{0}}.C(t)&\mbox{(head)}\\
\\
C(R_{j}A_{j}t)&=\underbrace{\Pi_{r\in R_{j}^{+}}\ \overline{r}.a(r).\overline{d}_{i(a(r))}.0}_{\textit{group 1}}\mid\underbrace{\Pi_{r\in R_{j}^{-}}\ \overline{r}.0}_{\textit{group 2}}\mid\underbrace{d_{j}^{\sharp A_{j}}.C(t)}_{\textit{group 3}}&\mbox{(body)}\\
\\
C(R_{n+1})&=\Pi_{r\in R_{n+1}}\ \overline{r}.0\mid w&\mbox{(tail)}\par\par\end{array}

Table 31.13:
Client
C(t)
associated with an object’s trace
t

Given a trace
t
, the definition of the test
C(t)

is split in three parts that concern the
head
, the
body
, and
the
tail
of the trace, as displayed in Table
31.13

where all the names
d_{j}
, for
j=0,\ldots,n
, are supposed to be
restricted at top level so that they cannot occur in a test’s trace.
We describe informally the behaviour of the process
C(t)
.
Let us start with the
head
.
If
A_{0}\neq\emptyset
then
the process must receive all answers in

A_{0}
in some arbitrary order before moving to the body.
Next we consider the
body
.
The process
C(t)
starts in parallel three groups of threads.
Group 1 issues in parallel all the requests in
R_{j}^{+}
,
immediately waits for the corresponding answer, and signals this fact on the
name
d_{i(a(r))}
which corresponds to the answer (in general,

j\leq i(a(r))
).
Group 2 issues in parallel all the requests in
R_{j}^{-}

without waiting for any answer.
Group 3 is a single thread which
checks on action
d_{j}
that
all the answers in
A_{j}
have been received
before moving to process
C(t)
.
Notice that the process
must
receive all the answers in
A_{j}

but it is not obliged to issue all the
requests in
R_{j}
. It must only issue the requests which are necessary
to receive an answer in
A_{j}
. The remaining requests may well
be issued, if at all, in a later step.
Finally, we arrive at the
tail
.
The process issues the requests without answers
and is ready to perform the
w
success action.

Proposition 31.4

Let
p\in O(N_{1},N_{2})
be an object and suppose

t
is a trace in
T(p)
with the alternating structure
described in (
31.4
). Let
C(t)
be the
associated process described above. Then:

1.

C(t)
is a client in
C(N_{1},N_{2})
.

2.

C(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{t}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
.

Exercise 31.5

Prove proposition
31.4
.

We can now state the key property that relates the traces of the
client
C(t)
with the trace pre-order.

Proposition 31.5

Let
p\in O(N_{1},N_{2})
be an object
and suppose
t\in T(p)
is an object’s trace with
the associated client
C(t)
.
If
C(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
then
t\leq s
.

Proof
. Let us denote with
\tilde{R}_{j},\tilde{A}_{j}

the set of requests and the set of answers, respectively,
which are executed in the trace
s
between
the last synchronization
d_{j-1}
and the first synchronization on

d_{j}
, for
j=1,\ldots,n
.
We stress that we are
not
assuming that the requests are executed
before the answers as it is the case when looking at trace
t
.
Requests and answers can be interleaved in an arbitrary way as long as they
respect the rule that a request
must come before the corresponding answer.

We also denote with
\tilde{A}_{0}
the set of answers executed before
the last synchronization on
d_{0}
and with
\tilde{R}_{n+1}
the
set of requests executed after the last synchronization on

d_{n}
.
Finally, we denote with
\tilde{R}_{n+2}
the set of requests
which have no answer in
t
and which are not executed at all in
s
.

We are going to show
t\leq s
by describing a strategy to reduce

t
to
s
. To start with, we label every request and answer in
t

with a number which describes its final position in
s
.
We distinguish
4
cases.

1.

r\in R_{i}^{-}
and
r\notin\bigcup_{j=1,\ldots,n+1}\tilde{R}_{j}
.
This is a request without answer which is in
t
but not in
s
.
Assign to it the position
n+2
.

2.

r\in R_{i}^{-}
and
r\in\tilde{R}_{j}
.
This a request without answer which is both in
t
and in
s
.
Because of the structure of
C(t)
we must have
j\geq i
.
Assign to it the position
j
.

3.

a\in A_{i}^{-}
. This is an answer with no request.
Because of the structure of
C(t)
,

a
must be in
s
too, say
a\in\tilde{A}_{j}
with
j\leq i
.
Assign to it the position
j
.

4.

r\in R_{i}^{+}
and
a(r)\in A_{j}^{+}
. This is a request
with corresponding answer. Because of the structure of
C(t)
we
must have
r\in\tilde{R}_{h}
and
a(r)\in\tilde{A}_{k}
with

i\leq h\leq k\leq j
. Assign to
r
the position
h
and
to
a(r)
the position
k
.

Let us label every request and every answer with its intended position and
denote the positions with
\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ldots

Then consider the following rewriting rules where
i\neq j
and
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
:

\begin{array}[]{llll}(L_{1})&tr_{i}^{\ell}r_{j}^{\ell^{\prime}}t^{\prime}&\rightarrow&tr_{j}^{\ell^{\prime}}r_{i}^{\ell}t^{\prime}\\
(L_{2})&ta_{i}^{\ell}a_{j}^{\ell^{\prime}}t^{\prime}&\rightarrow&ta_{j}^{\ell^{\prime}}a_{i}^{\ell}t^{\prime}\\
(L_{3})&tr_{i}^{\ell}a_{j}^{\ell^{\prime}}t^{\prime}&\rightarrow&ta_{j}^{\ell^{\prime}}r_{i}^{\ell}t^{\prime}\end{array}

These rules are just a
restricted
version of the rules
(R_{1}-R_{3})
;
a reduction according to rules
(L_{1}-L_{3})
is a legal reduction
according to the rules
(R_{1}-R_{3})
as soon as we erase the labels.
Call
inversion a pair
of symbols
x^{\ell},y^{\ell^{\prime}}
in the trace where
x
and
y

are requests or answers,
x^{\ell}
precedes
y^{\ell^{\prime}}
(not necessarily immediately) and
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
.
Every reduction sequence starting from a labelled trace
t
will terminate
since each reduction reduces the total number of inversions.
We notice that if an answer
a^{\ell}
is followed on the right
(not necessarily immediately followed) by a request

r^{\ell^{\prime}}
in
t
then necessarily
\ell<\ell^{\prime}
. This means that
to order the symbols by growing position we never need to commute
an answer
a^{\ell}
with a request
r^{\ell^{\prime}}
where
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
.
It follows that upon termination, the trace has
the structure:

t_{1}=\tilde{A}_{0}\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{A}_{1}\cdots\tilde{R}_{n}\tilde{A}_{n}\tilde{R}_{n+1}\tilde{R}_{n+2}~.

Then we can get rid of the requests in
\tilde{R}_{n+2}

by applying rule
(R_{4})
.

As a third and final step, we have to reduce each subtrace

\tilde{R}_{j}\tilde{A}_{j}
to the corresponding subtrace in
s
.
But this is always possible: the trace where all requests come before
all answers can always be rewritten to any trace which contains the
same requests and the same answers in some legal order.
First commute the requests so that their relative positions are
the same as in
s
. Then do the same for the answers. Finally,
use induction on the
length of the trace to reduce the first trace to the second by
applying rule
(R_{3})
.
{\Box}

Example 31.4

We illustrate the strategy described in the proof above.
Suppose:

\begin{array}[]{llll}t=r_{1}a_{0}r_{2}r_{3}a_{2}r_{4}a_{1}a_{3},&s=a_{0}r_{2}a_{2}r_{3}a_{3}r_{1}a_{1}\end{array}

So looking at the alternations in
t
, we have:

R_{1}=\{r_{1}\},A_{1}=\{a_{0}\},R_{2}=\{r_{2},r_{3}\},A_{2}=\{a_{2}\},R_{3}=\{r_{4}\},A_{3}=\{a_{1},a_{3}\}~.

In this case,
A_{0}=R_{4}=\emptyset
and the ‘synchronization barriers’
are on the names
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
, and
d_{3}
.
Now suppose that according to these barriers, in
s
we have:

\tilde{R}_{1}=\emptyset,\tilde{A}_{1}=\{a_{0}\},\tilde{R}_{2}=\{r_{2}\},\tilde{A}_{2}=\{a_{2}\},\tilde{R}_{3}=\{r_{1},r_{3}\},\tilde{A}_{3}=\{a_{1},a_{3}\}~.

This induces the following labelling of the elements in
t
:

r_{1}^{3}a_{0}^{1}r_{2}^{2}r_{3}^{3}a_{2}^{2}r_{4}^{5}a_{1}^{3}a_{3}^{3}~.

If we sort these elements with respect to the position following,
e.g., a bubblesort algorithm we end up with the following trace:

a_{0}^{1}r_{2}^{2}a_{2}^{2}r_{1}^{3}r_{3}^{3}a_{1}^{3}a_{3}^{3}r_{4}^{5}

Now, if we drop the positions and erase the last request with rule
(R_{4})
, we get:

a_{0}r_{2}a_{2}r_{1}r_{3}a_{1}a_{3}~.

Then we rewrite so that the relative positions of the requests and
the answers are the same as in
s
:

a_{0}r_{2}a_{2}r_{3}r_{1}a_{3}a_{1}~,

and finally we operate on the subtrace
r_{3}r_{1}a_{3}a_{1}
to make it
equal to
r_{3}a_{3}r_{1}a_{1}
thus getting to
s
.

We are now ready to prove the announced result.

Proposition 31.6

Let
p_{1},p_{2}\in O(N_{1},N_{2})
be objects.
If
p_{1}\leq_{{\sf may}}p_{2}
then
p_{1}\leq_{T}^{o}p_{2}
.

Proof
. Suppose
p_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t}}{{\Rightarrow}}
and consider the client
C(t)
.
Then
(p_{1}\mid C(t))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Rightarrow}}
by proposition
31.4
(2).
By the hypothesis, it follows that
(p_{2}\mid C(t))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Rightarrow}}
.
So there must be a trace
s
such that
p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\Rightarrow}}
and

C(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{s}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\rightarrow}}
. But then, by proposition
31.5
,

t\leq s
.
{\Box}

Example 31.5
(boolean variable, again)

We go back to the possible implementations of a boolean
variable object. Suppose

S
is the sequential specification described in Table
31.10

and
V_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
are the three variants given in
Table
31.11
.
We have:

\begin{array}[]{lll}V_{2}\leq_{{\sf may}}S=_{{\sf may}}V_{3}\leq_{{\sf may}}V_{1}&\mbox{but}&S\not\leq_{{\sf may}}V_{2}~\mbox{ and }~V_{1}\not\leq_{{\sf may}}S~.\end{array}

For simplicity, let us assume the initial value in the variable is
0
.
One may argue that
V_{2}
should be rejected as it is unable to
produce traces in the sequential specification (cf. exercise
31.2
).
For instance,
assuming the initial value in the variable is
0
, consider
the object’s trace
t=(w_{1},1)\overline{(w,1)}
.
On the other hand,
V_{1}
should be rejected as it may produce
traces which cannot be linearized to a trace of the
sequential specification. For instance, consider the object’s trace

t=(w_{1},1)\overline{(w,1)}(r,2)\overline{(r_{0},2)}
.
These considerations suggest may-testing equivalence or equivalently
trace equality up to rewriting as a basic and necessary
correctness criteria for concurrent objects.
However, one should keep in mind that this criteria is based
on a generalized version of trace equivalence;
it is not difficult to imagine buggy variants of the object

V_{3}
which are still may-equivalent to the sequential specification

S
but that may diverge or produce a deadlock. For instance,
consider the variants in Table
31.14
.
It seems an interesting (research) exercise to characterize
testing pre-orders on objects
which are sensitive to termination and deadlock (see chapter
26
).

\begin{array}[]{lll}A(x)&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.A(x)+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(\overline{x})+w_{\overline{x}}(n).B(x,n)&(V_{4})\\
B(x,n^{\prime})&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.B(x,n^{\prime})+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n^{\prime})}.\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+\tau.B(x,n^{\prime})\\
\\
A(x)&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.A(x)+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)+w_{\overline{x}}(n).\overline{(w,n)}.A(\overline{x})+w_{\overline{x}}(n).B(x,n)&(V_{5})\\
B(x,n^{\prime})&=r(n).\overline{(r,n)}.B(x,n^{\prime})+w_{x}(n).\overline{(w,n^{\prime})}.\overline{(w,n)}.A(x)\end{array}

Table 31.14
:
Two more (buggy) variants of the sequential object for a boolean variable

31.7
Summary and references

We have reviewed some basic notions of concurrent programming in

{\it Java}
and formalized the reduction and typing rules of a tiny
fragment of it.
{\it Java}
’s synchronization builds on the notion of

monitor
which is described in

[
Hoa74
,
Han75
]
. The
notion of
linearizability
is introduced in

[
HW90
]
.
The introduction of a universal construction to
transform any ‘sequential’ object into a ‘concurrent’ one without
introducing locks is due to
[
Her91
]
.
The article
[
GK97
]
analyzes
the complexity of determining if a trace is linearizable and
the article
[
DD15
]
surveys various proof
techniques for verifying linearizability.
The notion of linearizability is connected to a
notion of observational refinement in

[
FORY10
]
.
In this chapter, we have tried to cast this connection in the general
framework of labelled transition systems with synchronization.
